### [1] [IceCube data for astrophysical neutrinos exhibit q-statistical behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14129)
*Constantino Tsallis,Ugur Tirnakli*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: IceCube astrophysical neutrino spectra are well fitted by q-Gaussians across all measured energies, yielding a high nonadditive index q ≈ 1.6, which suggests very-long-ranged space-time correlations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper seeks to extend evidence that nonadditive entropies (of the S_q or S_delta class) play a crucial role in systems such as black holes and cosmological phenomena, by testing whether astrophysical neutrino data follow the same q-Gaussian description previously observed in cosmic rays and LHC proton-proton collisions.

Method: The author analyzes recent relevant IceCube astrophysical neutrino data and fits the corresponding energy spectra over the entire measured energy interval using q-Gaussians, comparing the fitted momenta-distribution nonadditive index q with values obtained for cosmic rays and LHC collisions.

Result: The IceCube spectra are satisfactorily described by q-Gaussians across the full measured energy range. The fitted nonadditive index is q ≈ 1.6, sensibly higher than the cosmic-ray value (q ≈ 1.2) and the LHC value (q ≈ 1.1).

Conclusion: The high q value reinforces existing evidence for the relevance of nonadditive entropies in black holes and cosmological phenomena, and neatly indicates very-long-ranged space-time correlations. The detailed associated physical mechanisms remain an instigating open issue at the present stage.

Abstract: Relevant IceCube recent data for astrophysical neutrinos are analyzed. Similarly to cosmic rays and proton-proton LHC collisions, the corresponding spectra are satisfactorily described, for the entire interval of measured energies, through q-Gaussians. This fact reinforces the already available evidence that nonadditive entropies (either of the $S_q$ class or of the $S_{delta}$ one, or even alternative ones) appear to play a crucial role in systems such as black holes and related cosmological phenomena (e.g., dark energy). The IceCube data yield sensibly high values for the momenta-distribution nonadditive index (q \simeq 1.6), in contrast with the cosmic rays values (q \simeq 1.2) and the LHC values (q \simeq 1.1). This fact neatly indicates very-long-ranged space-time correlations. The detailed associated physical mechanisms remain, at the present stage, as an instigating open issue.

</details>


### [2] [Competitive scaling of small-subsystem-optimization heuristics for LABS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14361)
*Ginger E. Lau,Adil A. Gangat*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Iteratively optimizing very small, decoupled subsystems of n=4 spins globally optimizes the LABS problem with a time-to-solution scaling of O(1.333^N), competitive with state-of-the-art direct global heuristics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LABS is formulated as a spin-lattice energy-minimization problem with a very rugged energy landscape, making it a hard combinatorial optimization challenge. The authors aim to show that optimizing small decoupled subsystems can match heuristics that optimize the full N-spin system directly, suggesting small-scale specialized hardware could be practically useful.

Method: An isolated-subsystem heuristic that iteratively optimizes subsystems of n spins decoupled from the rest of the system, tested with n = 4 (with comparison to direct global optimization heuristics).

Result: The n = 4 isolated-subsystem heuristic achieves time-to-solution scaling of O(1.333^N) on N = [27, 60], compared with O(1.34^N) for the current state-of-the-art classical approach and O(1.21^N) for the quantum approach.

Conclusion: Optimizing small decoupled subsystems can achieve global LABS optimization with competitive scaling, implying that specialized optimization hardware with only a small number of logical spins may be practically relevant.

Abstract: The low autocorrelation binary sequences (LABS) problem, often formulated as a spin-lattice energy-minimization problem, is a combinatorial optimization problem with a very rugged energy landscape. We demonstrate that iterative optimization of very small subsystems of n spins that are decoupled from the rest of the system achieves global optimization of LABS with a time-to-solution scaling that is competitive with heuristics that optimize the global system of N spins directly. Our isolated-subsystem heuristic with n = 4 achieves a time scaling of O(1.333N ) on the interval N = [27, 60], while the current state-of-the-art classical and quantum scalings are respectively O(1.34N ) and O(1.21N ). Our results suggest that specialized optimization hardware with only small numbers of logical spins may be of practical relevance.

</details>


### [3] [Extended-range percolation on lattices with cubic and non-compact neighborhoods in three dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15441)
*Zhe Zhang,Yuxuan Fan,Yang Yang,Yusong Tu,Zhipeng Xun*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Monte Carlo simulations using an efficient single-cluster growth algorithm determine high-precision percolation thresholds for ~100 extended-range percolation structures, revealing scaling relations between thresholds and lattice topology, and identifying ζ as an effective topological index governing p_c.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extended-range percolation models with cubic and non-compact neighborhoods are not well characterized, particularly how percolation thresholds relate to lattice topology beyond simple coordination number z, and whether a single index can remove degeneracy across different lattice types.

Method: MC simulations with an efficient single-cluster growth algorithm; analysis of bond and site percolation for cubic neighborhoods on SC lattice; examination of site percolation for non-compact neighborhoods up to the fifth coordination zone on SC, FCC, and BCC lattices; comparison of topological indices z, ξ=Σz_i r_i²/i, and ζ=Σz_i r_i for capturing threshold dependence.

Result: High-precision p_c for ~100 structures. Bond percolation: zp_c→1 asymptotically with finite-z correction zp_c-1~a_1 z^{-x}, x=(d-1)/d. Site percolation: zp_c varies non-monotonically with z, yielding zp_c~2.5987(1) for large z and continuum threshold η_c=0.324838(12). ζ effectively removes degeneracy; p_c∝(ζ/K)^{-γ} with γ^ζ_SC=0.703(12), γ^ζ_FCC=0.728(10), γ^ζ_BCC=0.699(12), and p_c∝ζ^{-γ} with γ^ζ=0.719(9) lattice-independent.

Conclusion: The topological index ζ=Σz_i r_i outperforms z and ξ in removing degeneracy of percolation thresholds across lattices; p_c follows a universal power law p_c∝ζ^{-γ} with γ≈0.719 independent of underlying lattice, and bond percolation approaches Bethe-lattice behavior with corrections governed by x=(d-1)/d.

Abstract: Using MC simulations based on an efficient single-cluster growth algorithm, we investigate two types of three-dimensional extended-range percolation models, namely cubic and complex (non-compact) neighborhoods. We obtain high-precision percolation thresholds $p_c$ for approximately 100 distinct structures and further explore the relationship between these thresholds and the topology of the lattices, which includes the total coordination number $z$, the number of sites $z_i$ and these sites' distance to the central site $r_i$ of the given neighborhood in the $i$th coordination zone. Both bond and site percolation are analyzed for cubic neighborhoods on the SC lattice. For bond percolation, the asymptotic value of $zp_c$ tends to Bethe-lattice behavior ($zp_c\sim1$), and finite-$z$ correction is found to be consistent with $zp_{c}-1\sim a_{1}z^{-x}$, where $x=(d-1)/d$. For site percolation, the value of $zp_c$ varies non-monotonically, exhibiting an initial increase followed by a decrease with the coordination number $z$. Linear fitting leads to $zp_c\sim2.5987(1)$ for large $z$, predicting a value of continuum percolation threshold of aligned cubes $η_c=0.324838(12)$ using $zp_c=2^{d}η_c$. We then examine site percolation for non-compact neighborhoods extending to the fifth coordination zone on SC, FCC, and BCC lattices. Through a comparison of the three indices of $z$, $ξ=\sum_iz_ir_i^2/i$, and $ζ=\sum_iz_ir_i$, we find that $ζ$ is effective in removing the degeneracy. The results obtained show power-law dependence $p_c\propto(ζ/K)^{-γ}$ with $γ_{\mathrm{SC}}^ζ=0.703(12)$, $γ_{\mathrm{FCC}}^ζ=0.728(10)$, and $γ_{\mathrm{BCC}}^ζ=0.699(12)$ for SC, FCC, and BCC lattices, and $p_c\proptoζ^{-γ}$ with $γ^ζ=0.719(9)$ independent of the underlying lattice. The number $K$ indicates lattice connectivity.

</details>


### [4] [Ergodicity breaking transition beyond qubits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15546)
*Tanay Pathak,Abhik Kumar Saha*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper studies how local Hilbert space dimension q controls the ergodicity-breaking transition in quantum many-body systems, using zero-dimensional qudit toy models, and derives a critical interaction strength that depends on q.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While the local Hilbert space dimension is a fundamental parameter in quantum many-body systems, its role in controlling the ergodicity-breaking transition remains relatively unexplored, motivating a systematic investigation.

Method: The authors introduce toy models of qudits in zero dimensions exhibiting an ergodicity-breaking transition. They analytically derive the dependence of the critical interaction value on the local Hilbert space dimension using the hybridization condition, and support this with numerical studies of spectral correlation measures. They also construct a distinct higher-spin version of the model and numerically analyze its critical behavior.

Result: The analytical prediction for the critical interaction value as a function of local Hilbert space dimension agrees with numerical spectral correlation results, confirming the ergodicity-breaking transition. The higher-spin variant yields the same critical behavior, indicating the critical point is independent of the microscopic structure of interactions.

Conclusion: The ergodicity-breaking transition in these zero-dimensional qudit models is governed by the local Hilbert space dimension, with a critical interaction strength analytically predicted and numerically verified, and the universal critical behavior is robust against changes in the microscopic interaction structure.

Abstract: The local Hilbert space dimension is a fundamental parameter in quantum many-body systems, but its role in controlling the ergodicity-breaking transition remains less explored. We systematically study this question by introducing toy models of qudits in zero dimensions that exhibit an ergodicity-breaking transition. Using the hybridization condition, we analytically obtain the dependence of the critical value of the interactions on the local Hilbert space dimension. Numerical studies of spectral correlation measures show agreement with the theoretical predictions, hence confirming the existence of the ergodicity-breaking transition in these models. We further introduce a distinct higher spin version of the model and numerically obtain the same critical behavior, demonstrating that the observed critical point is independent of the microscopic structure of interactions considered.

</details>


### [5] [Developing the Statistical Mechanics of Evolving Assembly Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15695)
*Anisha K. Yeddanapudi,Abhishek Sharma,Ian Seet,Leroy Cronin*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper integrates assembly theory (AT) with stochastic thermodynamics to explain how environmental influences drive physical systems to generate novel, complex objects, defining selection within large combinatorial spaces and quantifying the work needed to create complex objects via a memory-feedback assembler framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A gap exists in connecting biological evolution to statistical mechanics. While statistical mechanics was hoped to provide a general predictive principle for the emergence of novelty and complexity, stochastic-driven novel functional features are difficult to describe because such processes typically occur outside near-equilibrium or linear-response regimes. Understanding how environmental influences generate diverse, open-ended, complex objects by driving physical systems to specific phase-space regions over long spatial and temporal scales requires a new approach to non-equilibrium thermodynamics.

Method: The authors couple assembly theory (AT) with statistical mechanics as a thermodynamic-compliant framework. They map construction processes from physical space to assembly space (AS) and introduce the "assembler"—a machine that constructs objects contingent on previous states, represented by bitwise operations. They then apply principles from stochastic thermodynamics together with AT to define selection in large combinatorial spaces and to estimate the work required to create novel and complex objects.

Result: The approach allows complex processes such as selection within large combinatorial spaces to be represented as a set of rules for a memory-feedback loop followed by the assembler. It concretely defines relationships between thermodynamics and information theory by introducing a physics aspect that uses history and causal contingency to influence the probabilistic time evolution of complex systems.

Conclusion: By integrating AT with stochastic thermodynamics, the framework contextualizes novelty, complexity, and evolution within thermodynamic laws. It yields a novel physical principle in which history and causal contingency shape the probabilistic time evolution of complex systems, bridging non-equilibrium thermodynamics with information theory.

Abstract: A gap exists in connecting biological evolution within the framework of statistical mechanics. It has been hoped that statistical mechanics would provide us with a general predictive principle for the emergence of novelty and complexity. However, the emergence of novel functional features initiated by stochastic processes remains difficult to describe, because these processes commonly arise outside near-equilibrium or linear-response regimes. To understand how environmental influences generate diverse, open-ended, complex objects by driving physical systems to specific regions of phase space over long spatial and temporal scales, a new approach to non-equilibrium thermodynamics is necessary. Here, we present the coupling of assembly theory (AT) with statistical mechanics as a framework that works within the laws of thermodynamics, but can contextualise the processes of novelty, complexity, and evolution within these laws. We map construction processes from physical space to assembly space (AS) and introduce the concept of an assembler, a machine that constructs objects contingent on previous states and is represented by bitwise operations. Using this approach, we apply principles from stochastic thermodynamics with AT to define selection within large combinatorial spaces1 and estimate the work required to create novel and complex objects. This approach allows us to represent complex processes, such as selection, within large combinatorial spaces as a set of rules for a memory-feedback loop followed by the assembler. This framework concretely defines the relationships between thermodynamics and information theory by introducing a novel aspect of physics that uses history and causal contingency to influence the probabilistic time evolution of complex systems.

</details>


### [6] [Universal Boolean Computation in Negatively Coupled Circle Map Lattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16290)
*Priyanka D. Bhoyar,Prashant M. Gade*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Traveling waves in a negatively coupled circle-map lattice are used as information carriers; their collisions implement NAND/NOR gates, establishing universal Boolean computation in principle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper explores whether computational functionality can emerge from the collective nonlinear dynamics of a coupled map lattice, specifically linking traveling-wave dynamics to collision-based information processing.

Method: A one-dimensional lattice of negatively coupled circle maps is studied in its traveling wave regime. Logical states are encoded via wave propagation direction, and the presence/absence of a wave at an output location gives the logical output. Controlled wave collisions are exploited to implement Boolean logic gates.

Result: NAND and NOR gates, along with other basic gates, are realized via controlled wave collisions. Since NAND/NOR are functionally complete, the system supports universal Boolean computation in principle. The operations remain valid over a finite range of coupling strength ε and map parameter ω, indicating robustness to parameter variations.

Conclusion: Computational functionality can emerge from the collective nonlinear dynamics of a coupled map lattice, connecting traveling-wave dynamical properties with collision-based information processing.

Abstract: We investigate the computational capabilities of a one-dimensional lattice of negatively
  coupled circle maps in its traveling wave regime. We interpret propagating waves as information carriers and exploit their interactions to implement Boolean logic. The logical states are encoded by the propagation direction of the traveling waves, while the presence or absence of a wave at a designated output location represents the logical output. Using controlled wave collisions, we demonstrate the realization of NAND and NOR gates along with other basic gates. Since NAND and NOR operations are functionally complete, the results establish that the traveling wave regime of the negatively coupled circle-map lattice supports universal Boolean computation in principle. The demonstrated logical operations remain valid over a finite range of the coupling strength εand map parameter ω, indicating robustness of the computational scheme to parameter variations. Our results therefore demonstrate that computational functionality can emerge from the collective nonlinear dynamics of a coupled map lattice, providing a con- nection between the dynamical properties of traveling wave states and collision based information processing.

</details>


### [7] [Mutual linearity from classical response theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16894)
*Timur Aslyamov,Massimiliano Esposito*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper unifies mutual-linearity relations for Markov jump processes and chemical reaction networks using classical finite-response theory, showing an exact finite-response identity that underlies these relations even far from equilibrium.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mutual-linearity (or reciprocity-like) relations were known separately for Markov jump processes and chemical reaction networks, but lacked a common derivation. The authors aim to provide a unified framework that explains why these relations hold, including far from equilibrium.

Method: The authors employ classical finite-response theory. For a Markov jump process, they derive an exact finite-response identity stating that all stationary probability changes share a common factor when the rate of one edge is perturbed. Eliminating this common factor yields relations among probabilities, observables, and currents. They then extend the same argument to the case where one coefficient of a linear system changes, and to chemical reaction networks via Laplace-space and stationary complex-activity relations.

Result: The paper obtains an exact finite-response identity for Markov jump processes, from which relationships between stationary probabilities, observables, and currents follow by eliminating the common factor. The same reasoning recovers known relations in Laplace space and for stationary complex activities in chemical reaction networks, demonstrating the generality of the framework.

Conclusion: Classical finite-response theory provides a common basis for mutual-linearity relations in Markov jump processes and chemical reaction networks, valid even far from equilibrium, thereby unifying apparently distinct results.

Abstract: We unify known mutual-linearity relations for Markov jump processes and chemical reaction networks within a common framework based on classical finite-response theory. In a Markov jump process, an exact finite-response identity shows that all stationary probability changes share a common factor when the rates of one edge change. Eliminating this factor gives the relations between probabilities, observables, and currents, even far from equilibrium. We also explain how the same argument applies when one coefficient of a linear system changes and recovers the known relations in Laplace space and between stationary complex activities in chemical reaction networks.

</details>


### [8] [Proof of the exact correlation-length exponent $ν= \frac{\sqrt[3]{2}}{2}$ and algebraic relations for $β$ and $γ$ in the 3D Ising model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17797)
*Aleksandr Liivand*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper claims an exact value for the correlation-length exponent of the 3D Ising model, ν = ∛2 / 2, and derives exact algebraic expressions for the critical exponents γ and β that depend solely on this ν.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to establish exact, closed-form values for the critical exponents of the 3D Ising model, building on earlier critical-exponent estimates and scaling relations, and to match the most precise numerical estimates available.

Method: The authors use a group theoretical approach combined with propositions of constructive geometry, building on prior results on critical exponent estimates and scaling relations, to construct algebraic expressions for the exponents.

Result: The exact correlation-length exponent is proven to be ν = ∛2 / 2, in very close agreement with the most precise current numerical estimates. Exact algebraic expressions for γ and β were derived, shown to depend solely on the exact value of ν.

Conclusion: The critical exponents γ and β of the 3D Ising model admit exact algebraic forms determined entirely by ν = ∛2 / 2, supporting the proposed exact value through agreement with high-precision numerics.

Abstract: Based on our previous results regarding critical exponent estimates for the 3D Ising model and scaling relations [1], we have proven that the exact value of the correlation-length exponent is $\frac{\sqrt[3]{2}}{2}$. This is in very close agreement with the most precise numerical estimates currently available. Using a group theoretical approach and some propositions of constructive geometry, we derived an exact construction of algebraic expressions for the critical exponents $γ$ and $β$. We proved that these expressions depend solely on the exact value of $ν\,$.

</details>


### [9] [Extending the mean first-passage-time formalism to the reconstruction of high free-energy barriers: A top-start approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18653)
*Ying Jiang,Ivan Saika-Voivod*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper introduces a "top-start" extension of the mean first-passage time (MFPT) formalism that initiates trajectories near the barrier top to reconstruct free-energy profiles, remaining accurate for high free-energy barriers where conventional MFPT sampling is inefficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rare events such as crystal nucleation in mildly supercooled liquids have waiting times far exceeding the timescale of microscopic dynamics. The MFPT formalism can reconstruct one-dimensional free-energy profiles from the MFPT and steady-state distribution along a reaction coordinate, but conventional implementations become inefficient at sufficiently high barriers because spontaneous crossings are rarely observed.

Method: A "top-start" extension is proposed: trajectories are initiated near the barrier top and the free-energy profile is reconstructed from their subsequent evolution away from the barrier toward lower-free-energy regions. The equivalence between the top-start and two-region constructions is established using a Markov-chain transition-matrix formulation. The method is validated with one-dimensional random-walk simulations across low, medium, and high free-energy barriers, with transition-matrix calculations serving as an exact benchmark.

Result: The top-start reconstruction remains accurate in the high-barrier regime where conventional MFPT sampling becomes prohibitively inefficient, and the transition-matrix calculations provide an exact benchmark for the corresponding discretized models.

Conclusion: The top-start extension overcomes the inefficiency of conventional MFPT sampling for high free-energy barriers, providing an accurate route to reconstruct free-energy profiles in rare-event regimes.

Abstract: Rare events, such as crystal nucleation in mildly supercooled liquids, are characterized by waiting times that can be much longer than the timescale of the underlying microscopic dynamics. The mean first-passage time (MFPT) formalism [J. Wedekind and D. Reguera, J. Phys. Chem. B 112, 11060 (2008)] provides a route to reconstruct one-dimensional free-energy profiles from the MFPT and steady-state distribution measured along a reaction coordinate, but conventional implementations become inefficient for sufficiently high barriers because spontaneous crossings are rarely observed. We introduce a "top-start" extension that initiates trajectories near the barrier top and reconstructs the free-energy profile from their subsequent evolution away from the barrier toward lower-free-energy regions. We establish the equivalence between the top-start and two-region constructions using a Markov-chain transition-matrix formulation. We validate the method using one-dimensional random-walk simulations across low, medium, and high free-energy barriers, with transition-matrix calculations providing an exact benchmark for the corresponding discretized models. The top-start reconstruction remains accurate in the high-barrier regime where conventional MFPT sampling becomes prohibitively inefficient.

</details>


### [10] [Organisation and aggregation of calcium carbonate ions in water via molecular simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18128)
*Elizaveta Sidler,Rita S Dias,Raffaela Cabriolu*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This study uses OPES and infinite-swap replica exchange transition interface sampling to link the formation of the first CaCO3 coordination sphere to concentration-dependent ionic aggregation, revealing two distinct formation pathways at both supersaturation levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CaCO3 crystallisation is central to CO2 sequestration and sustainable material production, but classical nucleation theory poorly describes it because ionic crystals often form via amorphous precursor phases. A better description of early-stage ordering and its relation to ionic aggregation is needed.

Method: Two complementary simulations: on-the-fly probability-enhanced sampling (OPES) for free-energy landscapes, and asynchronous infinite-swap replica exchange transition interface sampling (infinity-RETIS) for reaction kinetics. Systems were studied at two supersaturation levels.

Result: The first coordination sphere showed distinct kinetic behaviour between the two concentrations, linked to concentration-dependent aggregation: limited aggregation at low concentration versus extensive spinodal-like aggregation at high concentration. At both concentrations, the first coordination sphere forms within pre-formed clusters via one of two pathways: within a cluster near the minimum size required, or within larger amorphous aggregates whose size and structure depend on concentration.

Conclusion: The coexistence of two pathways at both supersaturation levels reveals the multifaceted nature of early-stage CaCO3 crystallisation and underscores the importance of distinguishing ionic aggregation from organisation.

Abstract: Crystallisation of calcium carbonate (CaCO$_3$) in aqueous solutions is essential for carbon dioxide sequestration and the sustainable production of calcium carbonate materials, which are widely used in industry and increasingly explored for novel applications. While crystallisation is often analysed within the framework of classical nucleation theory, CaCO$_3$ and other ionic crystals often form through amorphous precursor phases, motivating the need for alternative descriptions. In this work, we investigate the relationship between the formation of the first CaCO$_3$ coordination sphere, a fundamental unit of local ordering, and ionic aggregation using two complementary simulation approaches: on-the-fly probability-enhanced sampling (OPES) and asynchronous and infinite-swap replica exchange transition interface sampling ($\infty$RETIS). OPES was used to explore free-energy landscapes, whereas $\infty$RETIS was used to determine reaction kinetics. We studied systems at two levels of supersaturation, between which the formation of the first coordination sphere exhibited distinct kinetic behaviour. These differences were found to be linked to concentration-dependent ionic aggregation. At lower concentration, aggregation remains limited, whereas higher concentration promotes extensive spinodal-like aggregation. For both concentrations, the first coordination sphere forms within the pre-formed clusters through one of two distinct pathways: either within a cluster close to the minimum size required for its formation, or within larger amorphous aggregates whose size and structure depend on concentration. The coexistence of these pathways at both levels of supersaturation reveals the multifaceted nature of early-stage CaCO$_3$ crystallisation and highlights the importance of differentiating ionic aggregation and organisation.

</details>


### [11] [Molecular-Dynamics Verification of Effective Gravity under Heat Conduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18250)
*Satoshi Uruno,Naoko Nakagawa*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Molecular dynamics of a 2D Lennard-Jones fluid under weak heat conduction confirms the effective-gravity prediction of global thermodynamics: the liquid-gas layering transition shifts from zero to finite downward gravity, with the observed shift matching predictions from measured saturation pressures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether global thermodynamics can quantitatively predict nonequilibrium structure formation, specifically the effective-gravity prediction for phase layering in a fluid subjected to a temperature gradient.

Method: Standard molecular dynamics simulations of a two-dimensional Lennard-Jones fluid under weak heat conduction, with the lower boundary hotter than the upper boundary. Independently measured equilibrium saturation pressures are used to compute the predicted transition shift, which is compared with the observed transition and hysteresis behavior plotted against effective gravity.

Result: The transition between liquid-below-gas and liquid-above-gas shifts from zero to finite downward gravity. The shift predicted by global thermodynamics agrees with the observed transition shift. The transition occurs at zero effective gravity, where hydrostatic pressure difference balances the saturation-pressure difference between boundaries. Hysteresis under heat conduction matches the equilibrium response when plotted against effective gravity.

Conclusion: Global thermodynamics is predictive for nonequilibrium structure formation, as demonstrated by its quantitative agreement with molecular dynamics results for phase layering under a temperature gradient.

Abstract: We test the effective-gravity prediction of global thermodynamics using standard molecular dynamics of a two-dimensional Lennard-Jones fluid. Under weak heat conduction with the lower boundary hotter than the upper boundary, the transition between liquid below gas and liquid above gas shifts from zero to finite downward gravity. Using independently measured equilibrium saturation pressures, we calculate the shift predicted by global thermodynamics and find agreement with the observed transition shift. The transition occurs at zero effective gravity, where the hydrostatic pressure difference balances the saturation-pressure difference between the two boundaries. The hysteresis response under heat conduction also agrees with the equilibrium response when plotted against effective gravity. These results establish the predictive power of global thermodynamics for nonequilibrium structure formation.

</details>


### [12] [Charge-selective topological locking and ultraslow coarsening in magnetoelastic media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18320)
*Tirthankar Banerjee,Amaresh Jaiswal*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The symmetry of magnetoelastic coupling controls how magnetic vortices bind elastic half-defects and qualitatively changes phase-ordering kinetics, with quadrupolar coupling producing anomalously slow coarsening (z ≈ 5) driven by anisotropic elastic friction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to understand how the symmetry of magnetoelastic coupling governs the topological organization of composite (magnetic + elastic) defects and whether it can qualitatively alter phase-ordering kinetics, a question not addressed by prior work.

Method: A two-dimensional theory of an in-plane magnetic vector field coupled to elastic strain is analyzed. Integer magnetic vortices are shown to bind pairs of elementary elastic half-defects. Isotropic vs. quadrupolar magnetoelastic couplings are compared, and an adiabatic elimination of the elastic field is performed to derive effective magnetic interactions and identify the origin of the slowdown.

Result: Integer magnetic vortices bind pairs of elementary elastic half-defects. Isotropic magnetostriction yields core-induced elastic winding independent of magnetic charge, giving charge-unlocked binding and XY-like coarsening. Quadrupolar coupling locks charges (q_φ = q_θ favored by continuous rotational symmetry; q_φ = -q_θ under crystalline anisotropy) and produces anomalously slow vortex annihilation: defect density ~ t^(-2/5), average defect velocity ~ t^(-4/5), consistent with dynamic exponent z ≈ 5, attributed to large anisotropic elastic friction.

Conclusion: Magnetoelastic coupling symmetry offers a controllable route to manipulate defect topology and ordering kinetics. Isotropic magnetostriction only renormalizes local magnetic interactions, whereas orientational (quadrupolar) coupling generates effective nonlocal spin interactions and slow, friction-dominated coarsening. The predictions are experimentally testable.

Abstract: We demonstrate that the symmetry of magnetoelastic coupling governs the topological organization of composite defects and can qualitatively alter phase-ordering kinetics. In a two-dimensional theory of an in-plane magnetic vector coupled to elastic strain, we find that integer magnetic vortices bind pairs of elementary elastic half-defects. Isotropic magnetostriction produces a core-induced elastic winding independent of the magnetic charge, giving defect binding without charge locking and leaving the magnetic sector with essentially XY-like coarsening. In contrast, quadrupolar magnetoelastic coupling locks magnetic and elastic defect charges: continuous rotational symmetry favors $q_φ=q_θ$, whereas crystalline anisotropy can reverse the locking to $q_φ=-q_θ$. We reveal that both locking sectors display anomalously slow vortex annihilation: defect density shows a power-law decay $t^{-2/5}$, while the average defect-velocity scales as $t^{-4/5}$. These results are consistent with a dynamic exponent $z\simeq 5$. We show that this coarsening slowdown is associated with large anisotropic elastic friction. An adiabatic elimination of the elastic field reveals that isotropic magnetostriction merely renormalizes local magnetic interactions, whereas orientational coupling induces effective nonlocal interactions between magnetic spins. Our experimentally testable results thus establish magnetoelastic coupling symmetry as a means of controlling defect topology and ordering kinetics.

</details>


### [13] [Macroscopic fluctuation theory of stochastic systems undergoing monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18500)
*João Costa,Pedro Ribeiro,Andrea De Luca*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper develops a macroscopic fluctuation theory (MFT) for diffusive classical lattice gases under continuous coarse-grained monitoring to study how measurement affects current fluctuations and dynamical phase transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monitoring quantum and classical systems alters their fluctuations, yet a systematic large-deviation framework for continuously measured diffusive lattice gases was lacking. The authors aim to understand how observation modifies the MFT action, the convexity of the current large-deviation function, the additivity principle, and the existence of dynamical phase transitions.

Method: The authors formulate an MFT for diffusive classical lattice gases subject to continuous coarse-grained monitoring, treating measurements in the typical-outcome (quenched) regime. They derive sufficient conditions for convexity of the current large-deviation function and the validity of the additivity principle, analyze particle-hole-symmetric one-dimensional models (including the Katz--Lewitz--Spohn model), and study the strong-measurement limit to obtain the current distribution and cumulant scaling.

Result: Monitoring adds a quadratic confining term to the MFT action that pins the density to its hydrodynamic profile. This term ensures, under sufficient conditions, that the current large-deviation function remains convex, the additivity principle holds, and dynamical phase transitions are excluded. In particle-hole-symmetric one-dimensional models, monitoring suppresses dynamical phase transitions and can change their order from second to first, as demonstrated in the Katz--Lewitz--Spohn model. In the strong-measurement limit, the current distribution becomes Gaussian, with cumulants following simple power laws when mobility remains nonzero throughout, but showing anomalous logarithmic scaling when mobility vanishes at the boundaries.

Conclusion: Continuous coarse-grained monitoring qualitatively alters the fluctuation properties of diffusive lattice gases: it confines fluctuations through a quadratic term in the MFT action, enforces convexity and additivity, eliminates dynamical phase transitions in particle-hole-symmetric one-dimensional systems (or changes their order), and drives the current distribution toward Gaussian with distinct cumulant scaling regimes depending on boundary mobility.

Abstract: We develop a macroscopic fluctuation theory for diffusive classical lattice gases subject to continuous coarse-grained monitoring. Treating measurements in the typical-outcome (quenched) regime, we show that observation adds a quadratic confining term to the MFT action that pins the density to its hydrodynamic profile. We derive sufficient conditions under which, in the presence of monitoring, the current large-deviation function remains convex, the additivity principle holds, and dynamical phase transitions are excluded. For particle-hole-symmetric one-dimensional models we find that monitoring suppresses dynamical phase transitions and can change their order from second to first, as we demonstrate in the Katz--Lebowitz--Spohn model. In the strong-measurement limit, we find that the current distribution becomes Gaussian and determine the scaling of its cumulants: they follow simple power laws when the mobility remains nonzero throughout the system, but exhibit anomalous logarithmic scaling when the mobility vanishes at the boundaries.

</details>


### [14] [Exact Microscopic One-Body Reduction in the Symmetric Exclusion Process](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18626)
*Olivier Bénichou,Aurélien Grabsch*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The full infinite many-body hierarchy governing current-density correlations in the symmetric exclusion process collapses exactly onto a single linear equation at arbitrary finite times on the lattice, equivalent to a persistence problem for one auxiliary random walker.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The current-density correlations of the symmetric exclusion process (SEP) obey an infinite many-body hierarchy of equations. This hierarchy was recently observed to collapse onto a single closed equation at large scales, raising the question of whether this simplification is merely an artifact of coarse graining or reflects a deeper property already encoded in the microscopic dynamics.

Method: The authors analyze the microscopic hierarchy of current-density correlation equations for the SEP directly on the lattice at arbitrary time, without taking any scaling limit, and demonstrate an exact collapse of the entire hierarchy onto a single linear equation. They further map this equation to a persistence problem for a single auxiliary random walker, establishing an exact one-body reformulation, and identify a microscopic operator underlying the reduction.

Result: The entire many-body hierarchy collapses exactly onto one linear equation at arbitrary finite time on the lattice, prior to any scaling limit. This reduction yields the complete finite-time correlation profile, including regimes of nonequilibrium relaxation, via an exact equivalence to a single-auxiliary-random-walker persistence problem.

Conclusion: The large-scale simplification of the SEP correlation hierarchy is not created by coarse graining but reflects a deeper microscopic property: an exact reduction of an interacting many-body problem to a genuine one-body problem. The uncovered microscopic operator provides a route to more general correlation functions in the SEP and may indicate a general organizing principle for nonequilibrium interacting particle systems.

Abstract: The symmetric exclusion process (SEP) is a paradigmatic model of interacting particle transport. Its current--density correlations are governed by an infinite many-body hierarchy of equations. Only recently, this hierarchy was found to collapse at large scales onto a single closed equation. Is this remarkable simplification created by coarse graining, or does it reflect a deeper property already present in the microscopic dynamics? Here we show that the second scenario is realized. At arbitrary time on the lattice, the entire hierarchy collapses onto a single linear equation which is exactly equivalent to a persistence problem for a single auxiliary random walker. Thus an interacting many-body problem reduces exactly to a genuine one-body problem before any scaling limit is taken. This reduction determines the complete finite-time correlation profile, including during nonequilibrium relaxation. More broadly, the microscopic operator uncovered here opens a route to more general correlation functions in the SEP, and may point to a general organizing principle for nonequilibrium interacting particle systems.

</details>


### [15] [Emergent Loop Geometry in Gauge-Equivariant Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18742)
*Ali Rayat,Gia-Wei Chern*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper studies the geometry of internal structures formed by gauge-equivariant graph neural networks, showing that increasing depth rapidly extends the length of generated closed lattice walks while spatial reach grows much more slowly, a hierarchy that mirrors loop expansions of fermionic observables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gauge-equivariant graph neural networks produce ordered products of gauge links via local message passing, yet the geometry of these internal structures is not captured by receptive field alone. The authors aim to characterize the statistical ensemble of closed lattice walks generated by recursive node- and edge-feature operations, independently of training.

Method: The authors analyze the statistical ensemble of closed lattice walks induced by recursive node- and edge-feature operations, using analytical derivations combined with numerical experiments, and interpret the resulting hierarchy through loop expansions of fermionic observables.

Result: Analytical and numerical results reveal a separation between rapid growth of contour length and much slower spatial expansion: deeper compositions generate increasingly long paths through repeated traversal of relatively compact regions, and loop expansions of fermionic observables provide a physical interpretation of this hierarchy.

Conclusion: These findings identify complementary roles of network depth in extending spatial reach and enriching representations constructed within that reach, offering a geometrical perspective on learning lattice gauge observables.

Abstract: Gauge-equivariant graph neural networks generate ordered products of gauge links through local message passing, but the geometry of these internal structures is not captured by the receptive field alone. We investigate the statistical ensemble of closed lattice walks generated by recursive node- and edge-feature operations, independently of network training. Analytical and numerical results reveal a separation between rapid growth of contour length and much slower spatial expansion: deeper compositions generate increasingly long paths through repeated traversal of relatively compact regions. Loop expansions of fermionic observables provide a physical interpretation of this hierarchy, showing how spatial locality remains compatible with high-order dependence on nearby gauge fields. These findings identify complementary roles of network depth in extending spatial reach and enriching the representations constructed within that reach, providing a geometrical perspective on learning lattice gauge observables.

</details>


### [16] [Towards synergy between theory and experiments: First-passage processes under resetting with stochastic return protocols](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18814)
*Ashutosh Dubey,Arnab Pal*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper analytically studies stochastic resetting with stochastic (finite-time) return for a 1-d diffusing particle in a harmonic trap with different stiffnesses during search and return, deriving the MFPT and mapping optimal regimes onto parameters from existing optical-trap experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most theoretical treatments of stochastic resetting assume instantaneous return to the resetting position, which is unrealistic since return typically takes finite time. There is a need to understand how finite-time return dynamics, particularly in experimentally relevant harmonic-trap settings, affect first-passage and search efficiency compared to the instantaneous-return idealization.

Method: The authors model a 1-d diffusing particle in a harmonic trap with stiffness k1 during the search phase and k2 during the return phase. The particle resets at rate r, entering a return phase that continues until it reaches either the origin or the absorbing target at L>0, with target detection allowed throughout the return phase. They analytically derive the mean first-passage time (MFPT), use the coefficient of variation criterion to build a phase diagram distinguishing regimes where stochastic return enhances or suppresses search efficiency, and apply an "SR criterion" to identify where SR outperforms IR. They then map parameter values from experiments by Goerlich et al., Biroli et al., and Besga et al. onto the theoretical phase diagram.

Result: The MFPT is derived analytically, and phase boundaries are determined via the coefficient of variation criterion, showing regions where stochastic return either improves or degrades search efficiency relative to the underlying dynamics. Using the SR criterion, regimes where SR beats instantaneous return are identified. Mapping experimental parameters onto the phase diagram reveals that the most favorable region—where SR is most beneficial—corresponds to larger potential strength during search and lower potential strength during return.

Conclusion: Finite-time return substantially reshapes the conditions under which resetting aids search, and the harmonic-trap model with distinct search/return stiffnesses yields experimentally testable predictions. The results bridge theoretical resetting models and optical-trap-mediated finite-time return dynamics, highlighting parameter regimes where stochastic return is preferable to instantaneous return.

Abstract: Stochastic resetting with stochastic return (SR) has recently emerged as an effective and practically relevant strategy for controlling first-passage phenomena and optimizing search processes by significantly reducing completion times in a wide class of stochastic systems. While most theoretical studies assume instantaneous return (IR), in realistic systems the return to the resetting position typically occurs over a finite time. We consider a 1-d diffusing particle in a harmonic trap with stiffnesses $k_1$ and $k_2$ during search and return, respectively. The particle searches for the target and, upon resetting at rate $r$, enters the return phase, continuing until it reaches either the origin or the absorbing target at $L>0$. Notably, the model permits target detection throughout the return phase, distinguishing it from IR scenarios. We analytically derive the MFPT for our model and determine parameter regions where SR enhances or suppresses search efficiency over the underlying dynamics, constructing the corresponding phase diagram using the coefficient of variation criterion. We further identify regimes in which SR is more efficient than IR using the SR criterion. This behavior is tested across different parameter sets taken from experimental studies by Goerlich et al., Biroli et al., and Besga et al. We map the parameter values reported in existing experimental studies onto our theoretical phase diagram, thereby identifying the regimes in which SR outperforms IR. In particular, we find that the most relevant region of the phase space, where SR is most beneficial corresponds to larger values of the potential strength in the search phase and lower values of potential in the return phase. Our results provide experimentally testable predictions and bridge the gap between theoretical resetting models and optical trap mediated finite-time return dynamics.

</details>
