### [17] [UGME: A python package for non-Markovian protein folding dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14234)
*Anthony J. Dominic,Bertram Cham,Andrés Montoya-Castillo*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The authors introduce ugme, a modular open-source Python package for constructing and validating generalized master equation (GME) models that explicitly capture non-Markovian (memory) effects in coarse-grained biomolecular dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Atomistic MD simulations cannot reach the long timescales of processes like protein folding, protein-protein association, and protein-DNA recognition, so practitioners project many short trajectories onto reduced kinetic models such as Markov state models (MSMs). MSMs assume memoryless dynamics at sufficiently long time lags, but projecting high-dimensional dynamics onto few states discards unresolved degrees of freedom (solvent reorganization, side-chain rearrangements, intermediates) whose influence leaves residual memory absent from a Markovian description.

Method: The paper presents ugme, a modular, open-source Python package that constructs and validates generalized master equation (GME) models. GME models explicitly incorporate non-Markovian memory effects rather than assuming memoryless projected dynamics.

Result: ugme provides a modular, open-source toolset for building and validating GME models that account for memory effects in reduced biomolecular kinetic models.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling non-Markovian memory via GMEs offers a principled alternative to the Markovian assumption underlying MSMs, and ugme makes such modeling accessible through an open-source Python implementation.

Abstract: Biomolecular processes, including protein folding, protein-protein association, and protein-DNA recognition, often occur on timescales beyond what individual atomistic molecular dynamics (MD) simulations can reach. To access these longer-timescale dynamics, practitioners commonly combine many parallelized, short trajectories into reduced kinetic models, such as Markov state models (MSMs), that describe how the biomolecules interconvert between a handful of biomolecular configurations (e.g., folded and unfolded states of a protein). Central to MSM construction is the assumption that, at sufficiently long time lags, these projected dynamics become memoryless, such that the future evolution of the system depends only on its current state. Yet, projecting high-dimensional biomolecular dynamics onto a small set of states necessarily discards information about unresolved degrees of freedom, including solvent reorganization, side-chain rearrangements, and intermediate configurations. Because these unresolved motions can continue to influence subsequent dynamics, the resulting dynamics retain memory that is absent from a Markovian description. Here, we present ugme, a modular, open-source Python package for constructing and validating generalized master equations (GME) models that explicitly account for these non-Markovian (memory) effects.

</details>


### [18] [Learning Electron Density with Virtual Atoms for Transferable Atomistic Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14521)
*Yuzhi Liu,Xinzijian Liu,Chengqian Zhang,Anyang Peng,Duo Zhang,Han Wang*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Introduces a virtual-atom formulation within the DPA3 framework that unifies electron density, energy, and force prediction, achieving state-of-the-art density accuracy and demonstrating electron density as useful joint supervision for interatomic potentials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most machine learning density models treat electron density merely as a reconstruction target and are developed separately from machine learning interatomic potentials, leaving density under-utilized as electronic-structure supervision.

Method: Density query points are represented as virtual nodes processed by the same parameterized encoder used for real atoms. An asymmetric neighbor construction lets virtual atoms receive information from nearby real atoms without altering the real-atom interaction graph. Task-specific readout heads enable density, energy, and force prediction.

Result: State-of-the-art density reconstruction on the Materials Project benchmark; matches best reported performance on NMC battery cathode materials; competitive with dedicated density models on QM9. Joint density-energy-force supervision improves in-domain accuracy, makes local environment information more accessible under linear probing, and reduces zero-shot force errors on larger QM40 molecules. Density-only checkpoints provide useful initializations for downstream energy-force learning.

Conclusion: Electron density can serve as a valuable source of electronic-structure supervision for interatomic-potential representations, supporting unified multi-task learning within a single framework.

Abstract: Electron density is a fundamental quantity of electronic structure, yet most machine learning density models treat it solely as a reconstruction target and are developed separately from machine learning interatomic potentials. Here, we introduce a virtual-atom formulation within the DPA3 framework, in which density query points are represented as virtual nodes and processed by the same parameterized encoder used for real atoms. An asymmetric neighbor construction allows virtual atoms to receive information from nearby real atoms without altering the real-atom interaction graph. The resulting framework supports density, energy, and force prediction through task-specific readout heads. The proposed model achieves state-of-the-art density reconstruction accuracy on the Materials Project benchmark, matches the best reported performance on nickel-manganese-cobalt battery cathode materials, and is competitive with dedicated density models on QM9. When density labels are used jointly with energy and force supervision, the model improves in-domain accuracy, makes local environment information more accessible under linear probing, and reduces zero-shot force errors on larger QM40 molecules. Density-only checkpoints also provide useful initializations for downstream energy-force learning under the fixed fine-tuning protocol. Together, these results support electron density as a source of electronic-structure supervision for interatomic-potential representations.

</details>


### [19] [Tersoff-NN: Hybrid Machine-Learned Bond-Order Extension of the Tersoff Potential](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15591)
*Yusuke Nishimura,Sho Kubota,Kotaro Takematsu,Kentaro Hirai,Takanobu Watanabe*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Tersoff-NN is a hybrid machine-learning potential for Si that keeps the analytic radial and cutoff structure of Tersoff while learning only the environment-dependent bond-order factor with compact neural networks, achieving 4-12x speed-up over Behler-Parrinello-type NN potentials and improved accuracy and dynamical stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine-learning interatomic potentials can reproduce first-principles potential-energy surfaces accurately, but they are typically more computationally expensive than empirical potentials and can be unreliable outside their training domains. The paper seeks a potential that combines the efficiency and physical robustness of empirical potentials with the accuracy of machine learning.

Method: Tersoff-NN retains the analytical radial interactions and cutoff structure of the Tersoff potential and represents only its environment-dependent bond-order factor using compact neural networks. A triplet-based architecture is selected and compared with an alternative atom-centered bond-order NN to assess the role of physical constraints.

Result: Benchmarks on 32,768-atom systems show Tersoff-NN is 4-12 times faster than the tested Behler-Parrinello-type NN potentials. It improves the short-range structure of liquid Si over conventional Tersoff, closely reproduces ab initio radial and angular distributions, reproduces diamond Si equilibrium structure and qualitative energetic trends among several crystalline phases, and yields a disordered four-coordinated tetrahedral-like solid in melt-quench simulations. Surface reconstruction motifs are retained despite exclusion from training. The triplet-based model remains stable, whereas the alternative model shows unphysical bond-order growth and atomic collapse despite comparable validation errors.

Conclusion: Learning only the bond-order term improves the description of diverse Si environments while retaining substantial computational efficiency and enhancing dynamical stability. Physical constraints are critical for ensuring dynamic stability in machine-learning interatomic potentials.

Abstract: Accurate, robust, and efficient interatomic potentials are highly desirable for large-scale molecular dynamics simulations. Although machine-learning interatomic potentials can reproduce first-principles potential-energy surfaces with high accuracy, they typically incur higher computational costs than conventional empirical potentials and may be less reliable outside their training domains. We developed Tersoff-NN, a hybrid machine-learning potential for Si, which retains the analytical radial interactions and cutoff structure of the Tersoff potential while representing its environment-dependent bond-order factor using compact neural networks (NNs). Benchmarks for 32,768-atom systems show that Tersoff-NN is 4-12 times faster than the Behler-Parrinello-type NN potentials tested in this study. Tersoff-NN improves the short-range structure of liquid Si relative to the conventional Tersoff potential, closely reproduces the ab initio radial and angular distributions, and reproduces the equilibrium structure of diamond Si and qualitative energetic trends among several crystalline phases. Furthermore, melt-quench simulations reveal a disordered solid with predominantly four-coordinated, tetrahedral-like local environments. Additionally, characteristic reconstruction motifs are retained on Si surfaces despite their exclusion from the training dataset. A direct comparison with an alternative atom-centered bond-order NN highlights the critical role of physical constraints in ensuring dynamic stability. Despite exhibiting comparable validation errors, the selected triplet-based architecture remains stable, while an alternative model exhibits unphysical bond-order growth and atomic collapse. These results confirm that learning only the bond-order term improves the description of diverse Si environments while retaining substantial computational efficiency and enhancing dynamical stability.

</details>


### [20] [Kernel-Level Constant-Potential Molecular Dynamics in GROMACS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15733)
*Sheng Bi,Mathieu Salanne,Jun Cheng,Guang Feng*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: An open-source implementation of the constant-potential method in GROMACS efficiently computes dynamic electrode charges on CPU and GPU, validated against established codes and demonstrated on charging, voltammetry, impedance, deformable electrodes, and nanotube interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Charge rearrangement within electrodes governs the behavior of batteries, supercapacitors, and other electrochemical devices. Molecular simulations can capture this by letting each electrode atom's charge adapt to its surroundings, but repeatedly recalculating these charges is computationally expensive, limiting such studies.

Method: The authors implement the constant-potential method in GROMACS, integrating the charge-calculation step into the program's optimized CPU and GPU routines. The implementation also supports moving or deforming electrodes and models of finite metallic screening and electron spillover.

Result: Electrode charges are validated against established codes. Charging, voltammetry, and impedance simulations reproduce the response to changing electrical conditions. For a pair of charged carbon nanotubes, permitting charge to move away from the facing surfaces roughly halves the repulsive force at contact at an intermediate charge, keeping the pair bound where a uniform-charge model opens within the simulated time.

Conclusion: The implementation makes the electrical response of electrodes accessible within GROMACS, enabling studies of interfacial structure, charging dynamics, and electrode motion.

Abstract: How charge rearranges within an electrode shapes the behavior of batteries, supercapacitors, and other electrochemical devices. Molecular simulations can capture this response by allowing the charge on each electrode atom to adjust to its surroundings, but repeatedly calculating these charges adds substantial computational cost. We present an open-source implementation of the constant-potential method in GROMACS that integrates these calculations into the program's optimized CPU and GPU routines. Comparisons with established codes validate the electrode charges, while charging, voltammetry, and impedance simulations demonstrate the response to changing electrical conditions. The implementation also supports electrodes that move or deform and models of finite metallic screening and electron spillover. In a pair of charged carbon nanotubes, allowing charge to move away from the facing surfaces reduces the repulsive force at contact by about half at an intermediate charge and keeps the pair bound where a uniform-charge model opens within the simulated time. These capabilities make the electrical response of electrodes accessible within GROMACS for studies of interfacial structure, charging dynamics, and electrode motion.

</details>


### [21] [The RAS-CCSD model, with applications for open-shell molecules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15848)
*Sarai Dery Folkestad*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a restricted active space coupled cluster singles and doubles (RAS-CCSD) model and its pilot implementation, offering a spin-adapted, pragmatic coupled cluster approach for open-shell and multireference systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional coupled cluster methods like unrestricted CCSD struggle with open-shell and multireference systems, and existing approaches often lack spin adaptation or a practical route to treat multireference character. A simpler, pragmatic formulation is needed.

Method: A restricted active space coupled cluster singles and doubles (RAS-CCSD) model is formulated, using a restricted active space to handle the multireference character while coupled cluster singles and doubles account for dynamical correlation. A pilot implementation of the approach is developed.

Result: For ground state energies, RAS-CCSD errors relative to FCI are similar to those of unrestricted (spin-orbital) CCSD. For excited states, RAS-CCSD errors are generally larger, indicating that methodological adaptations are required to obtain balanced accuracy for ground and excited states.

Conclusion: RAS-CCSD provides a simple, pragmatic, and spin-adapted coupled cluster framework for open-shell and multireference systems, capable of adding dynamical correlation to a multideterminantal reference state, unlike unrestricted CCSD. However, further refinement is needed for excited-state accuracy.

Abstract: The restricted active space coupled cluster singles and doubles (RAS-CCSD) model, and its pilot implementation is detailed. The approach offers a simple, pragmatic way to formulate a coupled cluster theory for open-shell and multireference systems. For ground state energies, errors (compared to FCI) are shown to be similar to those of unrestricted (spin-orbital) CCSD. For excited states, the errors of RAS-CCSD are generally larger, and adaptations to the methodology are needed to achieve an approach which is balanced for both ground and excited states. However, unlike unrestricted CCSD, the approach is spin-adapted and can be used to add dynamical correlation to a multideterminantal reference state.

</details>


### [22] [Single-electron Fluorine Bonding. I. The SEFB-15 and CSFB-14 Benchmark Datasets, Reference Binding Energies and Intermolecular Bond Distances: Assessment of M06-2X density functional](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16629)
*Govinda Prasad Khanal,Elangannan Arunan*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces SEFB-15 and CSFB-14 datasets to characterize single-electron and closed-shell fluorine halogen bonding, provides high-level ab initio reference binding energies and F-bond distances, and shows that M06-2X is reliable for closed-shell halogen bonding but systematically overbinds open-shell F-bonded complexes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Halogen bonding where covalently bound fluorine acts as the halogen-bond donor (F-bonding) is rare, and the open-shell variant involving radical electron donors is unexplored. Reliable reference data and assessment of density functionals, particularly M06-2X, are lacking for these systems.

Method: Two datasets were constructed: SEFB-15 (15 open-shell DF···CH3 complexes) and CSFB-14 (14 closed-shell DF···NH3 complexes). Reference binding energies were computed at DF-RMP2-F12/CBS//DF-RMP2-F12/VDZ-F12 and validated against CCSD(T), CCSD(T)-F12b, W1-F12, and W2-F12 for DF = F2, NCF, CNF, NH3F+, and NH2F2+. Reference F-bond distances at DF-RMP2-F12/VDZ-F12 were validated against CCSD(T)-F12b, CCSD(T), CCSD, and DCSD. M06-2X/AVTZ performance was then evaluated on both datasets.

Result: Reference binding energies show MA%D of 4.5% (SEFB) and 3.4% (CSFB) against CCSD(T)-F12b/CBS//CCSD(T)-F12b/VDZ-F12; W1-F12 and W2-F12 agree. Reference distances show MA%D of 0.9% (SEFB) and 0.4% (CSFB). M06-2X/AVTZ overbinds all SEFB-15 complexes (MA%D 75.8% on BEs, 8.9% on distances), predicting comparable BEs for F2···CH3 and F2···NH3 contrary to all ab initio results, an anomaly persisting across all 16 basis sets. Neither basis set nor BSSE correction resolves overbinding; BSSE correction worsens BEs for DF···NH3. For CSFB-14, M06-2X/AVTZ gives MA%Ds of 16.0% (BEs) and 3.3% (distances).

Conclusion: M06-2X is reliable for closed-shell halogen bonding but unreliable for single-electron F-bonding regardless of basis set, making it unsuitable for open-shell F-bonded systems. The SEFB-15 and CSFB-14 datasets provide validated reference data, and companion paper II extends the assessment to other Minnesota functionals and ab initio methods.

Abstract: Halogen bonding in which a covalently bound fluorine acts as halogen-bond donor (F-bonding) is rare, and its open-shell variant with radical electron donors remains unexplored. We introduce SEFB-15 dataset (15 open-shell DF$\cdots$CH$_3$ complexes) exemplifying single-electron F-bonding (SEFB) and CSFB-14 dataset (14 closed-shell DF$\cdots$NH$_3$ complexes) representing closed-shell F-bonding (CSFB). Reference binding energies (BEs) at DF-RMP2-F12/CBS//DF-RMP2-F12/VDZ-F12 are validated against CCSD(T), CCSD(T)-F12b, W1-F12 and W2-F12 for selected systems (DF = F$_2$, NCF, CNF, NH$_3$F$^+$ and NH$_2$F$_2^+$), with MA%D of 4.5% (DF$\cdots$CH$_3$) and 3.4% (DF$\cdots$NH$_3$) against CCSD(T)-F12b/CBS//CCSD(T)-F12b/VDZ-F12. BEs from W1-F12 and W2-F12 are identical. Reference F-bond distances at DF-RMP2-F12/VDZ-F12 are validated against CCSD(T)-F12b, CCSD(T), CCSD, and DCSD for the same systems, with MA%D of 0.9% (SEFB) and 0.4% (CSFB) against CCSD(T)-F12b/VDZ-F12. M06-2X is recommended for closed-shell halogen bonding with heavier halogens but has never been assessed for open-shell F-bonding. M06-2X/AVTZ systematically overbinds all SEFB-15 complexes and underestimates their F-bond distances, with MA%Ds of 75.8% on BEs and 8.9% on F-bond distances. M06-2X predicts comparable BEs for F$_2\cdots$CH$_3$ and F$_2\cdots$NH$_3$, contrary to all ab initio results, an anomaly persisting across all 16 basis sets. Neither basis set nor BSSE correction resolves the overbinding. BSSE correction worsens BEs for DF$\cdots$NH$_3$ complexes. For CSFB-14, M06-2X/AVTZ yields MA%Ds of 16.0% for BEs and 3.3% for F-bond distances. M06-2X is reliable for CSFB but unreliable for SEFB regardless of basis set. Companion paper II reports performance of other Minnesota functionals and ab initio methods for these datasets.

</details>


### [23] [Exact Gaussian--Hermite closure of the mapping quantum--classical Liouville equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16864)
*Sahand Nikzat Ali Nassimi,Rony Modak Parai*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The exact mapping-density expansion in Hermite functions closes on a finite sparse system of coefficient equations, reducing to the subsystem quantum-classical Liouville equation; PBME instead fails to close and is less accurate than the quantum-classical description for the dual avoided crossing model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Liouville equation contains an excess coupling term beyond the Poisson bracket. Neglecting it gives the Poisson bracket mapping equation (PBME), but the basis-dependence of the mapping density and how the exact dynamics relates to the subsystem quantum-classical Liouville equation are unclear. This work addresses the structure of the exact mapping dynamics and quantifies the accuracy lost by PBME.

Method: Expand the mapping dependence of the density in Hermite functions about the Gaussian factor common to all physical mapping densities. Analyze how the excess coupling term and back-reaction force act in this basis (raising/lowering operators), determine closure of the coefficient equations, and propagate the coefficient equations for Tully's dual avoided crossing model.

Result: The excess coupling term acts purely as a raising operator and exactly cancels the raising contribution from the back-reaction force, so the exact dynamics does not generate higher Hermite orders and closes on a finite sparse set of coefficient equations. Within the physical sector these reduce to the subsystem quantum-classical Liouville equation. Direct propagation confirms finite closure and agrees with independent solutions of the subsystem equation. PBME retains the force's raising contribution, continuously generating higher Hermite orders, and is found to be less accurate than the quantum-classical description for populations, coherences, and momentum distributions.

Conclusion: The Hermite-function expansion reveals why the exact mapping dynamics closes finitely and reduces to the subsystem quantum-classical Liouville equation in the physical sector, while PBME does not close and is systematically less accurate. The quantum-classical description is preferable to PBME across all regimes examined.

Abstract: Liouville equation contains an excess coupling term in addition to the Poisson bracket. Neglecting this term yields the Poisson bracket mapping equation (PBME). We expand the mapping dependence of the density in Hermite functions about the Gaussian factor common to all physical mapping densities. In this basis, the excess coupling term acts purely as a raising operator and exactly cancels the raising contribution generated by the back--reaction force. Consequently, the exact dynamics does not generate higher Hermite orders and closes on a finite, sparse system of equations for the coefficient fields. Within the physical sector, these equations reduce to the subsystem quantum--classical Liouville equation. In contrast, the PBME retains the raising contribution of the force, modifying the back reaction within the physical sector while continuously generating higher Hermite orders. For Tully's dual avoided crossing model, direct propagation of the coefficient equations confirms this finite closure and agrees with independent solutions of the subsystem equation. Comparison with converged full quantum dynamics further shows that, across all regimes examined, the PBME is less accurate than the quantum--classical description for populations, coherences, and momentum distributions.

</details>


### [24] [Solvent Fluctuations Enable Photoionization of Aqueous Nitrite Microdroplets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17021)
*Brandon J. Wallace,Eric R. Heller,Alex Prophet,Pranav Ballapuram,Ashok Ajoy,David T. Limmer,Kevin R. Wilson*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Visible light directly photoionizes aqueous nitrite in micron-sized droplets via an exceptionally weak but observable pathway, revealing an overlooked source of solvated electrons, hydroxyl radicals, and nitrogen oxides in atmospheric and natural waters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The prevailing view holds that aqueous nitrite photochemistry proceeds exclusively via photoexcitation followed by homolytic bond cleavage. The authors challenge this assumption, hypothesizing that direct photoionization of aqueous nitrite by visible light may constitute an additional, previously unrecognized reaction pathway relevant to environmental chemistry.

Method: Single-droplet (micron-sized) experiments using visible light to probe photoionization of aqueous nitrite; molecular simulations to rationalize the photoionization absorption spectrum; kinetic modeling of an aqueous-phase reaction mechanism to quantitatively reproduce observed chemistry and predict product formation.

Result: Visible light directly ionizes aqueous nitrite in individual micron-sized droplets. The photoionization cross section is exceptionally small, but the pathway is detectable at the high photon flux densities achievable in single-droplet experiments. Simulations attribute this to an exceptionally long wavelength tail of photoionization absorption arising from large solvent reorganization energy in aqueous solution. Kinetic modeling accounts for the observed chemistry and predicts formation of nitrate, hydroxyl radicals, and solvated electrons. The predicted broad photoionization continuum overlaps the visible solar spectrum.

Conclusion: Visible-light photoionization of aqueous nitrite is an overlooked environmental source of nitrogen oxides, solvated electrons, and oxidants in atmospheric aerosols, snowpack, and natural waters. Under dilute conditions where dissolved oxygen is the main sink for solvated electrons, photoionization is expected to be the dominant photochemical loss pathway for nitrite.

Abstract: Nitrite photochemistry in atmospheric aerosols and natural waters is a major source of hydroxyl radicals and reactive nitrogen oxides. Contrary to the prevailing view that in the environment aqueous nitrite photochemistry proceeds exclusively through photoexcitation followed by homolytic bond cleavage, we show that visible light directly ionizes aqueous nitrite in individual micron-sized droplets. Although the photoionization cross section is exceptionally small, this pathway is observable at the high photon flux densities accessible in single-droplet experiments. Molecular simulations reveal that this is because photoionization absorption has an exceptionally long wavelength tail due to the large solvent reorganization energy in aqueous solutions. Kinetic modeling of an aqueous-phase reaction mechanism quantitatively accounts for the observed chemistry and predicts the formation of nitrate, hydroxyl radicals, and solvated electrons. The overlap of the predicted broad photoionization continuum with the visible solar spectrum further suggests that visible-light photoionization is an overlooked source of nitrogen oxides, solvated electrons, and oxidants in atmospheric aerosols, snowpack, and natural waters. Under dilute conditions, where dissolved oxygen is the main sink for solvated electrons, photoionization is expected to be the dominant photochemical loss pathway for nitrite.

</details>


### [25] [Solvating machine-learning interatomic potentials for solution-phase chemistry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17226)
*Anton Z. Ni,Joonho Lee*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: AnySolv is a transferable implicit solvation potential that adds solvent-dependent energy and force corrections to existing gas-phase MLIPs without retraining, enabling accurate and scalable modeling of solution-phase chemistry and dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most MLIPs are trained on gas-phase data, restricting their applicability to solution-phase chemistry. Retraining them for solvation is costly, and a transferable way to inject solvent effects is lacking.

Method: AnySolv provides implicit solvation corrections (energies and forces) on top of frozen gas-phase potentials. It is trained on 1.4M solvation energies and forces from DFT-SMD across 36 solvents, covering charged, open-shell, and transition-state structures, with solvents encoded via continuous physical descriptors that generalize to 179 solvents.

Result: On external benchmarks, AnySolv reproduces DFT-SMD solvation energies with 2–4 meV MAE at >3 orders of magnitude lower cost and matches DFT-SMD accuracy against experimental solvation free energies. It enables screening of 120 nucleophilic aromatic substitution mechanisms across six phases and MD simulations reproducing solvent-dependent conformational preferences of alanine dipeptide.

Conclusion: AnySolv offers transferable solvation corrections to existing MLIPs, enabling scalable simulations of solvent-dependent reactivity and molecular dynamics.

Abstract: Most machine-learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) are trained on gas-phase data, which limits their use for chemistry in solution. Here, we introduce AnySolv, a transferable implicit solvation potential that provides solvent-dependent energy and force corrections to existing gas-phase potentials without retraining them. It is trained on 1.4 million solvation energies and forces computed with density functional theory (DFT) and SMD in 36 solvents, spanning charged, open-shell, and transition-state structures. Because solvents are represented by continuous physical descriptors, AnySolv supports 179 solvents, including those absent from training. On external benchmarks, AnySolv reproduces DFT-SMD solvation energies to 2-4 meV mean absolute error at more than three orders of magnitude lower cost, and it matches the accuracy of DFT-SMD against experimental solvation free energies. This enables screening of the mechanisms of 120 nucleophilic aromatic substitution reactions in six phases as well as molecular dynamics simulations that reproduce the experimentally observed solvent-dependent conformational preferences of alanine dipeptide. By providing transferable solvation corrections to existing MLIPs, AnySolv enables scalable simulations of solvent-dependent chemical reactivity and molecular dynamics.

</details>


### [26] [Chromatographic Peak Shape beyond the High-Péclet Approximation: Analytic Time-Domain Expression and Derivatives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17297)
*Hernán R. Sánchez*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops fast, accurate double-precision time-domain expressions for the elution-time PDF and its parameter derivatives in a stochastic model of linear isocratic chromatography, replacing the normal-inverse-Gaussian density with a finite Gaussian mixture and introducing linear-cost recurrences, a roundoff-error estimator, and a stable backward reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analytic elution-time densities and their parameter derivatives from a stochastic chromatography model are valuable for fitting and sensitivity analysis, but the previous formulation involving normal-inverse-Gaussian and Gaussian-Gamma convolutions is computationally costly (quadratic cost in truncation order) and prone to finite-precision loss (cancellation) in some regimes, limiting rapid and reliable evaluation.

Method: The normal-inverse-Gaussian density is approximated by a finite Gaussian mixture via an existing quadrature rule, making the elution-time PDF a weighted sum of series of Gaussian-Gamma form, with parameter derivatives likewise obtained. New recurrences reduce coefficient (and derivative) evaluation cost from quadratic to linear in truncation order for fixed mechanism count. A probabilistic analysis of series coefficients bounds the required number of terms. A relative roundoff-error estimator quantifies finite-precision loss in the inherited forward recurrence for Gaussian-Gamma convolutions, and a backward reconstruction is developed for the cancellation-prone regime, initialized from an exact ratio of consecutive Gaussian-Gamma terms expressed through parabolic-cylinder functions, with a highly accurate approximation for rapid evaluation of this ratio.

Result: The new recurrences lower evaluation cost from quadratic to linear in truncation order, and the required number of terms is shown to be small under usual chromatographic conditions, enabling rapid evaluation. The roundoff-error estimator characterizes precision loss, and the backward reconstruction handles the cancellation-prone regime. Independent high-precision calculations confirm the high accuracy of the resulting double-precision evaluation in the tested numerical case.

Conclusion: The proposed Gaussian-mixture approximation together with linear-cost recurrences, a roundoff-error estimator, and a backward reconstruction yields a rapid and accurate double-precision method for evaluating the elution-time PDF and its parameter derivatives, validated against high-precision computations.

Abstract: Analytic time-domain expressions for the elution-time density and its parameter derivatives were recently derived from a stochastic model of linear isocratic chromatography incorporating advective-diffusive transport, injection dispersion, and fast and slow retention mechanisms. A Gaussian approximation, justified asymptotically at high column-scale Péclet numbers, was adopted for the distribution of the combined mobile-phase and fast-retention time, while slow retention was incorporated through a series of Gaussian-Gamma convolutions. Here, the underlying normal-inverse-Gaussian density is approximated by a finite Gaussian mixture using an existing quadrature rule. The elution-time PDF is a weighted sum of series of the previously derived Gaussian-Gamma form. Its parameter derivatives are also obtained. Newly derived recurrences reduce the cost of evaluating the coefficients of that series and their derivatives from quadratic to linear in truncation order for a fixed number of mechanisms. Through a probabilistic analysis of the series coefficients, the required number of terms is found to be small enough for rapid evaluation under usual chromatographic conditions. A relative roundoff-error estimator is derived to quantify finite-precision loss in the inherited forward recurrence for Gaussian-Gamma convolutions. For the cancellation-prone regime, a backward reconstruction is developed, initialized from an exact ratio of consecutive Gaussian-Gamma terms expressed through parabolic-cylinder functions. A highly accurate approximation is derived for rapid evaluation of this ratio. Independent high-precision calculations confirm the high accuracy of the resulting double-precision evaluation in the tested numerical case.

</details>


### [27] [All-electron machine-learned orbital-free density functional for periodic CHNO systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17975)
*Marc K. Ickler,Andreas Hermann,Fred A. Hamprecht*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: STRUCTURES-P is a machine-learned all-electron orbital-free DFT functional for periodic CHNO systems that recovers Kohn-Sham ground states via variational density minimization, reducing density error to 0.4% and energy error to 0.25 mHa/atom.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Orbital-free DFT offers a route to efficient electronic structure for solids, but accurate kinetic and exchange-correlation functionals are lacking. This work aims to make machine-learned all-electron orbital-free DFT viable for variational density optimization and energy evaluation in fixed-geometry periodic systems.

Method: STRUCTURES-P evaluates electrostatics analytically in an atom-centered Gaussian basis and uses an equivariant neural network to learn kinetic and exchange-correlation energy from energies and gradients of non-ground-state densities. The functional is minimized starting from a superposition of atomic densities.

Result: On held-out crystals, density error drops from 6.7% to 0.4% of the electron count, and energy error reaches 0.25 mHa per atom—half that of a same-architecture interatomic potential. A single functional trains on both isolated molecules and periodic systems and generalizes to larger unit cells; it largely preserves relative-energy rankings and reproduces convex-hull vertices on the tested H-N composition slice.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate the viability of machine-learned all-electron orbital-free DFT for variational density optimization and energy evaluation in fixed-geometry solids, while noting limitations in composition coverage and scope.

Abstract: We introduce STRUCTURES-P, a machine-learned all-electron orbital-free density functional for periodic CHNO systems. Electrostatics are evaluated analytically in an atom-centered Gaussian basis, while an equivariant network learns the kinetic and exchange-correlation energy from energies and gradients of non-ground-state densities. Minimizing the functional from a superposition of atomic densities recovers the Kohn-Sham ground state: on held-out crystals, the density error falls from 6.7% to 0.4% of the electron count and the energy error reaches 0.25 mHa per atom, half that of a same-architecture interatomic potential. A single functional can be trained for both isolated molecules and periodic systems, and the model generalizes to larger unit cells. It largely preserves relative-energy rankings and reproduces convex-hull vertices on the tested H-N composition slice. Together, these results demonstrate the viability of machine-learned all-electron orbital-free DFT for variational density optimization and energy evaluation in fixed-geometry solids.

</details>


### [28] [Development of a Minimal Auxiliary Basis for the Particle--Particle Random Phase Approximation (ppRPA): Accelerating Excited-State Calculations in Diradical Nanoparticles and Periodic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18020)
*Yuchen Wang,Zhenyu Li*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces ppRPA-as, a minimal auxiliary-basis approximation for the particle–particle random phase approximation that yields singlet–triplet gaps and multiplicities for large molecules, clusters, and periodic point defects at greatly reduced cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Singlet–triplet gaps define the energy scale of the two-spin manifold in molecular quantum information materials, which often exhibit diradical character (metal nanoclusters, radical-linked MOFs, NV centers). TDDFT is the standard tool at this scale but poorly treats multireference systems: a closed-shell reference fails for multiconfigurational ground states and the adiabatic kernel excludes doubly excited configurations. The particle–particle RPA (ppRPA) changes particle number rather than spin, allowing singlet and triplet states to descend from a single closed-shell reference with doubles treated on equal footing to singles, but its computational cost has restricted it to small systems.

Method: The authors develop ppRPA-as, which factorizes the two-electron integrals through an auxiliary basis reduced to a minimal set of atom-centered Gaussian shells, whose exponents are derived from free-atom radii and one global scaling factor. The approximation enters only the excited-state step, so both calculations share the same self-consistent reference.

Result: Testing on molecules and clusters up to 286 atoms, including heavy elements, and seven point defects in four periodic hosts, the method yields both multiplicities and their splittings without symmetry breaking or spin projection. An order-of-magnitude smaller auxiliary basis gives up to eightfold speed-up while remaining within 0.03 eV of conventional ppRPA.

Conclusion: ppRPA-as makes diradical nanoclusters and periodic systems tractable, with guidance provided for choosing the optimized global scaling factor.

Abstract: Singlet--triplet gaps set the energy scale of the two-spin manifold in molecular quantum information materials, and such systems frequently carry diradical character: metal nanoclusters, metal--organic frameworks with radical linkers, and the nitrogen--vacancy center in diamond. Time-dependent density functional theory is the standard tool at this size but describes multireference systems poorly. A closed-shell reference is inadequate for a multiconfigurational ground state, and the adiabatic kernel excludes doubly excited configurations. The particle--particle random phase approximation (\pprpa{}) changes the particle number rather than the spin. Singlet and triplet states descend from one closed-shell reference, and double excitations enter on the same footing as single ones. Computational cost has confined \pprpa{} to small systems, and the two-electron integrals that drive it are factorized through an auxiliary basis. We develop \pprpa{}--as, which reduces that basis to a minimal set of atom-centered Gaussian shells whose exponents follow from free-atom radii and one global scaling factor. The approximation enters only the excited-state step, so both calculations proceed from the same self-consistent reference. We test molecules and clusters of up to 286 atoms, including heavy elements, and seven point defects in four periodic hosts, obtaining both multiplicities and their splitting without symmetry breaking or spin projection. We optimize the scaling factor and give guidance on choosing it. An order-of-magnitude smaller auxiliary basis delivers a speed-up of up to eightfold while staying within $0.03$~eV of conventional \pprpa{}, bringing diradical nanoclusters and periodic systems within reach.

</details>


### [29] [Can Machine Learning Potentials Be More Accurate Than the Methods They Are Trained On?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18027)
*Pavlo O. Dral,Yi-Fan Hou,Yuxinxin Chen,Hassan Nawaz,Mateusz Witkowski,Szymon Śmiga,Ireneusz Grabowski*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This Perspective proposes "method-transferable" MLPs that generalize across quantum-chemical methods, enabling predictions more accurate than any training method non-empirically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLPs are thought to be accuracy-limited by their training methods, with improvement beyond this limit previously achievable only via empirical refitting to experimental observables.

Method: Generalizing MLPs across a third space—that of quantum-chemical methods—rather than only configurational and chemical compound spaces, enabling prediction at unseen methods.

Result: Working implementations in density-functional and wavefunction-based theories show method-transferable MLPs can be more accurate than any of their training methods.

Conclusion: Method-transferable MLPs can make universal MLPs more accurate than single-method models and will reshape training-dataset construction and model evaluation.

Abstract: Although machine learning potentials (MLPs) generalize across configurational and chemical compound spaces, their accuracy is commonly believed to be limited by their training methods, with improvements beyond this limit achieved so far only by refitting to experimental observables. In this Perspective, we argue that MLPs can also be made more accurate than their training methods non-empirically by generalizing across a third space, that of quantum-chemical methods. We propose routes to method-transferable MLPs that predict at unseen methods, with working implementations in both density-functional and wavefunction-based theories showing that they can be more accurate than any of their training methods. Aiming beyond the accuracy of the training methods could also make universal MLPs more accurate than single-method models, even when the method-transferable MLPs remain less accurate than those QM methods. Method-transferable MLPs will shape the construction of training datasets and the evaluation of models, pushing the accuracy limits of chemical simulations.

</details>


### [30] [QUPEX: A Streamlit-Based Framework for Visualizing Quantum-Mechanical Property Spaces of Organic Molecules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18100)
*Mirtha Pillaca Quispe,Alejandra Hinostroza Caldas,Leonardo Medrano Sandonas*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: QUPEX is an open-source Streamlit framework for interactive exploration of quantum-mechanical property spaces in molecules, demonstrated on 41,537 QM7-X structures with clustering, XGBoost dipole-moment prediction, and SHAP interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: QM datasets are increasingly available and enable data-driven study of structure–property and property–property relationships in drug-like molecules, but interactive tools combining molecular visualization, statistical QM property analysis, clustering, and ML are scarce. Such tools are valuable for dataset exploration and interpretation.

Method: QUPEX (QUantum Property EXplorer) is a Streamlit-based open-source framework for interactive exploration of QM property spaces. It is demonstrated on a QM7-X subset of 41,537 equilibrium molecular structures (up to seven non-hydrogen atoms: C, N, O, S, Cl) with 24 associated QM properties. It integrates property-space analysis, clustering, and an XGBoost regression pipeline predicting PBE0 dipole moments from tight-binding-derived electronic descriptors, with model evaluation and SHAP-based interpretability.

Result: QUPEX enables interactive molecular visualization, statistical analysis, clustering, and predictive modeling on the QM7-X subset, allowing identification of patterns in structural and electronic properties and supporting chemical-space exploration.

Conclusion: By combining interactive visualization, data analysis, and predictive modeling, QUPEX offers an accessible, extensible environment for investigating molecular datasets and their structural/electronic properties, informing rational design of drug-like molecules with targeted QM properties.

Abstract: The growing availability of quantum-mechanical (QM) datasets offers new opportunities to investigate structure-property and property-property relationships in drug-like molecules using data-driven methods. However, interactive tools that integrate molecular visualization, statistical analysis of QM properties, clustering, and machine learning (ML) remain limited, despite their value in facilitating dataset exploration and interpretation. To address this gap, we introduce QUPEX (QUantum Property EXplorer), an open-source Streamlit-based framework for the interactive exploration of QM property spaces in molecular systems. As a demonstration, we apply QUPEX to a subset of the QM7-X dataset comprising 41,537 equilibrium molecular structures containing up to seven non-hydrogen atoms (C, N, O, S, and Cl) and their 24 associated QM properties. In addition to property-space analysis, QUPEX incorporates an XGBoost regression pipeline for predicting PBE0 dipole moments from tight-binding-derived electronic descriptors, together with model evaluation and SHAP-based interpretability functionality. By combining interactive molecular visualization, data analysis, and predictive modeling, QUPEX provides an accessible and extensible environment for investigating molecular datasets and identifying patterns in their structural and electronic properties. These capabilities can support the exploration of chemical space and inform the rational design of drug-like molecules with targeted QM properties.

</details>


### [31] [Photoinduced Modulation of Rectification and Charge Transport in Diarylethene-Viologen Molecular Junction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18251)
*Sofyan Tahri,Samuel Decorps,Stephane Lenfant,Denis Frath,Imen Hnid*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Au/DAEVIO/Ti/Au molecular junctions with 12–16 nm diarylethene-viologen layers show low currents and high rectification (300 at 1 V) in the OFF state; 385 nm irradiation raises the +1 V current 200-fold, collapses rectification to 2, and generates a photovoltaic response (Jsc ≈ 0.006 mA cm⁻², Voc ≈ −0.45 V).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-area molecular junctions that combine photochromic and redox-active functionalities offer an attractive route for controlling charge transport with light, but the interplay between light-induced switching and photoelectric charge generation in thick, multifunctional molecular layers remains insufficiently understood.

Method: Fabrication and characterization of Au/DAEVIO/Ti/Au large-area molecular junctions incorporating electro-grafted diarylethene-viologen (DAEVIO) layers of 12–16 nm thickness; current density–voltage measurements in the OFF state and under continuous in situ 385 nm irradiation; temperature-dependent transport measurements; rectification ratio determination; short-circuit current density and open-circuit voltage measurement under illumination.

Result: OFF-state junctions exhibit low current densities and strong rectification (ratio 300 at 1 V). Temperature-dependent measurements show a thermally assisted contribution at higher temperatures and weak temperature dependence below 160–180 K, consistent with multistep transport via molecular and/or interfacial states rather than direct coherent tunneling. Under 385 nm illumination, the response is strongly and asymmetrically modified: +1 V current density increases 200-fold while negative-bias current changes only weakly, reducing rectification from 300 to 2; a short-circuit current density of +0.006 mA cm⁻² and an open-circuit voltage of −0.45 V are generated.

Conclusion: Irradiation simultaneously modifies molecular charge transport and produces a measurable photoelectric response. The behavior is consistent with light-induced changes in electronic coupling associated with DAE photocyclization, combined with photoinduced charge generation and field-dependent charge separation involving the redox-active viologen units and asymmetric electrode interfaces. Such junctions thus provide a platform where photochromic switching and photovoltaic action coexist and can be harnessed for light-controlled charge transport.

Abstract: Large-area molecular junctions combining photochromic and redox-active functionalities provide an attractive platform for controlling charge transport by light. In this work, we study Au/DAEVIO/Ti/Au molecular junctions incorporated with electro-grafted layers of diarylethene-viologen with thicknesses of 12-16 nm. In the initial OFF state, the junctions exhibit low current densities and strong rectification, with a rectification ratio of 300 at 1V. Temperature-dependent measurements reveal a thermally assisted contribution at higher temperatures and a much weaker temperature dependence below 160-180K, consistent with multistep charge transport through molecular and/or interfacial states rather than direct coherent tunneling across the entire molecular layer. Under continuous in situ 385 nm irradiation, the electrical response is strongly and asymmetrically modified. The current density at +1V increases by a factor of 200, while the negative-bias current is only weakly modified, reducing the rectification ratio from 300 to 2. Illumination also produces a nonzero short-circuit current density of + 0.006mA cm-2 and an open-circuit voltage of -0.45V. These results indicate that irradiation simultaneously modifies molecular charge transport and generates a measurable photoelectric response. This behavior is consistent with light-induced changes in electronic coupling associated with DAE photocyclization, combined with photoinduced charge generation and field-dependent charge separation involving the redox-active viologen units and asymmetric electrode interfaces.

</details>


### [32] [Extrapolating Collisional Rate Coefficients for Molecules of Astrophysical Interest Using Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18278)
*Yigit Ergen,Jérôme Loreau*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Neural networks can extend collisional excitation rate coefficients for astrophysical NLTE models, achieving mean error factors of 1.1–1.5 without new scattering calculations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: State-to-state inelastic collision rate coefficients are essential for non-LTE radiative transfer modeling, but quantum scattering computations are prohibitively expensive for large molecules and highly excited rotational states. A cheaper route to extending existing datasets is needed.

Method: A multilayer perceptron was trained on molecular quantum numbers, level energies, energy gaps, and kinetic temperature, optimized on logarithmic rate coefficients. It was tested on diverse systems: CO, SiO, SO, H2O, SO2, benzonitrile, and vinyl cyanide colliding with H2 or He, with extrapolations to higher rotational levels, higher temperatures, and both combined.

Result: Predictions for unseen rotational states yielded mean error factors of 1.1–1.5, with dominant rate coefficients more accurate than weak transitions. Temperature extrapolation was particularly successful; combined temperature-rotational extrapolation stayed within a factor of two in most cases. Accuracy depends on dataset size, temperature range, molecular complexity, and the presence of clear collisional propensity rules.

Conclusion: Machine learning offers a practical route to extending collisional datasets for astrophysical applications, particularly for large molecules where additional quantum scattering calculations are computationally demanding.

Abstract: State-to-state rate coefficients for inelastic molecular collisions are essential inputs for non-local thermodynamic equilibrium radiative transfer models, yet their computation using quantum scattering methods is prohibitive for large molecules and highly excited rotational states. We investigate the ability of artificial neural networks to extend existing datasets of collisional excitation rate coefficients without performing additional scattering calculations. A multilayer perceptron model was trained using molecular quantum numbers, level energies, energy gaps, and kinetic temperature as descriptors, and optimized on logarithmic rate coefficients. The model was tested on diverse collision systems ranging from simple diatomic molecules (CO, SiO) to open-shell species (SO), small asymmetric tops (H$_2$O, SO$_2$), and complex organic molecules (benzonitrile and vinyl cyanide) colliding with H$_2$ molecules or He atoms. Extrapolations were performed toward higher rotational levels, higher temperatures, and combinations of both. For most systems, predictions of rate coefficients involving previously unseen rotational states yielded mean error factors between 1.1 and 1.5, with the dominant rate coefficients generally reproduced more accurately than weaker transitions. Temperature extrapolations proved particularly successful, while combined extrapolations in temperature and rotational levels remained accurate to within a factor of two in most cases. The prediction quality depends on the interplay between dataset size, temperature range, molecular complexity, and the presence of clear collisional propensity rules. Overall, the approach demonstrates that machine learning can provide a practical route to extending collisional datasets for astrophysical applications, especially for large molecules for which additional quantum-scattering calculations are computationally demanding.

</details>


### [33] [Ensuring Complete Consistency between Hops and Wavefunction Propagation in Fewest-Switches Surface Hopping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18743)
*Jonathan R. Mannouch*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops a fully-consistent hopping-probability formula for FSSH that matches wavefunction evolution across any time step, combined with a state-tracking algorithm, to accurately solve the trivial-crossing problem even with large propagation time steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: FSSH incorporates nonadiabatic effects via stochastic hops but does not guarantee hops are fully consistent with the underlying electronic wavefunction dynamics. This causes significant errors and necessitates small propagation time steps, hurting computational efficiency. These issues are especially severe at trivial crossings, where nonadiabatic coupling is entirely localized at the crossing point.

Method: The authors develop a fully-consistent hopping-probability formula that, by construction, generates attempted hops matching the wavefunction's time-evolution across any size time step. A state-tracking algorithm is also employed to ensure attempted hops are always accepted at trivial crossings.

Result: Applying the scheme to systems containing extremely challenging trivial crossings, the authors demonstrate that it obtains highly accurate results even when large propagation time steps are used.

Conclusion: The proposed fully-consistent hopping scheme with state tracking solves the trivial-crossing problem in FSSH, enabling accurate nonadiabatic dynamics with large time steps and thereby improving computational efficiency.

Abstract: Fewest-switches surface hopping (FSSH) is a popular and versatile approach for performing dynamical simulations beyond the Born-Oppenheimer approximation, in which nonadiabatic effects are incorporated through stochastic hops of the active potential energy surface. However, the FSSH algorithm does not ensure that hops are completely consistent with the underlying electronic wavefunction dynamics. This can lead to significant errors as well as requiring small propagation time steps, which can significantly compromise the computational efficiency of the method. These problems are known to be particularly severe at so-called trivial crossings, for which the nonadiabatic coupling is entirely localized at the crossing point. Here, we solve the trivial-crossing problem by developing a fully-consistent hopping-probability formula, which generates attempted hops that match the time-evolution of the wavefunction across any size time step by construction. A state-tracking algorithm is also utilized to ensure that attempted hops are always accepted at trivial crossings. Through application to systems that contain extremely challenging trivial crossings, we demonstrate that this hopping scheme is capable of obtaining highly accurate results, even when large propagation time steps are used.

</details>


### [34] [Embossing of Tapered, Isometric Micro-Interdigitated Flow Fields for Enhanced Desalination by Faradaic Deionization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18791)
*Md Habibur Rahman,Sadia Afrin,Kyle C. Smith*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: An integrated embossing-calendering, roll-to-roll compatible method patterns Prussian blue analog porous electrodes with embedded, tapered, isometric micro-interdigitated flow fields (eμ-IDFFs), improving desalination performance and energy efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Engineered transport architectures can improve electrolyte distribution and active-material utilization in porous electrodes, but existing fabrication methods compromise precision, throughput, active-material preservation, or scalability, limiting their practical implementation.

Method: The authors introduce an integrated embossing-calendering method that forms flow channels in PBA porous electrodes via controlled plastic deformation while simultaneously calendering, enabling non-subtractive, roll-to-roll compatible patterning. They use indentation experiments and a closed-form model to analyze force requirements, and design eμ-IDFFs with continuous width-tapering and isometric flow paths.

Result: The method preserves active material and reduces patterning time 100-fold versus mechanical milling, requires 15-fold less force than stamping, and produces local densification beneath embossed channels. Tapered, isometric eμ-IDFFs desalinate seawater-salinity feeds near potable levels with 1.5-fold higher thermodynamic energy efficiency and water recovery versus less scalable, non-isometric designs; brackish desalination also benefits.

Conclusion: The tapered, isometric eμ-IDFFs and the embossing method used to pattern them are readily translatable to other electrochemical energy storage and separations systems.

Abstract: Engineered transport architectures can improve electrolyte distribution and active-material utilization in porous electrodes, yet their implementation remains limited by fabrication methods that compromise precision, throughput, active-material preservation, or scalability. Here, an integrated embossing-calendering method is introduced to pattern Prussian blue analog (PBA) porous electrodes with embedded, micro-interdigitated flow fields (e$μ$-IDFFs) for electrochemical desalination. This non-subtractive, roll-to-roll compatible method forms channels by controlling plastic deformation while simultaneously calendering the electrode, preserving active material and reducing patterning time by 100-fold relative to mechanical milling. Indentation experiments and a closed-form model further show that embossing requires 15-fold less force than stamping, while electrode microstructures show local densification beneath embossed channels. PBA electrodes are embossed with novel e$μ$-IDFFs that use continuous width-tapering with isometric flow paths that promote uniform electrolyte distribution, increase rate capability, and reduce polarization. Compared with e$μ$-IDFFs patterned using less scalable means and non-isometric flow paths, these e$μ$-IDFFs desalinate seawater-salinity feeds near to potable levels with 1.5-fold of its thermodynamic energy efficiency and water recovery. Brackish desalination benefits similarly. Tapered, isometric e$μ$-IDFFs and the embossing method introduced to pattern them are readily translated other electrochemical energy storage and separations systems.

</details>
