### [35] [Pre-pairing and polarization of doped charge carriers in unconventional superconductors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14401)
*Lixin Zhang,Mengyan Nie,Yan Cheng,Ran Zhao*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: First-principles calculations indicate that dopants in cuprate superconductors form dimers rather than remaining isolated, creating pre-formed hole/electron pairs ("Z-pairs") that differ from Cooper pairs and Zhang-Rice singlets, driven by antiferromagnetic exchange and a doping-dependent on-site Coulomb repulsion U.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The prevailing view treats dopants in oxide high-TC superconductors as isolated, with pairing (Cooper pairs) emerging only at the superconducting transition. The paper challenges this assumption by asking whether carriers are already paired during sample preparation, which would reframe the origin of high-TC superconductivity.

Method: First-principles calculations were performed on hole-doped La2-xSrxCuO4, Na-doped Ca2CuO2Cl2, and electron-doped Nd2-xCexCuO4, examining the preferred configuration of dopants (dimer versus isolated), the binding of the two doped carriers into a polarized singlet, analogous behavior of oxygen dopants, the role of antiferromagnetic exchange as the attractive force, and the doping dependence of the on-site Coulomb repulsion U.

Result: Dopants preferentially form dimers in all examined cuprates, and oxygen dopants behave similarly. The dimer structure binds the two doped holes (or electrons) into a polarized singlet, termed a Z-pair. The attractive interaction originates mainly from strong antiferromagnetic exchange energy, while U becomes doping-dependent and can drop discontinuously around a doping site; this reduction stabilizes Z-pairs against isolated carriers. A Z-pair is a p-wave-like localized state that breaks the symmetry of the CuO2 plane and attracts along the direction perpendicular to its polarization.

Conclusion: The authors propose that in all oxide high-TC superconductors, holes/electrons are pre-paired into Z-pairs during preparation. Z-pairs are conceptually distinct from Cooper pairs and Zhang-Rice singlets, may be ubiquitous in unconventional superconductors, and their periodic assembly could generate coherence states and superconductivity.

Abstract: Based on first principles calculations, we show in hole-doped La2-xSrxCuO4, the Sr dopants prefer to form dimers, rather than be isolated as generally believed. Same trend applies to other superconductors such as Na-doped Ca2CuO2Cl2, and electron type Nd2-xCexCuO4. The dimer structure makes the two doped holes (electrons) bound up and form a singlet which is also polarized. Another type of dopant in cuprates is oxygen which behaves similarly. So, we propose that, for all oxide high TC superconductors, the holes (electrons) are pre-paired (Z-pairs) during the preparation of the superconductor. The spontaneously formed Z-pairs are conceptually different from not only the Cooper pairs, but also the Zhang-Rice singlets. The attraction force in a Z-pair originates basically from the strong antiferromagnetic exchange energy. We also found that the on-site Coulomb repulsion (U) is no longer a constant due to doping. Around a doping site, U can reduce its value discontinuously, which plays a key role in stabilizing the Z-pairs against isolated holes (electrons). A Z-pair is a p-wave like localized state which breaks the symmetry of the CuO2 plain and attracts each other along the direction perpendicular to the polarization. The Z-pairs may be popular in all of the unconventional superconductors and the periodic assembly can result in the emergence of coherence states and superconductivity.

</details>


### [36] [Quantum Geometry and Pair Breaking in Intra- and Intervalley Superconductivity of Rhombohedral Graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14480)
*Louis Primeau,Yang Zhang,Shi-Zeng Lin*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This work studies Kohn-Luttinger superconductivity in electron-doped rhombohedral tetralayer graphene using a DFT-fitted multiorbital tight-binding model with full Bloch form factors, showing that quantum-geometry-dressed interactions strongly favor a predominantly p_x-ip_y intravalley instability, while trigonal-warping-induced pair breaking restricts it to narrow Fermi-surface regimes near pair-Lifshitz transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Superconductivity emerging from a spin- and valley-polarized normal state in rhombohedral graphene poses a puzzle: intravalley Cooper pairs involve non-degenerate states due to trigonal warping, lacking the particle-particle nesting that underlies the conventional Cooper instability. Although model studies suggest nontrivial band geometry can promote chiral pairing, it is unclear how this geometric enhancement competes with realistic band-structure-induced pair breaking.

Method: The authors study Kohn-Luttinger superconductivity in electron-doped rhombohedral tetralayer graphene using a DFT-fitted multiorbital tight-binding model, retaining the full Bloch-wave-function form factors in both RPA screening and the projected Cooper interaction. They decompose the superconducting instability into three contributions: the symmetry-resolved form-factor-dressed interaction, the Cooper-pair phase space from the symmetrized pair dispersion, and the energy mismatch from trigonal warping.

Result: Bloch form factors strongly favor intravalley pairing, yielding a leading predominantly p_x-ip_y instability with a substantially larger maximum T_c than the intervalley reference state. However, trigonal-warping-induced mismatch cuts off the Cooper logarithm, confining intravalley superconductivity to a much narrower phase-diagram region. The dominant instability occurs near pair-Lifshitz transitions and mainly in reconstructed annular or three-lobed Fermi-surface regimes.

Conclusion: Superconductivity in rhombohedral graphene is controlled by the combined effects of quantum-geometry-dressed interactions, pair fermiology, and trigonal-warping-induced pair breaking.

Abstract: The observation of superconductivity emerging from a spin- and valley-polarized normal state in rhombohedral graphene raises a basic puzzle: intravalley Cooper pairs involve states that are not generically degenerate because of trigonal warping, and therefore lack the particle--particle nesting underlying the conventional Cooper instability. While recent model studies suggest that nontrivial band geometry can strongly promote chiral pairing, how this geometric enhancement competes with realistic band-structure-induced pair breaking remains unclear. Here we study Kohn--Luttinger superconductivity in electron-doped rhombohedral tetralayer graphene using a DFT-fitted multiorbital tight-binding model, retaining the full Bloch-wave-function form factors in both RPA screening and the projected Cooper interaction. We separate the superconducting instability into three contributions: the symmetry-resolved form-factor-dressed interaction, the Cooper-pair phase space determined by the symmetrized pair dispersion, and the energy mismatch generated by trigonal warping. We find that the Bloch form factors strongly favor intravalley pairing, with a leading predominantly $p_x-ip_y$ instability and a substantially larger maximum $T_c$ than the intervalley reference state. At the same time, trigonal-warping-induced mismatch cuts off the Cooper logarithm and confines intravalley superconductivity to a much narrower region of the phase diagram. The dominant instability occurs near pair-Lifshitz transitions and primarily in reconstructed annular or three-lobed Fermi-surface regimes. Our results show that superconductivity in rhombohedral graphene is controlled by the combined effects of quantum-geometry-dressed interactions, pair fermiology, and trigonal-warping-induced pair breaking.

</details>


### [37] [Chemical-Potential-Dependent Superconducting Spectroscopy on Bi2Te3/NbSe2 Heterostructures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14627)
*Pengyu Hu,Chun Yu Wan,Qiaoyan Yu,Chen Qian,Yisheng Gu,Xian Du,Xuhui Ning,Tingwen Miao,Shi Chen,Zihao Ma,Hao Yang,Hemian Yi,Weijiong Chen,Xiaoxue Liu,Liang Liu,Dandan Guan,Yaoyi Li,Shiyong Wang,Canhua Liu,Sangen Zhao,Fulin Wang,Junwei Liu,Hao Zheng,Jinfeng Jia*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Scanning tunneling spectroscopy at dilution-refrigerator temperatures reveals anomalous coherence peaks with inner kinks/peaks in Bi2Te3/NbSe2 heterostructures (5QL and 7QL films), attributed to bulk and surface states of Bi2Te3 that are selectively proximitized by a single superconducting gap from NbSe2, rather than to the multi-gap order parameter of NbSe2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Topological insulator/superconductor heterostructures are a platform for engineered topological superconductivity, but the tunneling spectra of such proximitized systems often deviate from the canonical single s-wave line shape. Clarifying whether such anomalies originate from the intrinsic multi-band superconductivity of the parent superconductor (NbSe2) or from the distinct electronic states of the topological insulator is essential for correctly interpreting proximity-induced pairing.

Method: High-resolution scanning tunneling spectroscopy (STS) at dilution-refrigerator temperatures on Bi2Te3/NbSe2 heterostructures with 5QL and 7QL Bi2Te3 films. The experimental spectra were combined with chemical-potential-dependent spectroscopic measurements and model simulations to disentangle the origins of the observed spectral features.

Result: Coherence peaks with inner kinks or inner peaks, deviating from a conventional single s-wave spectral line shape, were observed in both 5QL and 7QL Bi2Te3 films on NbSe2.

Conclusion: The anomalous spectral features arise from the contributions of bulk and surface states in Bi2Te3, which are selectively proximity-coupled to a single superconducting gap from NbSe2, rather than inheriting the complicated order parameter of NbSe2.

Abstract: High-resolution scanning tunneling spectroscopy performed at dilution refrigerator temperatures was employed to investigate the superconducting properties of topological insulator/superconductor (Bi2Te3/NbSe2) heterostructures. Coherence peaks with inner kinks or inner peaks, deviating from the conventional single s-wave spectral line shape, were observed in both 5 quintuple-layer (5QL) and 7QL Bi2Te3 thin films. Based on the combination of chemical-potential-dependent spectroscopic measurements and model simulations, we conclude that these spectral features arise from contributions of bulk and surface states in Bi2Te3 by selectively proximity-coupled to a single superconducting gap from NbSe2, rather than inheriting the complicated superconductor's order parameter of NbSe2.

</details>


### [38] [Kink boundary-induced polarity revealed by nonreciprocal charge transport in FeTe0.7Se0.3 superconductor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14728)
*Shengyao Li,Ya Deng,Han Yan,Dianyi Hu,Zherui Yang,Xueyan Wang,Ming Yue,Qiong Wu,Zheng Liu,X. Renshaw Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The paper achieves nonreciprocal charge transport in centrosymmetric superconductor FeTe0.7Se0.3 via kink boundary-induced strain engineering, which breaks inversion symmetry and enables direction-dependent electrical response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonreciprocal charge transport is a powerful probe of electronic structure and a platform for rectification, but its realization is typically constrained by intrinsic crystal symmetry, limiting the range of usable materials. This work aims to overcome the symmetry restriction for centrosymmetric superconductors.

Method: The authors exploit kink boundary-induced strain engineering. Stress relaxation during growth creates a cusp-like kink-band boundary with asymmetric curvature, generating a spatially nonuniform strain field and an effective in-plane polarity that breaks inversion symmetry in otherwise centrosymmetric FeTe0.7Se0.3 (FTS).

Result: Pronounced nonreciprocal charge transport is observed under out-of-plane magnetic fields, with strong dependence on current and temperature across the superconducting transition. Flat nanosheets show negligible response, contrasting with the kinked samples.

Conclusion: Kink boundary-induced symmetry breaking is identified as a general route to engineer nonreciprocal charge transport in centrosymmetric systems, offering a symmetry-independent design strategy beyond intrinsic crystal symmetry.

Abstract: Nonreciprocal charge transport, characterized by direction-dependent electrical response, provides a powerful probe of electronic structure and a platform for rectification. However, its realization is typically governed by intrinsic crystal symmetry, restricting the range of suitable materials. Here, we demonstrate nonreciprocal charge transport in an otherwise centrosymmetric superconductor, FeTe0.7Se0.3 (FTS), through kink boundary-induced strain engineering. The stress relaxation during growth generates a cusp-like kink-band boundary with asymmetric curvature, producing a spatially nonuniform strain field and an effective in-plane polarity that breaks inversion symmetry. Pronounced nonreciprocal transport is observed under out-of-plane magnetic fields, with strong dependence on current and temperature across the superconducting transition, in contrast to the negligible response in flat nanosheets. Our results identify kink boundary-induced symmetry breaking as a general route to engineer nonreciprocal charge transport in centrosymmetric systems.

</details>


### [39] [Driven Vortex Motion in Periodic Channel Potential Embedded in Superconducting Films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14775)
*A. Al Luhaibi,A. Glatz,Michael Vogl,Hocine Bahlouli,J. B. Ketterson*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Numeric TDGL study of vortex motion in width-modulated superconducting channels shows channel geometry can tune harmonic content of the generated electric field, suppressing higher harmonics to near-pure sinusoidal response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vortex dynamics in superconducting systems can generate oscillatory electric fields, but controlling their harmonic content (relevant for cryogenic microwave waveform generation and qubit control) remains a challenge. Prior work by Al Luhaibi et al. on vortex lattices in periodic pinning landscapes of circular holes did not achieve such harmonic control, motivating exploration of geometric modulation as an alternative tuning knob.

Method: Numerically solve the time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau (TDGL) equations for vortex dynamics in superconducting channels with periodically width-modulated profiles. The channels have sinusoidally varying widths parameterized by average width and modulation amplitude.

Result: Driven vortex motion produces an oscillatory electric field with fundamental frequency f0 set by channel periodicity plus higher-order harmonics. In contrast to vortex lattice dynamics in periodic pinning landscapes of circular holes, the harmonic content can be effectively controlled via channel geometry. Appropriately engineered channel profiles strongly suppress higher-order harmonics, yielding an electric-field response approaching a pure sinusoidal waveform.

Conclusion: Geometric modulation provides a viable route to engineering low-harmonicity microwave signals in superconducting devices, potentially enabling cryogenic on-chip waveform generators for controlling superconducting qubits and serving as a building block for scalable transmon-based quantum computing architectures.

Abstract: In this paper, we investigate vortex dynamics in periodically width-modulated superconducting channels by numerically solving the time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau (TDGL) equations. The channels are designed with sinusoidally varying widths characterized by their average width and modulation amplitude. The motion of driven vortices generates an oscillatory electric field with a fundamental frequency, $f_0$, set by the channel periodicity, together with higher-order harmonics. In contrast to the situation of vortex lattice dynamics in a periodic pinning landscape of circular holes considered by Al Luhaibi et al. [1], we show that the harmonic content of the generated electric field can be effectively controlled through the channel geometry. In particular, appropriately engineered channel profiles strongly suppress higher-order harmonics, yielding an electric-field response that closely approaches a pure sinusoidal waveform. These results demonstrate that geometric modulation offers a viable route to engineering low-harmonicity microwave signals in superconducting devices and may enable cryogenic on-chip waveform generators for controlling superconducting qubits. Such devices could provide a useful building block for scalable transmon-based quantum computing architectures.

</details>


### [40] [Local Suppression of Superconductivity in Niobium Films by Focused Silicon Ion Implantation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15200)
*Rochelle Y. Qu,Shunsuke Nishimura,Max Proepper,Joseph J. Barrera,Alexander Brooks,Joshua Vande Berg,Achim Nadzeyka,Aravind R. Sreeja,Jay C. LeFebvre,Roger K. Lake,Shane A. Cybart*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Focused-ion-beam silicon implantation locally suppresses superconductivity in Nb, creating flux-trapping "moats" without etching; a single power law links critical current, resistance, and reduced temperature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trapped magnetic flux in superconducting circuits shifts Josephson junction critical currents and disrupts logic operation. The conventional fix—etching a moat through the ground plane—requires removing film, so an alternative that suppresses superconductivity locally without etching is desirable.

Method: Single implanted lines were written with a focused silicon ion beam across Nb electrodes in films of three thicknesses, spanning two orders of magnitude in ion fluence. For each line, the transition temperature, resistance, and critical current density were measured. A time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau simulation was used to test whether an implanted region can capture and hold a vortex.

Result: The line transition temperature decreases with fluence while the surrounding Nb remains unchanged. Critical current density drops by roughly two orders of magnitude before the line becomes resistive at 4.2 K. The product of critical current and resistance follows a single power law in reduced temperature, enabling estimation of a line's critical current from its transition temperature and resistance. Simulations indicate implanted regions can capture and hold vortices.

Conclusion: Focused-ion-beam Si implantation is a viable etch-free route to moats in Nb ground planes. The authors propose a starting ion energy and fluence for implanted moats in a 100 nm Nb ground plane at 4.2 K and estimate the equivalent broad-beam fluence.

Abstract: Magnetic flux trapped in a superconducting circuit shifts the critical currents of nearby Josephson junctions and disturbs the operation of the circuit. The usual remedy is a moat etched through the ground plane to hold the flux away from the logic. We report direct write silicon ion implantation with a focused ion beam as a way to make moats without etching the film, by locally suppressing superconductivity in niobium (Nb). Single implanted lines were written across Nb electrodes in films of three thicknesses over two orders of magnitude in ion fluence, and the transition temperature, resistance, and critical current density of each line were measured. The transition temperature of the line falls with fluence while the surrounding Nb is unchanged, and the critical current density falls by about two orders of magnitude before the line becomes resistive at 4.2 K. The product of the critical current and the resistance of the lines follows a single power law in reduced temperature. This gives an estimate of the critical current of a line at any temperature from its transition temperature and resistance. A time dependent Ginzburg-Landau simulation indicates that an implanted region can capture and hold a vortex. From these results we suggest a starting ion energy and fluence for implanted moats in a 100 nm Nb ground plane at 4.2 K and estimate the equivalent broad beam fluence.

</details>


### [41] [Spatial interference changes the symmetry class of a Josephson element](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15746)
*Takayuki Kubo*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Spatial interference of multiple Josephson harmonics can break the inversion symmetry of the effective potential even in a laterally homogeneous, locally inversion-symmetric junction. A harmonic-dependent complex form-factor criterion predicts when inversion-incompatible phases, cubic interactions, and three-wave mixing appear. Phase reflection reverses all odd-order coefficients while preserving the even-order ones and the full quantum spectrum of a capacitively shunted junction, allowing odd-sector reversal without retuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inversion symmetry of an effective Josephson potential is usually assumed to follow from locally inversion-symmetric energy in a laterally homogeneous junction. The paper challenges this assumption, identifying a route to inversion asymmetry driven purely by spatial interference, which is important for engineering nonlinear (cubic) Josephson interactions and three-wave mixing without structural asymmetry.

Method: A general criterion based on harmonic-dependent complex spatial form factors is derived: a single local harmonic yields only a shifted cosine, whereas multiple harmonics can acquire phases incompatible with a common inversion center, allowing cubic interactions. Phase reflection is analyzed about corresponding minima, and its effect on the quantum spectrum of a capacitively shunted junction at fixed charging energy and arbitrary offset charge is examined. A centered cross junction with multiple local harmonics is used as a concrete example, and an imposed-field model with a three-component magnetic field is studied.

Result: Multiple harmonics can carry phases incompatible with any common inversion center, enabling cubic interactions and three-wave mixing. Phase reflection reverses all odd-order expansion coefficients about corresponding minima while preserving even-order ones. For a capacitively shunted junction at fixed charging energy and arbitrary offset charge, it preserves the complete quantum spectrum, enabling reversal of the odd nonlinear sector without spectral retuning. In the imposed-field model, a three-component magnetic field can generate an inversion-asymmetric effective potential, and reversing an odd number of field components exactly reflects it without altering the quantum spectrum.

Conclusion: Spatial interference provides a design mechanism for inversion-asymmetric Josephson potentials via multiple harmonics, yielding cubic nonlinearities and three-wave mixing. Phase reflection offers a symmetry operation that flips odd-order nonlinear terms while leaving the quantum spectrum intact, and external field-component reversal realizes this in the imposed-field model—useful for tunable nonlinear quantum circuits without spectral retuning.

Abstract: Spatial interference can break the inversion symmetry of an effective Josephson potential even in a laterally homogeneous junction with inversion-symmetric local energy. We derive a general criterion based on harmonic-dependent complex spatial form factors: a single local harmonic remains a shifted cosine, whereas multiple harmonics can acquire phases incompatible with any common inversion center, permitting cubic interactions and three-wave mixing. Phase reflection reverses all odd-order expansion coefficients about corresponding minima while preserving the even-order ones. For a capacitively shunted junction at fixed charging energy and arbitrary offset charge, it also preserves the complete quantum spectrum, enabling reversal of the odd nonlinear sector without spectral retuning. A centered cross junction with multiple local harmonics is one concrete example. Within the imposed-field model, a three-component magnetic field can generate an inversion-asymmetric effective potential, and reversing an odd number of field components exactly reflects it without changing the quantum spectrum of the capacitively shunted junction.

</details>


### [42] [Pressure Evolution of the Superconducting Order Parameter of Kagome Superconductor CsV3Sb5](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16253)
*Haiyan Zuo,Wangju Yang,Zhifan Wu,Yuqing Zhao,Qiuxia Chen,Weiming Lao,Yangpeng Qi,Hechang Lei,Haizi Cong,Cong Ren*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Via soft directional point-contact spectroscopy under hydrostatic pressure, CsV3Sb5 exhibits two distinct c-axis s-wave gaps, an anisotropic ab-plane gap, an enhanced 2Delta_avg/kBTc between P1 and P2, and a peak-dip-hump structure signaling bosonic coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CsV3Sb5 shows a pressure-induced double-dome superconducting phase diagram while CDW order is monotonically suppressed, yet the microscopic nature of the pressure-tuned superconducting state and its relation to CDW remain poorly understood.

Method: Soft directional point-contact spectroscopy (SDPCS) combined with superconducting resistive measurements under hydrostatic compression; anisotropic point-contact Andreev reflection (PCAR) and two-band Blonder-Tinkham-Klapwijk (BTK) fitting.

Result: Two distinct s-wave gaps resolved along the c-axis (large gap with 2Delta1/kBTc > 3.52, small gap with 2Deltas/kBTc < 3.52) and a strongly anisotropic ab-plane gap. Spectral-weight-averaged 2Deltaavg/kBTc is enhanced between P1 and P2. In ab-plane junctions, gap anisotropy is gradually suppressed above P1 with a pronounced peak-dip-hump structure.

Conclusion: Pressure-induced modifications of the low-energy electronic structure and enhanced quasiparticle-boson coupling likely drive the evolution of superconductivity, revealing intricate entanglement between CDW and SC in kagome superconductivity.

Abstract: Kagome superconductors AV3Sb5 (A = K, Rb, Cs) offer an outstanding platform for exploring the interplay among charge-density-wave (CDW) order, superconductivity (SC), and nontrivial topological electronic states. For CsV3Sb5, moderate hydrostatic pressure induces a double-dome superconducting phase diagram while monotonically suppressing the CDW phase, which signals an unconventional coupling between SC and CDW. Nevertheless, the microscopic nature of the pressure-modulated superconducting state and its relationship with CDW order remain poorly understood. Here we employ soft directional point-contact spectroscopy (SDPCS), combined with superconducting resistive measurements, to probe the superconducting order parameter of CsV3Sb5 under hydrostatic compression. Our anisotropic PCAR measurements resolve two distinct s-wave superconducting gaps along the c-axis and a strongly anisotropic gap within the ab plane. Two-band Blonder-Tinkham-Klapwijk (BTK) fits to the c-axis spectra yield a large gap satisfying 2Delta1/kBTc > 3.52 and a small gap with 2Deltas/kBTc < 3.52. The spectral-weight-averaged coupling ratio 2Deltaavg/kBTc is enhanced in the pressure window between P1 and P2, suggesting that pressure-induced modifications of the low-energy electronic structure may contribute to the evolution of superconductivity. For ab-plane junctions, the superconducting gap anisotropy is gradually suppressed above P1, accompanied by the emergence of a pronounced peak-dip-hump structure suggestive of enhanced coupling between superconducting quasiparticles and a bosonic excitation. These results shed new light on the intricate entanglement between CDW and SC in kagome superconductivity.

</details>


### [43] [Photon echo from Maki-Thompson interference in a fluctuating s-wave superconductor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16462)
*Hui Zhou,Yuan Wan*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The authors theoretically predict a photon echo in the terahertz nonlinear optical response of a fluctuating s-wave superconductor, originating solely from the Maki-Thompson process, and propose terahertz 2D coherent spectroscopy as a probe to distinguish fluctuation conductivity mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fluctuating superconductors host multiple paraconductivity mechanisms (Maki-Thompson, Aslamazov-Larkin) whose optical signatures are hard to separate; a mechanism-selective nonlinear spectroscopic probe is therefore needed.

Method: Theoretical study of the nonlinear optical response of a fluctuating s-wave superconductor driven by two successive terahertz pulses, analyzed in the framework of fluctuating Cooper pair and quasiparticle dynamics.

Result: A photon echo signal appears, arising from quantum interference between time-reversed electron and hole trajectories in the Maki-Thompson process, whereas the fluctuating Cooper pair transport underlying the Aslamazov-Larkin conductivity produces no echo.

Conclusion: The predicted echo is readily accessible via terahertz two-dimensional coherent spectroscopy, highlighting its potential to discriminate between different fluctuation conductivity mechanisms in fluctuating superconductors.

Abstract: We study theoretically the nonlinear optical response of a fluctuating s-wave superconductor driven by two successive terahertz pulses. We show that the response exhibits a photon echo signal that arises from the quantum interference between the time-reversed electron and hole trajectories in the Maki-Thompson process. By contrast, the transport of the fluctuating Cooper pairs responsible for the Aslamazov-Larkin conductivity does not generate an echo. The photon echo signal can be readily measured using terahertz two-dimensional coherent spectroscopy, highlighting its potential to distinguish different fluctuation conductivity mechanisms.

</details>


### [44] [Molecule-intercalated van der Waals Josephson junctions uncover complex multiband superconductivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16483)
*Daniel Margineda,David Caldevilla-Asenjo,Covadonga Álvarez-García,Daniel Tezze,Raul Arenal,Maxim Ilyn,Celia Rogero,Luis E. Hueso,Marco Gobbi*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Hybrid molecular Josephson junctions based on TaS2/NbSe2 heterostructures exhibit asymmetric Fraunhofer interference, field-induced and spontaneous Josephson diode effects, consistent with a time-reversal-symmetry-breaking multiband superconducting state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Molecular intercalation offers a route to tailor the electronic structure of van der Waals superconductors, but intercalated compounds have rarely been integrated into phase-sensitive quantum devices, limiting the study of their unconventional superconducting order parameters.

Method: The authors fabricated hybrid molecular Josephson junctions from TaS2/NbSe2 heterostructures. Selective intercalation of the TaS2 electrode was used to induce monolayer-like Ising superconductivity. Transport measurements probed Fraunhofer interference and Josephson diode effects, with control experiments reversing the electrode stacking and intercalating the lower NbSe2 electrode.

Result: The junctions show asymmetric Fraunhofer interference along with field-induced and spontaneous Josephson diode effects. The zero-field diode effect disappears upon reversing the electrode stacking and reappears after intercalating the lower NbSe2 electrode, indicating that the hybrid superconducting electrode hosts unconventional superconductivity underlying the nonreciprocal transport, with molecular intercalation acting as a tuning knob for its internal phase structure.

Conclusion: The observed behavior is consistent with a time-reversal-symmetry-breaking multiband superconducting state with a nontrivial relative phase between superconducting condensates. Hybrid molecular Josephson junctions are established as a quantum-device platform for phase-sensitive investigation and integration of molecularly engineered superconductors, opening opportunities to uncover and engineer unconventional superconducting phases.

Abstract: Intercalation of molecular species provides a route to tailor the electronic structure in van der Waals superconductors, yet intercalated compounds have rarely been integrated into phase-sensitive quantum devices. Here, we realize hybrid molecular Josephson junctions based on TaS2/NbSe2 heterostructures. Selective intercalation of the TaS2 electrode induces monolayer-like Ising superconductivity, while the junctions exhibit asymmetric Fraunhofer interference, field-induced and spontaneous Josephson diode effects. The zero-field diode disappears upon reversing the electrode stacking and re-emerges after intercalating the lower NbSe2 electrode. These findings indicate that the hybrid superconducting electrode hosts unconventional superconductivity underlying the nonreciprocal transport, while molecular intercalation acts as a tuning knob for its internal phase structure. This behavior is consistent with a time-reversal-symmetry-breaking multiband superconducting state characterized by a nontrivial relative phase between superconducting condensates. Our results establish hybrid molecular Josephson junctions as a quantum-device platform for phase-sensitive investigation and integration of molecularly engineered superconductors, opening opportunities to uncover and engineer unconventional superconducting phases.

</details>


### [45] [Superconductivity from altermagnetic fluctuations: the crucial role of sublattice polarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16507)
*Mats Barkman,Andreas Kreisel,Brian M. Andersen*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Altermagnetic fluctuations near a magnetic instability can drive unconventional superconductivity, with sublattice polarization (SLP) of normal-state bands playing a pivotal role: SLP blocks inter-sublattice Cooper pairing, suppressing nearest-neighbor AFM-mediated spin-singlet pairing and favoring longer-range same-sublattice bonds, yielding spin-triplet superconductivity for ferromagnetic next-nearest-neighbor exchange.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unconventional superconductivity in correlated systems frequently emerges near a magnetically ordered state. This applies to altermagnets, which feature a nonrelativistic spin-split electronic structure under Néel order. However, many proposed altermagnetic candidates never cross the magnetic instability, so the superconducting pairing mechanism in the fluctuation regime of such systems remains unexplored.

Method: The authors study superconductivity arising from altermagnetic fluctuations in systems that do not cross the magnetic instability. They analyze how the sublattice polarization (SLP) of the normal-state bands—an inherent property of altermagnetic band structures that persists even without magnetic order—affects Cooper pairing, examining nearest-neighbor antiferromagnetic exchange, longer-range same-sublattice bonds, and next-nearest-neighbor ferromagnetic exchange expected for frustration-free Néel order.

Result: SLP prevents Cooper pairs from forming between different sublattices, suppressing nearest-neighbor antiferromagnetic-exchange-mediated spin-singlet pairing and leaving longer-range same-sublattice bonds dominant. With ferromagnetic next-nearest-neighbor exchange (as expected for frustration-free Néel order), this yields spin-triplet superconductivity. This mechanism fundamentally differs from that in altermagnets with long-range Néel order, where spin-split Fermi surfaces favor equal-spin pairing. When longer-range interactions in the disordered phase instead favor singlet pairing, SLP leads to singlet states with characteristic properties distinct from nearest-neighbor pairing states.

Conclusion: Sublattice polarization is the key determinant of the superconducting state in altermagnetic-fluctuation-driven systems, enabling spin-triplet pairing via longer-range same-sublattice bonds (for ferromagnetic next-nearest-neighbor exchange) or distinctive singlet states. This constitutes a fundamentally distinct mechanism from that operating in altermagnets with long-range Néel order.

Abstract: Unconventional superconductivity in correlated systems often appears in the vicinity of a magnetically ordered state. This situation is also relevant for altermagnets, characterized by a nonrelativistic spin-split electronic structure in the presence of Néel order. Many proposed altermagnetic candidates, however, never cross the magnetic instability. We study superconductivity arising from altermagnetic fluctuations in such systems and show that the sublattice polarization (SLP) of the normal-state bands plays a pivotal role in determining the superconducting state. SLP, an inherent property of altermagnetic band structures that persists even without magnetic order, prevents Cooper pairs from forming between different sublattices. This suppresses pairing mediated by the nearest-neighbor antiferromagnetic exchange, which favors spin-singlet superconductivity, and leaves longer-range same-sublattice bonds dominant. When the next-nearest-neighbor exchange is ferromagnetic, as expected for frustration-free Néel order, this results in spin-triplet superconductivity. This mechanism is fundamentally different from that in altermagnets with long-range Néel order, where the spin-split Fermi surfaces favor equal-spin pairing. Finally, when longer-range interactions in the disordered phase instead favor singlet pairing, SLP leads to singlet states with characteristic properties distinct from nearest-neighbor pairing states.

  <summary>Details</summary>

MIT oxides are natural candidates, but the only one previously tested in a coil, V2O3, transitions at 150–170 K, well above the 30–70 K window useful for REBCO magnets. Lowering the transition temperature is therefore necessary.

Cryogenic measurements revealed a reproducible thermal hysteresis, and the measured specific heat of V2O3 corrects earlier model inputs by close to two orders of magnitude. The electro-thermal model reproduces current sharing through the smart-insulation layer.

Conclusion: Multi-phase vanadium-oxygen powders lower the effective MIT to ~127 K, a step toward the 30–70 K REBCO window, while hydrothermal V3O7 is unsuitable. The corrected V2O3 specific heat and the validated electro-thermal current-sharing model provide improved inputs for future design. Doped V2O3 and V8O15 are identified as the next targets.

Abstract: Smart insulation is a turn-to-turn layer that insulates in normal operation and conducts only near a hot spot. It could combine the fast ramping of insulated coils with the passive energy dump of non-insulated coils. Metal-insulator transition (MIT) oxides are natural candidates, but the only one tested in a coil so far, V2O3, switches at 150-170 K, far above the 30-70 K window useful for REBCO magnets. We explore the vanadium-oxygen system to lower the transition. TGA redox processing produced multi-phase powders with an effective transition near 127 K, about 25 K below V2O3. Hydrothermal V3O7, hydrated or annealed, was far too resistive and showed no usable transition. V2O3-epoxy specimens provided a robust reference and an acceptance criterion. Cryogenic measurements revealed a reproducible thermal hysteresis, and the measured specific heat of V2O3 corrects earlier model inputs by close to two orders of magnitude. A first electro-thermal model reproduces current sharing through the smart-insulation layer. Doped V2O3 and V8O15 are identified as the next targets.

*Siddhant Panda,Andreas Kreisel,Laura Fanfarillo,P. J. Hirschfeld*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Twistronics has been extended to twisted bilayer cuprates, where $d+id'$ states near a $45^\circ$ twist are predicted. However, experimental and theoretical results show large variability, and existing models disagree, demanding an accurate microscopic model, especially a realistic treatment of interlayer tunneling.

Method: The in-plane hopping is fitted and interlayer tunneling amplitudes are computed via orbital overlap between $d_{x^2-y^2}$ wave functions at each site of the twisted square-lattice model. Band structure, Fermi surfaces, and spectral functions are compared with a phenomenological model; a phase diagram is derived as a function of twist angle, filling, and direct tunneling strength (set by the $c$-axis interlayer distance). Josephson critical currents are computed across the phase diagram.

A substantial redistribution of spectral weight modifies the competition between superconducting ground states, and the region of TRSB $d+id'$ states is reduced compared with the phenomenological model. Josephson critical currents are strongly suppressed close to a $45^\circ$ twist, consistent with experiments.

Conclusion: A realistic microscopic description of interlayer tunneling substantially alters the phase diagram of twisted bilayer cuprates, shrinking the TRSB $d+id'$ region and producing suppressed Josephson currents near $45^\circ$, in agreement with experimental observations.

Abstract: The field of twistronics, recently extended to twisted bilayer cuprate systems, has generated considerable interest due to the prediction of $d+id'$ states close to a $45^\circ$ twist, as suggested by multiple experimental probes. Theoretical studies of these systems using a variety of models and many-body theoretical techniques have revealed multiple competing order parameters. The large variability in experimental results and theoretical predictions demands an accurate microscopic model for these systems, in particular a more realistic description of the interlayer tunneling. In this study, we fit the in-plane hopping and compute the interlayer tunneling amplitudes by calculating the orbital overlap between $d_{x^2-y^2}$ wave functions at each site of the twisted square-lattice model. We observe sign-changing tunneling amplitudes, a strong twist-angle dependence of the direct tunneling term, and long-range tunneling amplitudes that often dominate over direct tunneling. We investigate the band structure, Fermi surfaces, and spectral functions in comparison with those obtained from the phenomenological model and find a substantial redistribution of spectral weight that modifies the competition between different superconducting ground states. We determine the phase diagram as a function of twist angle, filling, and direct tunneling strength, which is controlled by the $c$-axis distance between the monolayers, and observe a reduction in the region of time-reversal-symmetry-breaking (TRSB) $d+id'$ states compared with the phenomenological model. Finally, we investigate the Josephson critical current across the phase diagram and find strongly suppressed critical currents close to a $45^\circ$ twist, consistent with experimental observations.

</details>

*Juan S. Cruz,Emilio De Biasi,Juan C. Zapata,Yeonkyu Lee,Jeehoon Kim,Nestor Haberkorn*

  <summary>Details</summary>

Abstract: Near-ideal, field-free superconducting diode behavior is demonstrated in room-temperature-grown Mo$_2$N/FePt bilayers. The response is consistent with remanent FePt stripe domains producing polarity-dependent thresholds for flux entry and vortex motion. Micromagnetic simulations reveal a periodic stray-field modulation across the stripes and asymmetric field enhancement near their ends at the bridge edges. One current polarity remains nearly dissipationless up to a sizable fraction of the estimated depairing current, whereas the opposite polarity develops dissipation at much lower current, yielding diode efficiencies up to approximately 85% in the thinner bilayer. The nonreciprocity can be continuously tuned by the in-plane magnetic preparation angle and reversibly switched by reverse-field excursions through the hysteretic reversal of the FePt layer. These results support edge-controlled flux entry and vortex motion as a route to high-efficiency superconducting diodes.

### [49] [H$_2$-Like Units and Pressure-Tunable High-$T_\mathrm{c}$ Superconductivity in NaH$_6$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17148)
*Tianran Chen,Taner Yildirim*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: First-principles study of NaH6 identifies competing R-3m and Pm-3m phases with calculated Tc up to 99 K and 230 K at 50 GPa, respectively, despite containing elongated H2-like units.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hydrogen-rich materials can host high-temperature conventional superconductivity but typically require extreme pressures; there is a need to find hydrides that achieve high Tc at more moderate pressures.

Method: Large-scale hydride search combined with first-principles calculations, including dynamical stability analysis, molecular dynamics, phonon-renormalization, mode-resolved electron-phonon coupling, and a minimal symmetry-based model of the vibration-induced second-moment tensor of the hydrogen sublattice.

Result: R-3m and Pm-3m phases dominate over broad pressure ranges. R-3m is dynamically stable down to 10 GPa with Tc = 34 K, rising to 99 K at 50 GPa. Pm-3m becomes thermodynamically competitive at higher pressures, reaching Tc = 230 K at 50 GPa and decreasing only weakly under further compression. Anharmonic effects are relatively small and structures persist over the sampled timescale.

Conclusion: NaH6 is an unusual molecular-like hydride in which hydrogen-sublattice reorganization, linked to tilt of H2 units and perpendicular response components, may enable high-Tc superconductivity at comparatively moderate pressures.

Abstract: Hydrogen-rich materials offer a route to high-temperature conventional superconductivity, but often require extreme pressures. Here we report a first-principles study of NaH$_6$, identified in our large-scale hydride search and independently predicted in a subsequent high-throughput study. Several phases compete, with $R\bar{3}m$ and $Pm\bar{3}m$ dominant over broad pressure ranges. The $R\bar{3}m$ phase remains dynamically stable down to 10 GPa, where its calculated $T_\mathrm{c}$ is 34 K, rising to 99 K at 50 GPa. Cubic $Pm\bar{3}m$ becomes thermodynamically competitive at higher pressures and reaches a calculated $T_\mathrm{c}$ of 230 K at 50 GPa, decreasing only weakly upon further compression. Molecular dynamics and phonon-renormalization calculations support structural persistence over the sampled timescale and indicate relatively small anharmonic effects. Despite their high $T_\mathrm{c}$, both phases contain elongated H$_2$-like units, often considered unfavorable for high-$T_\mathrm{c}$ hydrides. Mode-resolved electron-phonon coupling links Raman-active hydrogen vibrations to superconductivity. In $R\bar{3}m$, the linewidths of these modes increase with the pressure-induced tilt of H$_2$ units relative to the threefold axis, alongside the rise in $T_\mathrm{c}$. A minimal symmetry-based model of the vibration-induced second-moment tensor of the hydrogen sublattice shows that these trends are consistent with a significant contribution from response components perpendicular to the threefold axis. NaH$_6$ thus represents an unusual molecular-like hydride in which hydrogen-sublattice reorganization may enable high-$T_\mathrm{c}$ superconductivity at comparatively moderate pressures.

</details>


### [50] [Observation of stacked vortex structure with number controllable Majorana zero mode in Bi$_{2}$Te$_{3}$/FeTe heterojunction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17701)
*Xinru Han,Zhaokun Luo,Quanxin Hu,Xingjian Zhu,Xiaohong Pan,Dan Mu,Xinyu Liang,Zulin Xu,Qiheng Wang,Ryan L. Kong,Mingyuan Li,Zhengliang Zhang,Fazhi Yang,Mojun Pan,Xin Liu,Qiangqiang Gu,Baiqing Lv,Gan Wang,Hong Ding*

TL;DR: The paper reports spectroscopic evidence for number-controllable Majorana zero modes (MZMs) in stoichiometric one-quintuple-layer Bi₂Te₃/FeTe heterostructures, where moiré-modulated superconducting gaps and stacked vortex cores host isolated, field- and island-size-tunable MZMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Realizing fault-tolerant topological quantum computation requires the creation and control of MZMs in solid-state devices. A controllable platform hosting isolated and few-vortex Majorana configurations is needed to enable fusion of MZMs.

Method: Low-temperature scanning tunnelling microscopy and spectroscopy (STM/STS) on stoichiometric one-quintuple-layer (1QL) Bi₂Te₃/FeTe heterostructures; analysis of the moiré superlattice, superconducting gap modulation, vortex core structure, and vortex bound states.

Result: A long-period moiré superlattice from lattice mismatch induces nanoscale modulation of superconducting gaps. Magnetic vortices show a stacked core structure with two layer-derived bound state branches: a dispersive branch forming an X-shaped pattern away from the vortex center, and a quantum-limit branch with spatially unsplit MZMs plus discrete integer-quantized Caroli-de Gennes-Matricon states. MZMs appear in all measured vortices and their number is tunable via magnetic field and Bi₂Te₃ island size.

Conclusion: Stoichiometric 1QL-Bi₂Te₃/FeTe is established as a patterned superconducting interface enabling isolated and few-vortex Majorana configurations, offering a controlled platform for MZM fusion.

Abstract: Creation and control of Majorana zero modes (MZMs) in solid devices is the key challenge in realizing fault tolerant topological quantum computation. Here we report spectroscopic evidence for number controllable MZMs in stoichiometric one quintuple layer Bi$_{2}$Te$_{3}$/FeTe heterostructures, together with the observation of stacked vortices in superconducting islands. Low temperature scanning tunnelling microscopy and spectroscopy reveal a long period moire superlattice arising from lattice mismatch, which induces nanoscale modulation of superconducting gaps. Magnetic vortices exhibit a stacked core structure with two layer derived bound state branches: a dispersive branch evolving into an X-shaped pattern away from the vortex center, and a quantum-limit branch characterized by a spatially unsplit MZMs together with discrete integer quantized Caroli-de Gennes-Matricon states. Notably, MZMs are observed in all measured vortices, and their number can be tuned by magnetic field and the size of Bi$_{2}$Te$_{3}$ islands. These results establish stoichiometric 1QL-Bi$_{2}$Te$_{3}$/FeTe as a patterned superconducting interface that enables isolated and few-vortex Majorana configurations, providing a controlled platform for fuse MZMs.

</details>


### [51] [Imaging atomic-scale surface structure and superconducting gap of the misfit layered compound (SnS)1.17NbS2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17879)
*Dongming Zhao,Shamarita Deb,Lichen Ji,Souvik Das,Paul Simon,Raúl Cardoso-Gil,Steffen Wirth,Walter Schnelle,Sushmita Chandra,Jian-Feng Ge,Claudia Felser*

TL;DR: The misfit layered compound (SnS)1.17NbS2 forms a natural superlattice, yet its surface atomic registry differs from the bulk misfit structure; nevertheless, superconductivity probed at 2 K shows nearly identical gaps on both SnS and NbS2 terminations with no misfit-induced modulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Van der Waals superlattices enable band-structure engineering and emergent quantum states. Misfit layered compounds are naturally assembled superlattices combining crystallographically distinct 2D subsystems in one bulk crystal; their coexisting commensurate and incommensurate directions create structural modulation that could modify real-space electronic orders such as superconductivity. However, the atomic-scale surface structure and local superconducting behavior of such misfit heterostructures remain poorly characterized.

Method: The authors combine structural characterization (X-ray diffraction and cross-sectional scanning transmission electron microscopy), electrical transport measurements, and low-temperature scanning tunnelling microscopy/spectroscopy (STM/STS) to study the surface structure and local superconductivity of the misfit layered compound (SnS)1.17NbS2.

Tunnelling spectroscopy at 2 K shows nearly identical superconducting gaps on both terminations, and the spatial variation of the gap exhibits no visible modulation corresponding to the misfit lattice.

Conclusion: The work establishes an atomic-scale picture of the surface structure and superconducting states of the naturally assembled misfit heterostructure (SnS)1.17NbS2, showing that bulk misfit structural modulation does not translate into a measurable spatial modulation of the superconducting gap at the surface.

Abstract: Superlattices of van der Waals-coupled two-dimensional materials offer a platform for engineering electronic band structure and fostering emergent quantum states. Misfit layered compounds provide naturally assembled superlattices, in which crystallographically distinct two-dimensional subsystems coexist within a single bulk crystal. Therein, the presence of both commensurate and incommensurate lattice directions introduces structural modulation, which might alter real-space electronic orders such as superconductivity. Here, we investigate the atomic-scale surface structure and local superconductivity of the misfit layered compound (SnS)1.17NbS2 using structural characterization, electrical transport, and low-temperature scanning tunnelling microscopy (STM). While X-ray diffraction and cross-sectional scanning transmission electron microscopy confirm the bulk misfit structure, STM resolves large SnS- and NbS2-terminated terraces, where the surface atomic registry differs from the bulk misfit structure. Furthermore, tunnelling spectroscopy at 2 K reveals nearly identical superconducting gaps on both terminations, and the spatial variation of the gap shows no visible modulation that corresponds to the misfit lattice. These results establish an atomic-scale picture of the surface structure and superconducting states of this naturally assembled misfit heterostructure.

</details>


### [52] [Effects of the choice of the exchange-correlation functional on high-temperature superconductivity predictions in covalent-cage M(BN)5 family](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18171)
*Zhongyu Wan,Guo-Hua Zhong,Cheng-wei Ju,Ruiqin Zhang,Hai-Qing Lin*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This work quantifies how exchange-correlation functional choice affects electron-phonon coupling in M(BN)5 covalent cage superconductors, showing PBE overestimates lattice volumes and causes spurious Peierls instability, while PBEsol gives robust ambient-pressure Tc up to 113 K.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The experimental synthesis of covalent cage superconductors has raised hopes for ambient-pressure high-temperature superconductivity, but computationally predicting new such materials is difficult because superconducting properties are hard to estimate accurately. A key unresolved issue is how sensitive the predicted superconductivity of these covalent materials is to the choice of exchange-correlation functional.

Method: High-throughput first-principles calculations were used to quantify the response of electron-phonon coupling (EPC) properties in M(BN)5 superconductors to different exchange-correlation (XC) effects, comparing functionals such as PBE and PBEsol, and analyzing phonon spectra, EPC, electronic bands, and pressure dependence.

Result: Metallic covalent bonds responsible for superconductivity show anomalously high sensitivity to functional selection, unlike conventional metallic-bonding superconductors. PBE systematically overestimates lattice volumes, inducing phonon softening, enhanced EPC, and Peierls instability. PBEsol, with better short-range interaction treatment, removes imaginary frequencies in Rb(BN)5 predicted by PBE, yielding Tc of 113 K at ambient pressure and 138 K at 2 GPa. Enhanced electron localization and short-range correlation act via phonon hardening at lattice scale and band widening at electronic scale, suppressing Peierls distortion and delocalizing antibonding states. Compression attenuates phonon discrepancies between functionals, while electronic properties remain sensitive to XC-induced perturbations of fine bands near the Fermi level.

Conclusion: The choice of XC functional critically governs predicted superconducting properties of covalent cage superconductors, because their metallic covalent bonds are unusually sensitive to functional-dependent volume and short-range correlation effects. PBEsol is recommended over PBE for accurate predictions, and Rb(BN)5 emerges as a promising ambient-pressure high-temperature superconductor candidate.

Abstract: The recent experimental synthesis of covalent cage superconductors has ignited hopes for ambient-pressure high-temperature superconductivity, while the computational strategy for discovering new materials poses challenges for accurately estimating superconducting properties. Here, we quantified the response of electron-phonon coupling (EPC) properties in M(BN)5 superconductors to different exchange-correlation (XC) effects using high-throughput first-principles calculations. We found that the metallic covalent bonds responsible for superconductivity exhibit anomalously high sensitivity to functional selection uncertainty, distinguishing them from conventional superconductors mediated by metallic bonding. The widely used PBE functional systematically overestimates lattice volumes, inducing phonon softening, enhanced EPC, and consequent Peierls instability. In contrast, PBEsol, which is more accurate through enhanced treatment of short-range interactions, eliminates imaginary frequencies in Rb(BN)5 that appear at the PBE level, yielding an outstanding superconducting critical temperature (Tc) of 113 K at ambient pressure and 138 K at 2 GPa. Enhanced electron localization and short-range correlation in covalent bonds manifest through dual mechanisms: phonon hardening at the lattice scale and band widening at the electronic scale, which collectively suppress Peierls distortion and delocalize antibonding states. Pressure-dependent analysis reveals that compression attenuates phonon discrepancies between XC functionals, whereas electronic properties remain notably sensitive to XC-induced perturbations of fine electronic bands near the Fermi level.

</details>


### [53] [Quantitative Impact of Occupation Smearing Methods on Conventional Superconductivity: from Electronic Structure through Lattice Dynamics to Electron-Phonon Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18185)
*Zhongyu Wan*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This work reveals that the empirical smearing parameter in first-principles metallic calculations can severely distort predicted phonon-mediated superconductivity, and proposes a free-electron-gas scaling relation for objectively choosing the smearing width.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In first-principles calculations of metals, the discrete Brillouin-zone electronic mesh requires a smearing function and width (e.g., the conventional 0.02 Ry Methfessel-Paxton scheme) to ensure continuity of energy and occupation at the Fermi surface. Despite being empirical, the quantitative impact of these parameters on phonon-mediated superconductivity has been overlooked, with values chosen by mere convention.

Method: Using hexahydride CsH6 as a benchmark, the authors systematically quantify the magnitude and propagation mechanism of smearing-induced errors by comparing against the parameter-free optimized tetrahedron method, and derive a scaling relation for the smearing width from the free-electron-gas model.

Result: Long-tailed smearing delocalizes the occupation window, causing substantial Fermi-level shifts, density-of-states flattening, and phonon hardening, which weakens electron-phonon coupling. Short-tailed smearing yields insufficient sampling, producing density-of-states oscillations and enhanced Fermi-surface nesting; via amplified electronic screening and Peierls instabilities this softens acoustic modes and generates imaginary frequencies, erroneously rendering CsH6 dynamically unstable. The optimized tetrahedron method instead shows CsH6 is dynamically stable at ambient pressure with a superconducting transition temperature of 121 K.

Conclusion: Subjective empirical parameters can obscure theoretically promising high-temperature superconductors. A simple scaling relation for the smearing width, applicable to different smearing functions and derived from the free-electron-gas model, provides an objective, precise, and efficient protocol for parameter selection in high-throughput screening.

Abstract: In first-principles calculations of metallic systems, the discrete electronic mesh in the Brillouin zone necessitates a smearing function and its width (e.g., the widely used 0.02 Ry Methfessel-Paxton scheme) to enforce continuity of energy and occupation at the Fermi surface. However, the quantitative impact of such empirical parameters on phonon-mediated superconductivity has long been overlooked, leading to their habitual selection by mere convention. Here, using hexahydride CsH6 as a benchmark, we systematically investigate the magnitude and propagation mechanism of smearing-induced errors against the parameter-free optimized tetrahedron method. Long-tailed smearing delocalizes the occupation window, causing substantial Fermi-level shifts, flattening of the density of states, and hardening phonon modes, thereby weakening electron-phonon coupling. Conversely, short-tailed smearing suffers from insufficient sampling, producing density-of-states oscillations and enhanced Fermi-surface nesting; via amplified electronic screening and Peierls instabilities, this softens acoustic modes and generates imaginary frequencies, causing CsH6 to be dynamically unstable and discarded. However, the optimized tetrahedron method reveals that CsH6 is dynamically stable under ambient pressure with a remarkable superconducting transition temperature of 121 K. To prevent subjective empirical parameters from obscuring theoretically promising high-temperature superconductors, we derive a simple scaling relation for the smearing width, applicable to different smearing functions, from the free-electron-gas model, offering an objective, precise, and efficient protocol for parameter selection in high-throughput screening.

</details>


### [54] [Phonon-Mediated Superconductivity near Phase Boundaries in Rhombohedral Multilayer Graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18286)
*Konstantinos Sourounis,Peter M. Oppeneer*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This work shows that phonon-mediated pairing, treated via self-consistent Migdal-Eliashberg equations with first-principles input and interaction-driven symmetry breaking, reproduces the multiple superconducting regions observed in untwisted (rhombohedral) multilayer graphene as functions of displacement field and carrier density, and predicts additional undiscovered phases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Superconductivity in untwisted multilayer graphene appears in several distinct regions with different temperature and magnetic-field responses, suggesting possibly distinct microscopic mechanisms. Purely electronic pairing mechanisms have been extensively studied, but a detailed investigation of phonon-mediated superconductivity was still lacking.

Method: The authors solve the coupled self-consistent Migdal-Eliashberg equations, using inputs from first-principles calculations and including interaction-driven symmetry breaking of the electronic ground state. They vary carrier density and displacement field to map superconducting behavior.

Result: The calculations reproduce the experimentally observed superconducting regions with comparable superconducting properties, including the gap and Tc, and predict additional, as-yet undiscovered superconducting phases.

Conclusion: Several superconductivity regions in rhombohedral graphene can be satisfactorily explained by phonon-mediated pairing, demonstrating that a single phonon-based framework can account for the multiple observed superconducting regimes.

Abstract: The recent observation of superconductivity in untwisted multilayer graphene has sparked intense interest in its microscopic origin. As the displacement field and carrier density are varied, superconductivity emerges in several distinct regions with different temperature and magnetic-field responses. This behavior has been attributed to the presence of distinct electronic phases, raising the possibility that different superconducting regions originate from different microscopic mechanisms. While purely electronic pairing mechanisms have been extensively explored, an in-depth examination of phonon-mediated superconductivity is still missing. Here, we solve the coupled selfconsistent Migdal-Eliashberg equations using input from first-principles calculations and incorporating interaction-driven symmetry breaking of the electronic ground state. Varying the carrier density and displacement field, we reproduce the experimentally observed superconducting regions with comparable superconducting properties such as gap and $T_c$ and predict additional, as-yet undiscovered superconducting phases. Our findings exemplify that several superconductivity regions in rhombohedral graphene can be satisfactorily explained by phonon-mediated pairing.

</details>


### [55] [The role of dependency range in thermally coupled superconducting networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18418)
*Nahala Yadid,Bnaya Gross,Shlomo Havlin,Nadav M. Shnerb*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Thermally coupled amorphous superconducting networks are studied via a calibrated resistively shunted Josephson junction model, reproducing the three canonical transition regimes but revealing that physical, heat-mediated interdependence behaves differently from externally imposed geometric dependency links.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geometric network models dominate the theoretical understanding of cascading failure in interdependent networks, but they impose dependency range externally and treat coupling strength as distance independent. It is unclear whether these insights transfer to real materials, where interdependence arises from physical transport, so an experimentally grounded framework is needed.

Method: The authors model thermally coupled amorphous superconducting networks using an experimentally calibrated model of resistively shunted Josephson junctions, where dependency links emerge from current redistribution and heat-mediated interactions governed by thermal diffusivity.

Result: The system reproduces the three canonical transition regimes: continuous, nucleation-dominated, and mixed-order. Because heat-driven dependency simultaneously broadens and weakens with distance, long-range thermal coupling can stabilize the superconducting phase upon heating, opposite to its destabilizing role in geometric models. Cascades are anisotropic, exhibiting one-dimensional front propagation.

Conclusion: Physical transport-based interdependence yields behaviors that deviate qualitatively from geometric network predictions, offering an experimentally testable perspective that complements the geometric framework.

Abstract: Interdependent networks can be highly fragile because dependency links amplify local failures into cascades that trigger global collapse. Much of the theoretical understanding comes from geometric network models, where dependency range is imposed externally and coupling strength is distance independent, and it is unclear how these insights apply to real materials, where interdependence arises from physical transport. Here we study thermally coupled amorphous superconducting networks, in which dependencies emerge from current redistribution and heat-mediated interactions governed by thermal diffusivity. Using an experimentally calibrated model of resistively shunted Josephson junctions, we show the system reproduces the three canonical transition regimes: continuous, nucleation-dominated, and mixed-order. However, because heat-driven dependency simultaneously broadens and weakens with distance, long-range thermal coupling can stabilize the superconducting phase upon heating, in contrast with its destabilizing role in geometric models. Cascades are anisotropic, with one-dimensional front propagation. These results offer an experimentally testable perspective complementing the geometric framework.

</details>


### [56] [Twofold Symmetry Observed in Bi$_{2}$Te$_{3}$/FeTe Interfacial Superconductor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.05904)
*Xinru Han,Hailang Qin,Tianluo Pan,Bin Guo,Kaige Shi,Zijin Huang,Jie Jiang,Hangyu Yin,Hongtao He,Fei Ye,Wei-Qiang Chen,Jia-Wei Mei,Gan Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The paper reports a twofold superconducting gap symmetry in the interfacial superconductor Bi2Te3/FeTe observed via quasiparticle interference (QPI) in scanning tunneling microscopy and macroscopic magnetoresistance measurements, with the symmetry aligning with the Te-Te direction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the microscopic superconducting mechanism of a superconductor requires knowledge of its superconducting pairing symmetry. This study aims to determine the pairing symmetry of the interfacial superconductor Bi2Te3/FeTe to shed light on its topological properties and pairing mechanism.

Method: Quasiparticle interference (QPI) technique in scanning tunneling microscopy, along with macroscopic magnetoresistance measurements, were employed. The QPI patterns at energies inside and outside the gap were analyzed, and both in-plane angle-dependent magnetoresistance and in-plane upper critical field were measured.

Result: A clear anisotropic superconducting gap with twofold symmetry was observed. Both the in-plane angle-dependent magnetoresistance and the in-plane upper critical field exhibited a clear twofold symmetry. This twofold symmetry aligns with the Te-Te direction in FeTe.

Conclusion: The observed twofold symmetry aligns with the Te-Te direction in FeTe, weakening the possible generation by bi-collinear antiferromagnetism order. These findings provide key information for understanding the topological properties in the Bi2Te3/FeTe superconducting system and stimulate further theoretical interest in its pairing mechanism.

Abstract: Superconducting pairing symmetry are crucial in understanding the microscopic superconducting mechanism of a superconductor. Here we report the observation of a twofold superconducting gap symmetry in an interfacial superconductor Bi$_{2}$Te$_{3}$/FeTe, by employing quasiparticle interference (QPI) technique in scanning tunneling microscopy and macroscopic magnetoresistance measurements. The QPI patterns corresponding to energies inside and outside the gap reveal a clear anisotropic superconducting gap. Furthermore, both the in-plane angle-dependent magnetoresistance and in-plane upper critical field exhibit a clear twofold symmetry. This twofold symmetry align with the Te-Te direction in FeTe, which weakens the possible generation by bi-collinear antiferromagnetism order. Our finding provides key information in further understanding of the topological properties in Bi$_{2}$Te$_{3}$/FeTe superconducting system and propels further theoretical interests in the paring mechanism in the system.

</details>

### [57] [Real-Space Interference from Quantum Metric](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14048)
*Bo-Qing Wu,Xing-Lei Ma,Jin-Xin Hu,Jinchao Zhao,K. T. Law*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Quantum metric hot spots in momentum space act like wave-packet components, producing real-space interference in bound-state density profiles; this is observable via Josephson STM critical-current mapping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-space interference of wave packets is normally understood via central momentum separation and momentum width (setting oscillation wavelength and envelope). The paper asks whether an analogous mechanism exists in crystalline systems, where the quantum metric distribution of an isolated band could generate real-space interference through evanescent tails of a localized bound state.

Method: The authors analyze bound states in crystalline bands and relate quantum metric hot spots (localized maxima of the quantum metric in momentum space) to complex wavevector evanescent plane-wave components of the bound state. They consider single and multiple hot spots, and two spatially overlapped bound states, deriving the resulting density interference. They then propose a Josephson STM measurement in which the tip-position dependence of the Josephson critical current probes the real-space interference.

Result: A quantum metric hot spot's location and width define a complex wavevector for an evanescent component of the bound state. With multiple hot spots, the bound-state density (or total density of two overlapped bound states) shows an interference pattern whose oscillation period is set by the momentum-space separation of hot spots and whose long-range decay length is set by the width of the narrowest hot spot. The effect is most pronounced in (nearly) flat bands, where band-dispersion scales are suppressed. Josephson STM can experimentally detect it via the tip-position dependence of the critical current.

Conclusion: Momentum-space quantum geometry maps directly onto real-space interference: bound-state profiles encode the momentum-resolved quantum metric. This establishes a correspondence between quantum metric hot spots and wave-packet interference, and offers a Josephson STM route to experimentally access quantum geometry.

Abstract: Real-space interference of wave packets is governed by their central momentum separation and momentum width, which set the oscillation wavelength and spatial envelope, respectively. Here, we show that in crystalline systems, quantum metric distribution in an isolated band can similarly give rise to real-space interference through the evanescent tails of a localized bound state. The location and width of a quantum metric hot spot in the momentum space set a complex wavevector as one evanescent plane wave component of a bound state. Consequently, with multiple quantum metric hot spots, the density distribution of a bound state or the total density of two spatially overlapped bound states exhibits an interference pattern, where the oscillation period and long-range decay length are determined by the momentum-space separation of the hot spots and the width of the narrowest hot spots, respectively. This quantum-geometric interference is particularly pronounced in (nearly) flat bands, where competing scales from band dispersion are suppressed. Furthermore, we demonstrate that using a Josephson STM the real-space interference can be experimentally observed through the tip-position dependence of the Josephson critical current. Our results reveal a direct correspondence between momentum-space quantum geometry and real-space interference, showing how bound-state profiles encode the momentum-resolved quantum metric.

</details>


### [58] [Electric-dipole-induced nuclear electric resonance in silicon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14139)
*Jiyun Su,Shihang Zhang,Peihao Huang*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper theoretically proposes fast electric-dipole-induced nuclear electric resonance (EDNER) for phosphorus nuclear spins in silicon, using hyperfine interactions and magnetic field gradients to enable all-electrical, addressable, high-fidelity nuclear spin control for scalable silicon quantum computing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The nuclear spin of a phosphorus atom in silicon is a promising quantum information carrier due to its long coherence time. However, coherent control is typically achieved via nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR), which relies on magnetic driving and is less suited to scalable, all-electrical architectures. There is a need for faster, electrically addressable control of nuclear spins.

Method: The authors theoretically propose electric-dipole-induced nuclear electric resonance (EDNER) aided by hyperfine interactions and magnetic field gradients. The stronger EDNER (compared with NMR) arises from the combined effects of the hyperfine interaction between nuclear and electron spins and the coupling between electron spin and charge degrees of freedom mediated by magnetic field gradients. They also identify specific working points at which the nuclear-spin splitting becomes less sensitive to electrical noise.

Result: Fast EDNER is achievable and is stronger than conventional NMR. Operating at specific working points reduces the sensitivity of the nuclear-spin splitting to electrical noise, enabling long coherence times and high-fidelity quantum operations.

Conclusion: All-electrical, addressable control of nuclear spins is established, paving the way for scalable silicon-based quantum computing.

Abstract: The nuclear spin of a phosphorus atom in silicon is considered as a promising quantum information carrier for quantum computing due to its long coherence time. Coherent control of the nuclear spin is typically realized using nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR). In this work, we theoretically propose that with the aid of hyperfine interactions and magnetic field gradients, fast electric-dipole-induced nuclear electric resonance (EDNER) can be achieved. The stronger EDNER (compared with NMR) arises from the combined effects of hyperfine interaction between the nuclear and electron spins and coupling between the electron spin and charge degrees of freedom mediated by magnetic field gradients in this scheme. By operating at specific working points, the nuclear-spin splitting becomes less sensitive to electrical noise, enabling long coherence times and high-fidelity quantum operations. All elctrical, addressable control of nuclear spins paves the way for scalable silicon-based quantum computing.

</details>


### [59] [Establishing the role of thickness in LaOBr; an alternative gate dielectric for vdW heterostructures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14272)
*Isaac Antoine Lagaud,Justin Boddison-Chouinard,Antoine Labbé,Ayse Melis Aygar,Alex Bogan,Li-Lin Tay,Takashi Taniguchi,Kenji Watanabe,Aljoscha Söll,Zdeněk Sofer,Adina Luican-Mayer,Louis Gaudreau*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: LaOBr, a layered van der Waals dielectric, is experimentally validated as a gate dielectric for 2D heterostructures: it shows a bulk dielectric constant of ~12.2 (agreeing with theory), breakdown fields comparable to hBN, and dielectric constants 2–3× larger, though interfacial dead-layers reduce the effective permittivity at small thicknesses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) is the benchmark gate dielectric for 2D van der Waals heterostructures due to its wide bandgap and atomically flat interface, but its low dielectric constant (2–4) limits electrostatic control. This motivates the search for alternative layered dielectrics with higher permittivity that can provide stronger gate control without sacrificing other figures of merit.
