Method: Graphene field-effect devices were fabricated with layered lanthanum oxybromide (LaOBr) as the gate dielectric. Thickness-dependent leakage-onset and breakdown fields were measured. Dual-gate capacitance measurements combined with a dead-layer model were used to extract the bulk dielectric constant and quantify interfacial dead-layer effects across multiple thicknesses.

Result: Leakage-onset and breakdown fields decrease systematically with increasing LaOBr thickness. Dual-gate capacitance measurements with the dead-layer model yield a bulk dielectric constant of ε_bulk = 12.2, consistent with theoretical predictions (12.5). Interfacial dead-layers reduce the effective dielectric constant to values ranging from 6 to 11 depending on thickness. Relative to established gate dielectrics, LaOBr combines breakdown fields comparable to hBN with dielectric constants 2–3 times larger, and outperforms hBN for high-capacitance gate-dielectric operation.

Conclusion: LaOBr is validated as a promising high-permittivity layered dielectric for integration into 2D van der Waals heterostructures, offering stronger electrostatic control than hBN while maintaining comparable breakdown strength. The study also establishes thickness-dependent benchmarking, accounting for interfacial dead-layers, as essential for evaluating emerging layered dielectric materials.

Abstract: In electronic devices, the quality of the dielectric material is determined by figures of merit such as the band gap, leakage onset field, breakdown field and the number of built-in defects, which can all limit device performance. An additional and important metric to consider is the strength of the dielectric constant. Hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) is the benchmark gate dielectric for two-dimensional van der Waals heterostructures, owing to its wide bandgap, and atomically flat interface. At the same time, its dielectric constant (2--4) highlights the opportunity to explore alternative higher-permittivity van der Waals dielectrics that provide stronger electrostatic control. Previous work established lanthanum oxybromide (LaOBr), a layered dielectric with a predicted dielectric constant of 12.5, as a potential dielectric for van der Waals heterostructures. Using graphene field-effect devices with LaOBr, we show that the leakage-onset and breakdown fields decrease systematically with thickness. Dual-gate capacitance measurements combined with a dead-layer model reveal a bulk dielectric constant of $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bulk}} = 12.2$, in agreement with theory, while interfacial dead-layers cause the effective dielectric constant to vary from 6 to 11 across multiple thicknesses. Benchmarked against established gate dielectrics, LaOBr combines breakdown fields comparable to hBN with dielectric constants 2 to 3 times larger. By establishing the operating regime of devices with various thicknesses, we further confirm the advantage of LaOBr over hBN for high-capacitance gate-dielectric operation. These results validate LaOBr as a promising dielectric for integration into two-dimensional heterostructures, and establish thickness-dependent benchmarking as essential for evaluating emerging layered dielectric materials.

</details>


### [60] [Direct Observation of Chirality-Dependent Berry Curvature Dipole via Cross-Axial Nonlinear Hall Effect in Trigonal Te](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14367)
*Jaehyun Lee,Junhee Shin,Daryll J. C. Dalayoan,Miju Park,Changhee Sohn,Gang Qiu,Noejung Park,Binghai Yan,Tony Low,Hosub Jin,Seon Namgung*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Trigonal tellurium hosts an enantiomer-dependent Berry curvature dipole probed via cross-axial nonlinear Hall effect, with sign reversing with crystal chirality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum geometric quantities (Berry curvature, quantum metric) govern material properties but lack direct experimental access. Chiral crystals offer a platform where structural handedness controls quantum geometry, yet experimental probing has remained elusive.

Method: Angle-resolved measurement of cross-axial nonlinear Hall effect on left- and right-handed trigonal tellurium devices; extraction of nonlinear susceptibilities χ_yxz and χ_xxx; first-principles calculations of Berry curvature texture and Berry curvature dipole.

Result: A second-order transverse response reverses sign with crystal chirality. Cross-axial susceptibility χ_yxz exceeds in-plane χ_xxx by more than two-fold. Calculations attribute this to a topologically nontrivial hedgehog-like Berry curvature texture near H points and predict a BCD sign reversal between enantiomers.

Conclusion: Cross-axial nonlinear Hall transport serves as a symmetry-selective probe of chirality-controlled quantum geometry in tellurium and related chiral materials.

Abstract: Quantum geometric quantities, such as Berry curvature (BC) and quantum metric, are known to govern the physical and topological properties of materials, making access to these quantities critically important for the comprehensive understanding of the materials. Chiral crystals offer a natural platform in which structural handedness can control quantum geometry and nonlinear transport, but direct experimental access to the quantum geometric quantities has remained elusive. Here, we show that trigonal tellurium (Te) hosts an enantiomer-dependent Berry curvature dipole (BCD) that can be accessed through the measurement of cross-axial nonlinear Hall effect (NHE), unlike previously studied in-plane configurations where the BCD contribution vanishes. Angle-resolved measurements on left- and right-handed Te devices reveal a second-order transverse response whose sign reverses with crystal chirality. The extracted cross-axial nonlinear susceptibility, $χ_{yxz}$, exceeds the in-plane susceptibility, $χ_{xxx}$, by more than two-folds, indicating a strong BCD-driven contribution. First-principles calculations attribute this behavior to a topologically nontrivial hedgehog-like BC texture near the H points and predict a BCD that reverses sign between opposite enantiomers, consistent with experiment. These results establish cross-axial nonlinear Hall transport as a symmetry-selective probe of chirality-controlled quantum geometry in tellurium and related chiral materials.

</details>


### [61] [Transport kinetics in excitonic Mott insulator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14426)
*W. J. Brunner,Zhiwen Zhou,Luc M. Nguyen,C. A. Lozano,G. Mercado,L. H. Fowler-Gerace,L. V. Butov*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Indirect excitons in a moiré potential form a platform for Bose-Hubbard physics. Using a high-mobility MoSe₂/WSe₂ heterostructure, the authors observe the suppression of exciton transport characteristic of a Mott insulator, with the exciton cloud expanding then contracting as density grows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Realizing collective Bose-Hubbard states such as the superfluid and Mott insulator requires low-disorder materials, because disorder-induced localization must be distinguished from the localization caused by the Mott insulator. A system with anomalously high indirect-exciton mobility is needed to establish that disorder effects are negligible.

Method: The transport kinetics of indirect excitons (IXs) were studied in a MoSe₂/WSe₂ van der Waals heterostructure with anomalously high IX mobility. The IX density was varied via the excitation pulse, and the spatial and temporal evolution of the IX cloud was measured and compared with simulations.

Result: The high-mobility transport indicates that disorder-induced localization is negligible. A remarkable suppression of this efficient IX transport was observed, directly indicating the Mott insulator. As the IX density increases, the cloud initially expands and then contracts upon entering the Mott insulator phase, accompanied by a peak in the IX spatial distribution at the origin. Simulations reproduce the non-monotonic transport kinetics, including the temporal expansion and contraction and the spatial peak.

Conclusion: The observed suppression of IX transport and the non-monotonic expansion-contraction of the IX cloud provide direct evidence for the Mott insulator state in a low-disorder moiré heterostructure, with simulations quantitatively reproducing the transport kinetics.

Abstract: Indirect excitons (IXs) in a periodic moiré potential in a van der Waals heterostructure form an experimental platform for exploring Bose-Hubbard physics, including the superfluid state and the Mott insulator state. The realization of these collective states requires low-disorder materials. In particular, the localization due to the Mott insulator must be distinguished from disorder-induced localization. We studied the transport kinetics of IXs in a MoSe$_2$/WSe$_2$ van der Waals heterostructure with an anomalously high IX mobility, consistent with superfluidity. This high-mobility transport demonstrates that disorder-induced localization is negligible. We found a remarkable suppression of this efficient IX transport, directly indicating the Mott insulator. As the IX density increases during the excitation pulse, the IX cloud initially expands and then contracts upon entering the Mott insulator phase. The emergence of IX cloud contraction is accompanied by a peak in the IX spatial distribution at the origin. Our simulations reproduce the observed non-monotonic IX transport kinetics, including the temporal expansion and contraction of the IX cloud, as well as the peak in its spatial distribution.

</details>


### [62] [Magnon Klein paradox](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14648)
*D. Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper argues there is no magnon Klein paradox; apparent paradoxes stem from a sign error in the transmitted spin wave, since energy conservation forbids reflectivity greater than one.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Claims of a magnon Klein paradox exist in the literature, but it is unclear whether they are physically valid. The authors aim to resolve whether magnons genuinely exhibit such a paradox by examining energy transport.

Method: Analysis of energy transport of spin waves, tracking energy flow carried by magnons across the interface, analogous to the electron Klein paradox treatment, combined with a conservation-of-energy argument for waves scattering off a passive interface.

Result: Reflectivity is shown to never exceed one, so no magnon Klein paradox exists. The previously reported magnon Klein paradox is traced to assigning an incorrect sign to the transmitted spin wave.

Conclusion: Like electrons, magnons exhibit no Klein paradox for reflectivity; the effect reported earlier is an artifact of a sign misassignment in the transmitted spin wave, and energy conservation forbids superluminal reflectivity even for unstable ground states.

Abstract: By considering the energy transport of spin wave, we show that, like the electron Klein paradox, there is no magnon Klein paradox for reflectivity. Heuristically, this can be understood according to the fact that, even if the ground state is unstable, any energy-carrying wave cannot have greater-than-one reflectivity off a passive interface, which will violate the conservation of energy. The magnon Klein paradox discussed in the literature is derived from assigning a wrong sign to the transmitted spin wave.

</details>


### [63] [Valley-resolved high harmonics probe symmetry breaking beyond the Dirac cone](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15251)
*Pablo Pais,Alfredo Iorio,Marcelo F. Ciappina*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Valley-resolved high-harmonic generation under counter-rotating ω-2ω fields is proposed as a tunable probe of trigonal warping in graphene, with laser intensity playing the role of collision energy to expose ultraviolet lattice corrections atop the emergent Dirac theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graphene's low-energy electrons obey an emergent, rotationally invariant Dirac equation, with the honeycomb lattice appearing only through higher-order symmetry-breaking corrections (leading term: trigonal warping). This mirrors the quantum-gravity challenge of detecting ultraviolet symmetry-breaking granular corrections suppressed by a fundamental scale, on top of an emergent infrared relativistic continuum. A solid-state analogue is therefore sought.

Method: Use intense counter-rotating ω-2ω fields and valley-resolved HHG. Employ a family of models interpolating continuously between the isotropic Dirac cone and nearest-neighbour graphene at fixed Fermi velocity. Analyze valley contrast of harmonic yield via effective-field-theory power counting, treating laser intensity as the collision energy that drives electrons away from the Dirac points.

Result: The valley contrast vanishes identically in the symmetric theory, grows linearly with the warping coupling, and its valley-even counterpart appears only at second order, consistent with effective-field-theory power counting. For the fourth harmonic, the leading warping correction alone accounts for the valley contrast of real graphene up to intensities ~10^11 W/cm^2 (below the monolayer damage threshold); the fifth harmonic already reveals higher-order lattice corrections at ~10^10 W/cm^2. The two oppositely rotating harmonics are emitted by opposite valleys. Valley-resolved harmonics are far more sensitive than residual valley populations.

Conclusion: Valley-resolved harmonics constitute a practical detector of lattice-scale physics hidden beneath an emergent Dirac theory, providing a solid-state platform to probe ultraviolet symmetry-breaking corrections suppressed by a fundamental scale.

Abstract: Low-energy electrons in graphene obey an emergent, rotationally invariant Dirac equation, while the honeycomb lattice enters only through higher-order, symmetry-breaking corrections, the leading one being trigonal warping. This provides a unique solid-state arena to address a cornerstone challenge in quantum gravity: how to detect ultraviolet symmetry-breaking granular corrections, suppressed by a fundamental scale, atop an emergent infrared relativistic continuum. We show that intense counter-rotating $ω$-$2ω$ fields and valley-resolved high-harmonic generation (HHG) provide such a probe. Within a family of models that interpolates continuously between the isotropic Dirac cone and nearest-neighbour graphene at fixed Fermi velocity, the valley contrast of the harmonic yield vanishes identically in the symmetric theory, grows linearly with the warping coupling, and its valley-even counterpart appears only at second order, as expected from effective-field-theory power counting. The stronger the laser, the farther it drives electrons from the Dirac points, so the laser intensity plays the role of the collision energy. For the fourth harmonic, the leading warping correction alone accounts for the valley contrast of real graphene up to fundamental intensities of about $10^{11}$ W/cm$^2$, below the damage threshold of monolayer graphene, whereas the fifth harmonic already reveals higher-order lattice corrections at intensities around $10^{10}$ W/cm$^2$. The two harmonics, which rotate in opposite directions, are emitted by opposite valleys. Valley-resolved harmonics are far more sensitive than the residual valley populations, making them a practical detector of lattice-scale physics hidden beneath an emergent Dirac theory.

</details>


### [64] [Microscopic foundation for orbitronics: orbital exchange interactions, orbital-dependent transport, and orbital switching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15374)
*Hantao Zhang,Ran Cheng*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The authors develop a theoretical framework for orbital exchange interactions by generalizing the Anderson-Kondo model to include conduction-band orbitals, revealing orbital analogs of spin filtering and tunnel magnetoresistance, and demonstrating that orbital-polarized currents can torque localized moments and switch magnetization without spin-current conversion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent experiments show orbital degrees of freedom play an essential role in current-induced magnetic dynamics, yet a microscopic picture of how orbital angular momentum transfers between itinerant and localized electrons—and how this feeds back into transport—remains elusive.

Method: Generalizes the Anderson-Kondo model to incorporate the orbitals of conduction bands, illustrated with p-orbitals, to derive how electrons acquire orbital polarization and how this polarization affects transport; reciprocal torques on localized moments are also analyzed.

Result: Uncovers orbital counterparts of spin filtering and tunnel magnetoresistance; shows orbital-polarized currents exert torques on localized magnetic moments without converting into spin currents, enabling electrical switching of magnetization even when spin polarization is inactive.

Conclusion: These findings lay a microscopic foundation for orbitronics and reveal critical mechanisms for orbital manipulation of magnetic order.

Abstract: Recent experiments highlighted the essential role of the orbital degrees of freedom underlying current-induced magnetic dynamics. However, a microscopic picture of how orbital angular momenta are transferred between itinerant and localized electrons, and the way it feeds back into transport, remains elusive. Here we develop a theoretical framework for the orbital exchange interactions by generalizing the Anderson-Kondo model to incorporate the orbitals of conduction bands. Illustrating with the $p$-orbitals, we determine the way electrons acquire orbital polarization and its influence on the transport properties, uncovering the orbital counterparts of phenomena analogous to spin filtering and tunnel magnetoresistance. Reciprocally, we demonstrate that orbital-polarized currents can exert torques on localized magnetic moments without having to convert into spin currents, which enables electrical switching of magnetization even when spin polarization becomes inactive. These striking discoveries lay a microscopic foundation for orbitronics and reveal critical mechanisms for orbital manipulations of magnetic order.

</details>


### [65] [A Solvable Model in the Half-filled Landau Level](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15471)
*Anders Karlhede*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper presents a solvable microscopic model of interacting electrons in a half-filled Landau level, defined as a specific limit of the electron-electron interaction. Its low-energy physics reduces to independent, interlaced 1D systems of non-interacting fermionic dipoles, each with a Fermi sea, collectively reproducing the density of states of a 2D Fermi gas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The half-filled Landau level is a central setting for strongly correlated electron physics, but exact microscopic solutions are rare. The author aims to construct a tractable, solvable model that captures key features of the 2D half-filled Landau level, extending an existing exact solution on a thin torus to the genuine 2D bulk case.

Method: The Hamiltonian is constructed as a well-defined limit of the electron-electron interaction, adopting and generalizing an existing exact solution on a thin (but not too thin) torus to the two-dimensional bulk geometry.

Result: The low-energy sector consists of independent, spatially interlaced 1D systems of non-interacting fermionic dipoles, each with a Fermi sea ground state. The total number of dipoles across the Fermi seas equals the number of electrons, and the density of states at the Fermi level matches that of a 2D Fermi gas.

Conclusion: A solvable microscopic model of interacting electrons in a half-filled Landau level is obtained, whose 2D ground state is built from interlaced 1D Fermi seas of fermionic dipoles and reproduces the 2D Fermi gas density of states.

Abstract: A solvable microscopic model of interacting electrons in a half-filled Landau level is presented. The Hamiltonian is a well-defined limit of the electron-electron interaction. The low energy sector consists of a set of independent, spatially interlaced, one-dimensional systems of non-interacting fermionic dipoles, each system with a Fermi sea ground state. The total number of dipoles in the set of Fermi seas forming the two-dimensional ground state equals the number of electrons in the system and the density of states at the Fermi level is that of a two-dimensional Fermi gas. The model is obtained by generalizing, or rather adopting, the existing exact solution on a thin, but not too thin, torus to the two-dimensional bulk case.

</details>


### [66] [Approaching Irrational Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15753)
*Zhihong Wu,Zhimou Chen,Siyuan Zhang,Jinyang Chen,L. N. Pfeiffer,K. W. West,Yang Liu,Xi Lin*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that a quantized physical response can take an irrational value ($\sqrt{3}$) by using a sequence of finite geometric networks that converge to an infinite limit, realized experimentally as a high-precision finite approximation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The integer and fractional quantum Hall effects show quantization at integer and rational fractional values, raising the question of whether quantization can extend beyond rational numbers to irrational values.

Method: The authors develop a method based on infinite geometric structures, in which a sequence of finite networks converges rapidly toward an infinite limit. They identify a planar network whose normalized resistance converges to $\sqrt{3}$ and fabricate a finite approximation with high-precision geometric control.

Result: The measured normalized resistance is $1.735 \pm 0.001\, h/e^2$ for a fully occupied Landau level in the designed pattern, closely approaching the irrational value $\sqrt{3} \approx 1.732$.

Conclusion: The experimental results extend the concept of quantization beyond rational values, showing that an irrational quantized response can be approached through a finite physical system.

Abstract: The integer and fractional quantum Hall effects reveal that quantized physical responses can take integer and fractional values, respectively. This naturally raises the question of whether quantization can extend beyond rational numbers to an irrational value. Here we show that such a quantized response can emerge from the limiting behavior of a finite, experimentally realizable geometric network. We develop a method based on infinite geometric structures, in which a sequence of finite networks rapidly converges toward an infinite limit. Following this approach, we identify a planar network whose normalized resistance converges to the irrational value $\sqrt{3}$, and realize a finite approximation with high-precision geometric control. The measured normalized resistance is $1.735 \pm 0.001\, h/e^2$ for a fully occupied Landau level in the designed pattern. Our experimental results extend the concept of quantization beyond rational values and demonstrate that an irrational quantized response can be approached through a finite physical system.

</details>


### [67] [Probing the wave-vector gap in nanoscale magnonics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15945)
*Ondřej Wojewoda,Samer Kurdi,Toeno van der Sar,Daniel Schick,Sebastian Wintz,Alexei Maznev*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This Perspective reviews emerging measurement techniques that are beginning to close the experimental gap in accessing short-wavelength spin waves at intermediate wave vectors (~30-300 rad/µm), comparing five approaches side by side.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Short-wavelength spin waves are key to miniaturizing magnonic devices and studying exchange-dominated magnetization dynamics. However, the wave-vector range between roughly 30 and 300 rad/µm has been difficult to access experimentally, with challenges in both excitation and detection. Existing optical and microwave techniques are limited to near the Brillouin-zone centre, while inelastic neutron, electron and resonant hard x-ray scattering cover the full Brillouin zone but lack the energy resolution needed for low-frequency magnons at intermediate wave vectors.

Method: The paper is a Perspective that reviews and compares five emerging measurement approaches: Mie-enhanced Brillouin light scattering, colour-centre magnetometry, magnon momentum microscopy, extreme-ultraviolet transient-grating spectroscopy, and time-resolved scanning transmission x-ray microscopy. For each technique, it discusses the physical principle, demonstrated capabilities, current limitations, and development prospects.

Result: The techniques provide complementary access to magnon frequencies, local stray fields, real-space dynamics, and momentum-space distributions, differing in their sensitivity to coherent versus incoherent excitations. The Perspective identifies the main advantages and use cases of each technique and the practical requirements for addressing different scientific questions.

Conclusion: Further development opportunities include higher sensitivity, improved spatial and temporal resolution, and broader frequency and wave-vector coverage. These advances could enable systematic studies of nonlinear magnon interactions at nanoscale wavelengths and investigations of emerging magnetic materials such as altermagnets.

Abstract: Short-wavelength spin waves are central to miniaturizing magnonic devices and to studying exchange-dominated magnetization dynamics. Yet the wave-vector range between roughly 30 and 300 radians per micrometre has remained difficult to access experimentally, posing challenges for both excitation and detection. This Perspective reviews recent advances in emerging measurement techniques and discusses prospects for their future development. Optical and microwave techniques are restricted to the vicinity of the Brillouin-zone centre. Inelastic neutron, electron and resonant hard x-ray scattering cover the full Brillouin zone but lack the energy resolution needed for low-frequency magnons at intermediate wave vectors. This Perspective examines five emerging approaches that are beginning to close this gap: Mie-enhanced Brillouin light scattering, colour-centre magnetometry, magnon momentum microscopy, extreme-ultraviolet transient-grating spectroscopy, and time-resolved scanning transmission x-ray microscopy. For each, we discuss the physical principle, demonstrated capabilities, current limitations and prospects for development. Compared side by side, the techniques give complementary access to magnon frequencies, local stray fields, real-space dynamics and momentum-space distributions, and they differ in their sensitivity to coherent and incoherent excitations. We therefore discuss the main advantages and use cases of each technique and consider the practical requirements for addressing different scientific questions. We highlight opportunities for further development, including higher sensitivity, improved spatial and temporal resolution, and broader frequency and wave-vector coverage. Together, these advances could enable, for example, systematic studies of nonlinear magnon interactions at nanoscale wavelengths and investigations of emerging magnetic materials such as altermagnets.

</details>


### [68] [High-mobility spin transport mediated by indirect excitons in a van der Waals heterostructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16089)
*Zhiwen Zhou,W. J. Brunner,Luc M. Nguyen,C. A. Lozano,G. Mercado,L. H. Fowler-Gerace,L. V. Butov*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Indirect excitons in a TMD van der Waals heterostructure enable high-mobility spin transport, achieving spin mobility up to 10^5 cm^2/(eV·s), orders of magnitude beyond standard diffusive spin transport.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient spin transport with high spin mobility is fundamentally and practically important for spintronics. Conventional spin transfer via moving particles is limited by particle scattering, which caps spin mobility and prevents efficient transport.

Method: The authors study the kinetics of spin transport mediated by a quantum system of indirect excitons (IXs) in a van der Waals heterostructure built from atomically thin transition-metal dichalcogenides (TMDs).

Result: The IX-mediated spin transfer mechanism yields efficient spin transport with high spin mobility reaching 10^5 cm^2/(eV·s), orders of magnitude higher than standard diffusive spin transport in TMD materials.

Conclusion: Suppression of IX scattering enables high-mobility spin transport, demonstrating indirect excitons as a promising route for efficient spintronic transport.

Abstract: Efficient spin transport with high spin mobility is of both fundamental and practical significance for spintronics. Spin can be transferred by moving particles. However, particle scattering limits spin transfer and prevents high spin mobilities. In this work, we study the kinetics of spin transport mediated by a quantum system of indirect excitons (IXs) in a van der Waals heterostructure composed of atomically thin transition-metal dichalcogenides (TMDs). This spin transfer mechanism allows for the realization of efficient spin transport characterized by a high spin mobility reaching 10$^5$ cm$^2$/(eV$\cdot$s), which is orders of magnitude higher than that achieved via standard diffusive spin transport in TMD materials. The suppression of IX scattering enables high-mobility spin transport.

</details>


### [69] [1/f frequency-noise reduction in nanomechanical resonators by dissipation dilution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16711)
*Kostas Kanellopulos,Silvan Schmid*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The 1/f frequency-noise floor of resonant nanomechanical sensors is shown to be the thermal noise of tension, governed by the fluctuation-dissipation theorem. Its flicker coefficient has no free parameter and is diluted by tensile stress, matching measurements across silicon nitride devices and temperatures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frequency noise of resonant nanomechanical sensors is understood down to the thermomechanical limit, but the longest useful averaging time is limited by a flicker (1/f) frequency-noise floor of unknown origin. Understanding and predicting this floor is essential for optimizing sensor performance and integration time.

Method: The authors model the 1/f frequency-noise floor as thermal noise of the tension. Using the fluctuation-dissipation theorem, they derive that the tension of a fixed-end resonator fluctuates according to the material's loss angle. For structural damping, these fluctuations yield 1/f frequency noise with flicker coefficient h_{-1} = E k_B T/(2π V σ_0^2 Q_el), where Q_el is the intrinsic loss under elongation. The relation is tested experimentally on silicon nitride strings and membranes of 30 to 340 nm thickness, including stress-engineered strings, and compared with published data between 78 and 296 K.

Result: Silicon nitride strings and membranes of 30–340 nm thickness follow the predicted volume scaling, the predicted σ_0^{-2} dilution across resonators and within stress-engineered strings, and the predicted thickness dependence, all at the absolute level set by the independently measured intrinsic loss. This places noise-active two-level systems in the volume and at the surfaces. The same relation accounts for published data between 78 and 296 K; below 78 K, the loss measured at resonance no longer represents the loss probed by the noise, marking the limit of the structural-damping assumption.

Conclusion: Dissipation dilution lowers the 1/f floor, and the longest useful integration time of a resonant sensor is set by its prestress, volume, temperature, and intrinsic loss angle.

Abstract: The frequency noise of a resonant nanomechanical sensor is well understood down to its thermomechanical limit, but the longest useful averaging time is set by a flicker, or $1/f$, frequency-noise floor whose origin has remained unknown. We show that this floor is the thermal noise of the tension. By the fluctuation-dissipation theorem, the tension of a resonator with fixed ends fluctuates by an amount set by the loss angle of its material; for structural damping, these fluctuations produce $1/f$ frequency noise with a flicker coefficient $h_{-1}=E k_{\mathrm{B}} T/(2πVσ_0^2 Q_{\mathrm{el}})$, with $Q_{\mathrm{el}}$ the intrinsic loss under elongation, that contains no free parameter and is diluted by the tensile stress $σ_0$ in the same way as damping is in dissipation dilution. Silicon nitride strings and membranes from 30 to 340~nm thickness follow the predicted volume scaling, the predicted $σ_0^{-2}$ dilution across resonators and within stress-engineered strings, and the predicted thickness dependence, which places noise-active two-level systems in the volume and at the surfaces of the resonators, all at the absolute level set by the independently measured intrinsic loss. The same relation accounts for published data between 78 and 296~K; below 78~K, the loss measured at the resonance no longer represents the loss probed by the noise, marking the limit of the structural-damping assumption. Dissipation dilution therefore lowers the $1/f$ floor, and the longest useful integration time of a resonant sensor is set by its prestress, volume, temperature, and intrinsic loss angle.

</details>


### [70] [Chirally pinned flat bands, helical minibands, and bias-controlled topology of Jackiw-Rebbi states in a periodically modulated Dirac mass texture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16183)
*Tao Zhou*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A square-lattice model with a slowly modulated Dirac mass texture realizes an exactly analyzable, experimentally accessible platform unifying flat bands and topological (Jackiw-Rebbi) bound states, with a rich interpolation between chirally pinned flat, helical miniband, and bias-dimerized regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flat bands amplify interactions into correlated phases and topological bound states are robust, so a model combining both while being exactly analyzable and experimentally accessible is highly desirable. Existing approaches typically require engineered geometries, lattice-scale textures, or long-range hoppings.

Method: The authors introduce a square-lattice model with a slowly modulated Dirac mass m(x)=m+m_0 cos(2πx/L), L up to hundreds of sites. Exact nesting decouples the problem at each transverse momentum k_y into a 1+1D Dirac Hamiltonian with position-dependent mass. Each simple zero of m(x) binds Jackiw-Rebbi zero modes; the two walls of a period form an effective Su-Schrieffer-Heeger ring. Analyses cover transverse-momentum sectors, bandwidth decomposition (W_JR, W_edge, bias bump), and two lattice realizations (two-orbital hybridized and single-orbital bond-order).

Result: The model interpolates between a chirally pinned flat band at large L, a helical miniband with nodes enforced by the ring boundary phase, and a bias-dimerized regime where the bias sign sets a winding number. The miniband bandwidth is not a single scale: max-min conflates three widths—helical height W_JR (exponential in period), zone-edge upturn W_edge ∝ L^{-2/3} (dominant at large L), and a bias bump (exponential in L)—so the flat band is robust against bias in the thermodynamic limit. Two realizations reproduce the nodal physics; the single-orbital bond-order texture leaves van Hove points gapless.

Conclusion: Slowly modulated Dirac mass textures offer a clean, exactly solvable route to combining flat bands and topological bound states without engineered geometry or long-range hoppings. The three distinct bandwidth contributions clarify flat-band robustness, and the proposed realizations in cold-atom superlattices, Kekule-ordered graphene, STM designer lattices, and microwave arrays make the predictions experimentally testable.

Abstract: Flat bands amplify interactions into correlated phases, and topological bound states are robust by principle; combining the two in a model that is both exactly analyzable and experimentally accessible is therefore especially desirable. Here we show that a square-lattice model with a slowly modulated Dirac mass texture, $m(x)=m+m_0\cos(2πx/L)$ with $L$ up to hundreds of sites, provides such a setting, without engineered geometries, lattice-scale textures, or long-range hoppings. Exact nesting reduces the problem, independently at each transverse momentum $k_y$, to a $1+1$ dimensional Dirac Hamiltonian with a position-dependent mass: every simple zero of $m(x)$ binds Jackiw-Rebbi zero modes, and the two walls of a period form an effective Su-Schrieffer-Heeger ring. The model interpolates between a chirally pinned flat band at large $L$, a helical miniband whose nodes are enforced by a boundary phase of the ring, and a bias-dimerized regime in which the sign of the bias sets a winding number. A central result is that the miniband bandwidth is not a single scale: a max-min measurement conflates three widths of different origin, the helical height $W_{\rm JR}$ exponential in the period, the zone-edge upturn $W_{\rm edge}\propto L^{-2/3}$ dominating at large $L$, and a bias bump exponential in $L$, so the flat band is robust against bias in the thermodynamic limit. We analyze two lattice realizations, a two-orbital model with modulated inter-orbital hybridization and a single-orbital bond-order texture sharing the nodal physics while leaving the van Hove points gapless, and propose experiments on cold-atom superlattices, Kekule-ordered graphene, STM designer lattices, and microwave arrays.

</details>


### [71] [Wilson-loop formulations of the spin polarization and the spin Chern number](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16246)
*Kiminori Hattori,Ryuki Uchida*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper formulates spin polarization and the spin Chern number in both momentum and real space using Wilson loops, with a unitary-rotation trick to keep the spin gap open, and validates the equivalenceto the Z2 index in the BHZ model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Topological invariants such as the spin Chern number and the Z2 index are central to time-reversal-invariant topological insulators, but their expression via Wilson loops—and especially a unified treatment spanning momentum and real space for both ordered and disordered systems—is not fully established. The authors aim to provide such a formulation.

Method: They generalize the spin matrix in the occupied/unoccupied subspace by a unitary rotation to prevent the spectral gap from closing. Using the finite spin gap, spin eigenvectors recompose the Hamiltonian eigenstates into two spin sectors. A dimensional reduction yields charge polarization per spin sector via the Wilson loop along the effective Brillouin zone in 1D; a linear combination gives the spin polarization and naturally quantifies the spin Chern number. The spin Chern number is also derived directly from local Wilson loops on a discrete 2D BZ. The framework is extended to real space in a supercell scheme (handling ordered and disordered systems uniformly), formulating the real-space spin Chern number via the Wilson loop around the BZ boundary.

Result: Numerical evaluation of topological phases in the Bernevig-Hughes-Zhang (BHZ) model shows an equivalence between the spin Chern number and the Z2 index.

Conclusion: The Wilson-loop-based formulations provide a consistent, unified description of spin polarization and the spin Chern number in momentum and real space, applicable to ordered and disordered systems, and numerically confirmed to agree with the Z2 index.

Abstract: In this paper, we derive the spin polarization and the spin Chern number in momentum and real spaces in terms of the Wilson loop in each spin sector. For these derivations, the spin matrix in the subspace of occupied or unoccupied states is generalized by the unitary rotation to prevent the spectral gap from closing. In the presence of a finite spin gap, the spin eigenvectors recompose the eigenstates of the Hamiltonian in two spin sectors. A dimensional reduction leads to the charge polarization in each spin sector in terms of the Wilson loop along the effective Brillouin zone (BZ) in one dimension (1D). A linear combination of the charge polarizations defines the spin polarization, which naturally quantifies the spin Chern number in momentum space. It is also shown that the spin Chern number is directly derived from the local Wilson loops on a discrete 2D BZ. The theoretical treatment is further generalized for real space in a supercell framework, which can deal with ordered and disordered systems in a unified manner. In this framework, the spin Chern number in real space is formulated in terms of the Wilson loop around the BZ boundary. These formulations are used to numerically evaluate topological phases in the Bernevig-Hughes-Zhang model. Numerical results show an equivalence of the spin Chern number and the ${\mathbb{Z}_2}$ index.

</details>


### [72] [Symmetry Condition and Mechanism for the Anomalous Hall Effect in Odd-Parity Magnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16247)
*Xun-Jiang Luo,Su-Qi Zhang,Yi-Dong Liu,Yong-Ting Shi,Ding-Fu Shao,Mingliang Tian*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper establishes the symmetry condition and microscopic mechanism for the anomalous Hall effect (AHE) in odd-parity magnets (OPMs), showing the AHE requires a ferromagnetic magnetic point group and arises from the interplay of an intrinsic hidden Zeeman field with spin-orbit coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A giant AHE was recently observed in the candidate p-wave magnet Gd3(Ru1-δRhδ)4Al12, but its theoretical interpretation invokes a spontaneous magnetization to break time-reversal symmetry even though the magnetic order itself already breaks T. The microscopic origin of the AHE in OPMs therefore remains an open question.

Method: The authors derive the symmetry condition for the AHE in OPMs using spin-group versus magnetic-group classification, and identify the microscopic mechanism. They demonstrate the picture with models on square, honeycomb, and kagome lattices, including a square-lattice helimagnet with sublattice-resolved SOC that maps exactly onto an anisotropic Rashba-Zeeman model.

Result: The AHE in OPMs is allowed only when the magnetic point group is ferromagnetic, and forbidden whenever the effective time reversal Tτ (with τ a fractional translation) essential to the OPM is preserved. OPMs generically host a hidden Zeeman field (HZF) rooted in intrinsic T breaking, whose interplay with SOC generates the AHE without any external field.

Conclusion: The results establish the symmetry condition for the AHE in OPMs and identify the HZF-SOC interplay as a general route to realize it.

Abstract: The anomalous Hall effect (AHE) in unconventional magnets has attracted considerable attention. A giant AHE has recently been observed in the candidate $p$-wave magnet Gd$_3$(Ru$_{1-δ}$Rh$_δ$)$_4$Al$_{12}$ [Nature 646, 837(2025)], while its theoretical interpretation invokes a spontaneous magnetization to break time-reversal symmetry ($\mathcal{T}$), although the magnetic order itself already breaks $\mathcal{T}$. Consequently, the microscopic origin of the AHE in odd-parity magnets (OPMs) remains an open question. Here we establish the symmetry condition and microscopic mechanism for the AHE in OPMs. Since the OPM order is classified by the spin group whereas the AHE is governed by the magnetic group, the AHE is allowed only when the magnetic point group is ferromagnetic; conversely, it is forbidden whenever the effective time reversal $\mathcal{T}τ$ (with $τ$ a fractional translation), which is essential to the realization of the OPM, is preserved. Microscopically, OPMs generically host a hidden Zeeman field (HZF) rooted in this intrinsic $\mathcal{T}$ breaking, and its interplay with spin-orbit coupling (SOC) generates the AHE without any external field. We demonstrate this picture with models on the square, honeycomb, and kagome lattices. In particular, a square-lattice helimagnet with sublattice-resolved SOC maps exactly onto an anisotropic Rashba--Zeeman model, whose Zeeman term is nothing but the HZF and directly yields the AHE. Our results establish the symmetry condition for the AHE in OPMs and identify the HZF--SOC interplay as a general route to realize it.

</details>


### [73] [Emergence of Nonlocal Hydrodynamics in Many-Body Radiative Heat Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16488)
*Mauricio Gomez Viloria,Florian Herz,Riccardo Messina,Philippe Ben-Abdallah*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper develops a nonlocal hydrodynamic theory of radiative heat transport in many-body systems derived by coarse graining microscopic electrodynamic interactions, showing when Fourier transport emerges and when non-Fourier behavior persists.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Radiative heat transport in many-body systems is governed by long-range electrodynamic interactions, yet a systematic bridge between discrete fluctuational electrodynamics and a continuous hydrodynamic description has been lacking. Existing local (Fourier) treatments may fail when nonlocality survives coarse graining.

Method: Coarse graining of microscopic electrodynamic interactions yields a spatially nonlocal constitutive relation in which the heat current at a position depends on temperature gradients over an extended region, while energy conservation remains locally conservative. The interaction kernel's spatial moments control the transport regime.

Result: Fourier transport emerges as a controlled long-wavelength limit when the interaction kernel has a finite second spatial moment. When this second moment diverges, spatial nonlocality survives coarse graining and produces non-Fourier transport.

Conclusion: The framework provides a systematic bridge between discrete many-body fluctuational electrodynamics and a continuous description of radiative heat transport, delineating the conditions for Fourier versus non-Fourier behavior.

Abstract: We develop a nonlocal hydrodynamic theory of radiative heat transport in many-body systems by coarse graining their microscopic electrodynamic interactions. Long-range radiative exchange naturally generates a spatially nonlocal constitutive relation in which the heat current at a given position depends on temperature gradients over an extended region, while energy conservation retains its local conservative form. Fourier transport emerges as a controlled long-wavelength limit when the interaction kernel has a finite second spatial moment. When this moment fails to converge, spatial nonlocality survives coarse graining and gives rise to non-Fourier transport. The resulting framework establishes a systematic bridge between discrete many-body fluctuational electrodynamics and a continuous description of radiative heat transport.

</details>


### [74] [Probing quantum nonlinear phononics in multilayer graphene with time-resolved Raman scattering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16566)
*Jakob Dolgner,Michael Rübhausen,Dirk Manske*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This work extends nonlinear phononics from coherent atomic displacement to lattice fluctuation dynamics, benchmarking classical/semiclassical approximations against full quantum theory and proposing nonequilibrium spontaneous Raman scattering as a probe of driven fluctuations in graphene.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coherently driven phonons are conventionally treated classically, but nonlinear phonon-phonon interactions enable an infrared drive to reshape the fluctuation spectrum and produce substantial nonthermal enhancement of phonon fluctuations, requiring a description beyond coherent displacement alone.

Method: The authors benchmark classical and semiclassical approximations against full quantum theory for driven phonon dynamics, and propose nonequilibrium spontaneous Raman scattering as a probe of pump-induced fluctuation effects. They apply this framework to recent experiments on quantum nonlinear phononics involving E1u and E2g modes in multilayer graphene.

Result: Semiclassical treatment substantially improves agreement with quantum dynamics compared to a purely classical description. At very strong pump fields, driving the E1u mode significantly modifies the E2g Raman spectrum, though reproducing the reported response at experimental field strengths requires accounting for pump-induced Raman matrix element dynamics or alternative energy pathways into the E2g mode.

Conclusion: Nonlinear phononics extends beyond average atomic displacements to the dynamics of lattice fluctuations, highlighting the interplay between coherent motion and quantum correlations in driven materials.

Abstract: Coherently driven phonons are conventionally described classically. Nonlinear phonon-phonon interactions, however, allow an infrared drive to reshape the fluctuation spectrum and generate substantial nonthermal enhancement of phonon fluctuations, motivating a description beyond the coherent displacement alone. We benchmark classical and semiclassical approximations against the full quantum theory and find that the semiclassical treatment substantially improves agreement with the quantum dynamics. We propose nonequilibrium spontaneous Raman scattering as a sensitive probe of these effects and apply our framework to recent experiments suggesting quantum nonlinear phononics involving the $\mathrm{E}_{1u}$ and $\mathrm{E}_{2g}$ modes in multilayer graphene. At very strong pump fields, driving the $\mathrm{E}_{1u}$ mode significantly modifies the $\mathrm{E}_{2g}$ Raman spectrum; however, reproducing the reported response at experimental field strengths requires accounting for pump-induced Raman matrix element dynamics or alternative energy pathways into the $\mathrm{E}_{2g}$ mode. Our findings extend nonlinear phononics beyond average atomic displacements to the dynamics of lattice fluctuations, highlighting the interplay between coherent motion and quantum correlations in driven materials.

</details>


### [75] [Admittance extraction of quantum dot arrays for charge sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16590)
*Conor Power,Mathieu Moras,Andrii Sokolov,Robert Bogdan Staszewski,Elena Blokhina*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This work extracts the admittance of quantum dot arrays by extending a semiclassical, time-dependent rate equation framework to a double quantum dot and calibrating simulations with experimentally derived Fermi-Hubbard parameters, achieving close agreement with reflectometry measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reflectometry readout of quantum dot arrays requires a modeling framework that can link measured admittance to underlying device parameters, but existing rate-equation treatments have been limited to pumped arrays and capacitance extraction. A time-dependent extension is needed to capture the AC response relevant to reflectometry.

Method: A semiclassical rate equation framework is used, incorporating a perturbation of the source/drain chemical potential to model reflectometry readout. This time-dependent extension of the rate equation—previously applied to pumped quantum dot arrays and capacitance extraction—is extended here to a double quantum dot for admittance extraction. The simulations are calibrated using Fermi-Hubbard parameters extracted from experimental data.

Result: The calibrated simulations closely approximate the experimental reflectometry measurements.

Conclusion: A time-dependent semiclassical rate equation framework, calibrated with experimentally extracted Fermi-Hubbard parameters, enables admittance extraction for quantum dot arrays and reproduces reflectometry measurements with good agreement.

Abstract: This work demonstrates admittance extraction of quantum dot arrays using a rate equation frame- work matching experimental reflectometry measurements. Using a semiclassical rate equation, we consider a perturbation of the source/drain chemical potential to model reflectometry readout in a quantum dot array. This is a time-dependent extension of the rate equation which has been employed in literature for pumped arrays of quantum dots and capacitance extractions. Here we extend this to a double quantum dot for admittance extraction. The simulations are calibrated us- ing Fermi-Hubbard parameters extracted from experimental data, which it is then shown to closely approximate to.

</details>


### [76] [Nontrivial topological properties from the cooperation of static perturbations and Floquet fields in type-II semi-Dirac semimetals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16659)
*Wen-Hao Bian,Jing Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This work theoretically studies topological and anomalous transport properties of type-II SDSMs under static symmetry-breaking perturbations, two-frequency orthogonally polarized light (TFOPL) Floquet driving, and their hybrid combination, revealing Chern-insulator phases, Berry curvature dipole effects, and anomalous transport signatures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Type-II SDSMs host tilted Dirac cones with unusual topological and transport responses, yet a comprehensive understanding of how static perturbations, periodic light driving, and their cooperation jointly govern their topological phases and anomalous transport remains lacking.

Method: The authors perform a theoretical analysis of 2D type-II SDSMs under three scenarios: (i) static symmetry-breaking perturbations via a mass term m_z, (ii) Floquet driving by two-frequency orthogonally polarized light, and (iii) a hybrid scenario combining both. They compute Chern numbers, Berry curvature and its dipole, gap-closure conditions, and anomalous transport coefficients (AHC, ANC, ATHC).

Result: A static mass m_z opens a bulk gap and drives the system into a Chern insulator with Chern number C = ±2 sgn(m_z). TFOPL alone only shifts Dirac point positions without opening a full gap, but generates an x-odd Berry curvature producing a finite BCD along x proportional to sin(initial phase), with vanishing y-component. In the hybrid scenario, the gap-closure criterion becomes cubic, admitting up to three real roots for negative effective x-parameter, yielding multiple light-tunable gap-closing points and successive topological transitions. The BCD shows a double-peak structure strongly enhanced near gap-closing manifolds with synchronized sign reversal. AHC and ATHC exhibit quantized plateaus marking Chern-insulator phases, while ANC probes the Fermi surface and identifies light-induced gaps even when global topological invariants stay unchanged.

Conclusion: The results provide useful insights into the topological properties of 2D type-II SDSMs and related systems, showing that static mass, TFOPL driving, and their cooperation produce distinct and tunable topological phases and anomalous transport signatures.

Abstract: In this work, we present a comprehensive theoretical study for the topological and anomalous transport properties of type-II SDSMs under three scenarios, i.e., static symmetry-breaking perturbations, periodic Floquet driving by two-frequency orthogonally polarized light (TFOPL), and their cooperative effects in the hybrid scenario. We first show that a static mass $m_z$ opens a bulk gap and drives the system into a Chern insulator with Chern number $C^{\rm per}=\pm 2\,{\rm sgn}(m_z)$. In comparison, TFOPL alone only shifts the positions of Dirac points and cannot open a full gap. Nevertheless, it produces a Berry curvature odd in the $x$-direction, yielding a finite Berry curvature dipole (BCD) along $x$ proportional to the sine of the initial phase, while the $y$-component vanishes. The hybrid scenario, by contrast, exhibits a remarkably rich phenomenology. Specifically, its gap-closure criterion becomes cubic and thus admits up to three real roots when the effective parameter along the $x$-direction is negative, thereby producing multiple light-tunable gap-closing points and a series of topological phase transitions. In particular, the BCD exhibits a double-peak structure with strong enhancement near gap-closing manifolds and synchronized sign reversal of the full vector under external tuning. We further analyze the anomalous Hall conductivity (AHC), anomalous Nernst conductivity (ANC), and anomalous thermal Hall conductivity (ATHC). The AHC and ATHC exhibit quantized plateaus that serve as signatures of Chern-insulator phases, while the ANC, being a Fermi-surface probe, provides complementary local information and can identify light-induced gaps even when global topological invariants remain unchanged. Taken together, these results offer useful insights into the topological properties of 2D type-II SDSMs and related systems.

</details>


### [77] [WannierSTM: Efficient superconducting scanning-probe spectroscopy from maximally localized Wannier functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17943)
*Oscar Moreno Segura,Dorye L. Esteras,Andrei Shumilin,Gonzalo Rivero-Carracedo,Riku Tuovinen,José J. Baldoví*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A Python program couples maximally localized Wannier function first-principles electronic structure with the nonequilibrium Green's function formalism for efficient STM simulations of superconducting hybrid nanostructures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulating STM experiments on realistic superconducting hybrid nanostructures requires an approach that combines accurate material-specific electronic structure with tractable transport calculations. Existing methods face a challenge in efficiently computing spectroscopic quantities over broad parameter spaces while preserving realistic material properties.

Method: The workflow builds on standard DFT packages to construct compact transport Hamiltonians using maximally localized Wannier functions. Superconductivity is incorporated via an effective pairing potential within the Nambu-spinor formalism. Coupling to the STM tip and substrate electrodes is treated with the wide-band limit approximation, and transport quantities such as current-voltage characteristics and differential conductance are evaluated in closed analytical form, avoiding explicit numerical energy integrations.

Result: The framework enables rapid spectroscopic calculations over broad parameter spaces and supports modeling of superconducting hybrid nanostructures with spin-orbit coupling, magnetic textures, and spin polarization. Representative applications include spin chains and two-dimensional and molecular magnets on a superconductor.

Conclusion: The approach provides a unified and computationally efficient method for modeling STM on superconducting hybrid nanostructures, demonstrating robust capabilities for both simple model systems and realistic ab initio molecular structures relevant to modern STM experiments.

Abstract: We present a Python-based program that combines maximally localized Wannier functions-based first-principles electronic structure with the nonequilibrium Green's function formalism to enable efficient simulations of scanning tunneling microscopy (STM). The workflow builds upon standard density functional theory packages to construct compact transport Hamiltonians that preserve the properties of realistic materials. Superconductivity is incorporated via an effective pairing potential within the Nambu-spinor formalism, while coupling to the STM tip and substrate electrodes is treated using the wide-band limit approximation. Within this formulation, transport quantities such as current-voltage characteristics and differential conductance are evaluated in a closed analytical form. This avoids explicit numerical energy integrations and enables rapid spectroscopic calculations over broad parameter spaces. The framework provides a unified and computationally efficient approach for modeling superconducting hybrid nanostructures with spin-orbit coupling, magnetic textures, and spin polarization. Representative applications in spin chains, two-dimensional and molecular magnets on a superconductor demonstrate its robust capabilities for both simple model systems and realistic ab initio molecular structures relevant to modern STM experiments.

</details>


### [78] [Turning a superconducting diode into a Feynman ratchet to realize a nanoscale heat engine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17257)
*Konstantinos Kontogeorgiou,İnanç Adagideli,Géraldine Haack,Fabian Hassler*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper proposes a nanoscaled realization of Feynman's ratchet using Josephson junctions with the superconducting diode effect, functioning as a heat engine driven solely by a temperature gradient, and analyzes its power, efficiency, and reversible Peltier-cooling regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The ratchet extracts work from thermal fluctuations between two heat baths at different temperatures, but existing experimental realizations largely lack miniaturization, which matters for some applications. A compact, on-chip implementation is therefore desirable.

Method: A physical implementation of Feynman's ratchet based on Josephson junctions exhibiting the superconducting diode effect. The device operates as a heat engine generating work solely from a temperature gradient via thermal fluctuations; its output power and efficiency are analyzed, and the Seebeck coefficient is used to identify conditions for reverse operation.

Result: The setup functions as a thermal-gradient-driven heat engine; its output power and efficiency are characterized, and via the Seebeck coefficient the conditions under which the device operates in reverse as a Peltier cooler are identified.

Conclusion: Josephson junctions with the superconducting diode effect provide a miniaturizable platform for Feynman's ratchet that can act either as a heat engine or a Peltier cooler depending on the Seebeck-coefficient-determined operating regime.

Abstract: Feynman's ratchet illustrates how work can be extracted from thermal fluctuations in the presence of two heat baths at different temperatures. While some experimental realizations of the ratchet effect have been studied, most lack miniaturization, which is important for some applications. Here, we propose a physical implementation of Feynman's ratchet based on Josephson junctions exhibiting the superconducting diode effect. This setup functions as a heat engine that generates work solely from a temperature gradient via thermal fluctuations. We analyze its output power and efficiency, and, through the Seebeck coefficient, identify the conditions under which the device operates in reverse, functioning instead as a Peltier cooler.

</details>


### [79] [Emergent Spinorial Structure in Mesoscopic Irreversible Thermodynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16738)
*Giorgio Sonnino*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A mesoscopic canonical model reconstructs thermodynamic-coordinate geometry from transport coefficients, where a spectral distance depends only on the symmetric transport tensor, and Bi₂Te₃ film data yield a principal-distance ratio of ~2.12 at ZT≈0.68.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to reconstruct a geometry of thermodynamic coordinates from transport coefficients within a mesoscopic canonical model, connecting transport physics to a metric structure.

Method: Self-adjoint flux observables define a compatible evolution generator with symmetric and antisymmetric transport coefficients. A graded first-order factorization reproduces the generator on its scalar sector when the Hermitian transport tensor is positive. Commutators yield a spectral distance whose metric depends only on the symmetric tensor. A Clifford representation is used in the constant Onsager regime. A separate quadratic entropy-production observable is also analyzed.

Result: The spectral distance's metric depends only on the symmetric tensor, while gradients of the antisymmetric tensor alter evolution without changing the metric. The Clifford representation gives the same distance and an algebra fixed by the transport matrix in the constant Onsager regime. Published Bi₂Te₃ film data give a principal-distance ratio of ~2.12 in normalized coordinates at ZT≈0.68. The quadratic entropy-production observable has a discrete spectrum distinct from that of the evolution generator.

Conclusion: The construction supports coordinate-dependent coefficients and links transport coefficients to a thermodynamic-coordinate geometry. The spinorial representation introduces no physical spin degrees of freedom, and the entropy-production spectrum is distinguished from the generator's spectrum.

Abstract: We reconstruct a geometry of thermodynamic coordinates from transport coefficients within a mesoscopic canonical model. Self-adjoint flux observables determine a compatible evolution generator containing symmetric and antisymmetric transport coefficients. When the Hermitian transport tensor is positive, an exact graded first-order factorization reproduces this generator on its scalar sector. Its commutators define a spectral distance whose metric depends only on the symmetric tensor. Gradients of the antisymmetric tensor can change the evolution while leaving the metric unchanged. The construction allows coordinate-dependent coefficients. In the constant Onsager regime, a Clifford representation gives the same distance and an algebra fixed by the transport matrix. Published transport data for a $\mathrm{Bi_2Te_3}$ film give a principal-distance ratio of approximately $2.12$ in normalized coordinates at $ZT\simeq0.68$. We also examine a separate quadratic entropy-production observable and distinguish its discrete spectrum from the spectrum of the evolution generator. The spinorial representation adds no physical spin degrees of freedom.

</details>


### [80] [Transport Determines Spectral Distance in Mesoscopic Thermodynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16752)
*Giorgio Sonnino*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper derives a distance between thermodynamic states from a positive Hermitian transport tensor in a mesoscopic canonical model, showing the metric equals $2g^{-1}$ (with $g$ the symmetric transport tensor) and that transport coupling sets the metric anisotropy in a two-channel thermoelectric system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work is motivated by the need to define a natural distance (metric) between thermodynamic states intrinsically from transport properties, without introducing an independent metric or a physical spin variable.

Method: It uses a positive Hermitian transport tensor in a mesoscopic canonical model, performs an exact first-order factorization of the evolution generator (yielding a commutator norm), constructs the metric $G=2g^{-1}$ from the symmetric transport tensor $g$, and compares with a Clifford representation for constant Onsager coefficients. Coordinate-dependent coefficients are allowed.

Result: Gradients of the antisymmetric tensor can alter the evolution but leave the distance unchanged. For constant Onsager coefficients the Clifford representation gives the same metric. In a two-channel thermoelectric system, the ratio of normalized principal distances is $\sqrt{1+ZT}+\sqrt{ZT}$, so transport coupling fixes the metric anisotropy.

Conclusion: A transport-based thermodynamic distance can be defined self-consistently, requires neither an independent metric nor a physical spin variable, and its anisotropy is determined by the transport coupling.

Abstract: We derive a distance between thermodynamic states from a positive Hermitian transport tensor within a mesoscopic canonical model. An exact first-order factorization of the evolution generator gives a commutator norm and the metric $G=2g^{-1}$, where $g$ denotes the symmetric transport tensor. Gradients of the antisymmetric tensor can change the evolution, but they leave this distance unchanged. The construction allows coordinate-dependent coefficients. For constant Onsager coefficients, a Clifford representation gives the same metric. In a two-channel thermoelectric system, the ratio of normalized principal distances is $\sqrt{1+ZT}+\sqrt{ZT}$. Transport coupling thus fixes the anisotropy of this metric. The construction requires neither an independent metric nor a physical spin variable.

</details>


### [81] [Bifurcation Structure of Double Hysteresis and Isolated Branches in a Parametric Duffing Nanoresonator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17052)
*Dingda Zhang,Maria Kallergi,Chanyoung So,Eva M. Weig,Ahmed A. Barakat*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: At a 45° phase difference between direct and parametric driving, the steady-state response of a nonlinear resonator evolves through a defined sequence of bifurcations—peak splitting, two cusp bifurcations creating saddle-node pairs, and an isolated-branch structure above the parametric-instability threshold—verified experimentally on a SiN nanostring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct and parametric driving combined produce nonlinear response structures beyond conventional Duffing hysteresis (multiple switching regions, isolated branches), but how these structures are interconnected as parametric excitation varies is unclear. Identifying the bifurcations that reorganize the response is needed to understand and control multistability and switching.

Method: Experimental and analytical investigation of the steady-state response versus parametric excitation at a 45° phase difference between direct and parametric excitations. A parametric representation of the steady state distinguishes amplitude extrema from genuine saddle-node bifurcations and yields analytical conditions. Measurements are performed on a dielectrically actuated pre-stressed SiN nanostring.

Result: A single resonance peak first splits into two while the response remains single valued. Two successive cusp bifurcations create two saddle-node pairs, establishing a double-hysteretic response. At the parametric-instability threshold, one saddle node of each pair is displaced towards unbounded amplitude and detuning, and an isolated-branch structure emerges above threshold. Measurements reproduce the predicted response and switching behavior.

Conclusion: The bifurcation pathway connecting the different response structures at this phase configuration is established, providing a systematic basis for controlling multistability and switching in parametrically driven nonlinear resonators.

Abstract: Direct and parametric driving together generate nonlinear response structures beyond conventional Duffing hysteresis, including multiple switching regions and isolated branches. Understanding how these structures are connected requires identifying the bifurcations that reorganize the response as the parametric excitation is varied. At a phase difference of 45 degrees between the direct and parametric excitations, we experimentally and analytically determine how the steady-state response changes as the parametric excitation is increased. A single resonance peak first splits into two while the response remains single valued. Two successive cusp bifurcations then create two saddle-node pairs and establish a double-hysteretic response. At the parametric-instability threshold, one saddle node of each pair is displaced towards unbounded amplitude and detuning, and an isolated-branch structure emerges above threshold. A parametric representation of the steady state distinguishes amplitude extrema from genuine saddle-node bifurcations and provides analytical conditions for these changes. Measurements on a dielectrically actuated pre-stressed SiN nanostring reproduce the predicted response and associated switching behavior. These results establish the bifurcation pathway connecting the different response structures at this phase configuration, providing a systematic basis for controlling multistability and switching in parametrically driven nonlinear resonators.

</details>


### [82] [Non-volatile Orbital magnetoelectric effect in a two-dimensional electron gas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17073)
*Yu Chen,Martando Rath,Julien Brehin,Cinthia Piamonteze,Daniele Preziosi,Payal Wadhwa,Alessio Filippetti,Yuri Fukaya,Mario Cuoco,Filomena Forte,Daniela Stornaiuolo,Manuel Bibes,Marco Salluzzo*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Ferroelectric polarization non-volatilely switches the equilibrium orbital magnetization of oxide quasi-2D electron gases at a LaAlO3/EuTiO3/Ca-STO interface, evidenced by XLD and XMCD spectroscopy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonvolatile electrical control of an equilibrium orbital moment remains elusive, despite recent demonstrations of current-induced orbital polarization in inversion-asymmetric systems, motivating orbital-magnetism control via ferroelectricity.

Method: X-ray absorption spectroscopy with in situ electric-field control of the ferroelectric polarization, combining X-ray linear dichroism (XLD) and X-ray magnetic circular dichroism (XMCD) at the Ti-3d and Eu-M4,5 edges.

Result: XLD shows reversible, polarization-direction-driven modulation of Ti-3d crystal-field splitting and orbital occupancy, establishing a ferroelectric-like metallic state; XMCD reveals polarization-controlled switching of the Eu2+ orbital magnetic moment.

Conclusion: The work provides spectroscopic evidence of ferroelectric control of orbital magnetism in an oxide quasi-2D electron gas, advancing nonvolatile electrical manipulation of equilibrium orbital moments.

Abstract: Electrical control of orbital magnetism is a central goal of orbitronics. While current-induced orbital polarization has recently been demonstrated in inversion-asymmetric systems, nonvolatile electrical control of an equilibrium orbital moment has remained elusive.
  Here, using x-ray absorption spectroscopy with \textit{in situ} electric-field control of the FE polarization, we demonstrate non-volatile switching of the equilibrium orbital magnetization of the oxide q2DEGs formed at the LaAlO$_3$/EuTiO$_3$/Ca-STO (001) interface. X-ray linear dichroism reveals a reversible modulation, driven by the FE-polarization direction, of the Ti-3$d$ crystal-field splitting and orbital occupancy, establishing a ferroelectric-like metallic state. X-ray magnetic circular dichroism shows a polarization-controlled switching of the Eu$^{2+}$ orbital magnetic moment induced by the orbital magnetization of the q2DEG projected onto Eu-5$d$ states and detected at the Eu-$M_{4,5}$ edge through Eu-5$d$/Eu-4$f$ hybridization in the inversion-symmetry-broken interface. These results provide spectroscopic evidence of ferroelectric control of orbital magnetism in an oxide q2DEG.

</details>


### [83] [Spatial Engineering of Topological Quantum Matter with Structured Light](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17181)
*Matias Mustonen,Ali G. Moghaddam,Teemu Ojanen,Marco Ornigotti*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Structured light, specifically an optical skyrmion beam, can locally engineer the topological properties of a topological insulator's Dirac surface states, creating position-dependent gaps and Dirac velocities. This enables optically defined topological/trivial interfaces without material boundaries, whose geometry is controllable via the light's orbital angular momentum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structured light controls matter and itself possesses nontrivial spatial topology. The paper aims to exploit these spatial structures to locally engineer the topological properties of matter, exploring the interplay between topology in light and matter.

Method: Using a paradigmatic topological insulator with Dirac surface states driven by an optical skyrmion beam, the authors show that structured light spatially tailors the effective Hamiltonian, producing position-dependent topological gaps and Dirac velocities. The gap modulation generates topological and trivial regions separated by optically defined interfaces, with interface geometry controlled by the light's orbital angular momentum.

Result: Structured light yields position-dependent topological gaps and Dirac velocities, enabling optically defined interfaces between topological and trivial regions without material boundaries or chemical modifications. The interface geometry is tunable via the orbital angular momentum of the light.

Conclusion: Structured light offers a route to dynamically pattern topological phases and opens a broader perspective on the interplay between topology in light and matter, including the possibility of transferring topological characteristics between the two.

Abstract: Structured light provides a powerful means of controlling matter while itself exhibiting nontrivial spatial topology. Here, we show that these spatial structures can be harnessed to locally engineer the topological properties of matter. In a paradigmatic example of a topological insulator with Dirac surface states driven by an optical skyrmion beam, we show that structured light spatially tailors the effective Hamiltonian, yielding position-dependent topological gaps and Dirac velocities. The resulting gap modulation can create topological and trivial regions separated by optically defined interfaces, without introducing material boundaries or chemical modifications. We further show that the geometry of these interfaces can be controlled through the spatial structure of the light, in particular its orbital angular momentum. Our results establish structured light as a route to dynamically pattern topological phases and open a broader perspective on the interplay between topology in light and matter, including the possibility of transferring topological characteristics between the two.

</details>


### [84] [Revealing topological protection through unprotected operations in a Majorana parity qubit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17198)
*Sebastian Miles,Juan Daniel Torres Luna,Francesco Zatelli,Nick van Loo,Antonio Lombardi,Vincent P. M. Sietses,Florian J. Bennebroek Evertsz',Bart Roovers,Leo P. Kouwenhoven,Michael Wimmer*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Unprotected operations on a Majorana parity qubit made of two coupled Kitaev chains can still reveal the chains' exponential protection, appearing as an exponentially growing optimized number of coherent Rabi oscillations or coherence time with chain length.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A recent experiment demonstrated a Majorana parity qubit via coherent parity oscillations between minimal Kitaev chains, but its operations, controlled by inter-chain coupling, are intrinsically unprotected against noise in the coupling strength. The question is whether the exponential protection of the underlying chains can nonetheless be observed through such unprotected qubit operations.

Method: An analytical theory of quasistatic dephasing is developed for two coupled N-site Kitaev chains at the sweet spot, incorporating the non-Gaussian splitting statistics of the chains to quantitatively predict scaling with chain length. Rabi and Ramsey experiment protocols are analyzed, and the effect of residual noise and imperfections is determined.
