Result: In Rabi experiments, protection can be resolved by adapting the operating point with chain length: depending on the coupling architecture, it manifests as an exponentially increasing optimized number of coherent oscillations or as a longer coherence time. Ramsey experiments show an exponentially increasing coherence time, provided the chains can be sufficiently decoupled. Residual noise and imperfections limit the range of chain lengths over which the scaling is observable.

Conclusion: Qubit dynamics can be used to probe Majorana protection in experimentally realistic devices, revealing exponential protection through otherwise unprotected operations on coupled Kitaev-chain Majorana parity qubits.

Abstract: A recent experiment reported coherent parity oscillations between minimal Kitaev chains, demonstrating a Majorana parity qubit (arXiv:2607.09511). Operations on this qubit are controlled by coupling two Kitaev chains and are intrinsically unprotected against noise in the coupling strength. Here we show how the exponential protection in the underlying chains can nevertheless be revealed through unprotected qubit operations. To this end, we develop an analytical theory of quasistatic dephasing for two coupled $N$-site Kitaev chains at the sweet spot. In Rabi experiments, protection can be resolved by adapting the operating point with chain length: depending on the coupling architecture, it appears as an exponentially increasing optimized number of coherent oscillations or as a longer coherence time. Ramsey experiments show an exponentially increasing coherence time, provided the chains can be sufficiently decoupled. Our analytical theory takes into account the non-Gaussian splitting statistics of the Kitaev chains, allowing us to quantitatively predict the scaling with chain length. Moreover, we determine how residual noise and imperfections limit the range of chain lengths over which this scaling can be observed. Our results show how qubit dynamics can be used to probe Majorana protection in experimentally realistic devices.

</details>


### [85] [Energy levels of screened charged impurity in gapped graphene under magnetic field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17492)
*Andrii Iurov,V. P. Gusynin*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This theoretical work studies electronic states in gapped graphene with a charge impurity and magnetic field, using a WKB approach to analyze how the Coulomb potential modifies Landau levels, with emphasis on screening by metallic gates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analytical solutions of the Dirac equation are unavailable for gapped graphene with a charge impurity in a magnetic field, yet understanding impurity-induced modification of Landau levels is experimentally relevant, especially given the common presence of metallic gates at finite distance from the graphene sheet.

Method: The Wentzel-Kramers-Brillouin (WKB) approach is employed to treat the gapped Dirac equation under a magnetic field and an attractive or repulsive Coulomb potential, incorporating screening effects from metallic gates at finite distances. Both analytical and numerical methods are used to obtain the energy spectrum as a function of magnetic field strength and impurity charge for screened and unscreened Coulomb potentials.

Result: The energy spectrum is determined for both screened and unscreened Coulomb potentials, and the results reveal a significant dependence of the energy levels for sample-to-gate distances below 50 nm.

Conclusion: Screening by metallic gates strongly affects the impurity-modified Landau levels in gapped graphene when the sample-to-gate distance is less than 50 nm, making gate proximity a key parameter in such systems.

Abstract: In this paper, we present a comprehensive theoretical study of the electronic states in gapped graphene in the presence of a charge impurity and a magnetic field. We focus on the impurity-induced modification of the Landau levels under the influence of an attractive or repulsive Coulomb potential. Since analytical solutions of the Dirac equation are not available in this case, the study is carried out using the Wentzel-Kramers-Brillouin (WKB) approach. Special attention is paid to the screening of the Coulomb potential in the presence of metallic gates situated at some distances from graphene sheet, as is commonly the case in experiments. Using both analytical and numerical methods, we determine the energy spectrum as a function of the magnetic field strength and impurity charge for screened and unscreened Coulomb potentials. Our results reveal a significant dependence of the energy levels for sample-to-gate distances of less than 50 nm.

</details>


### [86] [A Full-Spectrum Theory of Moiré Superlattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17749)
*Tong Liu,X. R. Wang,Jiansheng Wu*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The authors develop a unified, approximation-free analytical formalism for generalized moiré superlattices that works beyond the Dirac-cone and first-shell limits, recovering the Bistritzer–MacDonald model as a low-energy limit while predicting higher-order magic angles and tunable flat bands.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional moiré theories, exemplified by the Bistritzer–MacDonald continuum model, rely on low-energy Dirac cone approximations and first-shell interlayer coupling truncations. These assumptions work for small-angle twisted bilayer graphene near charge neutrality but fail for strained lattices, high-energy bands, or arbitrary commensurate geometries, leaving no universal analytical framework.

Method: The formalism rests on three pillars: (i) a rigorous 2D-to-1D supercell cyclic mapping, (ii) exact block-circulant diagonalization of intralayer Hamiltonians, and (iii) quasi-degenerate Löwdin perturbation theory centered on arbitrary energy degeneracy points across the full spectrum, rather than anchored to the Dirac point. High-order interlayer coupling shells are also incorporated.

Result: The framework exactly recovers the Bistritzer–MacDonald model in the low-energy limit and extends to heterostrained systems and high-energy regimes. Including higher-order interlayer coupling shells reveals intrinsic scale self-similarity in moiré lattices and predicts higher-order magic angles and tunable flat bands driven by secondary degeneracies.

Conclusion: The work eliminates the long-standing absence of universal analytical tools for diverse moiré platforms, providing a full-spectrum theoretical foundation for band engineering and exploration of exotic correlated and topological states in van der Waals heterostructures.

Abstract: Conventional theoretical descriptions of moiré superlattices, epitomized by the Bistritzer--MacDonald continuum model, are fundamentally constrained by low-energy Dirac cone approximations and first-shell interlayer coupling truncations. While successful for small-angle twisted bilayer graphene near charge neutrality, these frameworks fail to provide universal analytical solutions for strained lattices, high-energy band behaviors, or arbitrary commensurate geometries. Here, we establish a unified, approximation-free analytical formalism for generalized moiré systems based on three pillars: (i) a rigorous two-dimensional-to-one-dimensional supercell cyclic mapping, (ii) exact block-circulant diagonalization of intralayer Hamiltonians, and (iii) quasi-degenerate Löwdin perturbation theory centered on arbitrary energy degeneracy points across the full spectrum. Unlike traditional schemes anchored to the Dirac point, our approach enables systematic perturbative expansions around any band crossing, unifying the characterization of low-energy flat bands with high-energy degeneracies. We demonstrate that this framework perfectly recovers the Bistritzer--MacDonald model in the low-energy limit while extending its reach to heterostrained systems and high-energy regimes. By incorporating high-order interlayer coupling shells, we reveal an intrinsic scale self-similarity in moiré lattices and predict the emergence of higher-order magic angles and tunable flat bands driven by secondary degeneracies. This work eliminates the long-standing lack of universal analytical tools for diverse moiré platforms, providing a full-spectrum theoretical foundation for band engineering and the exploration of exotic correlated and topological states in van der Waals heterostructures.

</details>


### [87] [Zeeman spin quantum geometry and intrinsic gyrotropic spin current](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18224)
*Srijan Chatterjee,Tarun Kanti Ghosh*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper uncovers an intrinsic gyrotropic spin current driven purely by an oscillating magnetic field, rooted in the spin version of Zeeman quantum geometry, and derives it via density matrix formalism in terms of the Zeeman spin quantum metric and Berry curvature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional spin currents typically require electric fields or spin-orbit coupling driving mechanisms. The authors aim to identify a spin current generated solely by an oscillating magnetic field, exploiting the spin analogue of quantum geometry (Zeeman quantum geometry), which has been underexplored. This could provide new routes for spin current detection and energy-efficient spintronics.

Method: The authors use the density matrix formalism to derive the intrinsic gyrotropic spin current (IGSC) in the low-frequency clean limit. The current is expressed in terms of the Zeeman spin quantum metric and the Zeeman spin Berry curvature. They study the effect in a minimal Rashba-Dresselhaus model, which also supports intrinsic gyrotropic charge currents arising from the Zeeman Berry curvature.

Result: The spin and charge currents exhibit a π/2 phase shift and are perpendicular to each other for certain parameter ranges. The currents switch directions between longitudinal and transverse relative to the applied field, depending on the relative strength of Rashba and Dresselhaus interactions.

Conclusion: The IGSC enables spin current detection and opens pure spin current channels for energy-efficient spintronics. The direction switching controlled by Rashba/Dresselhaus ratio offers a tunable mechanism for manipulating spin and charge transport.

Abstract: We uncover an intrinsic gyrotropic spin current (IGSC) driven solely by an oscillating magnetic field, arising from the spin version of Zeeman quantum geometry. Using the density matrix formalism, we derive the IGSC in the low-frequency clean limit, in terms of the Zeeman spin quantum metric and the Zeeman spin Berry curvature. The effect is studied in a minimal Rashba-Dresselhaus model that also supports intrinsic gyrotropic charge currents from the Zeeman Berry curvature. The spin and charge currents exhibit a $π/2$ phase shift and are found to be perpendicular to each other for certain parameter ranges. This not only enables spin current detection but also opens pure spin current channels for energy-efficient spintronics. We further observe that these currents switch directions between longitudinal and transverse relative to the applied field, depending on the relative strength of Rashba and Dresselhaus interactions.

</details>


### [88] [Role of dissipation dilution in achieving high mechanical quality factors in van der Waals resonators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18288)
*Lukas Schleicher,Leonard Geilen,Alexander Musta,Niklas Walter,Anne Rodriguez,Philipp Bredol,Alexander W. Holleitner,Eva M. Weig*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Monolayer MoS₂ drums achieve quality factors up to 372,000 at 4 K via a polymer-free exfoliation and dissipation-dilution engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dissipation dilution has revolutionized quality factors in top-down nanomechanical resonators but has not been applied to van der Waals (vdW) materials, leaving their potential for ultrahigh-Q operation unexplored.

Method: A clean, polymer-free exfoliation technique fabricates monolayer and multilayer MoS₂ circular drums. The authors develop a dissipation-dilution model for circular drums, separate intrinsic and extrinsic dissipation mechanisms, and perform a two-level-system analysis of temperature-dependent damping.

Result: Monolayer MoS₂ drums reach Q up to 372,000 ± 37,000 at 4 K, ~10× beyond the previous best for any vdW material at this temperature, with a dilution factor of order 1,000. Intrinsic Q is limited by surface losses at atomic thickness. Multilayer drums show lower dilution but high intrinsic Q, comparable to top-down materials of similar dimensions. Two-level-system analysis confirms the bulk-versus-surface picture.

Conclusion: Dissipation dilution governs high-Q mechanical vdW resonators. Combined with soft clamping or stress engineering, atomically thin resonators form a platform for ultrahigh-Q quantum transduction and sensing.

Abstract: Dissipation dilution has driven a revolution in the quality factors ($Q$) of top-down nanomechanical resonators. Yet it has not been leveraged for van der Waals (vdW) materials. Here we report quality factors up to $372{,}000\pm37{,}000$ in monolayer MoS$_2$ drums at 4 K, nearly an order of magnitude beyond the previous best for any vdW material at this temperature, enabled by a clean, polymer-free exfoliation technique. We develop a dissipation-dilution model for circular drums and analyze the contributing intrinsic and extrinsic dissipation mechanisms. The MoS$_2$ monolayer exhibits a dilution factor of order $1,000$, establishing sub-nanometer-thin resonators as ultimate dilution materials. The intrinsic quality factor, however, is limited by surface losses at atomic thickness. Conversely, multilayer drums show a lower dilution factor but a high intrinsic quality factor, on par with established top-down materials of similar dimensions, both resulting from the increased thickness. A two-level-system analysis of the temperature-dependent damping confirms this bulk-versus-surface picture and provides first insight into its microscopic origin. Our results identify dissipation dilution as the governing mechanism for high-$Q$ mechanical vdW resonators. Combined with strategies such as soft clamping or stress engineering, they establish atomically thin resonators as a platform for ultrahigh-$Q$ quantum transduction and sensing.

</details>


### [89] [Plasmon modes in triple-point semimetal, its quadratic cousin, and Hopf semimetal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18629)
*Ipsita Mandal*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This work analyzes collective charge excitations in pseudospin-1 triple-point semimetals using the random-phase approximation, comparing a linearized flat-band model with its quadratic-corrected version, and benchmarking against Weyl and Hopf semimetals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to understand how bandstructure and quantum geometry govern collective charge dynamics in triple-point semimetals, specifically examining the role of a flat band and its quadratic correction.

Method: The authors employ the random-phase approximation to compute density response, Drude weights, screening, plasmon dispersion and damping, particle-hole continua, and Friedel oscillations across long-wavelength to arbitrary-momentum regimes for both the linearized and quadratic-corrected models.

Result: The quadratic correction makes the flat band dispersive and gives it a Fermi surface, altering the overall response; Drude weights are set by the Fermi surface and band velocities, whereas interband screening, plasmon dispersion, and Friedel oscillations reflect the quantum geometry of the eigenstates.

Conclusion: Comparisons with Weyl and Hopf semimetals show that bandstructure and quantum geometry jointly control collective charge dynamics in pseudospin-1 triple-point semimetals.

Abstract: We study collective charge excitations in pseudospin-1 triple-point semimetals within the random-phase approximation, considering both the linearised model with a flat-band and its quadratic-corrected counterpart. We analyse their density response, Drude weights, screening, plasmon dispersion and damping, particle-hole continua, and Friedel oscillations from the long-wavelength to the arbitrary-momentum regime. The quadratic correction renders the flat-band dispersive and endows it with a Fermi surface, thereby affecting the overall response. Comparisons with Weyl and Hopf semimetals reveal how bandstructure and quantum geometry jointly govern collective charge dynamics. While the Drude weights are determined by the Fermi surface and band-velocities, interband screening, plasmon dispersion, and Friedel oscillations reflect the quantum geometry of the eigenstates.

</details>


### [90] [Dark state preparation in a zero-field-split molecular triplet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18661)
*Maria Pfahler,Andrea Donarini*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper proposes a protocol to prepare and probe dark states in single-molecule ESR-AFM, extending Rabi-oscillation techniques by simulating coherent manipulation of a three-level V-system in a molecule with a zero-field-split triplet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ESR-AFM techniques address Rabi oscillations, but dark states—coherent superpositions that do not evolve despite resonant driving—remain unexplored in this single-molecule context. Extending these techniques to probe dark states would broaden the capabilities of ESR-AFM.

Method: The authors simulate coherent manipulation of a three-level system in the V-configuration using a multifrequency magnetic field, analogous to atomic physics. They consider a neutral molecule with a zero-field-split first excited triplet and analyze, within a pump-probe scheme, the dynamics of the total triplet population. Using a density-matrix formalism for open systems, they compute expected dark state signatures for both ideal scenarios and realistic ESR-AFM parameterization on pentacene.

Result: The dark state is characterized as a coherent superposition of two ZFS states that does not evolve in time despite continuous resonant driving toward the third state. Its properties are compared to a fully incoherent counterpart, and dark state signatures are computed for ideal and realistic ESR-AFM conditions on pentacene.

Conclusion: The proposed protocol extends ESR-AFM to prepare and probe dark states in a single molecule, providing a framework for observing coherent dark-state signatures under realistic experimental parameterizations.

Abstract: We propose a protocol to prepare and probe dark states in a single-molecule electron spin resonance based on atomic force microscopy (ESR-AFM), thus extending existing techniques developed to address Rabi oscillations. To this end, we simulate in analogy to atomic physics the coherent manipulation of a three-level system in the V-configuration using a multifrequency magnetic field. More specifically, we consider a neutral molecule with a zero-field-split (ZFS) first excited triplet and analyze within a pump-probe scheme the dynamics of the total triplet population. In the triplet subspace, the dark state is a coherent superposition formed by two ZFS states which does not evolve in time despite a continuous resonant driving towards the third one. We characterize the properties of this state comparing it to its fully incoherent counterpart. Within a density-matrix formalism for open systems, we then compute the expected dark state signatures both for ideal scenarios and for the realistic parameterization of ESR-AFM on pentacene.

</details>


### [91] [Spin textures at the Fermi surface of ferromagnets and associated torque](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18800)
*T. Valet,J. Li,H. Jaffrès,V. Cros,M. Chshiev,R. Raimondi*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Spin-orbit interaction generically creates transverse spin textures at the Fermi surface of centrosymmetric ferromagnets, and these textures generate a current-gradient-induced torque that quantitatively matches experiments previously attributed to orbital torques.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Experiments on centrosymmetric ferromagnetic metals have reported torques that cannot be explained by conventional spin-orbit torques, and these have been speculatively attributed to orbital torques. The origin of such torques and whether they are spin-orbit driven remained unclear.

Method: The authors derive theoretically that spin-orbit coupling in centrosymmetric ferromagnetic metals generically induces transverse spin textures at the Fermi surface. They verify this prediction using ab initio computations for Fe, Co and Ni. By symmetry analysis, these textures cannot produce spin-orbit torques under a uniform current, but the authors show that a current-gradient-induced torque emerges in nanoscale geometries. They compute this torque for a series of Ni-based bilayers and compare the Ni thickness dependence to experimental measurements.

Result: Ab initio calculations confirm the predicted transverse spin textures in Fe, Co and Ni. The computed Ni thickness dependence of the current-gradient-induced torque in Ni-based bilayers quantitatively agrees with measurements previously attributed to orbital torques.

Conclusion: Transverse spin textures from spin-orbit interaction provide a viable origin for the observed torques in centrosymmetric ferromagnets, opening a new route for electrical control of magnetic orders, potentially including the Néel vector of metallic altermagnets.

Abstract: We establish theoretically that transverse spin textures are generically induced by the spin-orbit interaction at the Fermi surface of centrosymmetric ferromagnetic metals. We confirm this prediction with {\em ab initio} computations for Fe, Co and Ni. By symmetry, this cannot be associated with spin-orbit torques under a uniform current. However, a new {\em current-gradient-induced} torque emerges in nanoscale geometries. For a series of Ni-based bilayers, the computed Ni thickness dependence of the current-gradient-induced torque is in quantitative agreement with measurements previously attributed to orbital torques. We discuss how this opens a new avenue for the electrical control of magnetic orders, possibly also including the Néel vector of metallic altermagnets.

</details>


### [92] [Single anyons and anyon binding in a quantum Hall dot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18817)
*Marco Valentini,Will Wang,Amir Shapour Mohammadi,Noah Samuelson,Chengxuan Li,Liam Cohen,Mark Zakharyan,Jiechao Feng,Taige Wang,Takashi Taniguchi,Kenji Watanabe,Michael P. Zaletel,Andrea F. Young*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A gate-tunable graphene quantum point contact embedding a quantum Hall dot is used to trap a single anyon and study its charging behavior. The device reveals single-anyon transistor operation at ν = 1/3 and 2/3, a highly irregular few-quasiparticle addition spectrum, and evidence for an anyon binding energy comparable to the charging energy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although a single anyon can in principle be stabilized as a localized fractional charge in a topologically ordered 2D insulator, prior experiments probed anyons only in structures where many anyonic excitations coexist and interact. Isolating and controlling an individual anyon is therefore needed to access its intrinsic charging and interaction properties.

Method: The authors fabricate a graphene-based quantum point contact with an embedded tunable quantum Hall dot. They benchmark it at filling ν = 1 by measuring whole-electron Coulomb-blockade-mediated backscattering between Fermi liquid edge states, then deplete the dot to the last quasihole and observe spin blockade across opposite-spin Landau levels. They extend the measurements to ν = 1/3 and 2/3, tracing the charge addition spectrum for quasiparticle numbers Nqp = 1–5 and 10–20, and compare results with DMRG modeling.

Result: Backscattering resonances at ν = 1/3 and 2/3 occur with three times the frequency, consistent with resonant transmission of e/3 charges via a single anyon transistor. For Nqp = 1–5, addition energies are highly irregular, matching DMRG predictions of complex orbital shell filling. For Nqp = 10–20, a striking pattern appears: quasiholes at 1/3 and quasiparticles at 2/3 show regular addition spectra, while quasiparticles at 1/3 and quasiholes at 2/3 do not. By tuning the confining energy, the authors observe complete suppression of alternating charge sectors, after which the dot charges in increments of twice the fundamental charge.

Conclusion: The device realizes controlled depletion to the last quasiparticle, forming a single-anyon quantum dot. The irregular addition spectra and the confinement-tunable doubling of the charge increment are attributed to an attractive inter-anyon interaction, indicating an anyon binding energy as large as the charging energy. This establishes a platform for isolating and manipulating individual anyons and probing their interactions.

Abstract: Anyons are particles with quantized fractional statistics. In theory, a single anyon can be stabilized as a localized fractional charge within the bulk of a topologically ordered two-dimensional insulator. However, experiments to date have focused on probing anyons in structures in which many anyonic excitations coexist and interact. Here, we demonstrate the trapping of a single anyon in a device consisting of a graphene-based quantum point contact with an embedded tunable quantum Hall dot. Benchmarking our device at v = 1, we demonstrate backscattering between Fermi liquid edge states mediated by the whole-electron Coulomb blockade of the dot. The cleanliness and control available in our device allow depletion of the dot to the last quasihole, and we observe spin blockade for transitions to the opposite-spin Landau level. At v = 1/3 and 2/3, we observe backscattering resonances with three times the frequency, consistent with resonant transmission of e/3 charges via a single anyon transistor. We demonstrate controlled depletion to the last quasiparticle to form a single anyon quantum dot. Tracing the charge addition spectrum for quasiparticle number Nqp = 1-5, we observe highly irregular addition energies, consistent with DMRG modeling showing a complex orbital shell-filling behavior. At higher Nqp = 10-20, the addition energies show a striking pattern: quasiholes at 1/3 (and quasiparticles at 2/3) show a regular addition spectrum while quasiparticles at 1/3 (and quasiholes at 2/3) do not. We trace the origin of this irregularity to an attractive component of the inter-anyon interaction: by experimentally tuning the confining energy, we observe complete suppression of alternating charge sectors, after which the dot charges in increments of twice the fundamental charge. This observation indicates the emergence of an anyon binding energy as large as the charging energy.

</details>


### [93] [Composite Fermion Theory of Fractional Chern Insulators in Rhombohedral Graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07174)
*Peize Ding,Andrew J. Millis,Yongxin Zeng*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A composite fermion (CF) description is developed to explain how moiré modulation and Coulomb interactions stabilize fractional Chern insulator (FCI) states in rhombohedral graphene multilayers exhibiting the fractional quantum anomalous Hall effect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The experimental observation of the fractional quantum anomalous Hall effect in rhombohedral graphene multilayers raises open questions about the nature of the FCI states and the role played by the moiré potential. A theoretical framework clarifying how these states form and are stabilized is needed.

Method: The authors construct a composite fermion description. Via a flux-attachment transformation and a mean-field approximation, the CF spectrum is shown to form Landau levels that are further split by the moiré potential. They analyze the occupancy of CF bands at moiré filling fractions in the Jain sequence and examine the interplay between attached flux, Berry curvature, layer localization, and CF-CF interactions.

Result: At Jain-sequence moiré filling fractions, an integer number of CF bands is occupied, yielding an incompressible ground state with fractionally quantized Hall response. When the attached flux opposes the Berry curvature of the conduction band, CF Landau levels become widely spaced in energy and delocalized across layers, reducing the density of states, protecting the CF mean-field state, and amplifying the moiré potential's effect on the layer away from the doped electrons. Interactions between CFs further enlarge the moiré-induced band gap.

Conclusion: The work clarifies the nature of FCI states in rhombohedral graphene and offers a new theoretical framework for further studies of fractional Chern insulators.

Abstract: The experimental observation of the fractional quantum anomalous Hall effect in rhombohedral graphene multilayers has raised questions about the nature of the fractional Chern insulator (FCI) states and the role of the moiré potential. In this Letter we develop a composite fermion (CF) description and discuss how moiré modulation and Coulomb interactions stabilize the FCI ground states. Under a flux-attachment transformation and mean-field approximation, the CF spectrum forms Landau levels that are further split by the moiré potential. At moiré filling fractions in the Jain sequence, an integer number of CF bands are occupied, resulting in an incompressible ground state with fractionally quantized Hall response. Remarkably, when the attached flux opposes the Berry curvature of the conduction band of rhombohedral graphene, the CF Landau levels are widely spaced in energy and delocalized across layers, leading to a reduced density of states that helps protect the CF mean-field state and amplifying effects of the moiré potential acting on the layer away from the doped electrons. Interactions between CFs further enlarge the moiré-induced band gap and stabilize the FCI ground state. Our work sheds light on the nature of the FCI states in rhombohedral graphene and provides a new theoretical framework for further studies.

</details>


<div id='physics.atom-ph'></div>

# physics.atom-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [94] [Additional ionization during nuclear $β^{\pm}$ decay in atomic systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14109)
*Alexei M. Frolov*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a first non-approximate method to compute probabilities of bound-free atomic excitations occurring during nuclear β± decays in real atoms and ions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior treatments of atomic excitations accompanying nuclear β± decays were approximate. A rigorous, complete description applicable to actual multi-electron atoms and ions was needed.

Method: An original numerical method is developed. Closed analytical formulas for final-state probabilities of different excitations in few-electron atomic systems are derived and tested.

Result: The derived closed-form expressions enable numerical evaluation of bound-free excitation probabilities during nuclear β± decays, validated by testing.

Conclusion: This is the first non-approximate and essentially complete investigation of atomic bound-free excitations during nuclear β± decays in atoms.

Abstract: An original method is developed for numerical calculations of probabilities of the bound-free atomic excitations during the nuclear $β^{\pm}$ decays in actual atoms and ions. To achieve this goal we have derived and tested the closed analytical formulas for the final state probabilities of different excitations in the few-electron atomic systems. This study is the first non-approximate and essentially complete investigation of the atomic bound-free excitations during the nuclear $β^{\pm}$ decays in atoms.

</details>


### [95] [Interpretation of chirality imprinted on photoelectron momentum spectra by three-dimensional light fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14557)
*Junxin Yuan,Yuhong Li,Fei Li,Jinkai Zhang,Shiyang Zhong,Hao Teng,Yujun Yang,Yingchun Guo,Zhiyi Wei,Jing Chen,Xiaojun Liu,Bingbing Wang*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: Using frequency-domain nonperturbative QED, the authors show that chirality in the photoelectron momentum spectrum arises from coherence between adjacent quantum transition channels, with a symmetry-breaking phase shift tunable via laser parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Synthetic 3D light fields offer a route to imprint chirality on achiral matter, and prior numerical work showed chirality can be imprinted on atomic photoelectron momentum spectra. However, a transparent analytical physical picture connecting the driving field structure to the spectrum's chirality was still lacking.

Method: Frequency-domain theory based on nonperturbative quantum electrodynamics (QED); analysis of coherence between adjacent quantum transition channels; introduction of a symmetry transformation yielding a symmetry-breaking phase shift (Φ_SBPS) controlled by laser-field parameters; application to a noncollinear two-color 3D light field.

Result: Chirality originates from coherence between adjacent quantum transition channels. The symmetry-breaking phase shift, tunable via laser-field parameters, can preserve or break the relevant symmetry in the spectrum; once all such symmetries are broken, the momentum spectrum becomes chiral. For the noncollinear two-color 3D light field, the spectrum is chiral for even f = ω2/ω1 and achiral for odd f.

Conclusion: The results provide a microscopic interpretation of chirality imprinting and establish a framework for predicting and controlling chiral electron wave packets with synthetic 3D light fields.

Abstract: Synthetic three-dimensional (3D) light fields provide a new route to imprinting chirality on achiral matter through light--matter interaction. Although previous work has numerically demonstrated that chirality can be imprinted on the photoelectron momentum spectrum of atoms, a transparent analytical physical picture that links the structure of the driving field to the chirality of the spectrum is still an open question. Here, using frequency-domain theory based on nonperturbative quantum electrodynamics (QED), we demonstrate that this chirality originates from coherence between adjacent quantum transition channels. We further find that a symmetry transformation introduces into the coherence term a symmetry-breaking phase shift ($Φ_{\mathrm{SBPS}}$), which can be tuned by varying the laser-field parameters to preserve or break the corresponding symmetry in the spectrum. Once all such symmetries are broken, the momentum spectrum becomes chiral. In particular, for the noncollinear two-color 3D light field considered here, the spectrum is chiral for even $f=ω_2/ω_1$ and achiral for odd $f$. Our results provide a microscopic interpretation of chirality imprinting and establish a framework for predicting and controlling chiral electron wave packets with synthetic 3D light fields.

</details>


### [96] [Inverse identification of thermal-radiative parameters for reduced effective-temperature uncertainty in an ion optical clock](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14796)
*Jie Zhao,Yani Zuo,Shaoyang Dai,Chaowei Wang,Ang Huang,Jian Song,Guangyao Wang,Fangfei Wu,Haotian Shi,Zhengyong Li,Fang Fang*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This work develops a hybrid inversion framework to accurately determine the ion-position effective temperature needed for evaluating the blackbody-radiation (BBR) shift in room-temperature single-ion optical clocks, achieving a 155.0 mK temperature uncertainty and a corresponding BBR frequency-shift uncertainty of 1.647 × 10⁻¹⁹.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The BBR shift is a leading systematic uncertainty in room-temperature single-ion optical clocks. Its accurate evaluation requires the effective temperature at the ion position, but direct measurement is difficult due to RF heating, low and spatially varying surface emissivity, and inaccessible trap surfaces.

Method: A hybrid inversion framework combining radiance-domain infrared thermography, black-painted high-emissivity reference regions, a finite-element (FE) model of a ¹⁷¹Yb⁺ end-cap ion trap, and a reduced-order surrogate for repeated forward calculations and parameter identification.

Result: The surrogate reproduced FE test fields with a full-field mean absolute error (MAE) of 0.02220 mK. The calibrated model predicted the ion-position effective temperature with a combined standard uncertainty of 155.0 mK, corresponding to a temperature-related BBR frequency-shift uncertainty of 1.647 × 10⁻¹⁹ for the ²S₁/₂–²F₇/₂ (467 nm) E3 transition.

Conclusion: The proposed method provides a reproducible route for reducing and tracing the BBR uncertainty of room-temperature trapped-ion optical clocks.

Abstract: The blackbody-radiation (BBR) shift remains a leading systematic uncertainty of room-temperature single-ion optical clocks. Accurate evaluation of this shift requires the effective temperature at the ion position, which is difficult because RF heating, low and spatially varying surface emissivity and inaccessible trap surfaces make this quantity difficult to measure directly. Here, we developed a hybrid inversion framework that combines radiance-domain infrared thermography, black-painted high-emissivity reference regions, a finite-element model of a 171Yb+ end-cap ion trap and a reduced-order surrogate for repeated forward calculations and parameter identification. The surrogate reproduced the FE test fields with a full-field mean absolute error (MAE) of 0.02220 mK. The calibrated model predicted the ion-position effective temperature with a combined standard uncertainty of 155.0 mK, corresponding to a temperature-related BBR frequency-shift uncertainty of 1.647 x 10^-19 for the 2S1/2-2F7/2 (467 nm) E3 transition. This method provides a reproducible route for reducing and tracing the BBR uncertainty of room-temperature trapped-ion optical clocks.

</details>


### [97] [Proposal for a multi-ion optical clock with hundreds of ions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15609)
*Pavel Kamenskikh,Vasiliy Smirnov,Ilya Gerasin,Ilya Semerikov,Wei Zhang*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a circular surface-trap architecture for a multi-ion optical clock with hundreds of ions, using rotational symmetry to suppress inhomogeneous quadrupole shifts and enabling 10^-18 fractional uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling trapped-ion optical clocks to hundreds of ions is hindered by inhomogeneous frequency shifts in large ion crystals, especially the quadrupole shift.

Method: A circular surface-trap architecture hosting a ring crystal of 400 40Ca+ ions is proposed; its rotational symmetry suppresses quadrupole shift inhomogeneity, complemented by stray-field compensation and dynamical decoupling techniques.

Result: The circular configuration suppresses the quadrupole shift by two orders of magnitude compared to a linear trap, yielding fractional frequency uncertainty at the 10^-18 level and an expected 20-fold improvement in frequency stability over single-ion clocks.

Conclusion: The circular surface-trap architecture provides a viable pathway toward multi-ion optical clocks with hundreds of ions, achieving high accuracy and substantially improved stability.

Abstract: Scaling trapped-ion optical clocks to hundreds of ions remains a major challenge due to inhomogeneous frequency shifts in large ion crystals. Here, we propose a circular surface-trap architecture for a multi-ion optical clock with hundreds of ions. The rotational symmetry of the ion crystal suppresses the main sources of inhomogeneity, quadrupole shift.We investigate the feasibility of a clock based on a ring crystal of 400 $^{40}\mathrm{Ca}^{+}$ ions and propose a suitable experimental setup configuration. We have shown that circular configuration of ions leads to suppression by two orders of magnitude of the quadrupole shift comparing to the linear trap. Combined with stray-field compensation and dynamical decoupling techniques, this architecture provides a pathway toward multi-ion optical clocks with fractional frequency uncertainty at the $10^{-18}$ level and an expected 20-fold improvement in frequency stability compared to single ion clocks.

</details>


### [98] [Quantum tomography of bright squeezed vacuum via strong-field ionization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16077)
*Peizeng Li,Xiaoxiao Long,Haodong Liu,Xi Li,Yongkai Deng,Chuan Cheng,Yunquan Liu*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This work performs strong-field tunneling ionization of xenon driven by a phase-referenced combination of coherent and squeezed vacuum fields, showing that quadrature fluctuations of the squeezed field modulate tunneling barriers and electron wave packets, imprinting signatures on photoelectron momentum, number statistics, and energy spectra. Coincidence measurements enable reconstruction of the driving field's Wigner function, establishing a bidirectional interface between quantum optics and nonperturbative strong-field electron dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bright squeezed vacuum has recently entered the ultrafast strong-field regime, offering prospects for high-harmonic generation and photoionization, but linking its phase-space (quadrature) structure to subcycle electron dynamics has remained challenging. The paper aims to bridge this gap by directly connecting the quantum state of light to attosecond-scale tunneling ionization dynamics.

Method: Strong-field tunneling ionization of xenon is driven by a phase-referenced combination of a coherent field (defining the ionization clock) and a zero-mean squeezed field (providing quadrature control). Coincidence measurements of photon number and phase-sensitive photoelectron momentum distributions are used to perform quantum state tomography, reconstructing the Wigner function of the driving field.

Result: The squeezed field selectively modulates atomic tunneling barriers and electron wave packets, imprinting signatures on photoelectron momentum distributions, number statistics, and energy spectra. Phase-sensitive photoelectron momentum distributions, combined with photon-number coincidence detection, allow reconstruction of the driving field's Wigner function via quantum state tomography.

Conclusion: The results establish a bidirectional interface between quantum optics and nonperturbative strong-field electron dynamics: the driving quantum field shapes subcycle electron motion, while photoionization serves as a phase-sensitive probe of the field's quantum state. This opens routes toward controlling electron dynamics with quantum light and phase-sensitive quantum state metrology via ultrafast strong-field electron dynamics.

Abstract: Strong-field physics uses intense optical fields to probe and control electron motion on ultrafast timescales. Recent advances in bright squeezed vacuum have brought macroscopic quantum states of light into the ultrafast strong-field regime, opening prospects for high-harmonic generation and photoionization. Yet linking its phase-space structure to subcycle electron dynamics remains challenging. Here, we demonstrate strong-field tunneling ionization of xenon using a phase-referenced combination of coherent and squeezed fields, with the coherent field defining the ionization clock and quadrature fluctuations of the zero-mean squeezed field providing control. The squeezed field selectively modulates atomic tunneling barriers and electron wave packets, imprinting signatures on photoelectron momentum distributions, number statistics, and energy spectra. Using coincidence measurements of photon number and phase-sensitive photoelectron momentum distributions, we perform quantum state tomography of the driving field by reconstructing its Wigner function. These results establish a bidirectional interface between quantum optics and nonperturbative strong-field electron dynamics, in which the driving quantum field shapes subcycle electron motion while photoionization provides a phase-sensitive probe of the field's quantum state. This work opens new routes toward controlling electron dynamics with quantum light and phase-sensitive quantum state metrology through ultrafast strong-field electron dynamics.

</details>


### [99] [Kinematic saturation of the electromagnetically-induced-transparency peak in thermal atomic vapors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16418)
*Sheng-Xian Xiao,Jing-Yi Mao,Tao Wang*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: In thermal atomic vapors, the EIT transmission peak saturates: a threshold column density Ns is identified, above which the peak amplitude stays constant while its detuning shifts as sqrt(ln(N/Ns)); the optimum sensitivity point also differs from the peak maximum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The EIT readout in atomic vapor cells is widely used for precision measurements, yet the saturation behavior of the transmission peak and its implications for optimal operating conditions had not been clearly characterized.

Method: The authors investigated the amplitude of the velocity-selected double-resonance transmission peak, a purely kinematic effect arising from finite wavevector mismatch, and measured its dependence on atomic column density N to identify a threshold Ns.

Result: Below Ns, the peak maximum amplitude grows with column density; above Ns, the maximum amplitude remains constant and the corresponding detuning shifts away from resonance, scaling as the square root of ln(N/Ns). The optimum operating point for photon-shot-noise-limited sensitivity was found to deviate from that of the transmission peak maximum.

Conclusion: The findings provide guidance for optimizing EIT readout in vapor cells and for precision measurements built upon this scheme.

Abstract: We demonstrate that, in thermal atomic vapors, the electromagnetically-induced-transparency (EIT) transmission peak exhibits saturation. Investigating the amplitude of the velocity-selected double-resonance transmission peak-a purely kinematic effect arising from finite wavevector mismatch-we identify a threshold atomic column density Ns. For column densities N below Ns, the amplitude of the transmission peak maximum increases with column density. Once it exceeds Ns, the peak amplitude maximum remains constant, while the corresponding detuning shifts away from resonance and scales as the square root of ln(N over Ns). Furthermore, We find that the operating point for optimum photon-shot-noise-limited sensitivity deviates from that of the transmission peak maximum. Our study provides guidance for optimizing EIT readout in vapor cell and for precision measurements built upon this scheme.

</details>


### [100] [Cavity-modified static polarizabilities of atoms and small molecules within QED full configuration interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16806)
*T. Zalialiutdinov,D. Solovyev*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: QED-FCI calculations show that cavity confinement modifies static dipole polarizabilities through competition between dipole self-energy and photon exchange, suppressing molecular ground-state response but strongly enhancing the differential response of the helium clock transition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify how a single-mode optical cavity alters the static dipole polarizability of H2, LiH, and helium, and to provide reference data for cavity-modified response properties relevant to frequency standards.

Method: Quantum-electrodynamical full configuration interaction (QED-FCI); static dipole polarizabilities obtained as finite-field derivatives of the coupled electron-photon energy.

Result: Cavity-induced modification arises from competition between two opposite-sign contributions—dipole self-energy and photon exchange—whose relative strength depends on cavity frequency and photon number. The residual change suppresses molecular ground-state response, whereas for helium metastable states the balance changes qualitatively and the differential response of the 2^3S1 -> 2^1S0 clock transition is strongly enhanced.

Conclusion: Cavity confinement can either reduce or increase dc Stark and blackbody-radiation shifts of a frequency standard depending on the electronic structure of the states involved; exact treatment of light-matter correlation is needed for cavity-QED predictions.

Abstract: Static dipole polarizabilities of H$_2$, LiH, and helium confined in a single-mode optical cavity are computed using quantum-electrodynamical full configuration interaction (QED-FCI). Response properties are obtained as finite-field derivatives of the coupled electron-photon energy. The cavity-induced modification is found to arise from the competition of two contributions of opposite sign, the dipole self-energy and photon exchange, whose relative strength depends on both the cavity frequency and the number of photons involved in the exchange. The residual change suppresses the response of the molecular ground electronic states. For the metastable states of helium atom this balance changes qualitatively, and the differential response of the $2\,^3S_1 \to 2\,^1S_0$ clock transition, which governs its sensitivity to static electric fields and to thermal radiation, is strongly enhanced instead. Cavity confinement can therefore either reduce or increase the dc Stark and blackbody-radiation shifts of a frequency standard, depending on the electronic structure of the states involved. The reported data serve as a reference for cavity-modified response and underscore the need for an exact treatment of light-matter correlation in cavity-QED predictions.

</details>


### [101] [An Aubry-André localized optical lattice clock](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17499)
*Shuo Ma,Anke Stölzel,Bennett Christensen,Zhuoli Ding,Dhruva Ganapathy,Andrew Householder,Genyue Liu,Shimon Kolkowitz*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The authors demonstrate Aubry-André localization in a bichromatic optical lattice formed by two magic-wavelength lasers, which suppresses tunneling in a horizontal lattice clock emulating free fall. This recovers a Fourier-limited Rabi spectrum and yields Ramsey coherence times up to 2 s, a 100-fold improvement over the delocalized regime, offering a practical route to tunneling control in space-based and terrestrial optical lattice clocks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optical lattice clocks in space could enhance satellite navigation and enable fundamental physics tests such as gravitational-wave detection and relativity tests. However, in microgravity, atoms can tunnel to degenerate neighboring lattice sites, which broadens the clock transition and limits coherence times.

Method: The authors realize Aubry-André localization in a bichromatic optical lattice created by two magic-wavelength lasers, suppressing tunneling in a horizontal lattice clock that emulates free fall. They characterize the clock via Rabi spectroscopy and Ramsey coherence measurements.

Result: The team recovers a Fourier-limited clock Rabi spectrum and measures Ramsey coherence times of up to 2 s in shallow lattices, achieving up to a 100-fold improvement over the delocalized regime.

Conclusion: Aubry-André localization establishes a practical route to controlling tunneling in future space-based clocks and opens a path to operating terrestrial clocks at shallower lattice depths.

Abstract: Optical lattice clocks in space could improve satellite navigation systems and enable new tests of fundamental physics, including gravitational-wave detection and tests of relativity. In microgravity, atoms can tunnel to degenerate neighboring lattice sites, broadening the clock transition and limiting coherence times. We demonstrate Aubry-André localization in a bichromatic optical lattice formed by two magic-wavelength lasers, suppressing tunneling in a horizontal lattice clock emulating free fall. We recover a Fourier-limited clock Rabi spectrum and measure Ramsey coherence times of up to 2 s in shallow lattices, with up to 100-fold improvement over the delocalized regime. These results establish Aubry-André localization as a practical route to controlling tunneling in future space-based clocks, and open a path to operating terrestrial clocks at shallower lattice depths.

</details>


### [102] [Collision inside an atom: quantum ion-atom dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18345)
*Arijit Das,Yufeng Wang,Marcin Welter,Karl Horn,Dawid Dąbrowski,Michał Przybytek,Julia Narevicius,Michał Tomza,Edvardas Narevicius*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: Merging helium Rydberg and ground-state beams, cold collisions inside a Rydberg helium atom are resolved fully quantum-coherently, revealing matter-wave diffraction, tunnelling shape resonances, Mott interference, breakdown of the Born-Oppenheimer approximation, and a direct measurement of the helium isotope ionization-energy difference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rydberg atoms have been proposed as a neutral platform to observe quantum effects in collisions of the ionic core with neutral particles, a long-standing goal of ion-based chemistry and physics. However, all prior experimental results could be explained classically by internuclear motion, leaving genuine quantum collision dynamics unobserved.

Method: The authors merged beams of helium Rydberg atoms and ground-state helium and imaged individual scattering events, enabling cold collisions to be studied inside a single Rydberg helium atom. Collisions between identical helium nuclei and between distinct isotopes were examined to probe quantum statistics, molecular-frame effects, and benchmark parameter-free state-of-the-art theory.

Result: Fully quantum-coherent internuclear motion was resolved, with matter-wave diffraction and tunnelling-driven shape resonances observed down to collision energies of 90 mK. For identical helium nuclei, a new regime of identical-particle Mott interference was demonstrated in which quantum statistics govern the total scattering cross section. For distinct isotopes, breakdown of the Born-Oppenheimer approximation was shown and state-of-the-art parameter-free theory was benchmarked. The helium isotope ionization-energy difference was directly measured as 15.0 +/- 0.1 K.

Conclusion: The work establishes Rydberg atoms as a platform for direct observation of quantum dynamics involving a general class of ions, atoms and molecules, extending ion-based collision studies into the fully quantum-coherent regime.

Abstract: Rydberg atoms occupy a central role in quantum science; their loosely bound outer electron gives rise to properties that enable quantum information processing1 or the formation of exotic molecular bonds2. Beyond these roles, they have been proposed as a neutral platform for the observation of quantum effects in collisions and reactions of the ionic core with neutral particles3, 4, a long-standing goal of ion-based chemistry and physics. However, all of the experimental results so far could be explained using a classical description of the internuclear motion5. Here we demonstrate cold collisions inside a Rydberg helium atom, achieved by merging helium Rydberg and ground-state helium beams and imaging individual scattering events. We resolve the fully quantum-coherent motion and observe matter-wave diffraction and tunnelling-driven shape resonances down to collision energies of 90 mK. In the case of identical helium nuclei, we demonstrate a new regime of identical-particle Mott interference6, where quantum statistics begin to govern the total scattering cross section. For distinct isotopes, we show the breakdown of the Born-Oppenheimer approximation and benchmark parameter-free, state-of-the-art theory. We directly measure the helium isotope ionization-energy difference to be 15.0 +/- 0.1 K. Our experiments establish Rydberg atoms as a platform for the direct observation of quantum dynamics with a general class of ions, atoms and molecules.

</details>


### [103] [Theory of Sub-Doppler Spectroscopy of Alkali Metal Vapors in the Field of Counterpropagating Bichromatic Laser Beams: Probe Beam Regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18372)
*A. M. Mikhailov,D. V. Brazhnikov*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This paper develops, via the density matrix formalism and irreducible tensor operator algebra, an analytical expression for the probe-beam absorption coefficient in sub-Doppler resonances in miniature alkali-metal cells under counterpropagating bichromatic laser fields, valid for arbitrary polarization and dipole-transition angular momenta.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sub-Doppler resonances in miniature Rb/Cs cells under counterpropagating bichromatic beams are promising for miniature optical quantum frequency standards, but no theory fully accounting for the real atomic energy-level structure and yielding explicit analytical absorption-coefficient expressions existed. Existing simplified three-level models cannot correctly treat simultaneous nonlinear optical effects.

Method: Density matrix formalism combined with the algebra of irreducible tensor operators. Calculations are performed for arbitrary laser polarization parameters, arbitrary angular momenta of optical dipole transitions, and various relaxation processes, in a pump-probe regime.

Result: An analytical expression for the probe-beam absorption coefficient in the presence of a more intense counterpropagating beam, correctly capturing the simultaneous action of multiple nonlinear optical effects and enabling analysis of each effect's partial contribution to the observed resonance. A prediction is made of increased sub-Doppler resonance amplitude for counterpropagating beams with opposite circular polarizations relative to the commonly used orthogonal linear polarizations.

Conclusion: The theory overcomes limitations of the simplified three-level model, provides explicit analytical absorption-coefficient expressions accounting for real atomic level structure, and predicts an enhanced sub-Doppler resonance amplitude for opposite-circular-polarization counterpropagating beams versus orthogonal-linear-polarization configurations.

Abstract: Sub-Doppler resonances observed in miniature cells ($V \ll 1 cm^3$) with Rb or Cs atoms in the field of counterpropagating bichromatic laser beams are of interest for the creation of a miniature quantum frequency standard (QFS) of the optical range. However, a theory of these resonances that takes into full account the real structure of energy levels in an atom has not yet been constructed, which would make it possible to obtain explicit analytical expressions for the absorption coefficient of the medium. This paper addresses this gap using the density matrix formalism and the algebra of irreducible tensor operators. Namely, we obtained an analytical expression for the absorption coefficient of the probe beam in the presence of a more intense counterpropagating beam (pump-probe regime). Moreover, the calculations were carried out for arbitrary parameters of laser beam polarization, arbitrary angular momenta of optical dipole transitions in the atom and various relaxation processes. The constructed theory allows us to correctly take into account the simultaneous action of various nonlinear optical effects, in contrast to the simplified three-level model of the atom, and also to investigate the partial contribution of each of them to the overall observed resonant circuit. The analysis performed, in particular, made it possible to predict an increase in the amplitude of the sub-Doppler resonance when using counterpropagating light beams with opposite circular polarizations in relation to the configuration of counterpropagating beams with orthogonal linear polarizations, which is often used in practice.

</details>


### [104] [Information-theoretic measures of electron correlation in a double quantum dot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18796)
*W. S. Nascimento,A. M. Maniero,F. V. Prudente,Ginette Jalbert*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The study examines how spatial confinement controls electron correlation between two electrons in a symmetric double quantum dot, using mutual information to map correlated vs. uncorrelated regimes and linking mutual information to relative correlation energy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how spatial confinement governs electron correlation in coupled quantum dots is important for quantum dot design and for choosing appropriate computational methods. A clearer, entropy-based diagnostic of correlation could simplify the assessment of when correlations matter.

Method: Two electrons in a symmetric double quantum dot are modeled under varying confinement. In the strong-confinement regime, the ground-state energy is computed with the Hartree-Fock method while neglecting the electron-electron repulsion term; under weaker confinement, Full Configuration Interaction (FCI) is used for a more accurate description. Mutual information quantifies the correlation, and Shannon entropy is tested as a qualitative indicator of correlation.

Result: Confinement regions are identified where electronic motion is essentially uncorrelated. Mutual information is found to be approximately linearly related to the relative correlation energy, and Shannon entropy appears to serve as a qualitative guide to electron correlation.

Conclusion: Spatial confinement strongly controls the degree of electron correlation in a symmetric double quantum dot. Mutual information provides a quantitative correlation measure with an approximately linear connection to relative correlation energy, and Shannon entropy is conjectured to offer a useful qualitative indicator of correlation.

Abstract: In this work, we investigated how variations in spatial confinement affect the electron correlation between two electrons in a symmetric double quantum dot. In strong confinement regime, the ground-state energy can be obtained using the Hartree-Fock method neglecting the electron-electron repulsion term. Under a weaker confinement, the FCI method provides a more accurate description. Using the mutual information as a measure of correlation, we determine the confinement regions where electronic motion is uncorrelated. We conjecture that the Shannon entropy can serve as a qualitative guide to to study the electron correlation. We identified an approximately linear relationship between mutual information and relative correlation energy.

</details>

### [105] [SIREN: A nonlinear Rankine-source boundary element solver for the stationary motion of a marine vehicle in calm water](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14027)
*Daniele Peri*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: SIREN is a Rankine-source panel solver for steady ship wave resistance in the Dawson tradition, using an iterated linearization about a base flow solved as a Neumann problem on the actual hull and free surface, rather than the classical double-body flow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The classical double-body flow relies on an image about z=0, which is only valid for a flat surface and a hull cut at z=0, and requires desingularised free-surface panels. SIREN aims to remove these limitations by reformulating the base flow on the actual, moving boundary.

Method: The free-surface condition is linearised about a base flow and iterated. The base flow is a Neumann problem on the actual boundary: sources on all hull panels (wet and dry) and free-surface panels, with impermeability on both so the free surface acts as a rigid lid. In the nonlinear iteration, free-surface panels move vertically to the relaxed wave elevation at their centroid, hull panels are marked wet/dry against the moved surface, and the base flow is recomputed; impermeability applies to the whole hull, with marking only selecting panels for the force integral. Under-relaxation is confined to passes where the nonlinear correction accumulates without reset. The radiation condition uses a one-row upstream shift of collocation points.

Result: The wave resistance is obtained by pressure integration and, independently, by transverse wave-cut analysis.

Conclusion: SIREN provides a Rankine-source panel formulation that avoids the double-body assumption and desingularised free-surface panels, iterating a linearised free-surface condition on a rigid-lid base flow over the actual geometry, with wave resistance verified through two independent methods.

Abstract: SIREN (Ship Inviscid Resistance and Elevation eNgine) is a Rankine-source panel solver for the steady wave resistance of a ship, in the tradition of Dawson's method. The free-surface condition is linearised about a base flow, and the linearisation is iterated. In the present formulation the base flow is the solution of a Neumann problem on the actual boundary: sources on the entire hull, wet and dry, and on the free surface panels, with impermeability imposed on both, so that the free surface acts as a rigid lid at the position it currently occupies. This replaces the classical double-body flow, whose image about z=0 is meaningful only for a flat surface and a hull cut at z=0, and it removes the need for desingularised free-surface panels. In the non-linear iteration the free-surface panels are moved rigidly in the vertical to the relaxed wave elevation at their centroid, the hull panels are marked wet or dry against the moved surface, and the base flow is recomputed on the moved geometry; impermeability applies to the whole hull regardless of this marking, which is used only to select which panels enter the force integral, and under-relaxation is confined to the passes where the non-linear correction accumulates without being reset. The radiation condition is imposed by a one-row upstream shift of the collocation points, and the wave resistance is obtained by pressure integration and, independently, by transverse wave-cut analysis.

</details>


### [106] [A Gauge-Inspired Coupled Fokker--Planck System for Multistate Stochastic Dynamics: Formalism and 1-D Numerical Implementation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14052)
*Francesco Pio De Cosmo,Gianluca Di Natale,Leandro Cieri*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents the first numerical solver for an SU(2) gauge-coupled Fokker–Planck system in (1+1) dimensions, using operator splitting with a non-oscillatory advection scheme to guarantee probability conservation, non-negativity, and stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While extending the Fokker–Planck equation to coupled systems via non-Abelian symmetry groups provides a powerful theoretical framework for coupled multi-state stochastic systems, translating this abstract gauge-field formulation into viable numerical predictions remained an unaddressed challenge. The work is motivated by prior research on ice crystal habit evolution.

Method: An operator-splitting scheme combined with a non-oscillatory advection solver is applied to an SU(2) gauge-coupled Fokker–Planck system in (1+1) dimensions, validated across linear and quadratic coupling regimes up to strong interactions (g <= 1.0).

Result: The algorithm strictly ensures probability conservation, non-negativity, and numerical stability. Non-Abelian gauge coupling acts as a cross-reactive feedback loop that alters system dynamics, driving continuous probability exchange between states, suppressing localised density peaks, and forcing long-time spatial delocalisation.

Conclusion: The work establishes foundational computational infrastructure for integrating complex multi-state physics, offering a step toward improved ice cloud representation in atmospheric and climate models, while the underlying physical framework and computational engine remain general and multidisciplinary.

Abstract: Extending the Fokker--Planck equation to a coupled system of equations through non-Abelian symmetry groups offers a powerful theoretical framework for coupled multi-state stochastic systems. However, translating this abstract gauge field formulation into viable numerical predictions remains an unaddressed challenge. Motivated by our previous work on ice crystal habit evolution, in this work we bridge this gap by presenting the first numerical solver for an SU(2) gauge-coupled Fokker--Planck system in (1+1) dimensions. Using an operator-splitting scheme combined with a non-oscillatory advection solver, our algorithm strictly ensures probability conservation, non-negativity, and numerical stability. We demonstrate that non-Abelian gauge coupling acts as a cross-reactive feedback loop that fundamentally alters system dynamics, driving continuous probability exchange between states, suppressing localised density peaks, and forcing long-time spatial delocalisation. Validated across linear and quadratic coupling regimes up to strong interactions (g <= 1.0), this work establishes the foundational computational infrastructure needed to integrate complex multi-state physics. Overall, while our approach provides a key step toward improving ice cloud representation in atmospheric and climate models, the underlying physical framework and computational engine remain fundamentally general and multidisciplinary, offering a versatile paradigm for any domain requiring coupled multi-state stochastic equations.

</details>


### [107] [acousticHelmholtzFoam: an MPI-parallel unstructured finite-volume solver for frequency-domain acoustics in heterogeneous two-phase media with perfectly matched layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14057)
*Chuanchao Xu,Jun Liu,Dieter Bothe,Tomislav Maric*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: acousticHelmholtzFoam is an OpenFOAM application for time-harmonic acoustic pressure in heterogeneous gas-liquid media on unstructured polyhedral meshes, documented with verification and scaling studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper documents an implementation for computing time-harmonic acoustic pressure fields in heterogeneous gas-liquid media, providing a validated open-source workflow for the community.

Method: Complex pressure stored as real/imaginary volume fields; material coefficients built from VOF phase fraction with geometric face-fraction averaging; open boundaries truncated by Cartesian PMLs generated by setPMLFields; two coupled real equations discretized via cell-centred finite-volume operators, assembled into a distributed PETSc matrix, solved by default with MUMPS; outputs amplitude, complex velocity, radiation pressure, and time-averaged momentum flux.

Result: On an 8000-cell layered gas-liquid-PML case with analytical solution, whole-domain relative complex-pressure error is 1.197e-3; serial and MPI (up to 8 ranks) solutions agree within 1e-12 relative L2 norm. A single-axis levitator height sweep with constant-volume spheroidal drops demonstrates two-phase input and force output; strong and problem-size scaling characterize the direct-solver workflow.

Conclusion: The paper documents equations, case interface, assembly/solution algorithm, build procedure, and test infrastructure; the numerical formulation and verification appear in a companion paper (arXiv:2610.01593).

Abstract: acousticHelmholtzFoam is an OpenFOAM application that computes time-harmonic acoustic pressure fields in heterogeneous gas-liquid media on unstructured polyhedral meshes. The complex pressure is stored as real and imaginary volume fields, the material coefficients are constructed from a volume-of-fluid phase fraction with geometric face-fraction averaging, and open boundaries are truncated by Cartesian perfectly matched layers (PMLs) whose damping tensor is generated by the companion utility setPMLFields. The two coupled real-valued equations are discretized with the cell-centred finite-volume operators of OpenFOAM and assembled explicitly, including processor couplings and boundary contributions, into a distributed PETSc matrix that is solved by default with the MUMPS sparse direct solver. The solver writes pressure amplitude, complex velocity, acoustic radiation pressure and time-averaged momentum flux for acoustic-force evaluation. This paper documents the implemented equations, the case interface, the assembly and solution algorithm, the build procedure and the test infrastructure. A layered gas-liquid-PML case with an analytical solution serves as an end-to-end regression: on 8000 cells the whole-domain relative complex-pressure error is $1.197\times10^{-3}$, and serial and MPI solutions on up to eight ranks agree to within $10^{-12}$ in relative $L_2$ norm. A single-axis acoustic-levitator height sweep with constant-volume spheroidal drops demonstrates the two-phase material input and the force output, and strong- and problem-size-scaling measurements characterize the direct-solver workflow. The numerical formulation and its verification are presented in a companion paper (arXiv:2610.01593).

</details>


### [108] [FDTD Model of Noise Pollution from an AI Data Center](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14899)
*Gavin Buxton*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: An FDTD acoustic model shows that terrain topography near an AI data center strongly shapes low-frequency noise pollution, with certain elevated areas being highly exposed while distant regions are shielded.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid expansion of AI data centers raises environmental concerns, particularly low-frequency noise pollution that affects nearby residents and may cause significant health impacts. Understanding how topography influences the distribution of this noise is needed to guide mitigation.

Method: A finite-difference time-domain (FDTD) model was used to simulate sound propagation over the topography near an AI data center.

Result: Topography plays a major role in noise levels. Areas of sudden elevation on either side of the river near the AI data center are more susceptible to high noise levels, whereas areas further away are largely protected by the terrain.

Conclusion: Capturing how topography shapes noise pollution in specific terrains helps identify locations where noise mitigation strategies would be most effective.

Abstract: The sudden growth in AI data centers is raising numerous environmental concerns. Low-frequency noise pollution is a concern for local residents, that may not just be an annoyance but also carry significant health impacts. An FDTD model of sound propagation over the topography near an AI data center is presented. The topography plays a large role in the level of noise pollution, with areas of sudden elevation on either side of the river near the AI data center more susceptible to high noise levels, while areas further afield largely protected from noise pollution by the topography. Understanding the role that topography plays in noise pollution, and capturing the noise pollution in specific topographies, can help identify areas where noise mitigation strategies might be best employed.

</details>


### [109] [A quantum algorithm for meshless lattice Boltzmann method with unitary interpolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14943)
*Chi Zhang,Hefeng Wang,Hua Xiang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a mass-weighted meshless formulation for the shifted incompressible lattice Boltzmann method whose streaming step is rendered exactly unitary, enabling efficient block encoding via Carleman embedding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In traditional meshless LBM, the semi-Lagrangian streaming step on scattered nodes is an interpolation and thus non-unitary; block encoding such a matrix requires normalization proportional to its sparsity and logarithmically many ancillas, which is costly. The work aims to overcome this non-unitarity to reduce block-encoding cost.

Method: The interpolation is expressed as the exponential exp(a Dx + b Dy) of mimetic derivative operators. Each node is endowed with its Voronoi mass, and a mass-weighted similarity transform makes the derivative operators skew-Hermitian by enforcing moment constraints plus low-wave-number trigonometric differentiation conditions, yielding an exactly unitary interpolation. The local collision step is unchanged except for a rescaling of its quadratic term, with the same weights defining the amplitude encoding of distribution functions.

Result: For quasi-uniform node sets, the mass-weighting diagonal stays within a unit-order normalization that degrades only mildly as the Carleman truncation order grows, mitigating block-encoding cost. Numerical experiments on the Taylor-Green vortex confirm reproduction of the full meshless LBM, with velocity error decreasing as the Carleman order increases.

Conclusion: Mass-weighting the meshless LBM with a similarity transform unitarizes the streaming step while preserving the collision step, and this unitarization keeps block encoding well-conditioned, as validated by Taylor-Green vortex simulations.

Abstract: We present a mass-weighted meshless formulation for the shifted incompressible lattice Boltzmann method (LBM) whose streaming step is exactly unitary, combining with the Carleman embedding. In traditional meshless LBM, on a scattered node set the semi-Lagrangian streaming step is an interpolation, which is not unitary; a generic block encoding of this matrix therefore requires a normalization proportional to its sparsity and logarithmically many ancillas. To overcome the non-unitarity, we instead express the interpolation as the exponential exp(a Dx + b Dy) of mimetic derivative operators. Endowing each node with its Voronoi mass and applying a mass-weighted similarity transform renders these derivative operators skew-Hermitian by enforcing moment constraints together with low-wave number trigonometric differentiation conditions, so that the interpolation becomes exactly unitary. Such unitarization of interpolation leave the local collision step unchanged up to a rescaling of its quadratic term, where the same weights define the amplitude encoding of the distribution functions. For quasi-uniform node sets, the mass-weighting diagonal remains within a unit-order normalization that degrades only mildly as the Carleman truncation order grows, thereby mitigating the cost of block encoding. Numerical experiments on the Taylor Green vortex confirm that the method reproduces the full meshless LBM, with the velocity error decreasing as the Carleman order increases.

</details>


### [110] [Self-Adaptive Model-Order Reduction for Shock-Dominated Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15944)
*Ali Mohaghegh,Cheng Huang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: This work proposes a self-adaptive model-order reduction (MOR) framework that dynamically selects between adaptive and static reduced-order model (ROM) formulations on the fly based on evolving solution characteristics, aiming to balance computational efficiency and predictive accuracy for convection-dominated problems such as rotating detonation engines (RDEs).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional static ROMs using offline/online strategies have limited predictive capability for convection-dominated problems with strongly evolving physics such as shocks.
