TL;DR: The paper shows that a variant of the topological confinement transition found in bipartite dimer-loop models can also occur on non-bipartite lattices (kagome, triangular), where the two phases are short-range correlated liquids distinguished by a $Z_2$-valued winding number rather than a conserved $U(1)$ flux, yet the transition belongs to the same Ising universality class.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work established that fully-packed dimer-loop models on bipartite lattices (2D and 3D) host two power-law correlated Coulomb phases separated by a confinement transition of the $Z_2$ part of a $U(1)$ flux, with a low-energy description via a divergence-free polarization field. It was unclear whether an analogous topological confinement transition could arise in dimer-loop models on non-bipartite lattices, where no conserved $U(1)$ flux exists. This paper addresses that question.

Method: The authors construct and study variants of fully-packed dimer-loop models on non-bipartite lattices, specifically the kagome and triangular lattices. They analyze the low-energy description of these models, identifying the relevant topological invariant (a $Z_2$ winding number) and characterizing the two phases and the transition between them. They also connect these models to the low-energy limit of $S=1$ antiferromagnets with competing anisotropies on the ruby lattice, which comprises corner-sharing fully-frustrated squares and a lattice of corner-sharing fully-frustrated hexagons.

Result: In non-bipartite dimer-loop models, both phases are short-range correlated liquids, and the topological distinction between them is encoded in a $Z_2$-valued winding number instead of the flux of a divergence-free polarization field. Despite the absence of a conserved $U(1)$ flux and this key difference, the transition appears to lie in the same Ising universality class as the analogous transition in bipartite dimer-loop models.

Conclusion: A variant of the topological confinement transition can exist on non-bipartite lattices such as kagome and triangular lattices, with a $Z_2$ winding number replacing the $U(1)$ flux as the topological order parameter. The transition retains Ising universality. These kagome and triangular dimer-loop models correspond to the low-energy limit of a class of $S=1$ antiferromagnets with competing anisotropies on the ruby lattice.

Abstract: Recent work [Kundu, Damle, Phys. Rev. X 15, 011018 (2025)] has demonstrated that a class of $S=1$ kagome lattice antiferromagnets with competing anisotropies can exhibit unusual low-temperature behavior that is best described in terms of the statistical mechanics of an interesting interpolation between the fully-packed dimer model and the fully-packed loop model on the bipartite honeycomb lattice. On this and other bipartite lattices in two and three dimension, such fully-packed dimer-loop models generically have two distinct power-law correlated Coulomb phases, both of which admit a low-energy description in terms of a fluctuating divergence-free polarization vector field [Phys. Rev. X 15, 011018 (2025); arXiv:2510.11607]. The transition between these phases corresponds to a confinement transition for the $Z_2$ part of the corresponding $U(1)$ flux [Phys. Rev. X 15, 011018 (2025); arXiv:2510.11607]. Here we demonstrate that a variant of such a topological confinement transition can also exist in dimer-loop models on non-bipartite lattices such as the kagome and triangular lattices although the low-energy description does not feature any conserved $U(1)$ flux. In these non-bipartite dimer-loop models, both phases are short-range correlated liquids, and the topological distinction between them is in terms of a $Z_2$-valued winding number instead of the flux of a divergence-free polarization field. In spite of this key difference, this transition appears to be in the same Ising universality class as the analogous transition of the bipartite dimer-loop models. These kagome and triangular lattice dimer-loop models correspond respectively to the low-energy limit of a class of $S=1$ antiferromagnets with competing anisotropies on the ruby lattice comprising a corner-sharing arrangement of fully-frustrated squares and a lattice of corner-sharing fully-frustrated hexagons.

</details>


### [136] [Destructive Interference of a Charge Density Wave in the Skyrmion Magnet GdRu2Si2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16682)
*G. D. A. Wood,D. A. Mayoh,F. Orlandi,A. Bombardi,D. D. Khalyavin,P. Manuel,S. H. Moody,P. D. Hatton,R. D. Johnson,T. W. Chamberlain,O. A. Petrenko,M. R. Lees,G. Balakrishnan*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Temperature-dependent spin-charge coupling in GdRu2Si2 is measured via resonant X-ray scattering and neutron diffraction; a minimal Landau model with one critical exponent and two ordering temperatures reproduces all six observed order parameters, showing that spin-density-wave components drive charge ordering while a magnetic helix suppresses it through destructive interference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In skyrmion magnets, charge density waves are thought to induce higher-order exchange interactions capable of selecting multi-Q ground states. Clarifying the microscopic coupling between spin and charge ordering, and how it controls the multi-Q state selection, is the driving question.

Method: Resonant X-ray scattering and neutron diffraction are used to measure the temperature-dependent spin-charge coupling in GdRu2Si2. A minimal Landau model with one critical exponent and two ordering temperatures is constructed to describe the coupled order parameters.

Result: The minimal Landau model reproduces all six observed order parameters. Spin-density-wave components of the multi-Q ground state drive the charge ordering, whereas the onset of a magnetic helix suppresses the charge order via destructive interference.

Conclusion: Spin-charge coupling in GdRu2Si2 is governed by a simple Landau description with only one critical exponent and two ordering temperatures, and the competition between spin-density-wave and helical components determines whether charge ordering survives, providing a framework for multi-Q ground-state selection in skyrmion magnets.

Abstract: In skyrmion magnets, charge density waves induce higher-order exchange interactions that can select multi-Q ground states. Using resonant X-ray scattering and neutron diffraction, we measure temperature-dependent spin-charge coupling in GdRu2Si2. A minimal Landau model with one critical exponent and two ordering temperatures reproduces all six observed order parameters. Spin-density wave components of the multi-Q ground state drive charge ordering, but the onset of a magnetic helix suppresses it via destructive interference.

</details>


### [137] [Quantum Ising transition on the Cayley tree: boundary criticality, universality, and an effective Bethe line](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16774)
*Pietro Richelli,Natalia Chepiga*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The extensive boundary of finite Cayley trees drives a continuous quantum phase transition at h_x^c ≈ 1.76, distinct from the mean-field Bethe-lattice transition at h_x^c ≈ 2.23; fine-tuning a local boundary field recovers the boundary-free Bethe-lattice physics along an "effective Bethe line."


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior understanding of the transverse-field Ising model on Bethe lattices relies on the boundary-free (infinite) limit, which yields a mean-field transition. However, finite Cayley trees possess an extensive boundary whose effect on quantum criticality was not clearly established, motivating a systematic study of whether and how this boundary alters the transition.

Method: Tree tensor-network simulations of the transverse-field Ising model on finite Cayley trees with up to N = 12286 sites. Finite-size scaling of magnetization and magnetic susceptibility is used to extract critical exponents. Next-nearest-neighbor interactions are introduced to test robustness of the exponents, and a local boundary field is fine-tuned to recover Bethe-lattice behavior.

Result: A boundary-driven continuous transition is identified at h_x^c ≈ 1.76, distinct from the mean-field Bethe-lattice value h_x^c ≈ 2.23. A consistent set of critical exponents is extracted and shown to be robust against next-nearest-neighbor interactions, numerically supporting an Ising boundary universality class. Fine-tuning a local boundary field yields an effective Bethe line that screens the extensive boundary and restores both the critical point and mean-field critical scaling of the infinite Bethe lattice.

Conclusion: The extensive boundary of finite Cayley trees qualitatively changes the quantum phase transition relative to the boundary-free Bethe lattice, defining a distinct boundary universality class. Nonetheless, the mean-field Bethe-lattice physics can be efficiently recovered on a finite Cayley tree by fine-tuning a local boundary field, demonstrating that the boundary effect is controllable.

Abstract: We investigate quantum criticality in the transverse-field Ising model on finite Cayley trees and demonstrate that their extensive boundary fundamentally changes the nature of the quantum phase transition compared with the infinite, boundary-free Bethe lattice. Using tree tensor-network simulations for systems with up to $N=12286$ sites, we identify a boundary-driven continuous transition at $h_x^c\approx1.76$, distinct from the mean-field Bethe-lattice transition at $h_x^c\approx2.23$. We characterize this transition through finite-size scaling of the magnetization and magnetic susceptibility and extract a consistent set of critical exponents. By introducing next-nearest-neighbor interactions, we demonstrate the robustness of the extracted critical exponents, providing numerical argument for the Ising boundary universality class on the Cayley tree. Furthermore, we show that the physics of the boundary-free Bethe lattice can be efficiently recovered on a finite Cayley tree by fine-tuning a local boundary field. The resulting \textit{effective Bethe line}, whose existence is tied to the mean-field nature of the bulk transition, effectively screens the extensive boundary and restores both the critical point and the mean-field critical scaling of the infinite Bethe lattice.

</details>


### [138] [Magnetic properties and excitation spectrum of the S = 1/2 three-legged spin ladder, LiMoOP2O7](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16856)
*Aly H Abdeldaim,Alexander A. Tsirlin,Jacques Ollivier,Clemens Ritter,Robin S. Perry,Lucy Clark,Gøran J. Nilsen*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Comprehensive experimental and DFT study of the S = 1/2 Mo5+ pyrophosphate LiMoOP2O7, establishing its three-legged-ladder structure, T_N = 3.5 K reduced-moment commensurate order, 1 T field-induced transition, and a microscopic spin Hamiltonian with dominant AFM leg, weak FM rung, and interlayer FM couplings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine the magnetic ground state and resolve the spin Hamiltonian of the S = 1/2 Mo5+ pyrophosphate LiMoOP2O7, a compound hosting a three-legged ladder geometry of distorted Mo5+ octahedra whose magnetic behavior was previously undetermined.

Method: Neutron powder diffraction to confirm the P2_1/n structural model; magnetometry and specific heat to locate the ordering transition and field-induced transition; inelastic neutron scattering combined with density functional theory (DFT) calculations to construct and fit the minimal microscopic spin model.

Result: The P2_1/n structure was confirmed, comprising a three-legged ladder of distorted Mo5+ octahedra. Long-range magnetic order occurs at T_N = 3.5 K, with a field-induced transition at μ0H_c = 1 T. The zero-field order is commensurate with κ = 0, and the ordered moment is reduced by 30% from the full S = 1/2 value. The spin Hamiltonian was resolved up to the seventh leading exchange parameter, featuring dominant antiferromagnetic leg interactions, weaker ferromagnetic rung interactions, and a significant interlayer ferromagnetic coupling.

Conclusion: LiMoOP2O7 realizes a three-legged-ladder magnet with reduced ordered moment and a dominant-antiferromagnetic-leg, weak-ferromagnetic-rung, interlayer-ferromagnetic exchange hierarchy, providing a well-characterized minimal microscopic model of its magnetic ground state and excitations.

Abstract: We report a comprehensive experimental study including density functional theory calculations detailing the magnetic ground state of the $S = 1/2$ Mo$^{5+}$ pyrophosphate, LiMoOP2O7, and its magnetic excitations. Neutron powder diffraction measurements confirm the $P2_1/n$ structural model which is comprised of a three-legged ladder of distorted Mo$^{5+}$ octahedra. Magnetometry and specific heat reveal a long-range magnetic ordering transition at $T_\textrm{N} = 3.5$~K and a field-induced transition at $μ_{0}$H$_\textrm{c}$ = 1 T. This zero-field long-range magnetic order was determined to be a commensurate $κ= 0$ order with an ordered moment that is reduced from the full $S = 1/2$ magnetic moment by 30%. Through inelastic neutron scattering measurements and density functional theory calculations, we define the minimal microscopic model and resolve the spin Hamiltonian up to the seventh leading exchange parameter, resulting in a model with dominant antiferromagnetic leg interactions, weaker ferromagnetic rung interactions, and a significant interlayer ferromagnetic coupling.

</details>


### [139] [Mutual information across the commensurate--incommensurate crossover and near the Kosterlitz--Thouless point of the spin-$\tfrac12$ $J_1$--$J_2$ Heisenberg chain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16915)
*Wuttichai Pankeaw,Pruet Kalasuwan*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Using DMRG and an SU(2) decomposition of mutual information into correlation channels, this study clarifies what quantum mutual information reveals beyond conventional correlation functions in the spin-1/2 J1-J2 Heisenberg chain, examining both the dimerized and gapless phases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The additional content that quantum mutual information provides compared to conventional correlation functions is often unclear. This work aims to disentangle and identify the specific extra information mutual information carries, particularly through block (multi-site) contributions and SU(2) channel decomposition.

Method: The spin-1/2 J1-J2 Heisenberg chain is studied via DMRG on infinite chains and periodic rings, combined with an SU(2) decomposition of the mutual information into correlation channels. Block mutual information on rings is extrapolated to infinite size and compared with the two-interval prediction of SU(2)_1 conformal field theory. A marginal-coupling expansion based on level spectroscopy is also employed.

Result: For singlet ground states, two-site mutual information is an exact function of the spin correlator, so additional content comes from blocks. In the dimerized phase, blocks resolve bond-energy correlations in the SU(2)-scalar channel, with singlet and triplet correlation lengths agreeing to ~1%. The scalar channel dominates block mutual information at large distances up to J2/J1=0.40, but drops to a few percent beyond the disorder point (numerically coinciding with the Majumdar-Ghosh point J2/J1=1/2); the structure-factor peak splits only at the Lifshitz point J2/J1=0.520622(2). On the gapless side, for block lengths l<=6, the correction decays as l^-p with p=1.96-2.07, and any logarithmic term is at least 60 times smaller than in the Heisenberg chain. The first-order marginal-coupling coefficient is consistent with l^-2 scaling, and the second-order coefficient is positive for x<=1/4. The scalar-channel share approaches 1/4 at small cross ratio x, as expected from SO(4) symmetry; its first-order response is (3/8)ln x - 5/8, matched by l=4 data within 1-3% at x<=1/9.

Conclusion: Quantum mutual information's added value lies in block contributions and channel decomposition rather than two-site terms. The SU(2) scalar channel carries key long-distance bond-energy correlations in the dimerized phase and exhibits distinctive scaling near the KT point, with the mutual information relative to the field-theory value becoming stationary at the KT point for large blocks.

Abstract: What quantum mutual information adds to conventional correlation functions is often unclear. We study the spin-1/2 $J_1$-$J_2$ Heisenberg chain, using DMRG on infinite chains and periodic rings and an SU(2) decomposition of the mutual information into correlation channels. For singlet ground states the two-site mutual information is an exact function of the spin correlator, so any additional content must come from blocks. In the dimerized phase, blocks resolve bond-energy correlations in the SU(2)-scalar channel without an independent asymptotic length: singlet and triplet correlation lengths agree to about 1%. The scalar channel dominates block mutual information at large distances in the commensurate regime up to $J_2/J_1=0.40$, but falls to a few percent beyond the disorder point, which coincides numerically with the Majumdar-Ghosh point $J_2/J_1=1/2$; the structure-factor peak splits only at the Lifshitz point $J_2/J_1=0.520622(2)$. On the gapless side we compare block mutual information on rings extrapolated to infinite size with the two-interval prediction of SU(2)$_1$ conformal field theory across the Kosterlitz-Thouless point $J_2/J_1=0.241167$. There, for block lengths $l\le6$, the correction decays as $l^{-p}$ with $p=1.96$-$2.07$, and any logarithmic term is at least 60 times smaller than in the Heisenberg chain. Expanding in the marginal coupling (from level spectroscopy) the first-order coefficient is consistent with $l^{-2}$ scaling and the second-order coefficient is positive for $x\le1/4$, suggesting that the mutual information relative to the field-theory value becomes stationary at the KT point for large blocks. The scalar-channel share approaches 1/4 at small cross ratio $x$, as expected from SO(4) symmetry; under two stated assumptions its first-order response to the coupling is $(3/8)\ln x-5/8$ at small $x$, matched by the $l=4$ data within 1-3% at $x\le1/9$.

</details>


### [140] [Emergent low-energy thermodynamics and universal $B/T$ scaling in $α$-RuCl$_3$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16961)
*A. Khansili,B. J. Ramshaw,A. Bangura,H. -Y. Kee,R. D. McDonald,F. Assaad,A. Rydh,A. Shekhter,K. A. Modic*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: High-field specific heat measurements on α-RuCl₃ up to 35 T reveal universal B/T scaling with a single effective energy scale proportional to the field (except near the c-axis), challenging the interpretation of its low-energy excitations within the perturbative Kitaev limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: α-RuCl₃ is a leading candidate for the Kitaev quantum spin liquid, where low-energy excitations in magnetic fields are interpreted as itinerant Majorana fermions and local flux excitations. However, conflicting experimental signatures have raised doubts about whether a Kitaev-like state is the correct starting point for interpreting the low-energy states in α-RuCl₃, motivating a closer examination of the nature of these excitations.

Method: Extensive high-field specific heat measurements on α-RuCl₃ up to 35 T, analyzing the scaling behavior of the low-energy thermodynamics with respect to applied magnetic field and temperature.

Result: The low-energy thermodynamics exhibit universal B/T scaling with a single effective energy scale proportional to the applied field, provided the field is not close to the c-axis. This behavior persists continuously from the antiferromagnetic phase boundary up to the highest fields.

Conclusion: The observed universal B/T scaling suggests that the perturbative Kitaev limit, with its Majorana and flux excitations, may not be an appropriate starting point for describing the low-energy thermodynamics of α-RuCl₃ for fields away from the c-axis.

Abstract: Quantum spin liquids are exotic states of matter in which strongly interacting spins remain disordered down to zero temperature and host fractionalized excitations. This has motivated an extensive search for their realization in materials. Among candidate systems, $α$-RuCl$_3$ has drawn particular attention as a platform for the quantum spin liquid proposed by Kitaev, in which the low-energy excitations in applied magnetic fields are interpreted as itinerant Majorana fermions and local flux excitations. However, conflicting experimental signatures have cast doubt on whether a Kitaev quantum spin liquid-like state is really the right starting point to interpret the low-energy states in $α$-RuCl$_3$, motivating a closer look at the nature of these excitations. Here we report an extensive high field study of specific heat up to 35 T on $α$-RuCl$_3$. We find that the low-energy thermodynamics exhibit universal $B/T$ scaling with a single effective energy scale proportional to the applied field as long as the magnetic field is not close to the $c$-axis. This behavior persists continuously from the antiferromagnetic phase boundary to the highest fields, suggesting that the perturbative Kitaev limit, with its Majorana and flux excitations, may not be an appropriate starting point for describing the low-energy thermodynamics of $α$-RuCl$_3$ for fields away from the $c$-axis.

</details>


### [141] [Pairing from repulsive interactions in twisted WSe2 from functional renormalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17222)
*Hannes Braun,Dumitru Călugăru,Pietro M. Bonetti,Haoyu Hu,Yan Zhang,Yi Jiang,Quansheng Wu,Kin Fai Mak,Jie Shan,B. Andrei Bernevig,Michael M. Scherer,Laura Classen*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper analyzes correlated electron phases in twisted bilayer WSe₂ at ν = -1 and twist angle 3.65°, using Hartree-Fock, RPA, and functional renormalization group methods. Both continuum and Wannier/Hubbard models yield a mixed d-wave singlet / p-wave triplet superconducting instability adjacent to inter-valley-coherent spin-density-wave order, with inter-valley coherent spin fluctuations as the pairing glue. The gate-screened Coulomb continuum model produces pairing down to zero displacement field, matching experiment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Superconductivity has been observed in twisted bilayer WSe₂ across an extended range of displacement fields, from the correlated insulator regime down to zero field. This experimental observation motivates a theoretical investigation of the correlated electron phases and the pairing mechanism in this system.

Method: The authors study hole filling ν = -1 at twist angle 3.65° using Hartree-Fock, random phase approximation, and functional renormalization group calculations. They derive a continuum model with gate-screened Coulomb interaction from first principles and compare its instabilities with those of a realistic three-orbital Wannier model with Hubbard interaction and an artificial single-orbital Hubbard model. They analyze the phase diagram as a function of displacement field and interaction strength, and investigate the pairing mechanism.

Result: In both the continuum and Wannier/Hubbard treatments, a superconducting instability of mixed d-wave singlet and p-wave triplet character emerges adjacent to inter-valley-coherent spin-density-wave order in the displacement-field–interaction-strength phase diagram. The pairing mechanism is attributed to inter-valley coherent spin fluctuations acting as the pairing glue. The extent of the pairing regime and the critical temperatures depend on model input, with differences traced to the interaction range entering the projected interaction. Within the continuum model, the gate-screened Coulomb interaction yields pairing down to zero displacement field.

Conclusion: The gate-screened Coulomb continuum model reproduces superconductivity extending down to zero displacement field, bringing the theory into agreement with experiment. The results highlight inter-valley coherent spin fluctuations as the pairing glue and show that the pairing regime and critical temperatures are sensitive to the interaction range in the projected interaction.

Abstract: Motivated by the observation of superconductivity in twisted bilayer WSe$_2$ over an extended range of displacement fields, reaching from the correlated insulator down to zero field, we analyze the correlated electron phases of this system at hole filling $ν= -1$ and twist angle $3.65^\circ$ using Hartree--Fock, random phase approximation, and functional renormalization group calculations. We derive a continuum model with gate-screened Coulomb interaction from first principles and compare its instabilities to those of a realistic three-orbital Wannier model with a Hubbard interaction and an artificial single-orbital Hubbard model. In both cases, we find a superconducting instability of mixed $d$-wave singlet and $p$-wave triplet character, arising adjacent to inter-valley-coherent spin-density-wave order in the phase diagram spanned by displacement field and interaction strength. Our additional analysis of the pairing mechanism points to inter-valley coherent spin fluctuations as the pairing glue. The extent of the pairing regime and the associated critical temperatures depend on the model input, and we trace differences to the interaction range entering the projected interaction. Within the continuum model, the gate-screened Coulomb interaction yields pairing down to zero displacement field, bringing theory into agreement with experiment.

</details>


### [142] [Microscopic Theory of the Acoustic Faraday Effect in Spin-Orbit Coupled Magnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17010)
*Antoine Matar,Haoting Xu,Hae-Young Kee*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The acoustic Faraday effect is conventionally treated as circular birefringence probing phonon Hall viscosity, but magnetic order that breaks rotational symmetry generates anisotropic phonon responses and elliptically polarized eigenmodes, so Faraday rotation cannot be attributed to Hall viscosity alone. Using spin-phonon couplings derived from a generic J-K-Γ-Γ' model with interlayer coupling, the authors show for α-RuCl3 that the extracted Hall viscosity is about twice the value obtained assuming rotational symmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard interpretation of acoustic Faraday rotation in terms of circular birefringence and phonon Hall viscosity assumes rotational symmetry. However, in spin-orbit-coupled magnets with magnetic order, this symmetry is broken, which can invalidate the conventional extraction of Hall viscosity and obscure its microscopic origin.

Method: The authors develop a theoretical framework beyond circular birefringence, treating anisotropic phonon responses and elliptically polarized eigenmodes. They derive spin-phonon couplings from a generic J-K-Γ-Γ' model including interlayer coupling, identifying transverse strain couplings for c-axis propagation arising from the interplay of Kitaev (K) and Γ interactions, as well as strain-induced g-tensor contributions. These couplings are incorporated into a theory for the Kitaev spin-liquid candidate α-RuCl3.

Result: Symmetry breaking is found to modify the Faraday response, yielding a Hall viscosity approximately twice the value inferred under the assumption of rotational symmetry.

Conclusion: The work establishes a microscopic framework beyond circular birefringence and refines the connection between acoustic Faraday rotation and phonon Hall viscosity in symmetry-broken spin-orbit-coupled magnets.

Abstract: The acoustic Faraday effect probes phonon Hall viscosity through the rotation of transverse acoustic polarization, conventionally interpreted in terms of circular birefringence. We show that magnetic order breaking rotational symmetry generates anisotropic phonon responses and elliptically polarized eigenmodes, so that Faraday rotation cannot be attributed to Hall viscosity alone. To elucidate their microscopic origin and consequences, we derive spin-phonon couplings from a generic $J$-$K$-$Γ$-$Γ'$ model with interlayer coupling, identifying transverse strain couplings for propagation along the $c$-axis arising from the interplay of Kitaev $(K)$ and $Γ$ interactions, along with strain-induced $g$-tensor contributions. Incorporating these couplings into our theory for the Kitaev spin-liquid candidate $α$-RuCl$_3$, we find that symmetry breaking modifies the Faraday response, revealing a Hall viscosity approximately twice the value inferred assuming rotational symmetry. Our results establish a microscopic framework beyond circular birefringence, refining the connection between acoustic Faraday rotation and phonon Hall viscosity in symmetry-broken spin-orbit-coupled magnets.

</details>


### [143] [Berry curvature from plane-wave mixing: A three-band model with emergent symmetries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17209)
*Avi Vadali,Tomohiro Soejima*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: A three-band model truncated at linear order in momentum captures the low-energy band structure and Berry curvature of topological bands in two-dimensional materials, revealing that Berry curvature depends strongly on the momentum-dependence of the potential and that emergent symmetries and their breaking generate topological flat bands, quadratic band touchings, and multiple Dirac cones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Topological phases in two-dimensional materials can be stabilized by an intrinsic moiré potential or by topological crystallization. The topology of these phases hinges on the interplay between the self-consistently generated potential and the spinor structure, which makes the band-projected potential momentum-dependent. Understanding this interplay requires a tractable model that retains the essential momentum-dependence.

Method: The band-projected potential is truncated at linear order in momentum, yielding a three-band model. The Berry curvature is computed analytically as a function of the potential's momentum-dependence. Emergent symmetries, including U(1) and anti-unitary symmetries, are identified, and emergent-symmetry breaking perturbations are introduced. An un-truncated model of a λ-jellium spinor inside a periodic potential is also studied to validate the truncated model.

Result: The Berry curvature is found to depend strongly on the momentum-dependence of the potential. Tuning the model reveals emergent U(1) and anti-unitary symmetries. Breaking these emergent symmetries drives the system into regimes hosting topological flat bands, quadratic band touchings, and multiple Dirac cones. Many of these features are recovered in the un-truncated λ-jellium spinor model in a periodic potential.

Conclusion: The results reveal a rich interplay between spinor structure and the potential in determining the topology of the ground state, with the momentum-dependence of the potential playing a central role in shaping Berry curvature and emergent symmetry phenomena.

Abstract: Two-dimensional materials can host topological phases stabilized either by an intrinsic moiré potential or topological crystallization. Crucial to the topology of these phases is the interplay between the (self-consistently generated) potential and the spinor structure, which imbues the band-projected potential with momentum-dependence. Truncating this potential at linear-order in momentum, we obtain a three-band model that captures the low-energy band structure and Berry curvature of such topological bands. We analytically find that the Berry curvature strongly depends on momentum-dependence of the potential. Furthermore, we identify that the model can be tuned to have emergent symmetries, including $U(1)$ and anti-unitary symmetries. Emergent-symmetry breaking perturbations drive the system to regimes hosting topological flat bands, quadratic band touchings, and multiple Dirac cones. We recover many of these features when studying an un-truncated model of a $λ$-jellium spinor inside of a periodic potential. Our results reveal a rich interplay of spinor structure and a potential in determining the topology of the ground state.

</details>


### [144] [Phase-Coherent Spin and Lattice Modulations in a Y-type Hexaferrite](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17292)
*Oskar Stepančić,Annika Stellhorn,Emma Campillo,Kun Zhai,Niklas Ekström,A. Corani,D. Alba Venero,R. Cubitt,E. Blackburn,L. Shen*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Using half-polarized neutron scattering, the authors demonstrate that the Y-type hexaferrite Ba0.4Sr1.6Mg2Fe12O22 hosts a magnetic and a longitudinal lattice modulation at the same wavevector, phase-locked, in its saturated high-field state — evidenced by a large nuclear-magnetic interference asymmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In spin-driven multiferroics, polarization arises from lattice distortions following magnetic order, but for incommensurate magnets symmetry usually forbids such distortion at the fundamental magnetic wavevector, and the distortions are rarely observed. The authors aim to detect a fundamental-wavevector spin-lattice coupling in a regime previously treated as a simple ferrimagnet.

Method: Half-polarized neutron scattering on the Y-type hexaferrite Ba0.4Sr1.6Mg2Fe12O22 in its saturated high-field state, analyzing the nuclear-magnetic interference asymmetry r of the two conjugate satellite reflections, including zone contrast, field dependence, and temperature persistence.

Result: A large nuclear-magnetic interference asymmetry, r ~ 0.8, of the same sign at the two conjugate satellites, persisting to 275 K, is observed. The zone contrast and field dependence of the satellite indicate an internal, field-induced longitudinal distortion. The modulated state coexists with the transverse-conical magnetoelectric phase and outlives it.

Conclusion: The results reveal a fundamental-wavevector spin-lattice coupling in a magnetic field regime previously regarded as a simple ferrimagnet, showing that magnetic and longitudinal lattice modulations lock in phase at a common wavevector in the saturated high-field state.

Abstract: Spin-driven multiferroics owe their electric polarization to lattice distortions that follow the magnetic order, yet in incommensurate magnets symmetry normally forbids such a distortion at the fundamental magnetic wavevector, and the distortions themselves are rarely observed. Here we use half-polarized neutron scattering to show that the Y-type hexaferrite Ba$_{0.4}$Sr$_{1.6}$Mg$_2$Fe$_{12}$O$_{22}$ hosts, in its saturated high-field state, a magnetic modulation and a longitudinal lattice modulation at one and the same wavevector, locked in phase. The signature is a large nuclear-magnetic interference asymmetry, $r$ ~ 0.8, of the same sign at the two conjugate satellites and persisting to 275 K. The zone contrast and the field dependence of the satellite indicate an internal, field-induced longitudinal distortion. The modulated state coexists with the transverse-conical magnetoelectric phase and outlives it, revealing a fundamental-wavevector spin-lattice coupling in a magnetic field regime previously regarded as a simple ferrimagnet.

</details>


### [145] [Quantum skyrmion molecule: two quantum skyrmions bound by coherent magnon exchange](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17299)
*Ricardo L. Silva*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Two quantum skyrmions in a frustrated spin-1/2 triangular magnet form a bound molecule (binding energy U≈−0.007J1, separation ≈5.5 lattice spacings) stabilized by coherent magnon exchange that locks their relative helicity, suggesting a self-assembled two-qubit element for helicity qubits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum skyrmions in frustrated spin-1/2 magnets are bound states of a few flipped spins on a ferromagnetic background. While single skyrmions are understood, the interaction between two such objects—and whether they bind, merge, or repel—was unexplored, motivating a study of skyrmion-skyrmion interactions and their potential as qubit platforms.

Method: Exact diagonalization and density-matrix renormalization group (DMRG) calculations on the J1–J2 triangular-lattice model with easy-axis anisotropy K, computing binding energy per flipped spin, pair stability, equilibrium separation, number fluctuations, and in-plane spin correlations between skyrmion walls.

Result: Single-skyrmion binding energy per flipped spin minimizes at Nf*=7 for K=0.02–0.04. Skyrmions smaller than Nf* cannot exist as pairs—they coalesce at any distance, and pinning wells cannot prevent coalescence. Two natural-size skyrmions bind without pinning or external field: U≈−0.007J1 at K=0.03–0.04 with equilibrium separation 5.5 lattice spacings, independent of K and domain size; at K=0.02 (marginally bound single skyrmion) the pair is unbound. Constituents retain identity (7+7 flipped spins) and are coupled by coherent magnon exchange, seen as excess number fluctuations and inter-wall in-plane spin correlations reaching 0.16 across the gap; these correlations follow the vortex pattern and lock relative helicity.

Conclusion: Two natural-size quantum skyrmions form a bound molecule via coherent magnon exchange that locks their relative helicity—the quantum counterpart of classical helicity-dependent skyrmion interactions—and the molecule is proposed as a self-assembled two-qubit element for skyrmion helicity qubits in a single layer.

Abstract: Quantum skyrmions in frustrated spin-$1/2$ magnets are bound states of a few flipped spins in a ferromagnetic background. Using exact diagonalization and density-matrix renormalization group calculations for the $J_1$--$J_2$ triangular-lattice model with easy-axis anisotropy $K$, we ask what happens when two such objects are brought together. The binding energy per flipped spin of a single skyrmion has a minimum at $\Nf^{*}=7$ for $K=0.02$--$0.04$, and skyrmions smaller than $\Nf^{*}$ do not exist as pairs: at any distance they coalesce into a single larger object, and pinning wells cannot prevent it. Two skyrmions of natural size, by contrast, form a bound molecule without pinning or external field, with binding energy $U\simeq-0.007J_1$ at $K=0.03$--$0.04$ and an equilibrium separation of $5.5$ lattice spacings that does not depend on $K$ or on the size of the simulation domain; at $K=0.02$, where the single skyrmion is itself marginally bound, the pair is unbound within our resolution. The two constituents keep their identity ($7+7$ flipped spins) and are coupled by a coherent exchange of magnons, visible as number fluctuations between the two halves of the domain in excess of those of two independent skyrmions and as in-plane spin correlations between the two skyrmion walls that reach $0.16$ across the gap. These correlations follow the vortex pattern of the two textures and lock their relative helicity, the quantum counterpart of the helicity-dependent interaction of classical skyrmions. We discuss the molecule as a self-assembled two-qubit element for skyrmion helicity qubits in a single layer.

</details>


### [146] [Conformal soft modes at elastic instabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17920)
*Lars Franke,Nick Sander,Markus Garst*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Near a continuous elastic phase transition, critical displacement modes obey a conformal-Killing-like equation; isostructural instabilities yield four modes (one dilation + three special conformal transformations), while symmetry-breaking instabilities produce a continuum of modes, sometimes corresponding to 2D Minkowski conformal transformations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Crystals close to continuous elastic phase transitions exhibit pronounced critical fluctuations, but the geometric/algebraic structure of the associated soft displacement modes has not been characterized in these terms. The paper aims to reveal that structure and connect it to conformal geometry.

Method: Linear elasticity theory is applied to the critical (soft) modes of the displacement vector. The resulting condition is compared to the conformal Killing equation, and the mode content is analyzed separately for isostructural versus crystal-symmetry-breaking instabilities, including finite-size crystals.

Result: The displacement vector of critical modes satisfies a conformal-Killing-like condition. For an isostructural instability exactly four critical modes appear: one dilation and three special conformal transformations. For symmetry-breaking instabilities a continuum of modes exists even in finite-size crystals, and in certain cases these comprise conformal transformations of two-dimensional Minkowski space.

Conclusion: Critical elastic fluctuations near continuous phase transitions are governed by conformal-Killing-type modes, with distinct mode spectra depending on instability type. These findings have implications for critical elastic phenomena and for extremal mechanical metamaterials.

Abstract: In case a crystal is close to a continuous elastic phase transition, pronounced critical fluctuations emerge. We show that on the level of linear elasticity theory the displacement vector for these critical modes obeys a condition reminiscent of the conformal Killing equation. For an isostructural instability, four critical modes arise corresponding to one dilation and three special conformal transformations. At crystal symmetry-breaking instabilities, the critical fluctuations include a continuum of modes even for crystals with finite size. For certain cases, these comprise the conformal transformations associated with two-dimensional Minkowski space. These results have implications for critical elastic phenomena as well as for extremal mechanical metamaterials.

</details>


### [147] [Direct view of exciton and carrier dynamics in twisted bilayer graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17984)
*Gianmarco Gatti,Anders S. Mortensen,Zhihao Jiang,Alfred J. H. Jones,Gesa-R. Siemann,Thomas S. Nielsen,Søren Ulstrup,Naina Kushwaha,Bruce Weaver,Yu Zhang,Emma Springate,Charlotte E. Sanders,Vibha Reddy,Ulrich Starke,Philip Hofmann*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Position-, time-, and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy of twisted bilayer graphene (twist ~9°) on H-intercalated SiC reveals an in-gap feature attributed to a non-hydrogenic "ghost-Fano" exciton, and shows different electronic temperatures in the two layers despite similar cooling dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate the excitonic and carrier dynamics of twisted bilayer graphene, exploiting the momentum-space layer selectivity of the twist to probe interlayer behavior, and to test proposed correlated/excitonic states near the van Hove singularity.

Method: Position-, time-, and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES/tr-ARPES) on tBLG on H-intercalated SiC with a twist angle of 9 ± 1°. Momentum separation of the largely layer-polarized Dirac cones provides direct layer selectivity; time-resolved measurements track carrier temperature and cooling.

Result: Both graphene layers are hole doped with a 30 meV Dirac-point offset. tr-ARPES near the van Hove singularity shows a spectral feature within the hybridization-induced band gap, alongside excited conduction-band electrons. The in-gap feature's binding energy and k-space location match a proposed non-hydrogenic "ghost-Fano" exciton from destructive interference between two nearly degenerate excitonic channels. The two layers show markedly different peak electronic temperatures but similar subsequent cooling.

Conclusion: The in-gap feature shows distinct dynamics consistent with a ghost-Fano exciton. The differing peak electronic temperatures between layers are attributed to ultrafast interlayer electronic-energy redistribution driven by an interlayer electric field arising from the differing doping of the layers.

Abstract: Twisted bilayer graphene (tBLG) on H-intercalated SiC (twist angle of $9 \pm 1^{\circ}$) is studied by position\mbox{-}, time\mbox{-}, and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES). Both graphene layers are hole doped, with a Dirac-point offset of 30~meV. Time-resolved ARPES near the van Hove singularity reveals a spectral feature within the hybridization-induced band gap, together with excited electrons in the conduction band. The in-gap feature exhibits distinct dynamics, and its binding energy and $k$-space location are consistent with the proposed non-hydrogenic ``ghost-Fano'' exciton arising from destructive interference between two nearly degenerate excitonic channels. Near the Dirac points, the twist separates the largely layer-polarized Dirac cones in momentum space, providing direct layer selectivity. We find markedly different peak electronic temperatures in the two graphene layers, even though their subsequent cooling dynamics are very similar. We attribute this to an ultrafast interlayer redistribution of electronic energy, driven by an interlayer electric field that arises from the different doping in the layers.

</details>


### [148] [Non-Hermitian Statistical Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18175)
*Xi Hu,Yan-Qiong Ma,Su-Peng Kou*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper develops a theory of non-Hermitian statistical geometry where relaxing Hermiticity softens rigid braid/fusion data into complex spectral-geometric degrees of freedom, realized and tested in Kitaev-Majorana networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Particle exchange in two dimensions yields quantum statistics beyond bosons and fermions, central to fractional quantum Hall physics, topological quantum computation, and correlated matter. Conventional topological phases compress exchange geometry into rigid braid and fusion data, leaving no room for geometric degrees of freedom; the authors seek to expose and exploit such geometry.

Method: Introduces non-Hermitian statistical geometry, softening exchange data into a complex space of spectral and relative-geometric degrees of freedom. For adjacent braid generators, the relative eigenline geometry provides an extra gauge-invariant modulus beyond the projective exchange spectrum. On the irreducible two-channel branch, braid consistency locks coordinates via an exact spectrum-geometry relation. Uses Riesz projectors and Kato transport, realized in a five-arm Kitaev-Majorana network, tested against a two-dimensional four-vortex Kitaev model.

Result: Nonzero projective Kato curvature demonstrates that braid-consistent, fusion-coherent exchange can depend on the control path. Finite-time dynamics tests the exchange operation, microscopic fusion sewing tests composition across fusion descriptions, and matched-spectrum controls isolate the role of relative geometry.

Conclusion: Non-Hermiticity exposes exchange geometry, braid consistency re-locks it, fusion coherence further constrains it, and projective flatness determines when geometric exchange becomes intrinsically topological.

Abstract: Particle exchange in two dimensions gives rise to quantum statistics beyond the boson-fermion dichotomy, with central roles in fractional quantum Hall physics, topological quantum computation, and strongly correlated quantum matter. In conventional topological phases, microscopic exchange geometry is compressed into rigid braid and fusion data. We develop a theory of non-Hermitian statistical geometry in which relaxing Hermiticity softens these exchange data into a larger complex space of spectral and relative-geometric degrees of freedom. For two adjacent braid generators, a single projective exchange spectrum is insufficient: their relative eigenline geometry supplies an additional gauge-invariant modulus. On the generic irreducible two-channel branch, braid consistency locks these coordinates through an exact spectrum-geometry relation. Using Riesz projectors and Kato transport, we realize this geometry in a five-arm Kitaev-Majorana network and test spectral and relative-geometric responses in an independent two-dimensional four-vortex Kitaev model. Finite-time dynamics tests the exchange operation, microscopic fusion sewing tests its composition across fusion descriptions, and matched-spectrum controls isolate the role of relative geometry. Nonzero projective Kato curvature shows that braid-consistent, fusion-coherent exchange can depend on the control path. Non-Hermiticity thus exposes exchange geometry, braid consistency re-locks it, fusion coherence further constrains it, and projective flatness determines when geometric exchange becomes intrinsically topological.

</details>


### [149] [Unexpected stabilization of a single-$q$ charge density wave in pristine 1T-TiSe$_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18211)
*Kimoon Higashihira Han,Dongju Hwang,Dongsin Kim,Yeojin Ahn,Jaehun Cha,Seonggeon Gim,Gyubin Lee,Mingi Jho,Chan-young Lim,Sae Hee Ryu,Chris Jozwiak,Aaron Bostwick,Eli Rotenberg,Jonathan D. Denlinger,Makoto Hashimoto,Donghui Lu,Young Woo Choi,Gil Young Cho,Yeongkwan Kim*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The triple-$q$ charge-density-wave (CDW) order in 1T-TiSe$_2$ forms sequentially, not simultaneously: a unidirectional $2\times1$ single-$q$ order stabilizes first and the other two components emerge at lower temperatures, yielding inequivalent subcomponents in phase and amplitude.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: For 50 years the $2\times2$ triple-$q$ CDW in 1T-TiSe$_2$ was assumed to consist of three equivalent subcomponents. Recent studies questioned this equivalence, but a direct demonstration of non-identity and of how the order develops was lacking.

Method: Angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) tracking folded-band features to follow the temperature evolution of each CDW component, complemented by a free-energy analysis of inter-component coupling and first-principles calculations including phonon anharmonicity.

Result: A unidirectional $2\times1$ single-$q$ order condenses first, with the remaining two subcomponents appearing sequentially at lower temperatures to complete the triple-$q$ phase. Free-energy analysis attributes this hierarchy to repulsive coupling among the three subcomponents, consistent with first-principles results. The three subcomponents are inequivalent in both phase and amplitude.

Conclusion: The sequential, inequivalent development of subcomponents reconciles previously reported symmetry breaking and disparate characteristic temperatures, providing a framework in which a nominally simple, high-symmetry CDW emerges from temperature-dependent competition and cooperation among inequivalent subcomponents.

Abstract: Over the past decade, studies revisiting the classical charge-density-wave system 1T-TiSe$_2$, long understood to host the $2 \times 2$ triple-$q$ order with equivalent subcomponents, have suggested that these subcomponents may not be identical, challenging a 50-year-old paradigm. Here, we demonstrate not only that these subcomponents are non-identical but also that they develop sequentially rather than simultaneously. By tracking folded-band features, angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy reveals that a unidirectional $2 \times 1$ single-$q$ order stabilizes first, while the other two components emerge sequentially at lower temperatures, completing the triple-$q$ phase. Our free-energy analysis explains this hierarchy through repulsive coupling among the three subcomponents, as supported by first-principles calculations incorporating phonon anharmonicity. Consequently, the three subcomponents are inequivalent in both phase and amplitude. These results reconcile reported symmetry breaking and differing characteristic temperatures and establish a framework in which ostensibly simple, high-symmetry charge-density order emerges from temperature-dependent competition and cooperation among inequivalent subcomponents.

</details>


### [150] [A hierarchy of exact lower bounds on the Laughlin spectral gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18854)
*Michael G. Scheer,Eslam Khalaf*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper develops a bootstrap-style method combining analytic bounds with finite semidefinite programs to rigorously lower-bound the spectral gap of the first Haldane pseudopotential Hamiltonian on the sphere for arbitrary flux, including the thermodynamic limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Establishing rigorous lower bounds on the spectral gap of fractional quantum Hall model Hamiltonians (e.g., the Haldane pseudopotential) is difficult, especially in the thermodynamic limit. Existing numerics are not mathematically rigorous, and a general technique to convert numerical SDP bounds into proofs is lacking.

Method: Based on Knabe's criterion (gap ≥ ε iff H² ⪰ εH for a Hamiltonian with ground-state energy 0), the authors split H² into an analytically boundable part and a part bounded by a finite SDP whose size depends only on cutoff parameters and on flux N_Φ only parametrically. They introduce an algorithm to produce nearly-optimal exact feasible points for large rational SDPs, and another to establish rank stability of the SDP in the N_Φ → ∞ limit, certifying the bound in the thermodynamic limit.

Result: Numerically exact (up to solver precision) lower bounds on the gap are obtained, made mathematically rigorous via the feasibility-point algorithm. The Laughlin gap is established, and rank stability is certified in the thermodynamic limit, with the method generalizable in principle to other pseudopotentials such as Moore-Read or Read-Rezayi.

Conclusion: The work rigorously establishes the Laughlin spectral gap, offers a potential route to gap bounds for other model wavefunctions, and provides a broadly applicable general framework for turning numerical semidefinite programming bounds into rigorous mathematical proofs.

Abstract: We develop a bootstrap approach to deriving lower bounds on the spectral gap of the first Haldane pseudopotential Hamiltonian on the sphere with $N_Φ$ flux quanta for arbitrary $N_Φ$, including in the thermodynamic limit $N_Φ\to \infty$. Our approach can in principle be generalized to pseudopotentials describing other model wavefunctions such as the Moore-Read state or Read-Rezayi sequence. Our approach is based on Knabe's observation that if $H$ is a Hamiltonian with ground state energy $0$ then the spectral gap is at least $ε> 0$ if and only if $H^2 \succeq εH$. To show this inequality, we split $H^2$ into two parts, one that can be analytically bounded and another that can be bounded using a finite semidefinite program (SDP). Crucially, the size of this program is determined only by cutoff parameters, and the program depends on $N_Φ$ only parametrically, allowing us to obtain numerically exact (up to solver precision) lower bounds on the gap. In order to turn these numerical bounds into mathematically rigorous bounds, we introduce an algorithm that can produce nearly-optimal exact feasible points for large rational SDPs. We additionally provide an algorithm that establishes the rank stability of the semidefinite program in the limit $N_Φ\to \infty$ which is needed to certify the bound in the thermodynamic limit. Beyond establishing the Laughlin gap and revealing a potential method for deriving spectral gap lower bounds for other pseudopotentials, our work provides a general method for turning numerical semidefinite programming bounds into rigorous mathematical proofs, which may have wide applicability.

</details>


### [151] [Revisiting magnetoelectric response in collinear antiferromagnetic zigzag chains: A downfolding approach beyond conventional low-energy models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07118)
*Shuhei Kanda,Satoru Hayami*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper microscopically analyzes the magnetoelectric (ME) effect in a collinear antiferromagnetic zigzag chain using a multi-orbital tight-binding model with s- and p-orbitals, showing the ME response is orbital-driven via s-p hybridization and arises from vertex corrections rather than the bare low-energy Hamiltonian.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ME effects in antiferromagnets offer a promising platform for symmetry-driven cross-correlated responses, yet their microscopic origin remains elusive and is often obscured by simplified low-energy descriptions. The authors aim to revisit and clarify this microscopic mechanism using a more complete multi-orbital treatment.

Method: The authors employ a multi-orbital tight-binding model explicitly including s- and p-orbital degrees of freedom for a collinear antiferromagnetic zigzag chain. They use analytical and numerical Kubo formula calculations, derive an effective Hamiltonian projected onto the s-orbital subspace via the Schur complement, incorporate vertex corrections from orbital hybridization, and introduce a quasiparticle renormalization scheme to formulate a renormalized Kubo formula.

Result: The ME response is governed by orbital degrees of freedom activated through s-p hybridization, while the spin contribution vanishes due to spin conservation. A naive Kubo formula within the effective s-orbital model fails to capture the ME response, but this is resolved by systematically including vertex corrections from orbital hybridization; the renormalized Kubo formula preserves conservation laws and accurately reproduces the full multi-orbital results.

Conclusion: The work revises the conventional low-energy perspective, demonstrating that the ME effect originates from virtual interorbital processes encoded in vertex corrections rather than from the bare low-energy Hamiltonian. The developed effective framework offers a unified microscopic understanding of orbital-driven ME responses and a systematic route to incorporate hybridization effects beyond simple low-energy models.

Abstract: Magnetoelectric (ME) effects in antiferromagnets provide a fertile platform for exploring symmetry-driven cross-correlated responses. However, their microscopic origin remains elusive and is often obscured in simplified low-energy descriptions. In this study, we revisit the microscopic mechanism of the ME effect in a collinear antiferromagnetic zigzag chain by employing a multi-orbital tight-binding model that explicitly includes both $s$- and $p$-orbital degrees of freedom. Using analytical and numerical calculations based on the Kubo formula, we demonstrate that the ME response is governed by orbital degrees of freedom activated through $s$--$p$ hybridization, while the spin contribution vanishes due to spin conservation. To elucidate the low-energy description, we derive an effective Hamiltonian projected onto the $s$-orbital subspace using the Schur complement. We show that a naive application of the Kubo formula within this effective model fails to capture the ME response. This issue is resolved by systematically incorporating vertex corrections in terms of orbital hybridization into the response functions. Furthermore, by introducing a quasiparticle renormalization scheme, we formulate a renormalized Kubo formula that preserves conservation laws and accurately reproduces the full multi-orbital results. Our analysis revisits the conventional low-energy perspective and reveals that the ME effect originates from virtual interorbital processes encoded in vertex corrections, rather than from the bare low-energy Hamiltonian. The effective framework developed here provides a unified microscopic understanding of orbital-driven ME responses and offers a systematic route to incorporate hybridization effects beyond simple low-energy models.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.mtrl-sci'></div>

# cond-mat.mtrl-sci [[Back]](#toc)

### [152] [Hierarchical Multi-Agent Reasoning Systems for Knowledge-Grounded Material Substitution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14123)
*Tarjei Paule Hage,Subhadeep Pal,Wei Lu,Yu-Chuan Hsu,Gayla Lyon,Jiezhu Jin,Markus J. Buehler*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: MARS is a hierarchical multi-agent reasoning system for knowledge-grounded material substitution under scientific and manufacturing constraints, demonstrated on PFAS replacement and validated through a multi-seed ablation study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI-assisted scientific discovery needs to move beyond plausible idea generation toward reproducible, constraint-aware workflows supporting practical engineering decisions. Existing approaches often lack grounding in scientific knowledge, manufacturability awareness, and iterative refinement.

Method: MARS decomposes substitution into a closed-loop workflow: requirement analysis, grounded candidate generation, and manufacturability assessment with process design. It integrates retrieval-augmented generation, knowledge-graph reasoning, a database of lab-available materials, persistent memory of intermediate results, and rejection-aware iterative feedback.

Result: In a PFAS-substitution case study, MARS generates structured material requirements, identifies an evidence-grounded substitute candidate, and develops a lab-scale manufacturing route. In a multi-seed ablation evaluation, MARS achieves the highest overall performance across all three subsystems, with gains persisting when the backbone model is held fixed.

Conclusion: Hierarchical decomposition, knowledge grounding, and manufacturability-aware refinement improve material-substitution reasoning beyond model capability alone.

Abstract: AI-assisted scientific discovery increasingly seeks to move beyond generating plausible ideas toward reproducible, constraint-aware workflows that can support practical engineering decisions. We present MARS, a hierarchical Multi-Agent Reasoning System for knowledge-grounded material substitution under scientific and manufacturing constraints. We apply MARS to replace per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS), a demanding materials-design problem in which substitutes must satisfy functional performance, environmental, experimental, and processing requirements. MARS decomposes substitution into a closed-loop workflow comprising requirement analysis, grounded candidate generation, and manufacturability assessment with process design. The system integrates retrieval-augmented generation, knowledge-graph reasoning, a database of lab-available materials, persistent memory of intermediate results, and rejection-aware iterative feedback. In a PFAS-substitution case study, MARS generates structured material requirements, identifies an evidence-grounded substitute candidate, and develops a lab-scale manufacturing route. In a multi-seed ablation evaluation, MARS achieves the highest overall performance across all three subsystems, with gains that persist when the backbone model is held fixed. These results suggest that hierarchical decomposition, knowledge grounding, and manufacturability-aware refinement improve material-substitution reasoning beyond model capability alone.

</details>


### [153] [In-Situ TEM Observation of Solid-State Phase Evolution in High-Entropy Alloy Nanoparticles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14147)
*Nirmal Kumar Katiyar,Noorbasha Bhavani Sai,Guilherme Gurian Dariani,Douglas S. Galvao,Pulickel M. Ajayan,Pedro Alves da Silva Autreto,Sriram Vijayan,Chandra Sekhar Tiwary*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study investigates phase transformation and atomic mobility in CoCrFeMnNi high-entropy alloy nanoclusters at pre-melting temperatures using HRTEM and molecular dynamics simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional design principles no longer fully describe emergent materials like high-entropy alloys (HEAs), where multiple metallic elements coexist in a single unit cell. Although such systems exhibit configurational entropy as high as in the fully random or melted state (frozen into a solid solution), their behaviour at pre-melting temperatures—where atoms gain sufficient mobility and time to rearrange, shifting the balance from entropic to enthalpic interplay—remains largely unknown.

Method: The phase transformation of HEA nanoclusters (CoCrFeMnNi) was examined under high-resolution transmission electron microscopy (HRTEM) at room temperature (RT), 550 °C, and 850 °C. Molecular dynamics simulations were used to corroborate the experimental findings and provide a detailed mechanistic description.

Result: The clustered nanoparticles coarsen into larger clusters, accompanied by compositional heterogeneity attributed to surface and lattice diffusion. The experimental observation of atomic mobility was further confirmed by molecular dynamics simulations.

Conclusion: Atomic mobility drives coarsening and compositional heterogeneity in HEA nanoclusters at pre-melting temperatures, an effect revealed by HRTEM and supported mechanistically by molecular dynamics. This experimental finding of atomic mobility was further corroborated using molecular dynamics, which provides a detailed mechanistic description.

Abstract: The emergence of new materials has gone beyond conventional design principles. Among them are high entropy alloys (HEAs), in which multiple metallic elements coexist within a single unit cell. In this case, each atomic position in a solid material has a different chemical environment, leading to configurational entropy as high as that in the fully random or melted state, which has been frozen to a solid solution. However, the behaviour of such alloy systems at pre-melting temperatures is largely unknown, where atoms gain sufficient mobility and time to rearrange, leading to an enthalpic rather than an entropic interplay. This study demonstrated the phase transformation of HEA nanoclusters (CoCrFeMnNi) under high-resolution transmission electron microscopy (HRTEM) at different temperatures, including room temperature (RT), 550 °C, and 850 °C. The clustered nanoparticles coarsen into larger clusters, accompanied by compositional heterogeneity due to surface and lattice diffusion. This experimental finding of atomic mobility was further corroborated using molecular dynamics, which provides a detailed mechanistic description.

</details>


### [154] [Precursor stoichiometry controls dopant structure, defect chemistry, and valence in Mn-doped ZnGa$_2$O$_4$ nanocrystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14195)
*Mátyás M. Rudolf,Gábor Bortel,Nikoletta Jegenyés,Árpád Jakab,László Bencs,Ferenc Simon,Adam Gali,Dávid Beke*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Host cation ratio (Zn/Ga) is shown to be an equally decisive design variable as the activator in Mn-doped ZnGa2O4 nanocrystals, controlling both dopant capture and native-defect inventory, with the brightest phosphor being Zn-excess rather than nominally stoichiometric.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Luminescence of doped nanocrystals is typically optimized through the activator, while the role of the host cation ratio remains largely overlooked. The authors aim to establish host stoichiometry as a decisive design variable in doped phosphors.

Method: Mn-doped ZnGa2O4 nanocrystals (Mn:Zn = 4x10^-4) were grown hydrothermally at nominal Zn/Ga ratios of 0.30-0.75 and post-annealed at 400-1100 C. The composition-temperature matrix was characterized by X-ray- and UV-excited luminescence, EPR, Rietveld-refined XRD, Raman spectroscopy, and SEM.

Result: As-grown crystals are phase-pure spinel at all compositions, accommodating off-stoichiometry as internal point defects (0.4% lattice parameter variation); annealing exsolves excess Ga as beta-Ga2O3. Mn2+ emission shows two optima: near-stoichiometric crystals annealed at 400-600 C and Zn-excess crystals annealed at 1100 C, the latter twice as bright under X-ray excitation. A minimum at 800 C coincides with collapse of the Mn2+ EPR population, indicating transient defect-compensated dopant oxidation. The most Zn-rich composition shows no Mn2+ or Mn4+ emission but strong trace Cr3+ emission, indicating Mn rejection during growth.

Conclusion: Solution stoichiometry acts on two levels: dopant capture and native-defect inventory, while annealing controls dopant valence and lattice order. The brightest high-temperature phosphor is not the stoichiometric composition but the one whose excess is consumed on its path to stoichiometry.

Abstract: The luminescence of doped nanocrystals is usually optimised through the activator; here we show that the host cation ratio is an equally decisive, and largely overlooked, design variable. Mn-doped ZnGa$_2$O$_4$ nanocrystals (Mn:Zn = $4\times10^{-4}$) were grown hydrothermally at nominal Zn/Ga ratios between 0.30 and 0.75 (stoichiometric: 0.50) and post-annealed at 400--1100$^\circ$C, and the resulting composition--temperature matrix was mapped by X-ray- and UV-excited luminescence, EPR, Rietveld-refined X-ray diffraction, Raman spectroscopy and SEM. The as-grown crystals are phase-pure spinel at every composition and accommodate their off-stoichiometry internally as a point-defect inventory, reflected in a 0.4\% variation of the lattice parameter; on annealing, the excess gallium is exsolved as $β$-Ga$_2$O$_3$ in amounts set by the solution composition. The Mn$^{2+}$ emission separates into two optima, near-stoichiometric crystals annealed at 400--600$^\circ$C and Zn-excess crystals annealed at 1100$^\circ$C, the latter being twice as bright under X-ray excitation. Under X-ray and 365~nm excitation the two optima are separated by a minimum at 800$^\circ$C that coincides with a collapse of the Mn$^{2+}$ EPR population and has no counterpart in the Raman linewidths, identifying transient, defect-compensated oxidation of the dopant rather than lattice degradation as its origin. The most Zn-rich composition shows neither Mn$^{2+}$ nor Mn$^{4+}$ emission at any annealing stage, whereas its trace Cr$^{3+}$ emission is strong, indicating that Mn is rejected already during growth. Solution stoichiometry therefore acts on two levels, dopant capture and native-defect inventory, while annealing controls dopant valence and lattice order. The brightest high-temperature phosphor is thus not the nominally stoichiometric one, but the composition whose excess is consumed on its way to stoichiometry.

</details>


### [155] [Anisotropic pressure response of thermal conductivity in crystalline polytetrafluoroethylene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14220)
*Maissa Adi,Jamal Abou Haibeh,Samuel Huberman*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Directional thermal conductivity of orthorhombic Form III PTFE is computed from first principles at 1.503, 3.452, and 5.056 GPa. Chain-axis conductivity falls at every pressure step, while transverse components recover between 3.452 and 5.056 GPa (up 23.7% and 13.7% at 300 K) as longitudinal conductivity drops 14.5%. An exact factorization traces the transverse recovery to larger heat-capacity-weighted squared velocities (stiffer interchain restoring forces), while shorter effective lifetimes dominate the chain direction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Compression alters phonon velocities and lifetimes, so thermal conductivity should respond differently along versus across polymer chains. Understanding this anisotropic, direction-dependent response is needed to predict and engineer heat transport in compressed polymer materials.

Method: First-principles harmonic and cubic force constants within the single-mode relaxation-time approximation (SMRTA) are used to compute the thermal conductivity tensor of orthorhombic Form III PTFE at 1.503, 3.452, and 5.056 GPa and at 100, 200, and 300 K. An exact factorization of conductivity into a heat-capacity-velocity factor and a weighted effective lifetime is applied to attribute changes to velocities versus lifetimes. Cubic force-constant blocks are compared across pressures to assess interchain coupling.

Result: Chain-axis thermal conductivity decreases at every pressure step at all temperatures (100, 200, 300 K). Between 3.452 and 5.056 GPa, both transverse components recover: at 300 K they rise by 23.7% and 13.7%, while longitudinal conductivity decreases by 14.5%. The transverse recovery is driven by larger heat-capacity-weighted squared velocities, consistent with stiffer interchain restoring forces. Along the chains, shorter effective lifetimes dominate, and both conductivity-weighted Normal and Umklapp scattering rates increase at 300 K. Interchain coupling is already substantial at 1.503 GPa and strengthens under compression.

Conclusion: Compression produces an anisotropic thermal-conductivity response in Form III PTFE: longitudinal (chain-axis) conductivity decreases monotonically with pressure, whereas transverse components recover at higher pressure because of increased heat-capacity-weighted squared velocities from stiffer interchain restoring forces, while shortened effective phonon lifetimes govern the chain direction. Interchain coupling is significant even at the lowest pressure and grows with compression.

Abstract: Compression can change thermal conductivity differently along polymer chains and across them by altering phonon velocities and lifetimes. We examine this directional response in orthorhombic Form III polytetrafluoroethylene (PTFE) at calculated pressures of 1.503, 3.452, and 5.056 GPa, using first-principles harmonic and cubic force constants within the single-mode relaxation-time approximation (SMRTA). At 100, 200, and 300 K, the chain-axis conductivity decreases at every pressure step. Between 3.452 and 5.056 GPa, both transverse components recover: at 300 K they increase by 23.7% and 13.7%, while the longitudinal conductivity decreases by 14.5%. An exact factorization separates the conductivity into a heat-capacity--velocity factor and a weighted effective lifetime. The transverse recovery follows from larger heat-capacity-weighted squared velocities, consistent with stiffer interchain restoring forces. Along the chains, shorter effective lifetimes dominate, and both conductivity-weighted Normal and Umklapp rates increase at 300 K. Comparing the same cubic force-constant blocks at all three pressures, we find that interchain coupling is already substantial at 1.503 GPa and increases under compression.

</details>


### [156] [Emergence of quantum transport in SnTe films upon structural refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14233)
*Shrinkhala Sharma,Rabindra Basnet,Thomas Parker,Afolabi Akinfaderin,Alexander Samokhvalov,Rajeswari Kolagani,Ramesh C. Budhani*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Textured SnTe thin films grown on Bi2Te3-buffered sapphire by pulsed laser ablation show annealing-dependent crystallinity and stoichiometry, with low-temperature magnetoresistance governed by weak antilocalization and a non-Kohler scaling at higher temperatures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Topological crystalline insulators differ from conventional Z2 topological insulators because their surface states are protected by crystalline symmetry. Quantitatively characterizing these states requires magneto-transport measurements to extract the phase coherence length and the number of independent coherent channels, which had been lacking for textured SnTe thin films.

Method: SnTe thin films were grown on Bi2Te3-buffered c-plane sapphire (Al2O3) by pulsed laser ablation. A post-deposition annealing protocol was applied to control crystallographic texture. Structural, transport, and magnetoresistance measurements were performed, and the low-temperature magnetoresistance was analyzed using the Hikami-Larkin-Nagaoka theory in the strong spin-orbit coupling limit.

Result: Annealing at elevated temperatures improved crystallinity and epitaxial alignment along [111]. Films annealed at 100 oC exhibited higher carrier density and lower resistivity, suggestive of Sn vacancies acting as native acceptor defects, whereas films annealed at 200 oC approached 1:1 stoichiometry. At T < 10 K, all samples showed a zero-field cusp in magnetoresistance consistent with weak antilocalization. At T > 20 K, magnetoresistance was small and positive and deviated from conventional Kohler scaling; an empirical temperature-dependent scaling factor produced an approximate collapse of the magnetoresistance curves between 50 and 250 K, indicating that Hall-derived carrier density changes alone cannot explain the scaling behavior.

Conclusion: Post-growth annealing strongly governs the interplay between stoichiometry, crystallinity, and coherent transport in SnTe thin films, establishing annealing as a key control parameter for tuning topological crystalline insulator transport properties.

Abstract: Topological crystalline insulators are distinguished from the conventional Z2 topological insulators by crystalline symmetry protection of the surface states. Quantitative characterization of these states requires magneto-transport measurements from which the phase coherence length and number of independent coherent channels can be extracted. Here we report such measurements on textured thin films of SnTe grown on Bi2Te3 buffered c-plane sapphire (Al2O3) by pulsed laser ablation. A post deposition annealing protocol was implemented to control the crystallographic texture, which reveals improved crystallinity and enhanced epitaxial alignment along the [111] direction in films annealed at elevated temperatures. The higher carrier density and lower resistivity seen in films annealed at 100 oC are suggestive of Sn vacancies which act as native acceptor defects, while films annealed at 200 oC approach the 1:1 stoichiometry. The magnetoresistance of all samples at T < 10 K characterized by a cusp centered around zero-field is suggestive of weak antilocalization and interpreted in the framework of the Hikami-Larkin-Nagaoka theory in the strong spin-orbit coupling limit. At T > 20 K, the MR is small and positive, and deviates from conventional Kohler scaling. An approximate collapse of the MR curves between 50 and 250 K is obtained using an empirical temperature-dependent scaling factor, indicating that changes in the Hall-derived carrier density alone cannot account for the observed scaling behavior. These results demonstrate that post growth annealing strongly influences the interplay between stoichiometry, crystallinity, and coherent transport in SnTe thin films.

</details>


### [157] [Benchmarking machine-learning interatomic potentials using classical force fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14239)
*Johannes Sandberg,Geneviève Dusson,Julien Lam*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work benchmarks three machine-learning interatomic potentials (linear ACE, Behler-Parrinello HDNN, and graph neural network MACE) by fitting them to classical Lennard-Jones and Stillinger-Weber potentials, evaluating accuracy, overfitting, noise robustness, carbon footprint, and radial distribution function reproduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The choice among increasingly popular machine-learning interatomic potentials is often made without a controlled, rational comparison of their advantages and limitations, so the authors aim to assess these models on a common, well-understood footing.
