Method: They fit linear atomic cluster expansion, the Behler-Parrinello high-dimensional neural network potential, and the graph neural network-based MACE potential to classical Lennard-Jones and Stillinger-Weber potentials, systematically varying training dataset size and testing robustness to noise, carbon footprint, and reproduction of the radial distribution function.

Result: MACE models most accurately reproduce the potential energy surface with little sign of overfitting, but at a significantly higher computational cost.

Conclusion: The findings highlight important trade-offs and raise key questions regarding the choice of machine-learning interatomic potentials in computational materials science.

Abstract: We propose a rational comparison between several well-known machine-learning interatomic potentials (linear atomic cluster expansion, the Behler-Parrinello high-dimensional neural network potential, and the graph neural network-based MACE potential) by testing their performance for fitting classical potentials such as the Lennard-Jones and Stillinger-Weber potential. With this we assess their advantages and limitations regarding several aspects: varying training dataset size, robustness to noise, carbon footprint, as well as the ability to reproduce the radial distribution function as an example observable. Overall the MACE models are shown to most accurately reproduce the potential energy surface, with little sign of overfitting, but at a significant computational cost, highlighting important questions regarding the choice of models in computational materials science.

</details>


### [158] [Mn--Cr dopant association in dilute co-doped $\mathrm{ZnAl_2O_4}$ revealed by combined EPR and optical spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14262)
*Apoko S. Omondi,Mátyás M. Rudolf,Nikoletta Jegenyés,Árpád Jakab,Gábor Bortel,Bence G. Márkus,Adam Gali,Dávid Beke*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Quantitative EPR spin counting combined with optical and X-ray spectroscopy on two Mn/Cr co-doped ZnAl2O4 nanoparticle series reveals that Cr and Mn form an exchange-coupled edge-sharing defect complex; octahedral Mn distorts the Cr site, boosting Cr emission without energy transfer, and above a threshold Mn re-partitions into EPR-silent quenching configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Co-doping is widely used to brighten phosphors for solid-state lighting, NIR sensing, and X-ray/biomedical imaging, but the actual fate and speciation of co-dopants is rarely established, so the origin of concentration optima in co-doped phosphors remains unclear.

Method: Combined quantitative electron paramagnetic resonance (EPR) spin counting with photoluminescence, photoluminescence excitation, and X-ray-excited spectroscopy across two dilute Mn/Cr co-doping series of hydrothermally grown ZnAl2O4 nanoparticles.

Result: The two ions form an exchange-coupled, edge-sharing Cr--Mn defect complex with Mn2+/4+ occupying an octahedral site adjacent to Cr3+; octahedral Mn lifts the inversion symmetry of the Cr3+ site and strengthens its parity-forbidden absorption, enhancing Cr3+ emission without energy transfer. Defect-complex population follows the law of mass action, and above a threshold Mn concentration, Mn re-partitions into EPR-silent octahedral configurations that quench both emissions.

Conclusion: Concentration optima in co-doped phosphors can reflect defect speciation rather than only energy-transfer efficiency, providing a structural design handle for brighter emitters.

Abstract: Efficient and durable phosphors made from abundant elements underpin energy-saving solid-state lighting, near-infrared light sources for sensing, and X-ray and biomedical imaging. Co-doping is a standard route to brighter phosphors, yet the fate of the co-dopants is rarely established. We combine quantitative electron paramagnetic resonance (EPR) spin counting with photoluminescence, photoluminescence excitation, and X-ray-excited spectroscopy across two dilute Mn/Cr co-doping series of hydrothermally grown $\mathrm{ZnAl_2O_4}$ nanoparticles and show that the two ions can form an exchange-coupled, edge-sharing Cr--Mn defect complex, with $\mathrm{Mn^{2+/4+}}$ entering an octahedral site next to $\mathrm{Cr^{3+}}$. The octahedral Mn lifts the inversion symmetry of the $\mathrm{Cr^{3+}}$ site and strengthens its parity-forbidden absorption, enhancing the $\mathrm{Cr^{3+}}$ emission without energy transfer. The population of the defect complex follows the law of mass action, and above a threshold Mn concentration, Mn re-partitions into EPR-silent octahedral configurations that quench both emissions. Concentration optima in co-doped phosphors can thus reflect defect speciation, not only energy-transfer efficiency, which opens a structural design handle for brighter emitters.

</details>


### [159] [A Solution-Processed Ti/Pt/NiOx/Ag RRAM Device with Multiple Forming-Free Non-Volatile Intermediate States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14322)
*Abhrajit Roy,Chopali Chanchal Sahu,Rudraksh Sangore,Raman Hissariya,Poulomi Chakrabarty,Harsh Roniyar,Merlyne De Souza,Debanjan Bhowmik*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Forming-free NiO-based RRAM devices (solution-processed, Ti/Pt bottom electrode, Ag top electrode) exhibit binary HRS/LRS switching and 30 intermediate synaptic states (~5-bit resolution) with non-volatility up to 1000 s, enabling MNIST and CIFAR-10 classification on MLP and CNN models calibrated with measured LTP-LTD data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ANN hardware on crossbar arrays of non-volatile synaptic devices offers much lower energy consumption than conventional computing units. Solution-processed transition-metal-oxide RRAM is attractive for such synapses due to cheap, easy fabrication, stability, and suitable memristive properties. However, most prior reports (both solution-processed and sputtered RRAM) require a forming step to exhibit non-volatility, which increases variability, demands high-voltage pulses, and complicates crossbar design.

Method: Fabricated NiO-based RRAM devices with solution-processed NiO layer, Ti/Pt bottom electrode, and Ag top electrode. Experimentally characterized forming-free binary switching between HRS and LRS, and forming-free pulse-driven LTP/LTD modulation across 30 intermediate synaptic states. Assessed state retention stability. Confirmed oxygen vacancies in the material stack via X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy (XPS). Calibrated synaptic weight updates of an MLP and a CNN against the measured LTP-LTD characteristics, then performed data classification on MNIST and CIFAR-10.

Result: Observed forming-free binary HRS/LRS switching and forming-free pulse-driven LTP/LTD with 30 intermediate synaptic states (~5-bit synaptic weight resolution). Many states are stable for at least 1000 s, confirming non-volatility and suitability for synaptic weight storage in ANN crossbar arrays. XPS confirmed presence of oxygen vacancies. Achieved successful data classification on MNIST (MLP) and CIFAR-10 (CNN) using experimentally calibrated synaptic weight updates.

Conclusion: Forming-free operation in solution-processed NiO RRAM is demonstrated, distinguishing this work from most prior RRAM reports that require forming. Avoiding forming reduces variability, eliminates the need for high-voltage pulses, and simplifies crossbar array design. The devices provide stable multilevel (~5-bit) non-volatile synaptic states and support calibrated ANN classification tasks, showing promise as synaptic devices for energy-efficient crossbar-array ANNs.

Abstract: Artificial neural networks (ANN) implemented on crossbar arrays of non-volatile synaptic devices are known to consume much less energy compared to that implemented on conventional computing units. Solution-processed resistive random-access memory (RRAM) devices based on transition-metal oxides are suitable as such synaptic devices because of their cheap and easy fabrication method, stability, and suitable memristive properties. Here, in NiO-based RRAM devices with Ti/Pt as the bottom electrode and Ag as the top electrode (NiO layer deposited through solution processing), we experimentally report forming-free binary switching between a high resistance state (HRS) and a low resistance state (LRS) as well as forming-free pulse-driven modulation (long-term potentiation (LTP), long-term depression (LTD)) through 30 intermediate synaptic states between HRS and LRS (around 5-bit synaptic weight resolution). Many of these states are measured to be stable at least up to 1000 seconds confirming their non-volatility and their utility in storage of synaptic weights for ANN crossbar arrays. On the contrary, most prior experimental reports (including reports both on solution-processing-based and sputtering-based RRAM devices) require forming to exhibit non-volatility. Avoiding forming reduces variability, eliminates the need for high voltage pulses, and makes the crossbar array design simpler. Further, we confirm the presence of oxygen vacancies in our materials stack using X-ray photo-electron spectroscopy (XPS). Finally, we show successful data classification on MNIST and CIFAR-10 data sets using a multi-layer perceptron (MLP) model and a convolution neural network (CNN) model respectively, where the synaptic weight updates of these ANN models are calibrated against the experimentally measured LTP-LTD characteristics of the NiO RRAM devices.

</details>


### [160] [Atoms that Matter: Largest Materials Datasets Are 99% Redundant, and Local Chemistry Can Guide the Required 1%](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14392)
*Evgeny Moerman,Igor Poltavsky,Anton Charkin-Gorbulin,Alexandre Tkatchenko*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Training MACE MLFFs on <1% of OMat24 (6.5M atoms), selected randomly, by latent-space clustering, or by a new diversity-maximizing method (EnvTok), matches or exceeds the full-data model across forces, energies, and multiple properties, showing dataset redundancy exceeds 99%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose MLFFs are trained on ~100 million structures under the assumption that transferability requires this scale. This work tests whether such data scale is actually necessary, i.e., whether chemical environment diversity rather than dataset size defines information content.

Method: Using OMat24 (largest available materials dataset), the authors trained MACE models on subsets of 6.5M atoms (<1% of OMat24) selected in three complementary ways: (1) random sampling, (2) latent-space clustering of a pretrained MLFF (DIRECT), and (3) EnvTok, a new model-free method that maximizes diversity of discrete, interpretable local environments using atomic positions and species, optionally augmented by magnetic moments, prior to any labelling or training.

Result: All three models approached the full-data reference MACE-OMAT-0 to within a few tens of meV/Å in forces, and remained close to or exceeded it on formation energies, elastic moduli, heat capacities, Li+ migration barriers, and MD stability up to 3000 K and 500 GPa. Performance was nearly independent of selection strategy.

Conclusion: Chemical environment diversity, not dataset size, defines OMat24's information content; the remaining 99% of structures are largely redundant. Broad chemical-space sampling remains essential, but reference labels are only needed for structurally and chemically distinct configurations. EnvTok yields compact, architecture-independent datasets, making hybrid-DFT or higher-level relabelling of general-purpose training sets feasible and freeing budget for extended environmental coverage and multi-fidelity learning.

Abstract: General-purpose machine-learning force fields (MLFFs) for materials are now trained on the order of $100$ million structures, assuming transferability requires such scale. We test this premise on OMat24, the largest available materials dataset, by training MACE models on subsets of $6.5$ million atoms, less than $1\%$ of OMat24, selected in three complementary ways: at random, by latent-space clustering of a pretrained MLFF (DIRECT), and by a new model-free method maximizing the diversity of discrete, interpretable local environments (EnvTok). All three models approach the full-data reference MACE-OMAT-0 model to within a few tens of $\mathrm{meV}/\mathring{\mathrm{A}}$ in forces, and remain close to or exceed it on formation energies, elastic moduli, heat capacities, Li$^{+}$ migration barriers, and molecular-dynamics stability up to $3000\,$K and $500\,$GPa. This near-independence from the selection strategy shows that chemical environment diversity, not size, defines OMat24's information content: the remaining $99\%$ of structures are largely redundant. Broad sampling of chemical space thus remains essential, but reference labels are needed only for structurally and chemically distinct configurations. Using atomic positions and species, optionally augmented by magnetic moments, EnvTok identifies these before any labelling or training, yielding compact, architecture-independent datasets. This hundredfold reduction makes hybrid-DFT or higher-level relabelling of general-purpose training sets feasible, and frees computational budget for extending environmental coverage and multi-fidelity learning.

</details>


### [161] [Large Out-of-plane Polarization in Two-dimensional Bi2O3 Monolayers Predicted by an AI-driven Inverse-design Workflow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14429)
*Hongmei Tan,Jing Zhang,Shan Gao,Yueyu Zhang,Xiangmei Duan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using inverse structural design with a machine-learning interatomic potential and first-principles calculations, two previously unreported polar Bi2O3 monolayers (tetragonal and orthorhombic) are predicted, featuring strong out-of-plane polarizations of 34.12 and 37.15 pC/m, wide band gaps (~3.8 eV), and robust stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 2D polar oxides with built-in electric fields are attractive for nanoscale electronics, optoelectronics, and surface charge engineering, but their discovery is hindered by the immense structural complexity of nonlayered oxide networks.

Method: The authors combine inverse structural design with a machine-learning interatomic potential and first-principles calculations to explore 2D oxide configurations, using Bi2O3 as a representative nonlayered system.

Result: Two previously unreported polar monolayers with tetragonal (t) and orthorhombic (o) symmetries were identified. Both have vertically separated Bi and O sublayers, yielding Berry-phase 2D polarizations of 34.12 and 37.15 pC/m, respectively; the o-Bi2O3 monolayer exhibits one of the largest out-of-plane polarizations reported among 2D oxides. Both monolayers show dynamical stability, finite-temperature structural integrity, and wide band gaps (~3.8 eV).

Conclusion: The large polarization arises from dimensionality-induced reconstruction of the nonlayered Bi-O network, which breaks local coordination symmetry and induces out-of-plane charge redistribution, with the stereochemically active Bi 6s2 lone pair further favoring asymmetric Bi-O bonding. Dimensionality-induced local-coordination symmetry breaking is proposed as a design motif for high-polarization 2D oxides, highlighting the value of inverse design coupled with machine-learning-assisted screening for exploring unconventional polar phases in nonlayered oxide networks.

Abstract: Two-dimensional (2D) polar oxides with built-in electric fields offer promising opportunities for nanoscale electronics, optoelectronics, and surface charge engineering. However, their discovery remains challenging due to the enormous structural complexity of nonlayered oxide networks. Here, we combine inverse structural design with a machine-learning interatomic potential and first-principles calculations to explore 2D oxide configurations. Using Bi2O3 as a representative system, we identify two previously unreported polar monolayers with tetragonal and orthorhombic symmetries. Both structures exhibit vertically separated Bi and O sublayers, giving rise to pronounced intrinsic polarity with Berry-phase 2D polarizations of 34.12 and 37.15 pC/m, respectively. Notably, the o-Bi2O3 monolayer exhibits one of the largest reported out-of-plane polarizations among 2D oxides. Both monolayers exhibit dynamical stability, finite-temperature structural integrity, and wide band gaps (~3.8 eV). The large polarization originates from dimensionality-induced reconstruction of the nonlayered Bi-O network, which breaks local coordination symmetry and produces out-of-plane charge redistribution, while the stereochemically active Bi 6s2 lone pair further favors asymmetric Bi-O bonding. These findings suggest dimensionality-induced local-coordination symmetry breaking as a promising design motif for high-polarization 2D oxides, and demonstrate the utility of combining inverse design with machine-learning-assisted screening to explore unconventional polar phases in nonlayered oxide networks.

</details>


### [162] [Fluctuation-based evaluation of thermal conductivity using deviational phonon Monte Carlo simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14433)
*Takuma Hori,Yuzuho Oki*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A fluctuation-based Monte Carlo method within the phonon BTE framework computes thermal conductivity via mean square displacement of deviational phonon particles, requiring no thermal reservoirs and yielding multiple directions from one simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional approaches for evaluating thermal conductivity in the phonon BTE framework typically require imposing thermal reservoirs and separate simulations per direction, which is restrictive and inefficient. The authors aim to develop a reservoir-free, fluctuation-based formulation that expresses thermal conductivity directly through phonon particle statistics, enabling simultaneous multi-directional evaluation.

Method: Starting from the energy-based variance-reduced formulation of the BTE, they derive a relation between the spatial spreading of deviational phonon energy and the macroscopic heat conduction equation. This yields a formulation expressing thermal conductivity in terms of the mean square displacement of deviational phonon particles without imposing thermal reservoirs. The method is applied to crystalline silicon at room temperature in bulk, square nanowires, and anisotropic polycrystalline nanostructures.

Result: Calculated thermal conductivities agree with theoretical predictions, including the size dependence induced by phonon boundary scattering and mode-resolved contributions. For anisotropic polycrystalline structures, the method simultaneously resolves directional thermal conductivities and captures their dependence on grain morphology.

Conclusion: The fluctuation-based approach is validated for evaluating thermal conductivity across different transport regimes and structural configurations, and enables simultaneous multi-directional measurement from a single simulation without thermal reservoirs.

Abstract: We develop a fluctuation based Monte Carlo method for evaluating thermal conductivity within the phonon Boltzmann transport equation (BTE) framework. Starting from the energy based variance reduced formulation of the BTE, we derive a relation between the spatial spreading of deviational phonon energy and the macroscopic heat conduction equation. The resulting formulation expresses thermal conductivity in terms of the mean square displacement of deviational phonon particles, without imposing thermal reservoirs. Consequently, thermal conductivity along multiple directions can be evaluated from a single simulation. The method is demonstrated for crystalline silicon at room temperature in bulk, square nanowires, and anisotropic polycrystalline nanostructures. The calculated thermal conductivities agree with theoretical predictions, including the size dependence induced by phonon boundary scattering and the mode-resolved contributions. For anisotropic polycrystalline structures, the method simultaneously resolves the directional thermal conductivities and captures their dependence on grain morphology. These results demonstrate the validity of the proposed fluctuation-based approach for evaluating thermal conductivity across different transport regimes and structural configurations.

</details>


### [163] [A Fisher-Geometric Approach to Supercell Pair Distribution Function Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14597)
*Dawei Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper introduces a matrix-free, Fisher-geometric optimization method for refining atomistic supercell structures against powder pair distribution functions, enabling likelihood-aware, second-order coordinate updates without constructing dense Jacobians or curvature matrices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Refining atomistic supercells from powder PDFs involves optimizing many coupled atomic coordinates from a much smaller set of radial measurements—an underdetermined, ill-conditioned inverse problem. While reverse-mode differentiation provides gradients for large systems, standard first-order optimizers fail to exploit coupled PDF sensitivities to precondition coordinate updates.

Method: The authors formulate a matrix-free, Fisher-geometric approach that pulls the information metric of the observation likelihood back onto atomic coordinates, weighting collective displacements by their PDF response and experimental uncertainty. Under isotropic damping, the update solves a damped linear system over M PDF channels, using automatic differentiation to compute Jacobian-vector and vector-Jacobian products without forming the full Jacobian or coordinate curvature matrix.

Result: For Gaussian measurement errors, the update recovers classical Levenberg-Marquardt refinement while avoiding dense coordinate-space factorization. The same computational framework extends to correlated Gaussian errors and robust likelihood-based updates.

Conclusion: The proposed approach combines likelihood-aware collective updates with matrix-free backpropagation, enabling direct second-order refinement of large atomistic supercells against powder PDF data.

Abstract: Refining atomistic supercells against powder pair distribution functions (PDFs) requires optimizing many coupled atomic coordinates using a much smaller set of radial measurements. Reverse-mode differentiation makes gradients accessible for large systems, but standard first-order optimizers do not explicitly use coupled PDF sensitivities to precondition coordinate updates. We introduce a matrix-free, Fisher-geometric approach that pulls the information metric of the observation likelihood back onto atomic coordinates. The resulting update weights collective displacements according to their PDF response and experimental uncertainty. Under isotropic damping, the update is obtained by solving a damped linear system over $M$ PDF channels, using automatic differentiation to evaluate Jacobian--vector and vector--Jacobian products without constructing the full Jacobian or coordinate curvature matrix. For Gaussian measurement errors, the update recovers classical Levenberg--Marquardt refinement while avoiding dense coordinate-space factorization. The same computational framework accommodates correlated Gaussian errors and robust likelihood-based updates. This approach combines likelihood-aware collective updates with matrix-free backpropagation for direct second-order refinement of large atomistic supercells.

</details>


### [164] [Enhancing Quasi-Two-Dimensional Electronic Structure in Bi2O2Se via Chlorine Substitution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14619)
*Yong-Zhen Jiang,Qi-Xun Wen,Xiang Xu,Su-Tao Sun,Feng-Fan Ren,Zi-Xuan Zhou,Xing-Yuan You,Yu-Cheng Kan,Zheng Zhang,Jie-Yi Liu,Yu-Zhou Zhao,Zheng-Tai Liu,Mao Ye,Shu-Hua Yao,Jian Zhou,Yan-Bin Chen,Zhong-Kai Liu,Yu-Lin Chen,Yang-Yang Lv,Cheng Chen*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Chlorine substitution in Bi2O2Se introduces van der Waals gaps while preserving its key electronic features (indirect band gap, high mobility, effective mass), enhancing 2D character and enabling broad bandgap engineering (0.8–3.5 eV).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bi2O2Se is a leading semiconductor candidate for next-generation electronics and quantum research, but its strong ionic interlayer bonding of Se layers limits its full potential as a quasi-2D material.

Method: The authors use chlorine substitution as a strategy to introduce van der Waals gaps and modify the electronic structure. They perform comprehensive transport and ARPES measurements on Bi6O6Se2Cl2 single crystals, supported by first-principles calculations.

Result: Key electronic features—indirect band gap, high electron mobility, and carrier effective mass—are preserved while 2D character is sufficiently enhanced. Selective tunability is attributed to spatial separation between conductive Bi-O layers and dopant Se/Cl layers. Theory and experiment project broad bandgap engineering across 0.8–3.5 eV.

Conclusion: The findings expand application potential of Bi2O2Se-based materials in optoelectronics, nanoelectronics, and fundamental research, and highlight spatial decoupling as a viable strategy for modifying detailed electronic structure of quasi-2D quantum systems.

Abstract: Bismuth oxyselenide (Bi2O2Se) has emerged as a leading semiconductor candidate for next-generation electronics and quantum phenomena research. However, its strong ionic interlayer bonding of Se layers fundamentally restricts the full potential of this otherwise promising quasi-two-dimensional (2D) material. In this work, we demonstrate chlorine substitution as an effective strategy to introduce van der Waals gaps and modify the electronic structure of the system. Through comprehensive transport and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) measurements on Bi6O6Se2Cl2 single crystal, an optimal Cl-doping compound, we show that the key electronic features, including indirect band gap, high electron mobility, and carrier effective mass, are well preserved, while the 2D character of the system is sufficiently enhanced. First-principles calculations attribute this selective tunability to the unique spatial separation between conductive Bi-O layers and dopant Se/Cl layers, which enables independent optimization of interlayer coupling and intralayer transport. Remarkably, our theoretical calculations combined with experimental observations project a broad bandgap engineering across a wide range (0.8 ~ 3.5 eV) within the Bi2O2Se system. Our findings not only expand the application potential of Bi2O2Se-based materials in optoelectronics, nanoelectronics, and fundamental research but also highlight spatial decoupling as a viable strategy for modifying detailed electronic structure of quasi-2D quantum systems.

</details>


### [165] [Finite-temperature adiabatic-connection reduced density-matrix functional theory: a unified framework for i-DMFT and TAO-DFT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14706)
*Jingang Han,Huanjing Gong,Kai Luo,Hongming Weng,Xinguo Ren*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper introduces FT-AC-RDMFT, a unified finite-temperature variational framework in which i-DMFT and TAO-DFT appear as two different reductions, and shows finite-temperature occupations improve bond dissociation and strongly correlated solid-state properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Capturing strong/static electron correlation during bond breaking is difficult for single-reference SCF methods like Hartree-Fock and Kohn-Sham DFT. Fractional occupations mitigate this, as shown by i-DMFT and TAO-DFT, but these methods are built on different theoretical frameworks, are somewhat semi-empirical, and their relationship is unclear.

Method: The authors formulate finite-temperature adiabatic-connection reduced density-matrix functional theory (FT-AC-RDMFT). The natural-orbital eigenvalue equation and Fermi-Dirac occupations are derived from the variational principle, and a thermal adiabatic connection links i-DMFT's empirical entropy coefficient to a characteristic temperature of an auxiliary non-interacting system.

Result: i-DMFT is identified as the exact-exchange-based reduction of the framework, while replacing exact exchange with a density-based exchange-correlation functional yields TAO-DFT. Numerics confirm i-DMFT coincides with the exact-exchange realization up to a constant, and show improved dissociation behavior extends to hybrid and semilocal functionals. For strongly correlated V2O3, finite-temperature occupations substantially lower the overestimated non-magnetic LDA bulk modulus, approaching experiment.

Conclusion: FT-AC-RDMFT provides a common theoretical foundation for finite-temperature, occupation-based electronic-structure methods in both molecular and solid-state systems.

Abstract: Accurately describing chemical bond breaking requires capturing strong/static electron correlation, a major challenge for single-reference self-consistent-field methods such as Hartree--Fock and Kohn--Sham density-functional theory (DFT). Fractional occupations substantially improve this situation, as demonstrated by information-density-matrix-functional theory (i-DMFT) and thermally-assisted-occupation DFT (TAO-DFT). Although useful, they are formulated within rather different theoretical frameworks, are somewhat semi-empirical, and their relationship remains unclear. Here we introduce finite-temperature adiabatic-connection reduced density-matrix functional theory (FT-AC-RDMFT), in which i-DMFT and TAO-DFT emerge as different reductions of a unified framework. The effective eigenvalue equation for natural orbitals and the Fermi--Dirac occupation numbers follow directly from the variational principle, while a thermal adiabatic connection relates the empirical entropy coefficient of i-DMFT to a characteristic temperature of the auxiliary non-interacting system. Within this framework, i-DMFT is the exact-exchange-based reduction, whereas TAO-DFT results when exact exchange is replaced by a density-based exchange-correlation functional. Numerical calculations confirm that i-DMFT, apart from a constant, coincides with the exact-exchange realization of the framework, and show that the improved dissociation behavior from finite-temperature occupations extends beyond exact exchange to hybrid and semilocal density functionals. For strongly correlated V2O3, finite-temperature occupations substantially reduce the severely overestimated non-magnetic LDA bulk modulus, bringing it close to experiment. These results establish FT-AC-RDMFT as a common theoretical foundation for finite-temperature occupation-based electronic-structure methods in molecular and solid-state systems.

</details>


### [166] [Crossed Electric Spin Generation in Multilayers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14668)
*Xukun Feng,Wei Du,Jin Cao,Qian Niu,Shengyuan A. Yang,Cong Xiao*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The authors propose a "crossed electric spin generation" effect in multilayer (quasi-2D) structures, arising from hybrid Berry curvatures tied to spin-layer-orbital coupling. First-principles calculations predict sizable spin generation in Fe3GeTe2 and V2Te2O, offering an all-electric route for spin manipulation in 2D materials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing means of manipulating spin typically exploit either in-plane electric fields or gate (out-of-plane) fields separately. A mechanism that combines both while leveraging the unique quantum geometry of quasi-2D systems is lacking, and an all-electric, geometry-driven approach to spin generation in 2D materials is desirable.

Method: A theoretical framework is developed based on hybrid Berry curvatures unique to quasi-2D systems, which encode an inherent spin-layer-orbital coupling. This theory is combined with first-principles calculations to evaluate the effect in candidate materials.

Result: Sizable spin generation is predicted in the 2D ferromagnet Fe3GeTe2 and the altermagnet V2Te2O, confirming the effect's relevance in realistic materials.

Conclusion: The proposed crossed electric spin generation effect opens a new Berry-curvature route for all-electric spin manipulation in 2D materials, expanding the toolbox for spintronic control.

Abstract: We propose a crossed electric spin generation effect in multilayer structures. This effect combines the advantages of in-plane and gate electric fields for manipulating spin, and unveils a nice quantum geometric structure in terms of the underlying hybrid Berry curvatures that are unique to quasi-2D systems and manifest inherent spin-layer-orbital coupling. Combining our theory with first-principles calculations, we predict sizable spin generation in 2D ferromagnetic Fe3GeTe2 and altermagnetic V2Te2O. Our theory opens a new Berry-curvature route for all-electric spin manipulation in 2D materials

</details>


### [167] [Inverse Designing Reconfigurable Piezoelectric Tensors via Architectural and Modal Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14761)
*Anish Satpati,Marco Maurizi,Hyunmoon Cho,Victor Couedel,Nicolas Mule,Xiaoyu,Zheng*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper develops a graph-based framework to engineer the full effective piezoelectric tensor of architected materials, tracing each coefficient to structural elements and enabling frequency-selective tensor reconfiguration, demonstrated by a compact multi-degree-of-freedom actuator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural crystals are constrained by atomic periodicity and energetic stability, limiting piezoelectric behavior to only 20 symmetry classes. This restricts the accessible piezoelectric anisotropies, creating a need for a design approach that overcomes intrinsic crystal symmetry limitations.

Method: A general graph-representation-based framework is established that links macroscopic symmetry, local polarization, and deformation of structural elements to control the full effective piezoelectric tensor. This allows each tensor coefficient to be traced to specific members that reinforce or cancel it and to be tuned via targeted geometric changes. Frequency-selective tensor reconfiguration is also uncovered, where excitation frequency switches dominant coefficients within a single architecture.

Result: The framework enables programmable control over all effective piezoelectric coefficients through architecture alone, including frequency-dependent switching of dominant coefficients. A multi-degree-of-freedom actuator integrating distinct actuation modes in one block was realized, reducing volume by 90% compared with conventional multi-stack assemblies.

Conclusion: Architecture-based control of the effective piezoelectric tensor opens a broad class of applications where a single piezoelectric architecture delivers programmable sensing, actuation, and mode conversion across multiple directions and deformation states, transcending the symmetry restrictions of natural crystals.

Abstract: Piezoelectricity arises from the coupling between electric polarization and mechanical deformation, described by a third-rank tensor whose coefficients are constrained by crystal symmetry. In natural crystals, atomic periodicity and energetic stability restrict accessible symmetries, limited to 20 distinct anisotropies that can exist. Here we establish a general framework, based on graph representation, for controlling the full effective piezoelectric tensor through architecture by resolving how macroscopic symmetry, local polarization and deformation of structural elements act together. This enables each coefficient to be traced to the members that reinforce or cancel it and be controlled through targeted geometric changes. We further uncover frequency-selective tensor reconfiguration, in which excitation frequency switches the dominant piezoelectric coefficients of a single architecture. These principles open a broader class of applications in which a single piezoelectric architecture can deliver programmable sensing, actuation and mode conversion across multiple directions and deformation states. We realize a multi-degree-of-freedom actuator that integrates distinct actuation modes in one block and reduces volume by 90% relative to conventional multi-stack assemblies.

</details>


### [168] [A Transferable Machine-Learned Potential Enabling Large-Scale Copper-Carbon Simulations from Equilibrium to Extreme Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18545)
*Feiyu Zhou,Wei Qiu,Yan Chen,Jiahui Liu,Yilun Liu,Shengying Yue,Penghua Ying*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The authors develop NEP-CuC, a transferable neuroevolution potential for Cu-C systems, trained on 2,727 relabelled configurations with ZBL and D3(BJ) corrections, and validate it across elemental, carbon, interfacial, and non-equilibrium benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cu-C materials and interfaces are central to diamond thermal management, metallization, graphene growth, and carbon-coating technologies, but atomistic simulations of these applications require a single potential capable of describing metallic Cu, diverse carbon bonding, heterogeneous Cu-C interactions, and close atomic encounters.

Method: A transferable neuroevolution potential (NEP-CuC) is trained on 2,727 uniformly relabelled configurations selected from a heterogeneous Cu-C candidate pool. An ensemble of five independently trained models provides an internal sensitivity indicator via disagreement, a ZBL correction handles short-range nuclear repulsion, and a runtime D3(BJ) correction supplements dispersion-sensitive calculations.

Result: Tests on source-derived held-out and independent external datasets, plus structural, elastic, defect, interfacial, vibrational, and finite-temperature transport benchmarks, show broad but property-dependent fidelity. Large-scale simulations of amorphous-carbon formation and orientation-dependent shock compression of Cu test transferability under strongly non-equilibrium conditions. For Cu/diamond interfaces, preparation history leaves persistent interphase structural differences, and the mean peak nominal tensile stress at 500 K decreases from 12.35±0.71 to 6.65±0.98 GPa as incident Cu energy rises from 1 to 50 eV.

Conclusion: NEP-CuC provides an atomistic framework linking processing, interfacial structure, and physical response across Cu-C systems, with direct relevance to Cu/diamond thermal-management interfaces, diamond metallization, and non-equilibrium Cu-C processing.

Abstract: Cu-C materials and interfaces are central to diamond thermal management, metallization, graphene growth and carbon-coating technologies, but atomistic simulations across these applications require a potential that describes metallic Cu, diverse carbon bonding, heterogeneous Cu-C interactions and close atomic encounters. Here we develop NEP-CuC, a transferable neuroevolution potential trained on a dataset of 2,727 uniformly relabelled configurations selected from a heterogeneous Cu-C candidate pool. Disagreement within an ensemble of five independently trained models provides an internal indicator of model sensitivity. A Ziegler-Biersack-Littmark (ZBL) correction describes short-range nuclear repulsion, while a runtime D3(BJ) correction supplements dispersion-sensitive calculations. Tests on source-derived held-out and independent external datasets, together with structural, elastic, defect, interfacial, vibrational and finite-temperature transport benchmarks, establish broad but property-dependent physical fidelity across elemental Cu, diverse carbon environments and heterogeneous Cu-C configurations. Large-scale simulations of amorphous-carbon formation and orientation-dependent shock compression of Cu further test transferability under strongly non-equilibrium conditions. For Cu/diamond interfaces, the same potential describes deposition, annealing and tensile response: preparation history leaves persistent differences in interphase structure, while the mean peak nominal tensile stress at 500 K decreases from $12.35\pm0.71$ to $6.65\pm0.98$ GPa as the incident Cu energy during the initial deposition stage increases from 1 to 50 eV. NEP-CuC provides an atomistic framework for linking processing, interfacial structure and physical response across Cu-C systems, with direct relevance to Cu/diamond thermal-management interfaces, diamond metallization and non-equilibrium Cu-C processing.

</details>


### [169] [Selective faceting induced by Hexagonal Boron Nitride on curved Platinum substrate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14825)
*Alaa Mohammed Idris Bakhit,Muhammad Saad Mahmood,Khadiza Ali,Anna A. Makarova,Igor Piš,Federica Bondino,Frederik Schiller,Maria Peressi*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: hBN growth on curved Pt(331) induces selective faceting into Pt(111), Pt(221), Pt(441), Pt(881), and Pt(110); experiment and DFT show that facet stabilization arises from the interplay between facet-dependent hBN adhesion and clean-surface free energies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how a 2D overlayer restructures a metal substrate is central to controlling graphene/hBN-metal interfaces, yet the mechanism governing which facets are selected on a curved surface is not fully resolved. This work aims to identify the crystallographic facets formed upon hBN growth and to disentangle thermodynamic driving forces.

Method: Combined experimental and computational approach: LEED and STM for structural characterization, XPS and NEXAFS for electronic/chemical properties, and DFT calculations of hBN adhesion energies and clean surface free energies.

Result: hBN growth on the curved Pt(331) substrate produces faceting with five stable facets: Pt(111), Pt(221), Pt(441), Pt(881), and Pt(110). XPS/NEXAFS reveal that the hBN-substrate interaction strength increases monotonically from the weakest on Pt(111) to the strongest on Pt(110). DFT confirms that hBN adsorption selectively reorders the relative stability of the facets. Comparison of hBN adhesion energies with clean-surface free energies shows that some facets are stabilized by stronger overlayer-substrate interactions while others are governed primarily by clean-surface energetics.

Conclusion: hBN-induced selective faceting of Pt(331) cannot be explained by thermodynamics alone, but is well described by the balance between hBN adhesion energy and clean-surface free energy, providing a general framework for understanding overlayer-driven surface restructuring.

Abstract: This work combines experimental investigations and first-principles calculations to study hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) growth on a curved Pt(331) substrate. Low-energy electron diffraction (LEED) and scanning tunnelling microscopy (STM) were employed to investigate the structural properties, observing that hBN growth led to faceting with several stable facets: Pt(111), Pt(221), Pt(441), Pt(881), and Pt(110). Additionally, the electronic properties were examined by X-ray photoemission spectroscopy (XPS) and near-edge X-ray absorption fine structure (NEXAFS), revealing an increase in the hBN-substrate interaction strength, from the weakest at the Pt(111) to the strongest at the Pt(110). Density functional theory (DFT) calculations confirm that the selective faceting mechanism is induced by hBN, which changes the order of relative stability of the uncovered facets. Although a complete understanding of the process is not achievable using only thermodynamical arguments, the comparison between hBN adhesion energies and clean surface free energies clearly indicates that some facets are stabilized with respect to others by stronger overlayer-substrate interaction, whereas for others the clean surface energetics plays the dominant role.

</details>


### [170] [First-Principles Calculations of the Electron-Hole-Pair Creation Energy in Semiconductors and Dielectrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16949)
*Dallin O. Nielsen,Massimo V. Fischetti*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: First-principles calculation of electron-hole-pair creation energies in AlN, 3C-SiC, 4H-SiC, Si, and SiO2 using DFT, TDDFT, and Monte Carlo thermalization; GW/BSE corrections improve the Si value to ~3.50 eV/pair.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The electron-hole-pair creation energy is a key parameter governing carrier generation, radiation response, and device performance in semiconductors and dielectrics, but accurate first-principles prediction of this quantity—especially agreement with experiment for Si—remains challenging.

Method: DFT for band structure, density-functional perturbation theory for phonon dispersion, and TDDFT for the dielectric/electron energy-loss function. Full-band Monte Carlo simulations of electron thermalization up to ~100 eV gave average pair-creation energies. For Si, GW quasiparticle corrections and the Bethe-Salpeter equation were additionally used to refine band structure and dielectric response.

Result: Calculated pair-creation energies were ~12.2 (AlN), 6.5 (3C-SiC), 7.3 (4H-SiC), 3.10 (Si), and 16.7 eV/pair (SiO2), in good agreement with experiment except Si (~14.9% below the accepted ~3.6 eV/pair). With GW/BSE, the Si value became ~3.50 eV/pair, mainly due to a slight reduction of the energy-loss function below ~10-15 eV.

Conclusion: The creation energy is governed largely by the competition between phonon emission and ionization near the crossing of phonon-scattering and ionization rates, with the plasmon-peak position and shape in the energy-loss function as the other major factor. GW/BSE improves agreement with experiment for Si.

Abstract: We present first-principles calculations of the electron-hole-pair creation energy in semiconductors and dielectrics, focusing in particular on AlN, 3C-SiC, 4H-SiC, Si, and SiO2. We have employed density functional theory (DFT) to calculate the band structure, density-functional perturbation theory to obtain the phonon dispersion, and time-dependent DFT (TDDFT) to calculate the electron energy loss function (via the dielectric function). We performed full-band Monte Carlo simulations of electron thermalization for energies up to ~100 eV and calculated the average energy required to excite an electron-hole pair. We calculated pair-creation energies of approximately 12.2, 6.5, 7.3, 3.10, and 16.7 eV/pair for AlN, 3C-SiC, 4H-SiC, Si, and SiO2, respectively. These values are in good agreement with measured data, with the largest discrepancy being that of Si (about 14.9% lower than the accepted experimental value of ~3.6 eV/pair). To improve the agreement for Si, we used the GW approach and the Bethe-Salpeter equation (BSE) to calculate the quasiparticle shifts of the electronic band structure and the dielectric function, respectively. Finally, using the GW/BSE data, we calculated a creation energy of ~3.50 eV/pair. We find that the marked improvement in the accuracy of the calculated creation energy, when using the GW/BSE approach, is primarily attributable to the slight decrease in the magnitude of the electron energy-loss function when using the GW/BSE approach for energies below ~10-15 eV. Overall, it is clear that the competition between phonon emission and ionization near the intersection of the phonon scattering and ionization rates is largely responsible for determining the value of the creation energy, with the other major factor being the location and shape of the plasmon peak in the energy-loss function.

</details>


### [171] [Direct growth of crystalline spinel GaON films: A wide-bandgap mixed-anion semiconductor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18550)
*Baikui Li,Zecheng Yang,Wenhao Yin,Zijing Wu,Haitao Zhao,Yunwei Sheng,Honglei Wu,Jiannong Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Crystalline spinel γ-GaON films were grown by nitrogen-plasma-assisted PLD, with a band-edge transition near 4.3 eV confirmed via temperature-dependent photocurrent spectroscopy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mixed-anion semiconductors like GaON could bridge GaN and Ga2O3 for bandgap engineering, but competing phases and disorder hinder synthesis and the correlation between crystal structure, composition, and bandgap remains unclear.

Method: Nitrogen-plasma-assisted pulsed-laser deposition (PLD) was used to directly grow crystalline spinel γ-GaON films. By varying nitrogen and oxygen supply, the authors obtained monoclinic β-Ga2O3, predominantly disordered films, and crystalline spinel GaON. Structural and compositional characterization relied on complementary diffraction, atomic-resolution microscopy, and elemental analysis, with bandgap probed by temperature-dependent photocurrent spectroscopy.

Result: The spinel structure and nitrogen incorporation were established. Temperature-dependent photocurrent spectroscopy revealed a clear redshift of the crystalline γ-GaON response, supporting a band-edge transition near 4.3 eV at room temperature.

Conclusion: Direct growth of crystalline spinel γ-GaON films enables investigation of mixed-anion wide-bandgap semiconductor physics and the development of heterostructures.

Abstract: Mixed-anion semiconductors offer opportunities for bandgap engineering beyond conventional multication alloys, but competing phases and disorder complicate their synthesis and the identification of their electronic properties. Gallium oxynitride (GaON) could connect the technologically important GaN and Ga2O3 systems, yet establishing a clear relation between its crystal structure, composition and bandgap remains challenging. Here we report the direct growth of crystalline spinel gamma-GaON films by nitrogen-plasma-assisted pulsed-laser deposition. Varying the nitrogen and oxygen supply yields monoclinic beta-Ga2O3, predominantly disordered films and crystalline spinel GaON. Complementary diffraction, atomic-resolution microscopy and elemental analysis establish the spinel structure and nitrogen incorporation. Temperature-dependent photocurrent spectroscopy reveals a clear redshift of the crystalline gamma-GaON response, supporting assignment of a band-edge transition near 4.3 eV at room temperature. These results open opportunities to investigate mixed-anion wide-bandgap semiconductor physics and develop heterostructures.

</details>


### [172] [Pressure-transferable universal machine-learning potentials: the WBM-MatPES benchmark and Tuga-FF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14891)
*Pierre-Paul De Breuck,Thalis H. B. da Silva,Antoine Loew,Silvana Botti,Miguel A. L. Marques*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper releases WBM-MatPES, a 182k-structure dataset with reference convex hulls and test sets up to 150 GPa, benchmarks five MatPES potentials, and introduces Tuga-FF, a graph transformer achieving 13.6 meV/atom at ambient pressure and 21 meV/atom at 150 GPa.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Universal machine-learning interatomic potentials aim to replace costly ab initio calculations, but their accuracy and transferability are limited by training-data availability, especially under extreme conditions like high pressure.

Method: The authors release WBM-MatPES, a dataset of 182 thousand structures recomputed with consistent MatPES settings, along with two reference convex hulls of different depths and test sets at six pressures up to 150 GPa. Using this benchmark, they evaluate five published MatPES potentials and develop Tuga-FF, a graph transformer trained on datasets spanning ambient and high-pressure configurations.

Result: Five published MatPES potentials agree within 12 meV/atom at 25 GPa but their errors increase dramatically to 0.4-5 eV/atom at 150 GPa. Tuga-FF is the most accurate model in the benchmark at ambient pressure (13.6 meV/atom) and maintains 21 meV/atom at 150 GPa.

Conclusion: The benchmark reproduces a realistic discovery workflow, enables systematic ranking of potentials at this level of theory, and is expected to encourage the community to train higher-accuracy models and generate richer datasets, advancing machine-learned interatomic potentials.

Abstract: Universal machine-learning interatomic potentials hold the promise to replace costly ab initio calculations in materials science workflows. However, the accuracy and transferability of current models are limited by the availability of training data. We release WBM-MatPES, a dataset of 182 thousand structures recomputed with consistent MatPES settings, together with two reference convex hulls of different depths and test sets at six pressures up to 150 GPa. These datasets reproduce a realistic discovery workflow and allow potentials to be ranked systematically at this level of theory, similar to the Matbench Discovery leaderboard at the Materials Project settings. In this benchmark, five published MatPES potentials agree within 12 meV/atom at 25 GPa, yet their errors increase dramatically to 0.4-5 eV/atom at 150 GPa. To address this limitation, we develop Tuga-FF, a graph transformer trained on datasets spanning ambient and high-pressure configurations alike. Tuga-FF is the most accurate model in the benchmark at ambient pressure, with an energy error of 13.6 meV/atom, and maintains an error of just 21 meV/atom at 150 GPa. We believe this benchmark will encourage the community to train higher-accuracy models and generate richer datasets, advancing machine-learned interatomic potentials as a whole.

</details>


### [173] [Spiral Edge States in Topological 2D Sb2Te3 Crystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14905)
*Timothy Carlson,Daniel Lazega,Scott McGuire,Mihirangi Medahinne,Motahhare Mirhosseini,Gabriel Marcus,ChaoChao Dun,Maria Kolesnik-Gray,Vojislav Krstic,David Carroll*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work studies edge state conduction in twinned and spiraled 2D Sb2Te3 topological insulator crystals, where a topological defect connects two spiral and knotted edge conduction channels, modifying the edge state dispersion. Combined EELS, MFM, and transport measurements reveal a distinct edge-localized 0.155 eV excitation linked to two isolated in-gap states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by the prospect of modifying edge state bands by manipulating a system's quantum geometry, which could open new avenues for using 2D topological systems.

Method: The authors combine low-loss Electron Energy Loss Spectroscopy (EELS), Magnetic Force Microscopy (MFM), and traditional carrier transport measurements to probe twinned and spiraled 2D Sb2Te3 topological insulator crystals.

Result: The structure supports spatially continuous edge states within two spiral and knotted edge conduction channels connected through a topological defect. This geometry introduces boundary conditions that modify the edge state dispersion. A distinct excitation at 0.155 eV is identified in the spiral, apparently related to two isolated states within the gap and localized to the crystal edges.

Conclusion: Modifying edge state bands through manipulation of a system's quantum geometry may open new avenues for the use of 2D topological systems.

Abstract: This work explores the edge state conduction properties of twinned and spiraled, 2D Sb2Te3 topological insulator crystals. We show the structure supports spatially continuous edge states within two spiral and knotted edge conduction channels. The channels are connected through a topological defect. This geometry introduces boundary conditions into the 2D crystal that modifies the edge state dispersion. Using a combination of low loss Electron Energy Loss Spectroscopy, Magnetic Force Microscopy and traditional carrier transport measurements we identify a distinct excitation at 0.155 eV in the spiral that seems to be related to two isolated states within the gap. This state is localized to the crystal edges. The modification of edge state bands using manipulation of a systems quantum geometry may open new avenues for the use of 2D topological systems.

</details>


### [174] [Can Universal Machine-Learning Interatomic Potentials Capture Small Polarons? A Geometry-Based Benchmark in Transition-Metal Oxides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17352)
*Vladislav Korostelev,Pjotrs Žguns,Konstantin Klyukin*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Universal ML interatomic potentials can partially capture small-polaron physics in TM oxides, but their predictions depend strongly on training data and electronic-correlation treatment, and all tested uMLIPs fail to stabilize separated polaron-donor configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small polarons govern charge transport, catalysis, and degradation in transition-metal oxides. It is unclear whether universal machine-learning interatomic potentials, which have no explicit electronic degrees of freedom, can describe these localized electronic states.

Method: The authors study H-induced electron localization across several TM oxides, using a geometric descriptor based on TM-O bond-length expansion (Z-score) to detect polaronic distortions, and compare uMLIP behavior against DFT+U predictions, assessing sensitivity to training data and Hubbard U treatment.

Result: uMLIPs show a spectrum of behavior, from delocalized states to metastable small polarons, strongly dependent on training data and electronic-correlation treatment. Missing Hubbard U corrections for specific TM cations in the training data cause electron delocalization, contradicting DFT+U. Some uMLIPs reproduce polaron hopping barriers during coupled proton-polaron transfer, yet none stabilize spatially separated polaron-donor configurations, favoring localization near the H dopant.

Conclusion: Current uMLIPs are only partially reliable for modeling small polarons: they reproduce some kinetic features but fail on thermodynamic stabilization of separated polarons, so explicit electronic-correlation information (e.g., Hubbard U) remains essential.

Abstract: Small polarons, formed when excess electrons become localized through lattice distortions, play a critical role in charge transport, catalysis, and degradation processes in transition-metal (TM) oxides. Whether universal machine-learning interatomic potentials (uMLIPs), which lack explicit electronic degrees of freedom, can describe them remains unclear. Here, we investigate H-induced electron localization across a range of TM oxides using a geometric descriptor based on TM-O bond-length expansion (Z-score) to identify polaronic distortions. We find that uMLIPs exhibit varying degrees of electron localization, from delocalized states to metastable small polarons, with their behavior strongly dependent on the training data and treatment of electronic correlations. In particular, the absence of Hubbard U corrections for specific TM cations in the training data leads to electron delocalization, in contrast to DFT+U predictions. Although some uMLIPs reproduce polaron hopping barriers during coupled proton-polaron transfer, all of them fail to stabilize spatially separated polaron-donor configurations, instead favoring localization near the H dopant.

</details>


### [175] [Pressure-Driven Evolution of the Physical Properties of Layered Y2GaB and Y2InB MAB Phases: A Comparative First-Principles Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14908)
*M. Rabi Us Sani Shapon,Md. Asif Afzal,Jubair Hossan Abir,Raihana Shams Islam,Saleh Hasan Naqib*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: First-principles calculations compare Y2GaB and Y2InB MAB phases under 0–20 GPa, showing Y2GaB remains mechanically stable to 10 GPa while Y2InB is stable only at ambient pressure, and revealing pressure- and substitution-driven trends in elastic, electronic, bonding, optical, and vibrational properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MAB phases are nanolaminated ternary borides whose stability and functionality are governed by strong in-plane M-B bonds versus weaker interlayer interactions. Hydrostatic pressure provides a direct means to tune these interactions, but the comparative pressure response of layered Y2GaB and Y2InB, and the role of Ga/In substitution, remain unclear.

Method: First-principles (DFT) calculations were performed to compare structural, elastic, thermophysical, electronic, bonding, optical, and vibrational responses of Y2GaB and Y2InB from 0 to 20 GPa. Elastic stability criteria, Fermi-surface analysis, charge-density/overlap bonding analysis, and unit-cell as well as expanded-supercell phonon calculations were applied, including spin-orbit coupling effects.

Result: Elastic criteria show Y2GaB is stable at 0, 5, and 10 GPa, whereas Y2InB is stable only at ambient pressure. Within the stable Y2GaB range, compression increases elastic moduli, acoustic velocities, Debye temperature, and lattice thermal conductivity while reducing elastic anisotropy. Both compounds are multiband metals; spin-orbit coupling modifies local dispersions without opening a gap. Y2GaB retains nine Fermi-surface sheets from 0 to 10 GPa, while Y2InB has eight at ambient pressure, with no pressure-induced topological reconstruction. Bonding is mixed ionic-covalent Y-B within a delocalized metallic background; optical parameters are anisotropic. Unit-cell phonon spectra show positive frequencies at 0 and 10 GPa, though expanded-supercell calculations reveal low-frequency sensitivity.

Conclusion: The study establishes mechanically viable pressure windows for Y2GaB (up to ~10 GPa) and Y2InB (ambient only) and clarifies substitution-driven structure-property relationships in these nanolaminated borides, providing guidance for tuning their stability and functionality via pressure and Ga/In substitution.

Abstract: MAB phases are nanolaminated ternary borides in which strong in-plane M-B bonds and weaker interlayer interactions drive their distinctive stability and functionality. Hydrostatic pressure offers a direct route to tune the in-plane and interlayer interactions that govern the stability and functionality of layered Y2GaB and Y2InB borides. Here, first-principles calculations are used to compare their structural, elastic, thermophysical, electronic, bonding, optical, and vibrational responses from 0 to 20 GPa. Elastic criteria show that Y2GaB is stable at 0, 5, and 10 GPa, whereas Y2InB is stable only at ambient pressure. Within the stable Y2GaB range, compression increases the elastic moduli, acoustic velocities, Debye temperature, and lattice thermal conductivity while reducing the elastic anisotropy. Both compounds are multiband metals and spin-orbit coupling modifies local dispersions without opening a gap. Y2GaB retains nine Fermi-surface sheets from 0 to 10 GPa, whereas Y2InB has eight at ambient pressure, with no pressure-induced topological reconstruction. Charge-density and overlap analyses indicate mixed ionic-covalent Y-B bonding within a delocalized metallic background. Optical parameters are anisotropic. Unit-cell phonon spectra contain positive frequencies at 0 and 10 GPa, although expanded-supercell calculations reveal low-frequency sensitivity. These results establish mechanically viable pressure windows and clarify substitution-driven structure-property relationships for these borides.

</details>


### [176] [A grand potential for molten-salt electrochemical phase diagrams from first principles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17611)
*Lorenzo Bastonero,Lucio Colombi Ciacchi,Nicola Marzari*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper introduces a first-principles framework for constructing electrochemical phase diagrams of multicomponent molten-salt systems, using a grand potential and a fine-tuned machine-learned interatomic potential, validated on silicon in molten CaCl₂ and applied to screen ten halide electrolytes for aluminum electrowinning, identifying NaF/cryolite as the stable chemistry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The clean-energy transition drives massive demand for metals, whose extraction is a major industrial carbon source. Molten-salt electrolysis could enable carbon-free production of aluminum, iron, titanium, and critical metals, but corrosive conditions and complex multicomponent feedstocks make experimental development costly and difficult. A predictive, first-principles approach is needed to accelerate electrode and electrolyte design.

Method: The authors develop a first-principles framework to construct electrochemical phase diagrams for multicomponent systems. They derive a grand potential that enables diagram generation at scale, and supply free energies from a fine-tuned foundation machine-learned interatomic potential. The framework is applied to silicon in molten CaCl₂ (validated against experiment and extended to larger structure sets) and then used to screen ten alkali and alkaline-earth halide electrolytes for aluminum electrowinning using three thermodynamic criteria.

Result: For silicon in molten CaCl₂, the framework recovers the experimental phase diagram with a limited structure set and predicts additional domains when extended to more crystal structures. Screening ten halide electrolytes for aluminum electrowinning identifies NaF as favorable, with cryolite emerging as the stable aluminum-bearing phase, reproducing the chemistry discovered empirically by Hall and Héroult in 1886.

Conclusion: The framework provides a first-principles basis for selecting compatible electrodes, electrolytes, and operating conditions, supporting low-carbon metal production from complex source materials.

Abstract: The clean-energy transition is driving unprecedented demand for metals, whose extraction is among the largest industrial sources of carbon emissions. Molten-salt electrolysis offers a path toward carbon-free production of aluminum, iron, titanium, and critical metals. However, corrosive operating conditions and complex, multicomponent feedstocks make experimental development costly and challenging. Here, we introduce a first-principles framework for constructing electrochemical phase diagrams of multicomponent systems to accelerate electrode and electrolyte design and guide the selection of compatible materials and operating conditions for electrochemical cells. We derive a grand potential that generates these diagrams at scale, and supply free energies using first-principles data obtained through a fine-tuned foundation machine-learned interatomic potential. For silicon in molten CaCl$_2$, the construction not only recovers the experimental diagram when using the first-principles framework on a limited set of structures, but also predicts further domains when extended to a larger set of crystal structures. Finally, we demonstrate the predictive power of the approach by screening ten alkali and alkaline-earth halide electrolytes for aluminum electrowinning. Three thermodynamic criteria identify NaF, where cryolite emerges as the stable aluminum-bearing phase, recovering the chemistry that Hall and Héroult established empirically in 1886. The framework provides a first-principles basis for selecting compatible electrodes, electrolytes, and operating conditions toward low-carbon metal production from complex source materials.

</details>


### [177] [Investigation on Thermal Stability of Epi-GeSn Directly on Si (001)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14947)
*Tharundev V,Dushyant Singh,Vigneshswari SR,Subha Maity,Prince Rathor,Krista R Khiangte*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Thermal stability of metastable Ge1-xSnx epilayers grown directly on Si(001) is governed by the initial defect structure set during epitaxial growth, with thinner films being more stable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GeSn-based optoelectronic devices require understanding whether direct GeSn/Si heterostructures can withstand thermal processing, and how film thickness and initial strain/defect state affect thermal stability.

Method: Metastable Ge1-xSnx (~4.5 at.% Sn) epilayers of 70 and 140 nm were grown directly on Si(001) and characterized by high-resolution X-ray diffraction (HRXRD) and Raman spectroscopy. Thermal stability was evaluated up to 813 K using temperature-dependent Raman spectroscopy (532 nm excitation, surface-weighted) and scanning electron microscopy.

Result: Both films are fully relaxed; the 140 nm film shows a small tensile strain (+0.09%). The 70 nm film remains highly stable in Raman response and surface morphology, whereas the 140 nm film degrades severely, with attenuation and broadening of the Ge-Ge longitudinal optical phonon mode and enhanced surface Sn segregation.

Conclusion: Thermal stability of direct GeSn/Si heterostructures is controlled by the initial defect structure established during epitaxial growth, providing key design rules for GeSn-based optoelectronic device fabrication.

Abstract: In this paper we investigate the structural properties and thermal stability of metastable Ge1-xSnx epilayers (~4.5 at.% Sn) grown directly on Si(001) substrates by high-resolution X-ray diffraction (HRXRD) and Raman spectroscopy. Our results demonstrate that both 70 and 140 nm films are fully relaxed, with the thicker film exhibiting a small tensile strain (+0.09%). The thermal stability of the heterostructures was evaluated up to 813 K using temperature-dependent Raman spectroscopy and scanning electron microscopy. The surface-weighted Raman response (532 nm excitation) and surface morphology of the 70 nm film remain highly stable. In contrast, the 140 nm film undergoes severe degradation, characterized by significant attenuation and broadening of the Ge-Ge longitudinal optical phonon mode, alongside enhanced surface Sn segregation. These findings demonstrate that the thermal stability of direct GeSn/Si heterostructures is governed by the initial defect structure established during epitaxial growth, providing key design rules for understanding the fabrication of GeSn-based optoelectronic devices.

</details>


### [178] [A Parameter-Free Reference Hamiltonian for Tight-Binding Vanadium Oxides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17687)
*Jun-qiang Lu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A transferable DFTB model for vanadium oxides is built from superposed neutral atoms, full PBE exchange-correlation, and frozen-core orthogonality, with orbital-resolved screening from PBE linear response; it reproduces PAW/Harris-Foulkes energies and charge responses, but its spinless convexity prevents spontaneous symmetry-breaking site differentiation in Magnéli phases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard DFTB fails to give a transferable description of vanadium oxides. In the tested cells, two approximations (the neutral two-centre reference and the Mulliken charge model) deviate by electron volts and offset each other in the band-index centroid separation between V 3d and O 2p bands: the reference omits electron-volt environmental terms, while the Mulliken model assigns the compact V 3d shell less than half of the self-consistent potential shift seen in PBE. A new construction is needed that avoids adjustable terms and captures both the reference energy and the charge response accurately.

Method: Build a tight-binding model in which the reference Hamiltonian derives from superposed neutral atoms, the full PBE exchange-correlation potential, and frozen-core orthogonality, with no adjustable terms. The orbital-resolved charge response employs three screening factors obtained from PBE linear response plus an unscreened Coulomb range. Validate against PAW references and plane-wave Harris-Foulkes energies for VO2 (rutile and M1), including 3% volume compression and expansion, and against fitted and held-out PBE charge responses.

Result: At fixed geometry and stated numerical settings, the reference energy matches plane-wave Harris-Foulkes energy differences of VO2 with signed deviations of +2.6 meV/atom for M1 relative to rutile (energy difference 92 meV/atom) and +6.2 and -5.2 meV/atom under 3% volume compression and expansion. The charge response captures 21 of 22 fitted and all 8 held-out matched PBE responses within tolerance, giving the V 3d shell nearly the same self-consistent shift as PBE (-3.89 versus -3.92 eV in rutile).

Conclusion: The model reproduces reference energies and charge responses without adjustable terms, providing a transferable description of vanadium oxides. However, the convexity of the spinless model at fixed geometry prevents spontaneous differentiation of symmetry-equivalent sites under the symmetries respected by its operators and sampling; therefore, such differentiation in the Magnéli phases must arise from electron-lattice coupling or an explicit spin or correlation term.

Abstract: Density-functional tight binding has yet to yield a transferable description of vanadium oxides; for the standard second-order construction and the tested cells, we demonstrate that two of its approximations deviate by electron volts and offset each other in the band-index centroid separation between the V 3d and O 2p bands: the neutral two-centre reference omits electron-volt environmental terms, whereas the Mulliken charge model assigns the compact V 3d shell less than half of the self-consistent potential shift observed in PBE. Instead, we build a tight-binding model where the reference Hamiltonian derives from superposed neutral atoms, the full PBE exchange-correlation potential and frozen-core orthogonality, without adjustable terms, and where the orbital-resolved charge response employs three screening factors obtained from PBE linear response and an unscreened Coulomb range. Against the primary PAW reference, the reference energy at fixed geometry and the stated numerical settings matches the plane-wave Harris-Foulkes energy differences of VO2, exhibiting signed deviations of +2.6 meV/atom for M1 relative to rutile (an energy difference of 92 meV/atom) and +6.2 and -5.2 meV/atom under 3% volume compression and expansion; meanwhile, the charge response captures 21 of 22 fitted and all 8 held-out matched PBE responses within tolerance, providing the V 3d shell with nearly the same self-consistent shift as PBE (-3.89 versus -3.92 eV in rutile). The convexity of the spinless model at fixed geometry prevents it from spontaneously differentiating symmetry-equivalent sites under the symmetries respected by its implemented operators and sampling, so within this model such differentiation in the Magnéli phases must arise from electron-lattice coupling or an explicit spin or correlation term.

</details>


### [179] [EpNet-Si: Atomic Form Factors Learned from Band Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14992)
*Qiang Zhu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: EpNet-Si is an equivariant-network empirical pseudopotential method for silicon that predicts per-atom plane-wave form factors from the local environment, achieving meV-level band and gap accuracy on distorted and unseen structures while reproducing DFT-derived physical properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The empirical pseudopotential method (EPM) is fast—one diagonalization per k point, no self-consistency—but its fixed atomic form factors do not transfer between crystal structures, limiting its applicability beyond the fitted structure.

Method: EpNet-Si predicts each atom's plane-wave form factor from its local environment using an equivariant network with 6,195 parameters: a species potential plus monopole, dipole, and quadrupole corrections, mixed by a learned router over atomic densities, together with an s/p nonlocal projector. It is trained through the eigensolver on reference DFT band energies of only 77 cells.

Result: Among 231 distortions of the trained structure types, the 142 gapped cells show mean band and gap errors of 15 and 8 meV; among 192 unseen structures, the 165 gapped cells show mean errors of 38 and 49 meV. Derived properties—effective masses, gap pressure coefficients, deformation potentials, and Fermi-level density of states—agree with reference DFT.

Conclusion: The classical EPM, fitted to a small reference dataset, can be extended into a fast alternative for modern electronic band-structure calculations.

Abstract: The empirical pseudopotential method (EPM) computes band structures with one diagonalization per $k$ point and no self-consistency, but its fixed atomic form factors do not transfer between crystal structures. To address this limitation, we introduce EpNet-Si, an EPM for silicon in which an equivariant network predicts each atom's plane-wave form factor from its local environment: a species potential plus monopole, dipole and quadrupole corrections, mixed by a learned router over atomic densities, together with an $s/p$ nonlocal projector. With 6{,}195 parameters, the model is trained through the eigensolver on reference density functional theory (DFT) band energies of only 77 cells. Among 231 distortions of the trained structure types, the 142 gapped cells have mean band and gap errors of 15 and 8\,meV; among 192 unseen structures, the 165 gapped cells have mean errors of 38 and 49\,meV. Physical properties derived from EpNet-Si, including effective masses, gap pressure coefficients, deformation potentials and the density of states at the Fermi level, agree with those obtained from the reference DFT calculations. These results suggest that the classical EPM, fitted to a small set of reference data, can be extended into a fast alternative for modern electronic band-structure calculations.

</details>


### [180] [Colossal Spin--Lattice--Charge Coupling in Noncollinear Ferrimagnetic NbMnX$_2$ ($X$ = S, Se, Te) Monolayers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17849)
*Peng Wang,Nanshu Liu,Hongkuan Yuan,Xue Jiang,Jijun Zhao*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: NbMnX2 (X = S, Se, Te) monolayers are identified as stable noncollinear ferrimagnets with tunable coupling between magnetism, lattice, and charge, enabling multifunctional 2D spintronics, nanomechanics, and thermal control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Noncollinear ferrimagnets combine a net magnetic moment with internal spin degrees of freedom, offering a route to couple magnetic, mechanical, thermal, and electronic responses in a single material, which motivates the search for stable two-dimensional candidates.

Method: First-principles calculations, symmetry analysis, atomistic spin simulations, and transport modeling were combined to identify and characterize the NbMnX2 monolayers.

Result: Stable NbMnX2 monolayers exhibit noncollinear ferrimagnetic ground states with Curie temperatures up to 313 K. Competing exchange, Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions, and magnetic anisotropy yield field-responsive canted states. Magnetization rotation modifies the elastic modulus of NbMnTe2 by up to 36%, lattice entropy enhances the near-room-temperature magnetocaloric response of NbMnS2 and NbMnSe2, and in NbMnSe2 rotation restores a mirror-protected crossing, continuously closes an approximately 0.13 eV gap, and produces a conductivity-based anisotropic magnetoresistance ratio up to 10,000% at 100 K.

Conclusion: Noncollinear NbMnX2 monolayers constitute a multifunctional platform for two-dimensional spintronics, nanomechanics, and thermal control.

Abstract: Noncollinear ferrimagnets combine a net magnetic moment with internal spin degrees of freedom, creating opportunities to couple magnetic, mechanical, thermal, and electronic responses.
