Using first-principles calculations, symmetry analysis, atomistic spin simulations, and transport modeling, we identify stable NbMnX$_2$ ($X$ = S, Se, Te) monolayers with noncollinear ferrimagnetic ground states and Curie temperatures up to 313 K. Competing exchange, Dzyaloshinskii--Moriya interactions, and magnetic anisotropy produce field-responsive canted states that couple strongly to the lattice and charge carriers. Magnetization rotation changes the elastic modulus of NbMnTe$_2$ by up to 36\%, while lattice entropy substantially enhances the near-room-temperature magnetocaloric response of NbMnS$_2$ and NbMnSe$_2$. In NbMnSe$_2$, rotation restores a mirror-protected crossing, continuously closes an approximately 0.13 eV gap, and produces a conductivity-based anisotropic magnetoresistance ratio up to 10,000\% at 100 K. The combined results establish noncollinear NbMnX$_2$ monolayers as a multifunctional platform for two-dimensional spintronics, nanomechanics, and thermal control.

</details>


### [181] [DiffGA: Steering Frozen Diffusion Models for Materials Design via Evolutionary Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14999)
*Shivang Agarwal,Rodrigo Wang,Kevin Ryczko,Aayush R. Singh*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: DiffGA is a retraining-free framework that injects evolutionary search into the diffusion sampling trajectory, evolving partially denoised states under an arbitrary black-box oracle, and is accelerated by an on-the-fly Bayesian surrogate built on MLFF graph embeddings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Steering candidate generation toward user-defined objectives is central to inverse materials design, but is difficult when objectives are black-box, changing, or non-differentiable (e.g., simulation- or experiment-derived properties). Existing generative approaches require retraining/fine-tuning per objective (needing labeled data and compute) or rely on differentiable guidance that is brittle to gradient noise and undefined for many practical targets.

Method: DiffGA injects evolutionary search into the sampling trajectory of a pretrained (frozen) diffusion model, evolving partially denoised states under an arbitrary black-box oracle. To reduce dominant oracle cost, it couples DiffGA with an on-the-fly Bayesian surrogate: a Gaussian process is fit during the campaign on graph embeddings from state-of-the-art machine-learned force fields (MLFFs), and once it demonstrably ranks an unseen generation, it replaces expensive property evaluations under an uncertainty-aware acquisition rule. Evaluated on three crystal-design tasks using a frozen MatterGen backbone.

Result: DiffGA outperforms diffusion-only, compute-matched, purely evolutionary, and fine-tuned baselines in oracle-assessed rates of stable, unique, and novel hits. Density functional theory is used to quantify MLFF oracle fidelity, showing it is a faithful proxy in key regimes but systematically biased in others. On a representative sample, DiffGA's top hits survive first-principles re-evaluation as physically realistic hits, while the surrogate-accelerated pipeline preserves fidelity at a fraction of the cost.

Conclusion: DiffGA enables retraining-free, black-box-objective inverse materials design by combining evolutionary search within diffusion sampling with an MLFF-embedding-based Bayesian surrogate that cuts oracle cost with negligible accuracy loss. The work also delineates when proxy-driven generative discovery can be trusted by characterizing MLFF oracle fidelity against DFT. The authors emphasize the contribution is methodological rather than proposing specific scientific compounds.

Abstract: Steering candidate generation toward user-defined objectives is central to inverse materials design, yet remains difficult when objectives are black-box, changing, or non-differentiable, as in many simulation- and experiment-derived properties. Generative models offer a solution but typically require retraining or fine-tuning for each new objective, demanding labeled data and substantial compute, or rely on differentiable guidance that is brittle to gradient noise and undefined for many practical targets. We present DiffGA, a retraining-free framework that injects evolutionary search into the sampling trajectory of a retrained diffusion model, evolving partially denoised states under an arbitrary black-box oracle. Because oracle evaluation often dominates campaign cost, we couple DiffGA with an on-the-fly Bayesian surrogate that reuses representations from state-of-the-art machine-learned force fields (MLFFs). A Gaussian process is fit during the campaign on the force fields's graph embeddings and, once it demonstrably ranks an unseen generation, replaces expensive property evaluations under an uncertainty-aware acquisition rule, reducing oracle cost with negligible loss of accuracy. Across three crystal-design tasks using a frozen MatterGen backbone, DiffGA outperforms diffusion-only, compute-matched, purely evolutionary, and fine-tuned baselines in oracle-assessed rates of stable, unique, and novel hits. Rather than proposing scientific compounds, we use density functional theory to quantify MLFF oracle fidelity, showing that it is a faithful proxy in key regimes but systematically biased in others, delineating when proxy-driven generative discovery can be trusted. On a representative sample, DiffGA's top hits survive first principles re-evaluation as physically realistic hits, while the surrogate-accelerated pipeline preserves this fidelity at a fraction of the cost.

</details>


### [182] [Graphene-Confined Growth of Few-Layer PtSe2 via One-Step Selenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15022)
*Timea Ema Krajčovičová,Peter Kotrusz,Viera Skákalová,Agáta Laurenčíková,Igor Píš,Martin Hulman,Kimmo Mustonen,Michaela Sojková*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Graphene oxide capping during selenization confines PtSe2 growth to two dimensions, enabling centimetre-scale few-layer films and revealing temperature-dependent growth mechanisms via in situ STEM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PtSe2 is attractive for next-generation electronics and optoelectronics because its properties change with thickness, from semimetallic bulk to semiconducting few-layer. However, scalable fabrication of large-area ultrathin PtSe2 films is difficult: conventional selenization of pre-deposited Pt tends to produce undesired three-dimensional clustered growth rather than uniform 2D films.

Method: A graphene-mediated confinement strategy is used. A Pt layer is coated with graphene oxide (GO) and annealed in selenium vapor, so that PtSe2 forms while GO is simultaneously reduced to graphene. The graphene overlayer acts as a physical confinement layer that suppresses vertical growth. STEM, including temperature-resolved in situ atomic-resolution imaging, is used to track how selenization temperature affects the growth mechanism and structural evolution.

Result: Graphene-confined PtSe2 heterostructures are synthesized on centimetre-scale substrates, with STEM showing promoted lateral connectivity of PtSe2 regions. Graphene confinement stabilizes lateral growth and yields few-layer films with improved structural ordering; selenization temperature strongly influences the growth pathway and structural evolution.

Conclusion: The graphene-assisted approach offers a general route to fabricate and stabilize ultrathin TMD layers, including those susceptible to environmental degradation, and provides a new outlook for studying their intrinsic properties.

Abstract: Platinum diselenide (PtSe$_2$), a member of the transition metal dichalcogenide (TMD) family, is a promising material for next-generation electronic and optoelectronic applications due to its thickness-dependent properties, transitioning from semimetallic in bulk to semiconducting behavior at the few-layer limit. However, the scalable fabrication of large-area ultrathin PtSe$_2$ films remains challenging, as conventional selenization of pre-deposited Pt layers often leads to undesired three-dimensional clustered growth. This work explores a novel strategy for promoting two-dimensional growth of PtSe$_2$ by employing a graphene-mediated confinement approach. The method involves coating a Pt layer with graphene oxide (GO), followed by annealing in a selenium atmosphere, which results in the simultaneous formation of PtSe$_2$ and the reduction of GO to graphene. The graphene overlayer acts as a physical confinement layer, suppressing vertical growth and enabling the synthesis of graphene-confined PtSe$_2$ heterostructures on centimetre-scale substrates and promoting lateral connectivity of PtSe$_2$ regions observed by STEM. Scanning transmission electron microscopy (STEM), including temperature-resolved in situ atomic-resolution measurements, was used to investigate the influence of selenization temperature on the growth mechanism and structural evolution of PtSe$_2$ films. The results show that graphene confinement stabilizes lateral growth and promotes the formation of few-layer films with improved structural ordering. Beyond PtSe$_2$, this graphene-assisted approach provides a general strategy for fabricating and stabilizing ultrathin TMD layers, including those prone to environmental degradation, providing a new outlook for enabling detailed studies of their intrinsic properties.

</details>


### [183] [Role of defect trapping in the enhanced corrosion resistance of Ni-Mo alloys with yttrium and yttrium oxide additions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15089)
*Jia-Hong Ke,Xinchang Zhang,Jingfan Yang,Qiufeng Yang,Ruchi Gakhar,Rongjie Song*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study proposes diffusion control and vacancy trapping via dilute Y solutes and Y2O3 dispersions in Ni alloys as a corrosion mitigation strategy for molten salt reactors, validated by combined DFT, SCMF diffusion modeling, and corrosion experiments showing systematic reduction in penetration and mass loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Corrosion of structural alloys in advanced molten salt reactors is a critical challenge, as aggressive fluoride salts drive continuous dissolution at the salt-metal interface. Existing mitigation strategies focus predominantly on thermodynamic stability, while kinetic control of atomic transport remains comparatively underexplored. This work aims to exploit kinetic control via point defect engineering as an alternative alloy design strategy.

Method: A combined computational and experimental approach was used. DFT, self-consistent mean-field (SCMF) diffusion modeling, and an impurity trapping model quantified how defect trapping affects effective vacancy mobility, considering first-nearest-neighbor vacancy-Y binding, off-lattice vacancy configurations, and rotational/dissociation barriers, with Y2O3-associated sites treated as fixed traps. Ni-Mo (NM), Y-modified (NMY), and Y2O3-containing (NMYO) alloys were tested in molten fluoride salt at 750°C, evaluating corrosion penetration and mass change.

Result: Strong first-nearest-neighbor vacancy-Y binding, off-lattice vacancy configurations, and elevated rotational and dissociation barriers markedly suppress vacancy diffusivity, while fixed Y2O3 traps further reduce the mobile vacancy population. Corrosion penetration decreased systematically from NM to NMY to NMYO, consistent with reduced vacancy-mediated transport. Net weight loss decreased from 0.52% (NM) to 0.23% (NMY) and 0.09% (NMYO).

Conclusion: The experimental trends agree with predictions of reduced vacancy-mediated transport, supporting an alloy-side transport contribution from defect trapping. Point defect engineering is therefore suggested as a promising alloy design strategy for extreme nuclear environments.

Abstract: Corrosion of structural alloys remains a critical challenge for advanced molten salt reactors, where aggressive fluoride salts promote continuous dissolution at the salt-metal interface. While most mitigation strategies emphasize thermodynamic stability, kinetic control of atomic transport has received comparatively less attention. Here, we propose a corrosion mitigation strategy based on diffusion control and vacancy trapping by dilute yttrium (Y) solutes and yttrium oxide (Y$_2$O$_3$) dispersions in Ni alloys. A combined computational and experimental approach integrates density functional theory (DFT), self-consistent mean-field (SCMF) diffusion modeling, and an impurity trapping model to quantify the impact of defect trapping on effective vacancy mobility. Strong first-nearest-neighbor vacancy-Y binding, off-lattice vacancy configurations, and elevated rotational and dissociation barriers result in pronounced suppression of vacancy diffusivity, while Y$_2$O$_3$-associated sites treated as fixed traps in the model further reduce the mobile vacancy population. Ni-Mo (NM), Y-modified (NMY), and Y$_2$O$_3$-containing (NMYO) alloys were tested in molten fluoride salt at 750°C. Corrosion penetration decreases systematically from NM to NMY to NMYO, consistent with the predicted reduction in vacancy-mediated transport. The same trend is observed in mass change, with net weight loss decreasing from 0.52% for NM to 0.23% for NMY and 0.09% for NMYO. These results are consistent with an alloy-side transport contribution from defect trapping and reduced atomic transport. This study therefore suggests point defect engineering as a promising alloy design strategy for extreme nuclear environments.

</details>


### [184] [Interfacial Coupling Reshapes Nonlinear Polarization in a Spin-Defect van der Waals Heterostructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15189)
*Lena Dalifoski,Eveline Mayner,Poonam Singh*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Polarization-resolved SHG imaging of hBN/PbI2 heterostructures reveals a non-additive, interface-modified second-order nonlinear response that correlates with enhanced defect photoluminescence, linking spin-defect sensitization to an effective interface susceptibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Van der Waals heterostructures can couple optically active layers to 2D spin sensors, but the coherent nonlinear optical response at these interfaces is poorly understood and unresolved.

Method: Polarization-resolved second-harmonic-generation (SHG) imaging was used to resolve four regions of an hBN-based heterostructure: native hBN, neutron-irradiated hBN containing negatively charged boron vacancies (VB-), PbI2, and their complete overlap. Power-dependence and polarization scans characterized the nonlinear response.

Result: SHG intensity scales quadratically with incident power, indicating a dominant second-order process. Adding PbI2 transforms the distorted VB- SHG anisotropy into a predominantly fourfold response, with sixfold stronger SHG than native hBN alone. This non-additive response occurs in the same architecture that enhances defect photoluminescence.

Conclusion: The interface-modified effective second-order susceptibility links spin-defect sensitization to coherent nonlinear optical coupling, establishing SHG as a probe of interfacial nonlinearity in vdW heterostructures.

Abstract: Van der Waals heterostructures offer a direct route to couple an optically active layer to a two-dimensional spin sensor, yet their coherent nonlinear response at interfaces remains poorly resolved. Here, we use polarization-resolved second harmonic-generation imaging to resolve four distinct regions of a hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) -based heterostructure, native hBN, neutron-irradiated hBN containing negatively charged boron vacancies (VB-), PbI2 and their complete overlap. The detected intensity scales quadratically with incident power, consistent with a dominant second-order optical process. Polarization scans show that adding PbI2 transforms the distorted VB- SHG anisotropy into a predominantly fourfold response with a sixfold stronger SHG than native hBN alone. This non-additive nonlinear response occurs in the same layer architecture also shown to enhance defect photoluminescence, linking spin-defect sensitization to an interface-modified effective second-order susceptibility.

</details>


### [185] [Framework for an AI-Ready ARPES Community Database](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15236)
*Zhi-Xun Shen,Jin Gene Wong,Karen Lei,Leon Garcia,Felix Baumberger,Sergey Borisenko,Aaron Bostwick,Ting Cao,Tanny Chavez,Yulin Chen,Monika Choudhary,Andrea Damascelli,Dan Dessau,Thomas P. Devereaux,Sandy A. Ekahana,Alexei Fedorov,Atsushi Fujimori,Yucheng Guo,M. Zahid Hasan,Makoto Hashimoto,Yu He,Philip Hofmann,Zahid Hussain,Harold Y. Hwang,Zhurun Ji,Chunjing Jia,Timur K. Kim,Phil D. C. King,Takeshi Kondo,Alessandra Lanzara,Chun Lin,Donghui Lu,Matteo Michiardi,Sung-Kwan Mo,Brian Moritz,Laurenz Rettig,Eli Rotenberg,Takafumi Sato,Kyle M. Shen,Jonathan A. Sobota,Inna Vishik,Shuolong Yang,Ming Yi,Timothy Zaklama*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper advocates building a shared data ecosystem for ARPES to transform fragmented experimental records into a persistent, collective scientific memory, enabling reproducibility, reuse, and AI-driven discovery for condensed matter physics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Condensed matter stands to gain deeply from AI adoption, but its current publication-centered data organization impedes this. ARPES has accumulated over four decades of spectra, yet only a highly selected fraction of its experimental record is preserved. A shared data ecosystem is needed to unlock this value.

Method: This Perspective reports outcomes of a recent community workshop and proposes a practical path toward a shared ARPES data ecosystem. It reviews lessons from existing scientific databases, assesses the current state of AI in ARPES, and identifies areas of community convergence on data organization, access, credit, governance, and a staged pilot program.

Result: The proposed ecosystem delivers benefits across three timescales: near term, standardized, searchable, and citable datasets strengthen analysis pipelines independently of AI; intermediate term, community-validated tools operating across laboratories enable broader reuse and increasingly closed-loop automated experiments; long term, a diverse corpus of measured electronic spectra could support foundation models for quantum matter and new discoveries.

Conclusion: A shared data ecosystem for ARPES can create immediate value for reproducibility and reuse while laying the foundation for AI-enabled discovery. The paper outlines community convergence and a staged pilot program as a practical path forward.

Abstract: Condensed matter stands to gain profoundly from widespread adoption of artificial intelligence (AI). However, its current means of data organization impedes this endeavor. As a premier measure of electronic structure underlying material properties, Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) has accumulated over four decades of spectra, yet the community's publication-centered workflow preserves only a highly selected fraction of its experimental record. A shared data ecosystem could transform these fragmented measurements and their contexts into a persistent, collective scientific memory, creating immediate value for reproducibility and reuse while laying the foundation for AI-enabled discovery. In this Perspective, we report outcomes of a recent community workshop and propose a practical path toward such an ecosystem. Its benefits unfold along three timescales. In the near term, standardized, searchable, and citable datasets strengthen analysis pipelines independently of AI. In the intermediate future, community-validated tools operating across laboratories could enable broader reuse and increasingly closed-loop automated experiments. In the long term, a diverse corpus of measured electronic spectra could support foundation models for quantum matter. Looking forward, a diverse corpus of measured electronic spectra could support foundation models for quantum matter and new discoveries. We discuss lessons from existing scientific databases, the current state of AI in ARPES, as well as areas of community convergence on data organization, access, credit, governance, and a staged pilot program.

</details>


### [186] [Local-resolvent formulation and assessment of linear-scaling Kohn-Sham calculations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18137)
*Albert Linda*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: LRes-DFT replaces Kohn-Sham orbitals with the diagonal of the one-electron resolvent evaluated at a few complex energies, yielding a linear-scaling, orbital-free scheme that is accurate for insulators and faster than dense diagonalization beyond ~500 electrons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kohn-Sham DFT is expensive because it requires orbitals that extend over the whole system. For insulators, this orbital requirement can be circumvented, motivating a scheme that avoids orbitals and kinetic-energy functionals entirely.

Method: The scheme evaluates only the diagonal of the one-electron resolvent g(r,z)=<r|(z-h)^-1|r> at M equally spaced complex energies on a circle enclosing the occupied levels, offset by half a spacing so no point lies on the real axis. Density and band energy are linear in g, so no orbitals or kinetic-energy functional are needed. Summing the resolvent over these points is equivalent to an occupation 1/(1+u^M), a smooth step bounded in [0,1] for all M. Gauss-Legendre and Zolotarev quadrature reduce the number of points when the gap is small. In 3D, eigenpairs of small windows around each molecule are used instead of resolvents, i.e. divide-and-conquer (DC) with a different occupation function.

Result: For H2 molecular crystals up to 1024 electrons, LRes-DFT agrees with Kohn-Sham to 2.5×10^-7 Ha per electron. On one core it is slower than dense diagonalization below ~500 electrons but 3.5 times faster at 1024 electrons. Accuracy degrades for disordered or compressed systems and for diffuse basis functions. Forces at fixed electron number are exact if the circle moves with the chemical potential.

Conclusion: LRes-DFT offers an orbital-free, linear-scaling alternative to Kohn-Sham DFT for insulators, with accuracy comparable to Fermi-Dirac-occupied divide-and-conquer and favorable scaling at larger system sizes, though limited for disordered/compressed systems and diffuse basis sets.

Abstract: Kohn-Sham density-functional theory is expensive because it needs orbitals that extend over the whole system. For insulators this can be avoided by using only the diagonal of the one-electron resolvent, $g(\mathbf{r},z)=\langle\mathbf{r}|(z-\hat h)^{-1}|\mathbf{r}\rangle$, at a few complex energies $z$. We call the scheme local-resolvent density-functional theory (LRes-DFT). The density and the band energy are linear in $g$, so neither orbitals nor a kinetic-energy functional are needed. The complex energies are $M$ equally spaced points on a circle that encloses the occupied levels, offset by half a spacing so that no point lies on the real axis. Summing the resolvent over them is equivalent to occupying each level with $1/(1+u^M)$, where $u$ is the level's energy measured from the circle's center in units of its radius. This is a smooth step that is near 1 inside the circle and near 0 outside, and it stays between 0 and 1 for every $M$. If the circle moves with the chemical potential, forces at fixed electron number are exact. Gauss-Legendre and Zolotarev rules need fewer points when the gap is small. In three dimensions we use eigenpairs of small windows around each molecule instead of resolvents. This is divide-and-conquer (DC) with a different occupation function, and it is as accurate as DC with Fermi-Dirac occupations. For H$_2$ molecular crystals of up to 1024 electrons it agrees with Kohn-Sham to $2.5\times10^{-7}$ Ha per electron. On one core it is slower than dense diagonalization below about 500 electrons and 3.5 times faster at 1024 electrons. It loses accuracy for disordered or compressed systems and for diffuse basis functions.

</details>


### [187] [Nucleation at the GST--oxide interface and beam-driven ordering in amorphous Ge--Sb--Te: HAADF, EDS and electron pair distribution functions across a 4D-STEM dose series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15196)
*Mridul Kumar,Kirill Sobolev,Alexander Upcher,Yevgeny Rakita*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: In magnetron-sputtered Ge-Sb-Te films, nucleation sites are not distinguished by composition or density; instead, nucleation localizes to a thin oxide/GST interface environment, as revealed by correlative 4D-STEM, HAADF, and EDS analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In amorphous phase-change materials, the location of crystal nucleation governs switching behavior. It was unclear whether nuclei form preferentially at sites with distinct composition or density, or due to another factor.

Method: Six successive 4D-STEM scans with simultaneous HAADF and EDS on a magnetron-sputtered GST film, restricted to the GST layer and its oxide interface. Nucleation sites were characterized via HAADF-EDS composition/density correlations, Poisson counting statistics, and virtual images. The film was classified four ways: HAADF intensity, I(q) phase mapping, PCA of full diffraction patterns, and a self-supervised SimCLR encoder. Electron pair distribution functions (PDFs) were compared after reciprocal-space calibration on the Au cap. Analyses used PySTEMLab, a purpose-built interactive 4D-STEM environment.

Result: Nuclei share the same HAADF-EDS relation as the matrix, and at fixed HAADF the EDS signal spread equals Poisson counting noise (ratio 1.03 +/- 0.05), indicating no compositional or density difference. Yet nuclei are crystalline, prominent in the 4D-STEM max-intensity virtual image (median z = 11.0) but not in HAADF (z = 1.3) or EDS (z = 0.3). All bulk classes share one short-range structure (first shell 2.70-2.74 Angstrom); toward the interface the FSDP moves to lower q, and all methods isolate a thin interface-edge environment with a shorter or missing first shell. The I(q) classification isolates, in every scan, an interface environment containing 62-100% of nucleus pixels (1.2-2.5-fold above depth), persisting even after nucleus pixels are removed. Under 4.7e3 e/Angstrom^2 dose, HAADF falls 17%, the first shell shortens by 0.03 Angstrom, and the FSDP narrows by 9%.

Conclusion: The oxide/GST interface, rather than a distinct composition or density of the nucleating volume, marks where nuclei form. Beam damage apparently orders the film as it damages it (HAADF decrease, first-shell shortening, FSDP narrowing).

Abstract: Where crystal nuclei form in amorphous phase-change materials decides how they switch. We study six successive 4D-STEM scans, with simultaneous HAADF and EDS data, of a magnetron-sputtered Ge-Sb-Te (GST) film, restricted to the GST layer and its interface with the oxide beneath it. Nucleation sites do not differ in composition or number density: the nuclei lie on the same robust HAADF-EDS relation as the matrix, and at fixed HAADF the spread of the summed EDS signal equals its Poisson counting noise (ratio 1.03 +- 0.05). The nuclei are nevertheless crystalline and stand out in the 4D-STEM max-intensity virtual image (median z = 11.0) but not in HAADF (z = 1.3) or EDS (z = 0.3). We classify the film in four ways (HAADF intensity, I(q) phase mapping, PCA of the whole diffraction patterns and a self-supervised SimCLR encoder) and compare the electron pair distribution functions (PDFs) of all classes after calibrating reciprocal space on the Au cap. All bulk classes share one short-range structure (first shell 2.70-2.74 Å); towards the interface the first sharp diffraction peak (FSDP) moves to lower q, and every method isolates a thin interface-edge environment with a shorter or missing first shell. The I(q) classification isolates, in every scan, an interface environment that holds 62-100% of the nucleus pixels (1.2-2.5-fold above depth) and persists when the nucleus pixels are removed before classification. The interface, rather than a distinct composition or density of the nucleating volume, therefore marks where nuclei form. Over 4.7e3 e/Å^2 the HAADF signal falls by 17%, the first shell shortens by 0.03 Å and the FSDP narrows by 9%, apparently ordering the film as it damages it. All analyses used PySTEMLab, an interactive 4D-STEM analysis environment developed for this work.

</details>


### [188] [A Topological Spin on Racetrack Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18297)
*Benjamin Zingsem*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A 360° domain wall in a hollow ferromagnetic tube acts as a localized, stable magnetic carrier that moves at hundreds of metres per second under current drive, without Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction or a maintaining field, and supports readable multi-bit patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnetic racetrack memory needs localized carriers combining rapid motion, robustness to perturbations, and a detectable signal. Existing schemes often rely on microscopic Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction or a maintaining field, which limits material choice and stability. This work seeks a single continuous magnetic shell that intrinsically satisfies all three requirements.

Method: The study uses a 360° domain wall in a hollow ferromagnetic tube formed by two successive magnetization reversals, creating a bound object with surrounding domains aligned in the same direction. Analytical energetics and micromagnetic simulations characterize its finite separation, response to perturbations, and current-driven transport. The role of winding and radial rotation, relative constituent-spin rotation during current onset, plus curved-track and differential-current tests probe radial stiffness and drive uniformity.

Result: The wall has a finite separation, recovers from tested perturbations, and is transported by current without Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction or a maintaining field. Its winding resists unwinding while radial rotation carries an energy cost, giving specific control over stability. Relative spin rotation quantitatively accounts for extra displacement at current onset. Motion reaches hundreds of metres per second, with transient velocities approaching one kilometre per second under stronger drive. A prepared seven-bit pattern retains carriers and local readout contrast after pulsed transport and recovery.

Conclusion: The 360° domain wall in a hollow ferromagnetic tube provides a physical basis for fast, locally distinguishable tubular magnetic bits. Radial stiffness and drive uniformity are identified as controls for intact motion, and the system offers a continuous switching pathway targetable by tailored excitation.

Abstract: Magnetic racetrack memory requires localized carriers that combine rapid motion, resistance to perturbations and a detectable signal. Here we show how a 360° domain wall in a hollow ferromagnetic tube combines these properties within a single continuous magnetic shell. Two successive magnetization reversals form a bound object whose surrounding domains point in the same direction. Analytical energetics and micromagnetic simulations establish its finite separation, recovery from tested perturbations and current driven transport without microscopic Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction or a maintaining field. The texture's winding resists unwinding while radial rotation carries an energy cost, providing a specific control over stability. Relative rotation of the constituent spins accounts quantitatively for additional displacement during current onset. The wall supports motion at hundreds of metres per second, with transient velocities approaching one kilometre per second at stronger drive. A prepared seven-bit pattern retains its carriers and local readout contrast after pulsed transport and recovery. Curved-track and differential-current tests identify radial stiffness and drive uniformity as controls for intact motion. These results establish a physical basis for fast, locally distinguishable tubular magnetic bits and identify a continuous switching pathway that can be targeted by tailored excitation.

</details>


### [189] [Transition-Metal Chalcogenide Nanowires: A First-Principles Study of Stability, Electronic Structure, and Mechanical Response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15214)
*Danilo de Araújo Moura,Flavio Bento de Oliveira,Andreia Luisa da Rosa*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: DFT plus machine-learning force-field MD examine six Mo/W chalcogenide nanowires; chalcogen substitution tunes metallicity, band gaps, stiffness, and Young's modulus, with strain enabling further band-gap control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Subnanometer transition-metal chalcogenide nanowires offer a one-dimensional platform whose electronic and mechanical properties can be tuned by chemical composition, motivating a systematic study across the chalcogen series.

Method: DFT calculations combined with machine-learning force-field (MLFF) molecular dynamics for MoS, MoSe, MoTe, WS, WSe, and WTe nanowires; harmonic phonon/dynamical stability analysis, HSE06 band structures, effective axial stiffness and Young's modulus (circular cross section), formation energies, and 2.5 ns MLFF MD at 300 K, plus axial strain and curvature response.

Result: Optimized structures are dynamically stable within the harmonic approximation; MoS, MoSe, WS, WSe are metallic while MoTe and WTe are semiconducting with HSE06 gaps of 0.52 and 0.20 eV. Axial stiffness decreases from sulfides to selenides to tellurides, tracking metal-chalcogen bond-length increase; Young's moduli range from 392 to 718 GPa. All six have negative formation energies. MLFF trajectories up to 2.5 ns at 300 K preserve 1D morphology and local atomic order. Strain induces band-gap modulation in the tellurides.

Conclusion: Composition and mechanical deformation act as complementary routes for tuning electronic and mechanical properties of ultrathin transition-metal chalcogenide nanowires, supporting their use in flexible nanoelectronic systems.

Abstract: Subnanometer transition-metal chalcogenide nanowires provide a one-dimensional platform in which electronic functionality and mechanical response can be tuned through chemical composition. Here, density functional theory calculations are combined with machine-learning force-field (MLFF) molecular dynamics to investigate MoS, MoSe, MoTe, WS, WSe, and WTe nanowires. The optimized structures are dynamically stable within the harmonic approximation, and their electronic character evolves systematically across the chalcogen series: MoS, MoSe, WS, and WSe are metallic, whereas MoTe and WTe are semiconducting, with HSE06 band gaps of 0.52 and 0.20 eV, respectively. The effective axial stiffness decreases from sulfides to selenides and tellurides, consistent with the increase in metal--chalcogen bond length; using a circular effective cross section, the corresponding Young moduli range from 392 to 718 GPa. All six nanowires have negative formation energies relative to the selected elemental reference phases. MLFF trajectories extending to 2.5 ns at 300 K preserve the one-dimensional morphology and local atomic order, providing evidence of finite-temperature dynamical stability on the simulated time scale. The corresponding MLFF molecular-dynamics energy traces are shown in. We further examine the response of the nanowires to axial strain and curvature, including strain-induced band-gap modulation in the tellurides. These results establish composition and mechanical deformation as complementary routes for tuning the electronic and mechanical properties of ultrathin transition-metal chalcogenide nanowires and support their further investigation as components of flexible nanoelectronic systems.

</details>


### [190] [Three-dimensional reconstruction of weakly scattering objects from single-defocus 4D-STEM data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15303)
*Kousuke Ooe,Yufan Shen,Yuichi Shimakawa,Daisuke Kan,Scott D. Findlay*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper introduces 3D-structure linear inversion (3D LINV) STEM, a fast and direct method to reconstruct 3D atomic structures from a single-defocus 4D-STEM dataset by solving linear equations under the thick weak phase object approximation, validated via simulations and experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D atomic-structure analysis is central to (S)TEM, but existing 3D reconstruction techniques face limitations: tilt-series or defocus-series annular dark-field methods are best suited to heavier atomic species, while dynamical-scattering-model inversion applied to single-defocus 4D-STEM data requires computationally expensive post-experiment analysis. There is a need for a fast and direct 3D reconstruction approach.

Method: The authors propose a 3D-structure linear inversion (3D LINV) STEM algorithm based on solving linear equations relating the measured 4D-STEM data to the unknown 3D specimen potential under the thick weak phase object approximation. This allows direct reconstruction from a single-defocus 4D-STEM dataset. The method is evaluated through systematic 4D-STEM simulations across various observation parameters, including specimen thickness, partial coherence, and shot noise, and further demonstrated experimentally.

Result: 3D LINV STEM achieves fast, direct 3D reconstruction from a single-defocus 4D-STEM dataset. Simulations confirm its capabilities across varying specimen thickness, partial coherence, and shot noise conditions. Experimentally, it reconstructs the 3D structure of a stacked freestanding membrane with atomic-scale lateral resolution and nanometre-scale depth resolution.

Conclusion: 3D LINV STEM provides a fast and direct alternative for 3D atomic-structure reconstruction from single-defocus 4D-STEM data, overcoming the computational expense of dynamical-scattering-model inversion and demonstrating atomic-scale lateral and nanometre-scale depth resolution in an experimental membrane reconstruction.

Abstract: Three-dimensional (3D) atomic-structure analysis is of central importance in (scanning) transmission electron microscopy (STEM). STEM techniques to reconstruct 3D structure include tilt-series or defocus-series annular dark-field, best suited to heavier atomic species, and dynamical-scattering-model inversion applied to single-defocus four-dimensional (4D) STEM data, requiring computationally expensive post-experiment analysis. In this study, we propose a 3D-structure linear inversion (3D LINV) STEM algorithm, which enables a fast and direct reconstruction of 3D structures from a single-defocus 4D-STEM dataset. This method is based on solving linear equations relating the measured 4D-STEM data and the unknown 3D specimen potential under the thick weak phase object approximation. We show the capabilities of 3D LINV STEM through systematic 4D-STEM simulations across various observation parameters such as specimen thickness, partial coherence, and shot noise, and through an experimental demonstration of 3D structure reconstruction of a stacked freestanding membrane with atomic-scale lateral resolution and nanometre-scale depth resolution.

</details>


### [191] [Programmable memristive circuits for reconfigurable collective pseudomagnetic ordering, transport, and learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15346)
*Sichang Qiu,Yuxin Jiang,Yongchao Wu,Peng Han,Xinxin Gao,Zekun Huang,Ce Shang,Ronny Thomale,Tie Jun Cui,Shuo Liu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Identical memristive units linked by programmable connections act as Ising-type pseudospins, enabling reconfigurable collective order, symmetry-governed transport, and reservoir computing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed-architecture programmable hardware lacks the diverse collective behavior that complex physical systems achieve by connecting reusable local elements via different interaction rules. Memristive devices offer writable states and nonlinear dynamics, but translating these local properties into reconfigurable collective order and symmetry-governed transport remains challenging.

Method: Modular circuits are configured through programmable connections between identical memristive units. Binary conductance states serve as Ising-type pseudospins, and signed feedback between units selects uniform or staggered order. Circuit-model simulations examine domain-wall motion and pattern recovery. A symmetry encoder combines measured conductance patterns with sublattice labels and a selected symmetry rule to configure a fixed transport layer. Device-calibrated simulations then probe reservoir computing.

Result: Experiments establish binary conductance states as Ising-type pseudospins with selectable uniform or staggered order. Simulations show controllable domain-wall motion and pattern recovery. The symmetry encoder implements circuit analogs of ferromagnetic, antiferromagnetic, and altermagnetic transport symmetries; antiferromagnetic and altermagnetic encodings share a compensated pattern yet exhibit distinct voltage spreading. Device-calibrated simulations show memristive dynamics generates history-dependent features for reservoir computing.

Conclusion: By separating local memory from programmable interactions, symmetry encoding, and task-specific readout, this architecture provides a modular route from device dynamics to collective order, symmetry-dependent transport, and learning.

Abstract: Programmable hardware typically uses fixed architectures, whereas complex physical systems obtain diverse behavior by connecting reusable local elements through different interaction rules. Memristive devices combine writable states with nonlinear dynamics, but translating these local properties into reconfigurable collective order and symmetry-governed transport remains challenging. Here we develop modular circuits configured through programmable connections between identical memristive units. Experiments establish binary conductance states as Ising-type pseudospins and show that signed feedback between units selects uniform or staggered pseudospin order. Circuit-model simulations then examine controllable domain-wall motion and pattern recovery. A symmetry encoder combines measured conductance patterns with sublattice labels and a selected symmetry rule to configure a fixed transport layer, implementing circuit analogs of ferromagnetic, antiferromagnetic, and altermagnetic transport symmetries. Although the antiferromagnetic and altermagnetic encodings share a compensated pattern, their distinct voltage spreading illustrates how programmed circuit symmetry affects transport. Device-calibrated simulations show that memristive dynamics generates history-dependent features for reservoir computing. By separating local memory from programmable interactions, symmetry encoding and task-specific readout, this architecture provides a modular route from device dynamics to collective order, symmetry-dependent transport, and learning.

</details>


### [192] [Enhancement of local magnetic ordering temperature due to non-magnetic disorder in anti-Heusler alloy Al$_2$MnCu](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15622)
*Soumya Bhowmik,Suvayan Saha,Saikat Debnath,Arka Patra,Biswarup Satpati,Maxim Avdeev,Shishir Kumar Pandey,Chandan Mazumdar*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: In the anti-Heusler alloy Al₂MnCu, magnetic critical analysis and magnetometry reveal no single universal ferromagnetic class but inhomogeneous ferromagnet behavior with locally ordered clusters above T_C. Theory shows that small non-magnetic (Al/Cu) disorder strengthens exchange interactions and raises T_C, while large magnetic (Mn) site disorder has little effect—demonstrating nonmagnetic disorder-induced elevation of T_C.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structural disorder is generally considered detrimental to magnetism, but since magnetic properties depend on both magnetic atom arrangement and the crystal environment (including non-magnetic atoms mediating exchange), the distinct roles of magnetic versus non-magnetic disorder remain poorly understood. Al₂MnCu, with ~50% intra-symmetry B2 site disorder involving magnetic Mn and ~5% inter-symmetry disorder of non-magnetic Al/Cu, provides an ideal platform to disentangle these separate contributions.

Method: Magnetic critical analysis in the vicinity of the Curie temperature (T_C) to identify the universality class via effective critical exponents; dc magnetometry complemented by higher-harmonic ac magnetic susceptibility measurements to probe local magnetic ordering above T_C; and theoretical analysis to quantify how exchange interactions respond to magnetic versus non-magnetic atomic disorder.

Result: Critical analysis yields no conventional single universality class expected for a standard ferromagnet; instead, the non-monotonic temperature dependence of effective critical exponents indicates complex magnetic interactions akin to inhomogeneous ferromagnets. Dc magnetometry plus higher-harmonic ac susceptibility confirm persistent locally ordered magnetic clusters above T_C. Theory establishes that the enhanced local magnetic ordering temperature arises from strengthened exchange interactions due to the small degree of non-magnetic disorder, while T_C is hardly affected by the much larger magnetic-atom disorder.

Conclusion: Combined experimental and theoretical study of Al₂MnCu reveals and explains the unusual non-magnetic disorder-induced elevation of T_C, offering a paradigm for the counterintuitive role of disorder in magnetic materials and showing that non-magnetic atomic disorder can strengthen exchange interactions and enhance local magnetic ordering.

Abstract: Structural disorder is generally regarded as detrimental to magnetism. Since magnetic properties are governed by both the arrangement of magnetic atoms and the crystal environment, including non-magnetic atoms that mediate exchange interactions, the distinct role of magnetic and non-magnetic disorder remains less understood. The recently reported anti-Heusler alloy, Al$_2$MnCu, forming with $\sim$50\% intra-symmetry site disorder (B2) involving the magnetic Mn atom and $\sim$5\% inter-symmetry site disorder associated with non-magnetic Al and Cu atoms, offers a good platform to disentangle the individual roles of magnetic and non-magnetic atomic disorder. Our magnetic critical analysis, in the vicinity of the Curie temperature ($T_{\rm C}$), does not lead to any conventional single universality class expected for a standard ferromagnet; instead, the non-monotonic temperature dependence of effective critical exponents points towards more complex magnetic interactions, akin to inhomogeneous ferromagnets. Dc magnetometry complemented by higher-harmonic ac magnetic susceptibility data, further confirms the persistence of locally ordered magnetic clusters above $T_{\rm C}$. Theoretical analysis establishes that the enhancement of the local magnetic ordering temperature arises directly from the strengthening of exchange interactions due to the small degree of non-magnetic disorder, whereas $T_{\rm C}$ is hardly influenced by the much larger level of magnetic-atom disorder. The combined experimental and theoretical investigations on the anti-Heusler alloy Al$_2$MnCu reveal and explain the unusual observation of nonmagnetic disorder-induced elevation of $T_{\rm C}$, providing a paradigm for the counterintuitive role of disorder in magnetic materials.

</details>


### [193] [Hidden Spin-Valley Hall Effect and Gate-Switchable Electric Hall Effect in 2D Altermagnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15630)
*Jiajun Lu,Mu Tian,Chaoxi Cui,Zhi-Ming Yu,Yugui Yao,Run-Wu Zhang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that in 2D altermagnets with spin-valley-layer coupling (SVLC), a gate electric field unveils the hidden spin-valley Hall response by breaking the symmetry-enforced cancellation between valleys with opposite layer polarizations, producing a gate-switchable electric Hall effect. It classifies 158 altermagnetic collinear spin layer groups, finds 32 supporting SVLC, and predicts Mg2Os2(SO5)2 and the Ca(CoN)2 family as candidates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Altermagnets exhibit spin-up and spin-down Hall currents of opposite sign whose net charge Hall response vanishes due to symmetry-enforced cancellation—the currents are concealed, not absent. When spin states are locked to distinct valleys, these hidden currents form a spin-valley Hall effect. The motivation is to find a mechanism to unveil and electrically control this hidden response for all-electrical valley-spin manipulation.

Method: The authors employ collinear spin layer group (cSLG) theory to classify all 158 altermagnetic cSLGs and identify those supporting spin-valley-layer coupling (SVLC). They predict candidate materials including monolayer Mg2Os2(SO5)2 and the Ca(CoN)2 family, and perform first-principles calculations for Mg2Os2(SO5)2.

Result: A gate field lifts the symmetry-enforced compensation between valleys with opposite layer polarizations, converting the hidden spin-valley Hall response into a net charge Hall signal—a gate-switchable electric Hall effect (EHE). Of the 158 altermagnetic cSLGs, 32 support SVLC. First-principles calculations for Mg2Os2(SO5)2 reveal reversible valley splitting, a gate-switchable Hall response, and a Kerr rotation exceeding 1 mrad whose sign reverses together with the EHE signal.

Conclusion: The results establish a symmetry-based route to all-electrical valley-spin control and identify altermagnets as promising platforms for high-performance valley transport and electrically switchable magneto-optics.

Abstract: Hidden beneath the vanishing charge Hall response of altermagnets are spin-up and spin-down Hall currents of opposite sign---concealed, not absent---whose cancellation is enforced by symmetry. When these spin states are locked to distinct valleys, the hidden currents constitute a spin-valley Hall effect. Here we show that in 2D altermagnets with spin-valley-layer coupling (SVLC), a gate field unveils this hidden spin-valley Hall response.~By lifting the symmetry-enforced compensation between valleys with opposite layer polarizations, the field converts the hidden response into a net charge Hall signal, yielding a gate-switchable electric Hall effect (EHE) enabled by SVLC. Using collinear spin layer group (cSLG) theory, we classify all 158 altermagnetic cSLGs, identify 32 that support SVLC, and predict monolayer Mg$_2$Os$_2$(SO$_5$)$_2$ and the Ca(CoN)$_2$-family as candidate materials. First-principles calculations for Mg$_2$Os$_2$(SO$_5$)$_2$ reveal reversible valley splitting, a gate-switchable Hall response, and a Kerr rotation exceeding 1 mrad whose sign reverses together with the EHE signal. These results establish a symmetry-based route to all-electrical valley-spin control and identify altermagnets as promising platforms for high-performance valley transport and electrically switchable magneto-optics.

</details>


### [194] [Indirect prediction and screening of frustrated magnetic materials using machine learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15683)
*Regina Burganova,Irina Piyanzina,Ruslan Akhmerov,Alexander Sigov,Maxim Fedorov*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work develops a machine learning workflow that indirectly predicts the magnetic frustration index by separately predicting Curie-Weiss and Neel temperatures, enabling screening of frustrated magnetic compounds. Composition-based CatBoost models trained on experimental data with chemically informed dataset subdivision achieved best accuracy for rare earth-oxide (Neel) and rare earth (Curie-Weiss) systems. A conservative frustration index incorporating ensemble uncertainty guided screening of ~67,000 Materials Project compounds, yielding 20 high-confidence frustrated magnetic candidates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The frustration index requires both Curie-Weiss and Neel temperatures, but these are rarely available simultaneously for most materials, making direct prediction of magnetic frustration challenging.

Method: A machine learning workflow for indirect prediction of the frustration index: composition-based CatBoost models trained on experimental data, optimized via chemically informed dataset subdivision, independently predict Curie-Weiss and Neel temperatures. Ensemble uncertainty is incorporated through a conservative frustration index to improve candidate selection reliability.

Result: Highest predictive accuracy was achieved for the rare earth-oxide model predicting Neel and the rare earth model predicting Curie-Weiss. Screening of ~67,000 Materials Project compounds identified 20 high-confidence frustrated magnetic candidates, including experimentally known frustrated magnets and previously unexplored oxide systems.

Conclusion: Indirect ML prediction of the frustration index via separate Curie-Weiss and Neel temperature models enables effective screening of frustrated magnetic compounds, with the conservative frustration index enhancing reliability and uncovering promising candidates for further investigation.

Abstract: Magnetic frustration is commonly quantified by the frustration index, calculated from the Curie-Weiss and Neel temperatures. Because both quantities are not simultaneously available for most materials, direct prediction of the magnetic frustration index remains challenging. Here, we propose a machine learning workflow for indirect prediction of the frustration index and consequent screening of potential frustrated magnetic compounds through independent prediction of Curie-Weiss and Neel temperatures. In our work, composition-based models implemented via the CatBoost algorithm, trained on experimental data were optimized using chemically informed dataset subdivision. The highest predictive accuracy was achieved for the rare earth-oxide model in predicting Neel and for the rare earth model in predicting Curie-Weiss. Ensemble uncertainty was incorporated through a conservative frustration index to improve the reliability of candidate selection. Screening of approximately 67000 Materials Project compounds identified 20 high-confidence frustrated magnetic candidates, including several experimentally known frustrated magnets and previously unexplored oxide systems potential for further investigation.

</details>


### [195] [Reversible intrinsic-extrinsic crossover of the anomalous Hall effect in PrAlSi via Al/Si ratio tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15726)
*Huiyang Yang,Xiaohua Luo,Hongwei You,Wei Ren,Yuntao Gao,Changcai Chen,Zhishuo Zhang,Chunsheng Fang,Shengcan Ma*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Off-stoichiometric tuning of the Al/Si ratio in PrAlSi single crystals reversibly drives an intrinsic-extrinsic-intrinsic crossover in the anomalous Hall effect, with a giant extrinsic skew-scattering-dominated AHC of 1413 Ω⁻¹cm⁻¹ at the intermediate composition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The AHE in magnetic Weyl semimetals originates from distinct microscopic mechanisms (intrinsic Berry curvature vs. extrinsic scattering), but reversibly switching between these regimes within a single material platform had remained elusive.

Method: Systematic off-stoichiometric tuning of the Al/Si ratio (from ~1.05 to ~0.75) across four PrAlSi compositions (PrAl0.93Si0.89 to PrAl0.79Si1.06), combined with anomalous Hall conductivity measurements, carrier mobility and carrier-type analysis to identify the dominant AHE mechanism in each composition.

Result: The AHC exhibits a non-monotonic arch (170–1413–507–164 Ω⁻¹cm⁻¹) across compositions. The intermediate composition PrAl0.90Si0.94 (Al/Si ~0.96) shows high carrier mobility and multi-carrier transport, yielding a giant AHC of 1413 Ω⁻¹cm⁻¹ dominated by extrinsic skew-scattering. Si-poor and Si-rich compositions show lower AHC and intrinsic Berry-curvature-dominated AHE. Deviating from the intermediate composition suppresses mobility and reverses carrier type from hole-like to electron-like.

Conclusion: The Al/Si ratio acts as a chemical knob primarily modulating the Fermi level via compositional band filling, with off-stoichiometry-induced chemical disorder playing a subordinate role. Off-stoichiometric PrAlSi is thus a tunable platform where subtle compositional variation reversibly switches the AHE mechanism.

Abstract: The anomalous Hall effect (AHE) in magnetic Weyl semimetals arises from distinct microscopic origins-intrinsic Berry curvature and extrinsic scattering mechanisms-yet reversible switching between these regimes within a single material platform remains elusive. Here, we demonstrate that off-stoichiometric tuning of the Al/Si ratio (from ~1.05 to ~0.75) reversibly drives an intrinsic-extrinsic-intrinsic crossover of the AHE in PrAlSi single crystals. Across four compositions spanning a narrow Si variation (PrAl0.93Si0.89 to PrAl0.79Si1.06), the anomalous Hall conductivity (AHC) exhibits a non monotonic arch (170-1413- 507-164 Ω-1cm-1). The intermediate composition PrAl0.90Si0.94 (Al/Si ~0.96) exhibits high carrier mobility, multi carrier transport, yielding a giant AHC of 1413 Ω-1cm-1 dominated by extrinsic skew-scattering. The Si-poor and Si-rich compositions exhibit lower AHC values and intrinsic Berry-curvature-dominated AHE, establishing a composition-driven intrinsic-extrinsic-intrinsic crossover. Deviating from the intermediate composition suppresses mobility and reverses carrier type from hole-like to electron-like. The Al/Si ratio thus serves as a chemical knob that primarily modulates the Fermi level via compositional band filling, while off-stoichiometry-induced chemical disorder plays a subordinate role in determining the AHE mechanism. These results position off-stoichiometric PrAlSi as a tunable platform where subtle compositional variation reversibly switches the AHE mechanism.

</details>


### [196] [Ionomer-free Iridium Anodes with Low Loading for Zero-gap Electrolyzers via Direct Atomic Layer Deposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15798)
*Peter M. Piechulla,Iris Blommestein,Anne van Gerven,Henri Pelzer,Ezgi Demiröz,Jonas Englhard,Mingliang Chen,Julien Bachmann,J. Ruud van Ommen*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A novel ionomer-free anode for PEM water electrolysis is fabricated by atomic layer deposition of Ir on custom microporous substrates, achieving performance comparable to commercial anodes at 7x lower Ir loading and enabling catalyst-layer transferability to anion exchange membrane CO2 electrolysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling up proton exchange membrane water electrolysis (PEMWE) is limited by the scarce and costly anode catalyst Ir. Reducing Ir loading while preserving cell-level performance and durability is therefore critical for deployment.

Method: Ionomer-free catalyst layers were built on custom-made microporous substrates using two atomic layer deposition (ALD) routes with the Ir(EtCp)(CHD) precursor: thermal ALD with O3 as counter-reactant, and plasma-enhanced ALD, which tailors catalyst distribution.

Result: Thermal ALD gives mostly metallic Ir/IrOx at 0.21 mg/cm2 Ir, matching commercial catalyst-coated membrane anode performance despite 7x lower loading and surviving an accelerated durability test equivalent to 600 h of intermittent operation. With a low-loading ALD cathode, total PGM loading is 0.24 mg/cm2. Plasma-enhanced ALD lowers Ir to 0.04 mg/cm2 but yields less active IrO2.

Conclusion: Direct ALD catalyst deposition eliminates the need for ionomer-bearing ink, making the anodes compatible with various membrane materials, as shown in a CO2 electrolysis experiment using an anion exchange membrane.

Abstract: Proton exchange membrane water electrolysis (PEMWE) is among the most promising techniques to provide hydrogen as both energy carrier and feedstock for the chemical industry, due to its compatibility with intermittent renewable electricity sources. However, a major bottleneck in scaling up PEMWE is the limited supply of the anode catalyst Ir. This work shows a novel approach to tailoring ionomer-free catalyst layers based on custom-made microporous substrates and two different atomic layer deposition (ALD) processes, both using Ir(EtCp)(CHD) precursor. Thermal ALD using O3 as counter-reactant yields mostly metallic Ir/IrOx and an electrode Ir loading of 0.21 mg/cm2. The PEMWE cell-level performance is comparable to a commercial catalyst-coated membrane anode despite 7x lower loading, and is maintained over an accelerated durability test equivalent to 600 h of intermittent operation. Moreover, using a low-loading ALD-made cathode, the total platinum group metal loading is only 0.24 mg/cm2. Plasma-enhanced ALD reduces the Ir loading to 0.04 mg/cm2 by tailoring the catalyst distribution, however, yields lower-activity IrO2. Finally, direct ALD catalyst deposition needs no ionomer-bearing ink. The anodes are therefore compatible with various membrane materials, as demonstrated in a CO2 electrolysis experiment with an anion exchange membrane.

</details>


### [197] [Latent Heat and Raman Spectrum Change on Phase Transition of Water Including Calcium Chloride](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15821)
*Dongha Kim,Changyoun Lee*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Raman O-H stretching metrics track latent-heat energetics across water's phase transitions; CaCl2 doping systematically shifts these signatures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Latent heat and phase-change energetics are typically accessed via calorimetry, which lacks molecular specificity. The study asks whether Raman O-H stretching signatures can serve as a spectroscopic probe of latent-heat-related thermodynamics and of impurity effects in water.

Method: A theoretical framework linking O-H frequency shifts to hydrogen-bond strength is used to relate entropy/enthalpy changes to temperature-dependent peak position and integrated intensity. Pure water and CaCl2-doped samples were measured under controlled temperatures; spectra were deconvoluted and integrated over defined bands.

Result: Raman metrics show reproducible temperature dependence that captures the phase-transition window, while CaCl2 induces systematic peak shifts and measurable modification of the apparent latent-heat signature.

Conclusion: Raman spectroscopy can act as a quantitative probe of thermodynamic evolution and impurity effects in water and related systems.

Abstract: This study investigates how Raman O-H stretching signatures may be able to track latent-heat-related energetics during water's phase transitions. Prior work on latent heat and Raman analysis of phase change is reviewed to establish theoretical approach. Building on a framework that links O-H frequency shifts to hydrogen-bond strength, we relate entropy/enthalpy variations to temperature-dependent changes in peak position and integrated intensity. Experiments on pure water and impurity-doped samples, specifically CaCl$_2$, were performed under controlled temperatures, and spectra were deconvoluted and integrated over defined bands. The Raman metrics exhibit reproducible temperature dependence that captures the transition window, while CaCl$_2$ induces systematic shifts and measurable modifications in the apparent latent-heat signature. These results highlight Raman spectroscopy as a quantitative probe of thermodynamic evolution and impurity effects in water and related systems.

</details>


### [198] [Epitaxial Oxide Thin Films with High Entropy and Anomalous Crystallinity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15861)
*Joseph Petruska,Christina M. Rost,Jon-Paul Maria,Saeed S. I. Almishal*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This review links PLD growth kinetics and epitaxial constraints to the role of individual cations in high-entropy oxide (HEO) thin films, using rock salt MgCoNiCuZnO as the primary model with fluorite, spinel, and perovskite examples for broader context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PLD enables far-from-equilibrium growth and kinetic arrest of metastable configurations, expanding the compositional and structural possibilities of HEO thin films beyond what equilibrium synthesis allows. Understanding how growth kinetics, epitaxial constraints, and individual cation behavior interact is needed to control the resulting film properties.

Method: A review-based synthesis that connects growth kinetics and epitaxial constraints with cation-specific chemistry across HEO systems. The central case study is rock salt MgCoNiCuZnO and its derivatives, supplemented by selected fluorite, spinel, and perovskite examples. Emphasis is placed on Co valence and Cu coordination as levers for lattice tuning and coherent nanostructure formation, and on aliovalent and size-misfit additions that expand defect and compositional landscapes.

Result: Coupled kinetic and chemical degrees of freedom produce anomalous crystallinity: extensive chemical disorder coexists with exceptional crystalline quality and epitaxial order, including full coherence in selected systems, notably rock salts grown on MgO. These effects enable lattice tuning and coherent nanostructure formation, while aliovalent and size-misfit substitutions broaden accessible defect and compositional ranges.

Conclusion: The combination of chemical disorder with high crystalline and epitaxial quality offers opportunities to discover and engineer functional configurations in high-quality single-layer HEO films and heterostructures.

Abstract: Pulsed laser deposition (PLD) expands the compositional and structural possibilities of high-entropy oxide (HEO) thin films through far-from-equilibrium growth and kinetic arrest of metastable configurations. This review connects growth kinetics and epitaxial constraints to the roles of individual cations in shaping the resulting films. We focus primarily on rock salt MgCoNiCuZnO and its derivatives, with selected fluorite, spinel, and perovskite examples illustrating broader applicability. In MgCoNiCuZnO, Co valence and Cu coordination illustrate how chemistry and growth conditions jointly enable lattice tuning and coherent nanostructure formation, while aliovalent and size-misfit additions expand the accessible defect and compositional landscapes. These coupled degrees of freedom underpin anomalous crystallinity, the coexistence of extensive chemical disorder with exceptional crystalline quality and epitaxial order, including full coherence in selected systems, particularly rock salts grown on MgO. This combination opens opportunities to discover and engineer functional configurations in high-quality single-layer films and heterostructures.

</details>


### [199] [Extreme anharmonicity determines the superionic transition in the prototypical antifluorite Li$_2$O](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15986)
*Lorenzo Bastonero,Chiara Cignarella,Nicola Marzari,Lorenzo Monacelli*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work shows that anharmonicity, not volume-driven harmonic phonon softening, governs the fast-ion transition in Li₂O, and reveals the breakdown of the quasi-particle picture in the superionic regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The high-temperature transition to the fast-ion regime in antifluorite lithium-ion oxide superionic conductors has conventionally been attributed to volume-driven harmonic phonon softening, but this quasi-harmonic description ignores the strong lattice anharmonicity inherent to the fast-ion regime, leaving the microscopic origin of the transition unresolved.

Method: The authors combine non-perturbative lattice dynamics with a first-principles-accurate machine-learning interatomic potential to study the phase transition in Li₂O, and analyze spectral properties using the time-dependent self-consistent harmonic approximation.

Result: Anharmonicity, rather than volume expansion alone, plays the decisive role in setting the transition temperature and mechanism. The spectral function and infrared response reveal a breakdown of the quasi-particle picture in this material and provide experimentally detectable signatures of the phase transition via inelastic neutron or X-ray scattering and infrared spectroscopy.

Conclusion: The results establish the extreme anharmonicity of Li₂O near the superionic regime and provide a lattice-dynamic framework for understanding and engineering the fast-ion transition in this class of materials.

Abstract: Superionic conductors are promising electrolyte candidates for all-solid-state batteries, thanks to their liquid-like ionic mobility. In the prototypical lithium-ion oxide superionic conductor with antifluorite structure, the high-temperature transition to the fast-ion regime has long been attributed to volume-driven harmonic phonon softening. However, this quasi-harmonic picture neglects the strong lattice anharmonicity inherent to the fast-ion regime, leaving the microscopic origin of the transition unresolved. Here, we apply non-perturbative lattice dynamics combined with a first-principles-accurate machine-learning interatomic potential to study the phase transition in Li$_2$O. We demonstrate that anharmonicity, rather than volume expansion alone as commonly assumed, plays the decisive role in determining the transition temperature and the underlying mechanism. Finally, we analyze the spectral properties using the time-dependent self-consistent harmonic approximation, revealing the breakdown of the quasi-particle picture in this material. The spectral function and the infrared response provide signtures of the phase transition that can be investigated experimentally using inelastic neutron or X-ray scattering and infrared spectroscopy. All these results establish the extreme anharmonicity of Li$_2$O near the superionic regime and provide a lattice-dynamic framework for understanding and engineering the fast-ion transition in this type of materials.

</details>


### [200] [An empirical model for deriving information on ion dynamics from $μ$SR data via the conventional Kubo-Toyabe function analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16006)
*Ryosuke Kadono*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper derives analytical relations between the extended Kubo-Toyabe function (with Edwards-Anderson parameter Q) and the conventional KT function, converting artifacts in μSR ion-diffusion analysis into a reliable method for extracting Arrhenius parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When μSR data on ion diffusion are analyzed with the conventional Kubo-Toyabe function, the extracted linewidth Δ_KT(T) and fluctuation rate ν_KT(T) exhibit anomalous temperature dependences. These are artifacts rather than genuine physical behavior, and a framework is needed to relate them to the true extended-KT parameters and recover correct diffusion quantities.

Method: An empirical model is presented that analytically relates the parameters of the extended Kubo-Toyabe function G_z^EA(t; Δ, ν_i, Q), based on the Edwards-Anderson parameter Q, to those of the conventional KT function G_z^KT(t; Δ_KT, ν_KT) used as an approximation. The model is applied to published Δ_KT and ν_KT data from battery materials.

Result: The proposed formulae successfully reproduce the anomalous temperature dependences of Δ_KT(T) and ν_KT(T). Applying the model to literature data on battery materials yields activation energy E_i and pre-exponential factor ν_i0 in the Arrhenius equation ν_i(T)=ν_i0 e^{-E_i/kT} that agree excellently with values deduced by other techniques.

Conclusion: The artifacts arising from conventional KT analysis of μSR ion-diffusion data can be quantitatively explained and corrected, transforming them into a powerful diagnostic tool for extracting accurate ion-diffusion parameters.

Abstract: We present an empirical model that analytically relates the parameters of the extended Kubo-Toyabe (KT) function $G_z^{\rm EA}(t;Δ,ν_{\rm i},Q)$, which describes muon spin relaxation ($μ$SR) induced by jump diffusion of ions using the Edwards-Anderson parameter $Q$, to those of the conventional KT function $G_z^{\rm KT}(t;Δ_{\rm KT},ν_{\rm KT})$ that serves as an approximation of $G_z^{\rm EA}(t)$. The proposed formulae successfully reproduce the anomalous temperature dependences of the linewidth $Δ_{\rm KT}(T)$ and the fluctuation rate $ν_{\rm KT}(T)$ which are artifacts observed upon analyzing $μ$SR data on ion diffusion using $G_z^{\rm KT}(t)$. Applying this model to $Δ_{\rm KT}$ and $ν_{\rm KT}$ published in the literature of battery materials yields activation energy $E_{\rm i}$ and pre-exponential factor $ν_{\rm i0}$ in the Arrhenius equation for ion diffusion, $ν_{\rm i}(T)=ν_{\rm i0} e^{-E_{\rm i}/kT}$, that are in excellent agreement with those deduced using other techniques, transforming peculiar artifacts into a powerful diagnostic tool.

</details>


### [201] [Nonrelativistic Optically-Induced Spin Torque in Noncollinear Magnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16052)
*Zhiyuan Li,Jian Zhou*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper establishes a general spin group-theoretical framework showing that symmetric operators in spin and lattice spaces govern nonrelativistic light-induced spin torque and quantum geometry in noncollinear magnets, yielding much stronger opto-spintronic effects than relativistic (SOC-based) mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optically manipulating spin in a contactless, non-invasive way is a forefront topic in spintronics. However, prior studies on optically-induced spin reorientation generally scale with spin-orbit coupling (SOC) strength, which restricts material choice to systems containing heavy elements. The authors aim to overcome this SOC-imposed limitation by extending the effect into the nonrelativistic regime.

Method: Using a general spin group-theoretical investigation, the authors analyze how symmetric operators in both spin and lattice spaces control the spin torque and the quantum geometry of Bloch wavefunctions, including the quantum metric and Berry curvature. They describe site-resolved spin symmetries and derive the associated selection rules. They then adopt a Kagomé lattice model and a realistic material family to demonstrate local spin torque under light irradiation.

Result: The analysis reveals selection rules governing nonrelativistic light-induced spin torque and quantum geometry in noncollinear magnetic configurations. The demonstrated local spin torque under light irradiation is significantly stronger than SOC-based counterparts, because nonrelativistic spin splitting typically reaches the order of electron-volts, far exceeding typical spin-orbit-induced splitting.

Conclusion: This work extends opto-spintronics from the relativistic limit to the nonrelativistic limit, greatly broadening the range of applicable material platforms for contactless, non-invasive optical spin manipulation.

Abstract: Manipulating spin using contactless and non-invasive optical approaches is one of the forefront topic in the modern spintronic science and technology. Prior studies on optically-induced spin reorientation usually scales with spin-orbit coupling strength, setting a limitation on composing heavy elements. In this work, we extend such a limit into nonrelativistic regime by exploring noncollinear magnetic configuration. Using a general spin group-theoretical investigation, we show how the symmetric operators in both spin and lattice spaces control the spin torque and quantum geometry of Bloch wavefunction--including both quantum metric and Berry curvature. We describe their site-resolved spin symmetries and unravel their selection rules. Furthermore, we adopt a Kagomé lattice model and a realistic material family to showcase the local spin torque under light irradiation. Since the nonrelativistic spin splitting usually spans on the order of electron-volt, significantly larger than typical spin-orbit induced splitting, the nonrelativistic light-induced spin torque is concomitantly much stronger. This work extends the previous opto-spintronics from relativistic to nonrelativistic limit, significantly broadening its realization platform.

</details>


### [202] [Vibrational Fingerprints of Competing Structural and Polar Phases in CuInP2S6](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16091)
*Pegah Mohammadi,Sobhit Singh*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: First-principles DFT calculations provide a comprehensive vibrational framework (phonon spectra, Raman/IR-active modes, and Porto-notation selection rules) to unambiguously identify the competing paraelectric, ferroelectric, and antiferroelectric phases of layered CuInP2S6.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CuInP2S6 exhibits a rich sequence of temperature- and pressure-driven structural phase transitions yielding multiple phases with distinct properties, but their unambiguous identification is difficult due to structural similarities and complex vibrational responses. A reliable vibrational fingerprinting approach is needed.

Method: First-principles density functional theory (DFT) calculations of vibrational properties for reference paraelectric C2/c and P-31c phases, their dynamically stable ferroelectric Cc and P31c counterparts, and antiferroelectric P-1 and P-3 phases. Phonon spectra and symmetry analysis yield Raman- and infrared-active modes; Porto notation is used to derive polarization- and crystal-orientation-dependent Raman selection rules for different backscattering geometries.

Result: Characteristic vibrational fingerprints distinguishing each candidate structure are identified; Raman- and IR-active modes are determined for all phases, and modes expected to emerge or vanish under specific experimental configurations are pinpointed via selection rules.

Conclusion: The work delivers a comprehensive theoretical framework for interpreting Raman and infrared measurements of CuInP2S6 and for reliably identifying its competing structural, ferroelectric, and antiferroelectric phases across temperature- and pressure-driven transitions.

Abstract: Layered van der Waals CuInP2S6 crystals exhibit a rich sequence of temperature- and pressure-driven phase transitions, giving rise to multiple structural phases with distinct physical properties. Despite extensive experimental and theoretical efforts, unambiguous identification of these phases remains challenging because of their structural similarities and complex vibrational responses. In this work, we employ first-principles density functional theory calculations to systematically investigate the vibrational properties of the reference paraelectric C2/c and P-31c phases and their corresponding dynamically stable ferroelectric Cc and P31c phases, as well as the antiferroelectric P-1 and P-3 phases. From calculated phonon spectra and symmetry analysis, we determine the Raman- and infrared-active modes of each candidate structure and identify characteristic vibrational fingerprints that enable their phase determination through vibrational spectroscopy. Furthermore, using Porto notation, we establish polarization- and crystal-orientation-dependent Raman selection rules for different backscattering geometries and identify the modes that are expected to emerge or vanish under specific experimental configurations.
