Thus, our results provide a comprehensive theoretical framework for interpreting Raman and infrared measurements of CuInP2S6 and for reliably identifying its competing structural, ferroelectric, and antiferroelectric phases across temperature- and pressure-driven phase transitions.

</details>


### [203] [Angle-Resolved Photoemission as a Sensitive Probe of Surface Quality: Sputtered versus Annealed Cu(001)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16421)
*Hyeonhee Na,Jeonghwan Oh,Jinju Lee,Hanjun Sim,Myeongun Go,Jimin Sohn,Jeonggu Kang,Byungmin Sohn*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Sharp LEED patterns and clean XPS core levels do not guarantee an ARPES-suitable surface: Ar+ sputtering of Cu(001) preserves the (1x1) LEED pattern and Cu 2p spectra but destroys band dispersion, which only recovers after annealing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ARPES requires a well-ordered surface, yet surface quality is routinely judged by LEED and XPS rather than by ARPES itself. The paper tests whether these conventional probes are sufficient indicators of ARPES readiness.

Method: The authors compared in-situ LEED, XPS, and ARPES measurements on a Cu(001) single crystal at four preparation stages: as-prepared, aged 24 h at 6 K, Ar+ sputtered, and annealed.

Result: Sputtered and annealed surfaces showed nearly identical (1x1) LEED patterns and Cu 2p core levels, but the sputtered surface gave a momentum-independent ARPES spectrum; band dispersion was restored only after annealing.

Conclusion: The contrast arises because LEED reflects the average lattice, which remains periodic after sputtering, whereas ARPES reflects electronic states whose crystal momentum becomes ill-defined. A sharp LEED pattern and clean core-level spectra therefore do not guarantee an ARPES-suitable surface.

Abstract: Angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) requires a well-ordered surface, whose quality is routinely judged from low-energy electron diffraction (LEED) and x-ray photoelectron spectroscopy (XPS). We compare LEED, XPS, and ARPES of a Cu(001) single crystal measured in situ at four stages of surface preparation: as-prepared, after aging for 24 h at 6 K, after Ar$^+$ sputtering, and after annealing. The sputtered and annealed surfaces show nearly identical $(1\times1)$ LEED patterns and Cu 2$p$ core levels, but the sputtered surface yields a momentum-independent ARPES spectrum, and the band dispersion recovers after annealing. We attribute this contrast to how each probe responds to sputtering-induced defects: LEED reflects the average lattice, which remains periodic, whereas ARPES reflects the electronic states, whose crystal momentum is no longer well defined. Our results indicate that a sharp LEED pattern and clean core-level spectra do not guarantee a surface suitable for ARPES.

</details>


### [204] [Decoupling Trap States in Amorphous Oxide Semiconductor Transistor Using a Conductive-Semiconducting-Conductive MOS Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16438)
*Kai Chen,Wei Zhang,Xuanyao Fong,Shengman Li,Yunlong Li*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A conductive-semiconducting-conductive (CSC) MOS test structure is proposed to decouple and bias-resolve trap-induced conductance losses in amorphous oxide semiconductor (AOS) transistors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional test structures suffer from non-ideal metal-AOS contacts that convolute conductance loss arising from various trap states, preventing clean separation of trap contributions.

Method: A CSC MOS test structure is introduced that mitigates contact-related artifacts, enabling decoupling of interface and shallow states. Contributions from leakage, series resistance, and device-geometry effects are identified and calibrated to extract bias-resolved trap profiles.

Result: Three regimes are revealed: (1) depletion — interface states dominate with sub-microsecond time constants, donor-like, density rising with deeper depletion; (2) weak-to-moderate accumulation — shallow states dominate, with a Gaussian-like volumetric DOS and response times from microseconds to nanoseconds; (3) strong accumulation — interface states regain dominance with increased quantity but invariant time constants.

Conclusion: The bias-resolved profiles clarify how distinct trap populations affect AOS transistors under different operating conditions, offering intuitive reliability-degradation metrics. The CSC structure provides a simple, scalable, robust platform for trap characterization.

Abstract: We propose a novel amorphous oxide semiconductor (AOS)-based conductive-semiconducting-conductive (CSC) MOS test structure to effectively sense, discriminate, and analyze distinct trap-induced conductance losses in AOS transistors. In conventional test structures, non-ideal metal-AOS contacts convolute the conductance loss generated by various trap states. The CSC architecture mitigates these contact-related artifacts, enabling the decoupling of interface states and shallow states. Furthermore, the contributions from leakage, series resistance, and device-geometry effects are identified and calibrated. Our results yield bias-resolved trap profiles and reveal three distinct regimes: (1) In the depletion region, interface states dominate the conductance loss. These states exhibit sub-microsecond time constants and their density increases slightly with deeper depletion, which is associated with donor-like states. (2) From weak to moderate accumulation, shallow states become the primary contributor, displaying a Gaussian-like volumetric density-of-states distribution with response times spanning from microseconds to nanoseconds. (3) In the strong accumulation regime, interface states regain dominance with an increased state quantity, while their time constants remain invariant. These bias-resolved profiles clarify how different trap populations impact AOS transistors under distinct operating conditions, providing intuitive metrics for understanding reliability degradation. Consequently, the CSC structure offers a simple, scalable, and robust platform for trap characterization in AOS transistors.

</details>


### [205] [Modelling the dynamics of the martensitic transformation in Ni$_2$MnGa with machine-learning force-fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16467)
*Markus E. Gruner,Yulia Klunnikova,Mike J. Bruckhoff,Konstatin P. Skokov,Olga N. Miroshkina*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Machine-learning force fields trained on DFT data reproduce the Ni2MnGa soft TA2 phonon and nanotwinning, enabling large-scale MD of magnetic shape memory alloys.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Martensitic transformations in Ni2MnGa and related Heusler alloys involve a delicate interplay of electronic structure, magnetism, and lattice dynamics. DFT has mapped the energy landscape of modulated martensites but is computationally prohibitive for finite-temperature dynamics across relevant length and time scales. A gap therefore exists between accurate DFT energetics and accessible large-scale simulations of martensitic transitions.

Method: DFT-trained machine-learning force fields (ML-FFs) were constructed by interpolation from ab initio molecular dynamics data. Classical molecular dynamics simulations using these ML-FFs were then employed to study the austenite-martensite transition and adaptive nanotwinning.

Result: The ML-FFs reproduce key features of Ni2MnGa, including the soft TA2 phonon and a preference for nanotwinned martensites. Adaptive nanotwinning is shown to facilitate both forward and backward austenite-martensite transformations. The negative twin boundary energy along the entire transformation path, predicted by DFT at T=0, is found to support the formation of ordered modulated martensites at finite temperatures.

Conclusion: DFT-trained ML-FFs enable classical MD simulations of magnetic shape memory alloys with near-DFT accuracy at extended length and time scales, providing insight into the role of nanotwinning in the austenite-martensite transition.

Abstract: The martensitic transformations in stoichiometric Ni$_2$MnGa and related Heusler alloys are governed by a delicate interplay between electronic structure, magnetism, and lattice dynamics. While density functional theory (DFT) has successfully mapped the energy landscape of modulated martensites, it remains computationally prohibitive for capturing finite-temperature dynamics across relevant length and time scales. Here, we bridge this gap by constructing DFT-trained machine-learning force-fields (ML-FF) interpolated from ab initio molecular dynamics. We demonstrate that ML-FF trained on DFT data can reproduce key features of Ni$_2$MnGa, including the soft TA$_2$ phonon and a preference for nanotwinned martensites. Using classical molecular dynamics simulations, we explore the impact of adaptive nanotwinning on the austenite-martensite transition, which facilitates the dynamics of both, forward and backward transformation. We demonstrate, that the negative twin boundary energy along the entire transformations path predicted by DFT for $T=0$, supports the formation of ordered modulated martensites at finite temperatures. Our results pave the way for large-scale simulations of magnetic shape memory alloys with near-DFT accuracy.

</details>


### [206] [Theoretical Investigation of Oxygen-Vacancy Diffusion in SrTiO$_3$ using Neural Network Potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16521)
*Kazutaka Nishiguchi,Ryota Yamamoto,Seiichiro Ten-no*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A neural network potential (NNP) trained on AIMD data reproduces DFT energetics and enables large-scale, long-time MD of oxygen-vacancy diffusion in SrTiO3, yielding migration enthalpy (0.64 eV) close to experiment (0.67 eV).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Oxygen-vacancy formation and diffusion govern electronic and atomic transport in SrTiO3, but direct ab initio MD (DFT-based) study of these defects is restricted to small systems and short time scales due to high computational cost, while conventional classical MD lacks accuracy.

Method: Develop a neural network potential (NNP) for pristine and oxygen-deficient STO using a reference dataset generated from AIMD simulations; validate against DFT energies, forces, and virials; perform large-scale, long-time NNP-MD simulations of oxygen-vacancy diffusion and carry out an Arrhenius analysis.

Result: The trained NNP accurately reproduces DFT reference energies, atomic forces, and virials for pristine and oxygen-deficient STO, and captures supercell-size dependence of total energies and vacancy formation energies, including the dilute-limit extrapolation. NNP-MD oxygen-vacancy diffusion coefficients agree substantially better with experimental data than classical MD, with a migration enthalpy of 0.64 eV versus the experimental 0.67 eV.

Conclusion: NNPs trained on first-principles data provide an accurate and computationally efficient framework for investigating defect formation and long-time vacancy transport in perovskite oxides.

Abstract: Oxygen-vacancy formation and diffusion play important roles in determining the electronic and atomic transport properties of SrTiO$_3$ (STO). Accurate analysis of such point defects requires sufficiently large-scale and long-time molecular dynamics (MD) simulations; however, direct investigations of oxygen-vacancy dynamics using {\it ab initio} MD (AIMD) based on density functional theory (DFT) are limited to relatively small systems and short time scales because of their high computational cost. In this study, we develop a neural network potential (NNP) for pristine and oxygen-deficient STO using a reference dataset generated from AIMD simulations. The trained NNP model accurately reproduces the DFT reference energies, atomic forces, and virials for both pristine STO and STO containing an oxygen vacancy. The NNP also reproduces the supercell-size dependence of the total energies and oxygen-vacancy formation energies, including the formation energy extrapolated to the dilute limit. Large-scale and long-time MD simulations using the NNP (NNP-MD) are then performed to investigate oxygen-vacancy diffusion. The resulting oxygen-vacancy diffusion coefficients show substantially better agreement with experimental data than those obtained from conventional classical MD simulations. An Arrhenius analysis yields an oxygen-vacancy migration enthalpy of 0.64 eV, in close agreement with the experimental value of 0.67 eV. These results demonstrate that NNPs trained on first-principles data provide an accurate and computationally efficient framework for investigating defect formation and long-time vacancy transport in perovskite oxides.

</details>


### [207] [Interlayer Registry Controls Polar Topology in Twisted Bilayer h-BN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16528)
*Di Fan,Changming Ke,Shi Liu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Twist-controlled interlayer registry governs moiré polarization topology in twisted bilayer h-BN; decreasing twist angle drives a crossover from vortex-like to triangular AB/BA domain textures, robust up to 1000 K and tunable by layer-selective strain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Twisting van der Waals bilayers creates a moiré stacking field with periodically varying local interlayer registry, enabling spatially patterned polarization. Yet a systematic understanding of how this registry-controlled polar topology evolves with twist angle, temperature, and mechanical strain is lacking.

Method: First-principles-trained deep-potential molecular dynamics simulations of twisted bilayer h-BN, complemented by a mismatch-matrix registry-mapping model to describe deformation-induced changes in the polarization texture.

Result: As twist angle decreases, the moiré polarization texture evolves from compact, nearly circular vortex-like patterns to extended triangular AB/BA domains. This crossover stems from enhanced lattice relaxation at small twist angles, expanding low-energy oppositely polarized AB/BA regions and confining in-plane polarization mainly to domain boundaries. Local in-plane winding remains tied to interlayer registry and persists up to 1000 K. Homogeneous deformation of both layers produces only weak distortions, whereas layer-selective deformation strongly reconstructs the moiré polarization texture. The mismatch-matrix model captures deformation-induced changes in vortex radii at larger twist angles and triangular-domain geometry at smaller twist angles.

Conclusion: Interlayer registry is identified as the central control parameter for forming and tuning moiré polar topology in twisted bilayer h-BN.

Abstract: Twisting van der Waals bilayers creates a moir'e stacking field with periodically varying local interlayer registry, enabling spatially patterned polarization. However, a systematic understanding of how this registry-controlled polar topology evolves with twist angle, temperature, and mechanical strain is still lacking. Here, using first-principles-trained deep-potential molecular dynamics simulations, we show that spatially varying interlayer registry governs the polarization texture and can be directly tuned by twist and relative layer deformation. As the twist angle decreases, the moir'e polarization texture evolves from compact, nearly circular vortex-like patterns to extended triangular AB/BA domains. This crossover arises from enhanced lattice relaxation at small twist angles, which expands low-energy, oppositely polarized AB/BA regions and confines in-plane polarization mainly to domain boundaries. Despite this reconstruction, the local in-plane winding remains tied to interlayer registry and persists up to 1000 K, indicating robust topology against thermal fluctuations. We further show that the mechanical response is dictated by whether deformation changes the relative registry: homogeneous deformation of both layers produces only weak distortions, whereas layer-selective deformation strongly reconstructs the moiré polarization texture. A mismatch-matrix registry-mapping model is developed to capture this response, explaining deformation-induced changes in vortex radii at larger twist angles and triangular-domain geometry at smaller twist angles. These results identify interlayer registry as the central control parameter for forming and tuning moir'e polar topology in twisted bilayer h-BN.

</details>


### [208] [Real-space obstruction control of the shift current in two-dimensional Quantum Spin Hall insulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16544)
*Michele Bagaglini,Mattia Iannetti,Jonas Erhardt,Jörg Schäfer,Ralph Claessen,Giorgio Sangiovanni,Gianni Profeta*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using a triangular-lattice tight-binding model of a quantum spin Hall insulator, the authors show that obstructed topological phases—trivial, QSH, and higher-order topological insulating phases—exhibit strongly enhanced shift-current (bulk photovoltaic) responses, despite similar band structures, with the enhancement traced to optical transitions between distinct Wannier-center sectors, and identify indenene as a promising candidate material.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The search for a large bulk photovoltaic effect has expanded toward low-band-gap, infrared-operating systems for optoelectronics. The shift current, a dominant geometric photocurrent mechanism, depends not only on optical transition probability but also on the real-space displacement of the photoexcitation, making non-centrosymmetric materials with obstructed electronic wave functions attractive candidates—yet the link between real-space electronic obstruction and shift-current magnitude/controllability remains underexplored.

Method: The authors employ a tight-binding model of a quantum spin Hall insulator on a triangular lattice and compute the nonlinear photocurrent across trivial, quantum spin Hall, and higher-order topological insulating phases. They relate the shift-current response to optical transitions between states belonging to distinct Wannier-center sectors, analyzing how the associated real-space displacement controls both the magnitude and sign of the shift current.

Result: Obstructed topological phases display a substantially enhanced shift-current response compared with trivial phases, even when electronic band structures are similar. The enhancement originates from optical transitions between states in distinct Wannier-center sectors, which permit large real-space displacements upon photoexcitation and, depending on the topological phase, determine the sign of the shift current. Indenene, a monolayer of In on SiC(0001), is identified as a promising candidate for realizing this phenomenon.

Conclusion: Real-space electronic obstruction provides a route to controllably enhanced bulk photovoltaic effects, extending the search for large shift-current materials to obstructed topological phases and highlighting indenene as a practical platform for infrared optoelectronic applications.

Abstract: The search for materials exhibiting a large bulk photovoltaic effect has recently expanded to low-band-gap systems capable of operating in the infrared regime, with potential applications in optoelectronics. Among the microscopic mechanisms contributing to this phenomenon, the shift current is one of the dominant sources of photocurrent generation. Owing to its geometric origin, the shift current is not only determined by the optical transition probability but also by the real-space displacement associated with the optical excitation, making non-centrosymmetric materials with obstructed electronic wave functions particularly promising candidates. In this work, using a tight-binding model of a quantum spin Hall insulator on a triangular lattice, we investigate the nonlinear photocurrent across trivial, quantum spin Hall, and higher-order topological insulating phases. We show that obstructed topological phases exhibit a substantially enhanced shift-current response, despite the similar electronic band structure. We identify the origin of this enhancement in optical transitions between states belonging to distinct Wannier-center sectors, which allow for a large real-space displacement upon photoexcitation and, depending on the topological phase, control the sign of the shift current. Our results extend the search for controllable bulk photovoltaic effects to materials characterized by real-space electronic obstruction and identify indenene, a monolayer of In on SiC(0001), as a promising candidate for realizing this phenomenon.

</details>


### [209] [Domain-wall-to-domain interconversion in a polar conducting oxide](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16681)
*Martin F. Sarott,Majid Ahmadi,Johanna van Gent González,Ewout van der Veer,Mads C. Weber,Jacopo Pinna,Daniel A. Chaney,Arijit Sinha,Hamideh Hassani,Horatio R. J. Cox,Thomas Perrault,Amir Sabet Ghorabaei,Maria A. Loi,Philippe Ghosez,Bart J. Kooi,Beatriz Noheda*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates reversible dimensionality control of nanoscale domain walls in polar epitaxial WO3, expanding 2D conducting domain walls into 3D functional regions via alternating-electric-field-induced localized heating, enabling switching between domain polarity and domain-wall conductivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Domain walls in ferroic crystals are topologically distinct from the domain interior and host functionalities forbidden in the host material. However, their strict confinement to 2D interfaces hinders understanding of their intrinsic nature and limits practical applicability. A method to controllably expand domain walls into 3D regions is therefore needed.

Method: In polar epitaxial tungsten trioxide (WO3) films, the conducting, non-polar domain walls adopt the parent-phase structure. A scanning probe tip applies an alternating electric field that induces localized heating, driving the domain-wall phase to expand into the adjacent polar domains, converting domain-wall regions into 3D domains (domain-wall-to-domain interconversion).

Result: Reversible dimensionality control was achieved: localized tip-induced heating expands the domain-wall phase into 3D functional regions, and the process is reversible, allowing spatial switching between the mutually exclusive properties of domains (polarity) and domain walls (conductivity).

Conclusion: Reversible interconversion between 2D domain walls and 3D functional domains in WO3 provides a route to reconfigurable oxide electronics, enabling spatial control over polarity and conductivity states.

Abstract: Domain walls in ferroic crystals are topological defects with a symmetry distinct from the domain interior, allowing them to display functionalities that are otherwise forbidden in the host material. Despite their unique properties, domain walls are strictly confined to two-dimensional interfaces, which hinders unveiling their true nature and limits their applicability. Here, we demonstrate reversible dimensionality control by expanding nanoscale domain walls into three-dimensional functional regions in polar epitaxial tungsten trioxide ($\text{WO}_3$). This domain-wall-to-domain interconversion is enabled by the conducting, non-polar domain walls adopting the parent-phase structure. An alternating electric field applied through a scanning probe tip induces localized heating, which expands this domain-wall phase into the adjacent polar domains. This approach enables reversible spatial control to switch between mutually exclusive properties of domains (polarity) and domain walls (conductivity), offering a new paradigm for reconfigurable oxide electronics.

</details>


### [210] [Crystal growth, magnetic, and transport properties of the candidate altermagnet Cr$_{1/4}$NbS$_{2}$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17452)
*Nashra Pistawala,Martin R. Lees,Geetha Balakrishnan,Daniel A. Mayoh*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: High-quality single crystals of Cr₁/₄NbS₂ are grown for the first time, confirming hexagonal symmetry and an antiferromagnetic Néel temperature of ~141 K, establishing it as an experimental platform for altermagnetism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Altermagnetism is an emerging paradigm in condensed matter magnetism, but experimentally confirmed altermagnetic candidates remain scarce. Although Cr₁/₄NbS₂ was theoretically proposed as a promising altermagnet, no experimental studies on its single crystals existed.

Method: The authors grew high-quality single crystals of Cr₁/₄NbS₂ and characterized them using single-crystal X-ray diffraction, resistivity, heat capacity, and magnetotransport (magnetoresistance and Hall) measurements.

Result: X-ray diffraction confirmed hexagonal crystal symmetry (P6₃/mmc), satisfying one criterion for altermagnetic behavior. Resistivity and heat capacity measurements identified an antiferromagnetic Néel temperature of T_N ~ 141 K. Magnetotransport revealed non-trivial magnetoresistance and Hall features indicative of quantum diffusive transport.

Conclusion: Cr₁/₄NbS₂, synthesized for the first time in single-crystalline form, provides an experimental platform for probing the rich phenomena predicted for altermagnetic materials.

Abstract: Altermagnetism is a rapidly emerging paradigm in condensed matter magnetism, yet the number of experimentally confirmed altermagnetic candidates remains remarkably limited. Cr$_{1/4}$NbS$_2$ has long been theoretically proposed as a promising altermagnet, but there are no reports on experimental investigation of single crystals. Here, we report the successful growth of high-quality single crystals of Cr$_{1/4}$NbS$_2$ and present its structural, thermal, magnetic, and transport properties. Single-crystal X-ray diffraction provides detailed crystallographic information and confirms the hexagonal crystal symmetry ($P6_3/mmc$), one of the criteria for altermagnetic behavior. Resistivity and heat capacity measurements unambiguously identify an antiferromagnetic Néel temperature of $T_N \sim 141$ K. Magnetotransport measurements reveal non-trivial features in the magnetoresistance and Hall response, indicative of quantum diffusive transport phenomena. Cr$_{1/4}$NbS$_2$, synthesized here for the first time in single-crystalline form, therefore serves as an experimental platform for probing the rich spectrum of phenomena predicted for altermagnetic materials.

</details>


### [211] [Engineering Ferroelectric Phases in BaTiO$_3$ Films via Buffer-Layer Induced Misfit Strain Reversal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16766)
*A. S. Mikheykin,N. V. Lyanguzov,A. S. Konstantinov,V. B. Shirokov,E. D. Linnik,T. Bondar,A. G. Razumnaya*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A 50-nm LSMO buffer layer reverses the effective strain in 500-nm BaTiO3 films on MgO from compressive to tensile, driving an unconventional paraelectric -> orthorhombic (aa) -> monoclinic (r) phase sequence and enabling built-in field effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strain engineering of ferroelectric phase symmetry is key to tailoring polarization topology and device functionality, but substrate-induced strain in BaTiO3/MgO is complicated by competing lattice-misfit and thermal-expansion contributions that are difficult to control predictably.

Method: Temperature-dependent polarized Raman spectroscopy, X-ray diffraction, and piezoresponse force microscopy were used to track the phase evolution of 500-nm BaTiO3 films on MgO with and without a 50-nm La0.67Sr0.33MnO3 buffer layer.

Result: Despite the tensile lattice misfit expected from MgO(001), thermal-expansion mismatch dominates in the bare 500-nm BaTiO3 film, producing effective compressive stress on cooling. Inserting the LSMO buffer reverses the effective strain to tensile and yields an unconventional cooling sequence: paraelectric -> orthorhombic aa-phase (~407 K) -> monoclinic r-phase (~300-345 K), with in-plane and tilted polarization components. Interfacial charge redistribution creates a built-in electric field, evidenced by self-polarization and asymmetric PFM hysteresis.

Conclusion: Conductive buffer layers play a decisive role in setting ferroelectric symmetry and polarization texture in oxide heterostructures, offering a route to tailored polarization states for low-power spintronics, tunable microwave devices, and neuromorphic computing.

Abstract: Controlling ferroelectric phase symmetry through strain engineering is crucial for tailoring polarization topology and functional properties in oxide-based devices. Here, we show that a thin conductive buffer layer can override substrate-induced strain in BaTiO$_3$ (BTO) films on MgO. Although the lattice parameter of MgO(001) would be expected to induce tensile misfit strain, in 500~nm BaTiO$_3$ films the thermal-expansion mismatch dominates, resulting in effective compressive stress upon cooling. Inserting a 50-nm La$_{0.67}$Sr$_{0.33}$MnO$_3$ (LSMO) buffer layer reverses the effective strain state from compressive to tensile. Using temperature-dependent polarized Raman spectroscopy, X-ray diffraction, and piezoresponse force microscopy, we reveal an unconventional phase sequence upon cooling: paraelectric $\rightarrow$ orthorhombic ($\textit{aa}$-phase, $\sim$407 K) $\rightarrow$ monoclinic ($\textit{r}$-phase, $\sim$300-345 K), characterized in-plane and tilted polarization components. The LSMO layer not only serves as a bottom electrode but also modifies the strain state, while interfacial charge redistribution induces a built-in electric field, evidenced by self-polarization and asymmetric PFM hysteresis. These findings highlight the critical role of conductive buffer layers in controlling ferroelectric symmetry and enabling oxide heterostructures with tailored polarization textures for low-power spintronics, tunable microwave devices, and neuromorphic computing.

</details>


### [212] [Molecular Precursor Chemistry Shapes Early-Stage Reaction Pathways in Nitrogen-Doped Carbon Nanotube Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16831)
*Narayan N. Som,Lukasz Nowicki,Tomasz Gizewski,Karolina Z. Milowska,Anna Wroblewska,Caghan Tuncer,Maciej Trzaskowski,Olga Iwasinska-Kowalska,Tomasz Plocinski,Mariusz Zdrojek,Krzysztof K. Koziol,Malgorzata Jakubowska,Agnieszka Lekawa-Raus*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using experiments and reactive MD, the authors show that the chemistry of the carbon precursor (pyridine vs. pyrazine vs. nitrogen-free) shapes the very earliest stages of catalyst formation and precursor decomposition, thereby steering carbon nanotube structure—well before nanotube nucleation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Carbon nanotube structural characteristics are conventionally attributed to processes during nucleation and growth from an already-formed catalyst nanoparticle. The authors hypothesize that the structural trajectory actually begins much earlier, within the first nanoseconds of precursor decomposition and catalyst formation, and that precursor chemistry (not just nitrogen quantity) plays a decisive role.

Method: Combined experiment and reactive molecular dynamics (MD) simulations comparing nitrogen-free CNT synthesis with two closely related aromatic nitrogen precursors: pyridine (one N per molecule) and pyrazine (two N per molecule). The simulations track local Fe–carbon and Fe–SiO2 interactions and precursor decomposition pathways during early catalyst evolution.

Result: Both nitrogen-containing precursors promote bamboo-like CNT formation and yield substantially more structurally disordered CNTs than the nitrogen-free reference. Nitrogen incorporation does not scale simply with the amount of nitrogen supplied, indicating a strong contribution from precursor chemistry. Reactive MD shows nitrogen-containing precursors significantly modify local Fe–carbon and Fe–SiO2 interactions during catalyst evolution, and that pyridine and pyrazine follow distinct decomposition pathways, with pyrazine decomposing significantly more within the simulated timescale.

Conclusion: Precursor chemistry influences the local chemical environment and early catalyst evolution, underscoring its potential role in the initial stages of CNT growth and offering a lever for tailoring CNT structure via precursor design.

Abstract: The structural characteristics of carbon nanotubes are usually discussed in terms of processes occurring during nanotube nucleation and subsequent growth from an already formed catalyst nanoparticle. Here, we show that their structural trajectory can begin much earlier, within the first nanoseconds of precursor decomposition and catalyst formation. We combine experiment and reactive molecular dynamics to compare nitrogen-free CNT synthesis with two closely related aromatic nitrogen precursors, pyridine and pyrazine, containing one and two nitrogen atoms per molecule, respectively. Experimentally, both nitrogen-containing precursors promote bamboo-like CNT formation and produce substantially more structurally disordered CNTs than the nitrogen-free reference. Notably, nitrogen incorporation does not scale simply with the amount of nitrogen supplied, demonstrating a strong contribution from precursor chemistry. Reactive molecular dynamics reveals that nitrogen-containing precursors significantly modify local Fe-carbon and Fe-SiO2 interactions during catalyst evolution, while pyridine and pyrazine exhibit distinct decomposition pathways, with pyrazine showing significantly greater decomposition within the simulated timescale. These results demonstrate that precursor chemistry influences the local chemical environment and early catalyst evolution, highlighting its potential role in the initial stages of CNT growth.

</details>


### [213] [Coherent Phonon Generation from Prethermal Carrier Populations in a Semiconductor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18279)
*Mingyu Shi,Matteo Savoini,Patrick Friebel,Andrea Annunziata,Thomas Metzger,Yunpei Deng,Laura Cattaneo,Steven L. Johnson*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Contrary to thermalized-carrier models, coherent A1 phonon generation in tellurium near the band gap is governed by transition-selective prethermal forces, with a fitted excited-state lifetime of a few femtoseconds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coherent optical phonon generation in semiconductors is conventionally attributed to a thermalized transient carrier population. The authors test whether this picture holds when excitation is tuned around the electronic band gap, where specific interband transitions, rather than a thermalized total carrier density, may dominate the driving force.

Method: Systematic measurements of the photon-energy dependence of ultrafast-laser-driven coherent phonon generation in tellurium, using excitation wavelengths around the band gap, combined with first-principles calculations. The data are modeled with a transition-resolved prethermal force weighted by optical matrix elements and band- and momentum-dependent A1 deformation potentials.

Result: The coherent amplitude of the fully symmetric A1 mode changes only weakly when the wavelength is tuned across the band gap, contradicting thermalized-carrier models. The measured spectral dependence is instead reproduced by the transition-resolved prethermal force model, whose fit yields an excited transition state lifetime of a few femtoseconds.

Conclusion: Transition selectivity, rather than a thermalized total carrier density, is the key factor controlling coherent-phonon generation in semiconductors excited near a band gap.

Abstract: Ultrafast laser excitation of carriers in semiconductors often results in the creation of fully symmetric coherent optical phonons, typically understood in terms of a thermalized transient carrier population. We test this understanding with systematic measurements and first-principles calculations of the photon-energy dependence of ultrafast-laser-driven coherent phonon generation in tellurium, using excitation wavelengths around the electronic band gap. We observe that, contrary to the expectations of models assuming a thermalized carrier population, the coherent amplitude of the fully symmetric $A_1$ mode changes only weakly when tuning wavelength across the band gap. The measured spectral dependence is instead reproduced by a transition-resolved prethermal force weighted by optical matrix elements and band- and momentum-dependent $A_1$ deformation potentials. Fitting this model to the data yields an excited transition state lifetime of a few femtoseconds. These results identify transition selectivity, rather than a thermalized total carrier density, as a key factor controlling coherent-phonon generation in semiconductors excited near a band gap.

</details>


### [214] [Trust-Limited Machine-Learning-Accelerated Nudged Elastic Band Calculations for Small-Polaron Hopping in Rutile TiO2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16850)
*Ashish Kumar Singh,Irina V. Lebedeva*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A Gaussian-process-assisted NEB workflow is developed for small-polaron hopping, where coupling between nuclear motion and self-consistent localized electronic states complicates surrogate optimization near the charge-transfer region. Demonstrated on electron small-polaron hopping in rutile TiO2 (DFT+U, SIESTA), it yields highly reproducible barriers (0.1645, 0.1637, 0.1640 eV; spread 0.793 meV) and demonstrates feasibility at larger supercell sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NEB calculations of small-polaron hopping are computationally demanding because nuclear motion couples to a self-consistent localized electronic state. Near the charge-transfer region, slow self-consistent-field convergence, localization switching, and competing electronic solutions all hamper surrogate-assisted optimization, so a workflow is needed that handles this difficult regime reliably.

Method: A Gaussian-process-assisted NEB workflow for electron small-polaron hopping in rutile TiO2 is developed using spin-polarized DFT+U with SIESTA. A joint energy-force GP with a Matern covariance model advances the elastic band, while active-atom RMS and maximum-atom displacement limits constrain surrogate motion relative to evaluated configurations. Reaching these limits triggers first-principles sampling without requiring full surrogate convergence. Three independently DFT-verified 96-atom [001] calculations (11, 13, 15 images) with different surrogate settings and GP parameterizations were run; in the 15-image case, repeated central-image sampling activates a separately calibrated local GP during surrogate optimization.

Result: The three 96-atom calculations yield barriers of 0.1645, 0.1637, and 0.1640 eV, spanning only 0.793 meV. Final full-band DFT verification gives a maximum projected NEB force of 0.0346 eV/A. A 324-atom calculation yields a lower barrier of 0.1302 eV, indicating supercell sensitivity and demonstrating feasibility at larger GP observation dimension.

Conclusion: The workflow combines controlled surrogate extrapolation, targeted charge-transfer-region interpolation, and independent DFT verification to obtain polaron migration pathways, emphasizing reproducible barriers and full-band force convergence.

Abstract: Nudged elastic band (NEB) calculations of small-polaron hopping are demanding because nuclear motion is coupled to a self-consistent localized electronic state. Near the charge-transfer region, slow self-consistent-field convergence, localization switching, and competing electronic solutions complicate surrogate-assisted optimization. We develop a Gaussian-process-assisted NEB workflow for this regime and demonstrate electron small-polaron hopping in rutile TiO2 using spin-polarized density functional theory with a Hubbard U correction (DFT+U ) with SIESTA. A joint energy-force Gaussian process (GP) with a Matérn covariance model advances the elastic band, while active-atom root-mean-square and maximum-atom displacement limits constrain surrogate motion relative to evaluated configurations. Reaching these limits triggers first-principles sampling without requiring full surrogate convergence. Three independently DFT-verified 96-atom [001] calculations using 11, 13, and 15 images and different surrogate settings and GP parameterizations yield barriers of 0.1645, 0.1637, and 0.1640 eV, respectively, spanning 0.793 meV. In the 15-image calculation, repeated central-image sampling activates a separately calibrated local GP during surrogate optimization. Final full-band DFT verification gives a maximum projected NEB force of 0.0346 eV/Å. A 324-atom calculation yields a lower barrier of 0.1302 eV, indicating supercell sensitivity and demonstrating feasibility at larger GP observation dimension. The workflow combines controlled surrogate extrapolation, targeted charge-transfer-region interpolation, and independent DFT verification to obtain polaron migration pathways, emphasizing reproducible barriers and full-band force convergence.

</details>


### [215] [Capturing Coordination Core Topology in Metal--Organic Frameworks Using Persistent Mayer Homology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17056)
*Caleb Simiyu Khaemba,Chun-Long Chen,Guo-Wei Wei*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper introduces persistent Mayer homology (PMH) to quantitatively capture local coordination cores in MOFs and POMs, showing that PMH representations improve the prediction of eight nitrogen- and oxygen-related adsorption and self-diffusion properties over element-resolved persistent homology (ERPH) alone, with third-order Mayer representations performing best.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Local coordination cores govern the fundamental chemical and structural properties of MOFs and POMs, yet existing methods such as element-resolved persistent homology (ERPH) cannot fully translate these cores into quantitative, scalable, and interpretable information. Conventional topology also fails to detect certain geometric variability within coordination environments.

Method: The authors develop persistent Mayer homology (PMH) to detect geometric variability invisible to classical topology, focusing on coordination cores formed by periodic metal-centered heavy-atom, carbon, and donor channels. They examine explainable PMH of orders N=3 and N=5, combine each with ERPH (which characterizes multiscale organization of chemically defined element groups), and use the resulting descriptors to predict eight nitrogen- and oxygen-related adsorption and self-diffusion properties.

Result: PMH distinguishes different arrangements of the local coordination environment, linker framework, and potential coordinating atoms. Coordination-core PMH representations consistently improve predictive performance over ERPH alone, and the third-order Mayer representation delivers the strongest overall performance.

Conclusion: Inner coordination configurations captured by PMH contain essential structural information not fully represented by conventional element-resolved topological descriptors. PMH offers a quantitative and interpretable topological framework for understanding local coordination environments, potentially facilitating the rational design of MOFs and POM-based materials.

Abstract: Local coordination cores are critically important in metal-organic frameworks (MOFs) and polyoxometalates (POMs), as they govern the fundamental chemical and structural properties of these materials. Existing methods, including element-resolved persistent homology (ERPH), have limited ability to translate local coordination cores into quantitative, scalable, and interpretable information. Here, we introduce persistent Mayer homology (PMH) to enable the detection of the geometric variability invisible by classical topology. By focusing on coordination cores formed by periodic metal-centered heavy-atom, carbon, and donor channels, PMH distinguishes different arrangements of the local coordination environment, linker framework, and potential coordinating atoms. We examine explainable PMH of orders $N=3$ and $N=5$ and separately combine each with ERPH, which characterizes the multiscale organization of chemically defined element groups across the extended framework, to predict eight nitrogen- and oxygen-related adsorption and self-diffusion properties. The resulting coordination-core PMH representations consistently improve predictive performance over ERPH alone, with the third-order Mayer representation providing the strongest overall performance. These results demonstrate that the inner coordination configurations captured by PMH contain essential structural information that is not fully represented by conventional element-resolved topological descriptors. This finding provides a quantitative interpretable topological framework for understanding local coordination environments and may facilitate the rational design of MOFs and POM-based materials.

</details>


### [216] [Quantitative Modelling of Solvation in 2D Materials via Alchemical Decoupling and Potential Energy Surfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18490)
*Lindsey N. Ioos,Duncan A. Peterson,Scott C. Warren*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: An improved simulation strategy using the Gibbs free energy of solvation to calibrate and join fully desolvated and fully solvated potential energy surfaces predicts 2D material stability in solvents with high accuracy, revealing a much higher barrier to full desolvation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modeling why 2D materials remain suspended in solution has been hindered by the long timescales and large activation energies required, limiting mechanistic understanding of aggregation and exfoliation.

Method: The authors use the Gibbs free energy of solvation to accurately calibrate two disconnected potential energy surfaces corresponding to the fully desolvated and fully solvated 2D material, then join these surfaces via the solvation energy.

Result: Predictions of 2D material stability in various solvents are qualitatively different from prior simulations and show excellent agreement with experimental results; the approach predicts a much higher barrier to full desolvation.

Conclusion: The work suggests that apparently aggregated materials still retain monolayers of solvent between adjacent 2D layers, providing improved understanding of solvent-2D material interactions to guide future experiments.

Abstract: Understanding why 2D materials remain suspended in solutions is essential to understanding the mechanisms of aggregation and exfoliation. These dynamics have been difficult to model because of the long timescales and large activation energies involved in these simulations. Here we describe an improved strategy for modelling the solvation and stability of 2D materials that uses the Gibbs free energy of solvation to accurately calibrate two disconnected potential energy surfaces for the fully desolvated and fully solvated 2D materials. By joining these surfaces using solvation energy, we obtain predictions for 2D material stability in various solvents that are qualitatively different than prior simulations and that have excellent agreement with experimental results. Our approach predicts a much higher barrier to full desolvation of the material, suggesting that materials that appear to be aggregated in fact still retain monolayers of solvent between adjacent layers of 2D materials. This work provides much needed understanding on the interactions between the solvent and 2D material that can guide future experimental work.

</details>


### [217] [Symmetry-constrained coexistence of charge-three and charge-one Weyl points in nonmagnetic crystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17071)
*Ze-Xin Xue,Ke-Xin Pang,Yun-Yun Bai,Yanfeng Ge,Yan Gao*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work classifies 230 space groups for hosting an eight-node Weyl configuration of two charge-three and six charge-one Weyl points via topological charge conservation and symmetry analysis, finding 12 (no SOC) and 23 (with SOC) candidate groups, and identifies TBR-B48 and chiral OBD-B48 as real materials realizing this configuration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ideal charge-three Weyl semimetals are rare, and the symmetry-constrained coexistence of charge-three and charge-one Weyl points in nonmagnetic crystals remains largely unexplored. This limits both understanding of unconventional topological properties and the search for realistic material candidates.

Method: Seventeen of 230 space groups were screened using topological charge conservation combined with space-group symmetry analysis, yielding 12 (without SOC) and 23 (with SOC) candidate groups. First-principles calculations were performed on boron allotropes TBR-B48 and chiral OBD-B48, complemented by surface-state Fermi-arc calculations, chirality reversal tests of OBD-B48, and C6-symmetry-breaking studies in TBR-B48.

Result: Without SOC, TBR-B48 and chiral OBD-B48 realize the eight-node configuration (two C-3 WPs plus six C-1 WPs), hosting hourglass-type and non-hourglass-type C-3 WPs, respectively. Surface states show six Fermi-arc branches at the shared projection of the charge-three pair and three at each individual projection. Reversing the structural chirality of OBD-B48 reverses all Weyl charge signs, linking crystal and momentum-space chirality. Breaking the protecting C6 symmetry in TBR-B48 splits each C-3 WP into three C-1 WPs and reconstructs the Fermi arcs.

Conclusion: The study provides symmetry-based guidelines for designing and searching for nonmagnetic Weyl semimetals that host coexisting charge-three and charge-one Weyl points, and establishes a direct correspondence between crystal chirality and momentum-space chirality.

Abstract: High-charge Weyl semimetals (WSMs), particularly those hosting charge-three Weyl point (C-3 WPs), have attracted considerable interest owing to their unconventional topological properties and enhanced boundary phenomena. However, ideal C-3 WSMs remain rare, and the symmetry-constrained coexistence of charge-three and charge-one Weyl point (C-1 WPs) in nonmagnetic crystals remains largely unexplored. Here, we investigate a representative eight-node Weyl configuration, consisting of two C-3 WPs and six C-1 WPs with opposite chirality, corresponding to two identical $\{3,-1,-1,-1\}$ irreducible Weyl molecules. By combining topological charge conservation with space-group symmetry analysis, we systematically classify all 230 space groups and identify 12 and 23 candidate groups without and with spin--orbit coupling (SOC), respectively. First-principles calculations without SOC reveal that the boron allotropes TBR-B$_{48}$ and chiral OBD-B$_{48}$ realize this configuration, hosting hourglass-type and non-hourglass-type C-3 WPs, respectively. Surface-state calculations reveal six Fermi-arc branches at the shared projection of the charge-three pair and three at each individual projection. Reversing the structural chirality of OBD-B$_{48}$ reverses the signs of all Weyl charges, establishing a direct correspondence between crystal and momentum-space chirality. Furthermore, breaking the protecting $C_6$ symmetry in TBR-B$_{48}$ splits each C-3 WP into three C-1 WPs and reconstructs the surface Fermi arcs. Our work establishes symmetry-based guidelines for designing and searching for nonmagnetic WSMs hosting charge-three and charge-one Weyl-point coexistence.

</details>


### [218] [Spin-orbit torque effects in all-electrical spin-wave spectroscopy experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17095)
*José Solano Córdova,Jone Mencos,Richard Schlitz,Quentin Rossi,Denise Reustlen,Javed Ali,Sebastian Sailler,Michaela Lammel,Matthieu Bailleul,Daniel Stoeffler,Felix Casanova,Sebastian T. B. Goennenwein,Helena Reichlova*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Pt antennas excite coherent spin waves in 105-nm-thick epitaxial YIG films via both RF Oersted fields and spin-orbit torques; after excluding fabrication-induced and interface inhomogeneity artifacts, the residual two-wave propagation is consistent with combined Oersted-field and SOT excitation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electrical excitation and detection of propagating spin waves are key to magnonic devices. Conventional Au/Cu antennas excite spin waves mainly via RF Oersted fields, whereas Pt antennas can additionally generate spin-orbit torques (SOTs) through the spin Hall effect. Recent theory proposes RF-SOT as an alternative coherent spin-wave excitation mechanism with distinct characteristics from inductive excitation, motivating experimental verification.

Method: The authors investigate Pt antennas for exciting coherent spin waves in 105-nm-thick epitaxial Y3Fe5O12 (YIG) films, considering two mechanisms: an oscillating Oersted field and an oscillating spin current. They analyze raw experimental transmission spectra and account for fabrication-induced modifications to the YIG film from the etching process, as well as spatially inhomogeneous interface conditions induced by Pt, in order to isolate the intrinsic excitation contributions.

Result: Raw transmission spectra are strongly affected by etching-induced modifications to the YIG film and by spatially inhomogeneous Pt-induced interface conditions. After removing these contributions, the remaining two wave propagation is consistent with a combined excitation from Oersted fields and SOTs.

Conclusion: Pt antennas can excite coherent spin waves in epitaxial YIG through a combination of RF Oersted fields and spin-orbit torques, supporting theoretical predictions of RF-SOT as a distinct coherent spin-wave excitation mechanism, once fabrication- and interface-related artifacts are properly removed.

Abstract: Electrical excitation and detection of propagating spin waves are key elements of magnonic devices. While conventional antennas consisting of non-magnetic metals such as Au or Cu excite spin waves predominantly through radio-frequency (RF) Oersted fields, antennas comprising Pt can additionally generate spin-orbit torques (SOTs) via the spin Hall effect. Recent theoretical work has proposed RF-SOT as an alternative mechanism for coherent spin-wave excitation, predicting distinct characteristics compared with conventional inductive excitation. Here, we investigate Pt antennas for the excitation of coherent spin waves in 105-nm-thick epitaxial $\text{Y}_3\text{Fe}_5\text{O}_{12}$ (YIG) films through two potential mechanisms: an oscillating Oersted field and an oscillating spin current. The raw experimental transmission spectra are strongly affected by fabrication-induced modifications to the YIG film associated with the etching process together with spatially inhomogeneous interface conditions induced by Pt. After removing these contributions, we find the remaining two wave propagation consistent with a combined excitation from Oersted fields and SOTs.

</details>


### [219] [Comprehensive Workflow for Uncertainty-Aware Ionic Liquid Solvent Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17171)
*Alexander Lobo,Urvi Awasthi,Leonid Zhukov*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper presents a coupled property-prediction and optimal candidate selection workflow for ionic liquids, integrating data processing, deep learning with uncertainty estimation, and physics-based filtering to enable reliable out-of-distribution ranking, demonstrated on designing novel ILs for CO2 capture from flue gas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing synthesizable materials with targeted properties is a central goal in materials science, and ILs are attractive due to their tunability. However, current data-driven property models for ILs degrade in scarce, noisy, and uneven data regimes, where measurements cover only a small fraction of chemical space and limited thermodynamic conditions. This makes out-of-distribution screening brittle: point predictions may look plausible but comparing candidates consistently, quantifying downstream implications, and trusting rankings for molecular discovery remain difficult.

Method: The authors develop a property-prediction and optimal candidate selection workflow that treats data processing, deep learning modeling, and physics-based calculations as a coupled system. It links property prediction with uncertainty estimation and physics-based filtering, tightening the connection between predicted properties and the criteria used for down-selection to support reliable out-of-distribution ranking.

Result: The workflow is demonstrated by designing novel ionic liquids for CO2 capture from flue gas, predicting their properties and selecting Pareto-optimal candidates based on CO2 solubility, IL viscosity, and working capacity, enabling prioritization of previously untested IL candidates with favorable predicted trade-offs.

Conclusion: Coupling data processing, deep learning with uncertainty estimation, and physics-based filtering into a unified workflow improves the reliability of out-of-distribution ranking and supports trustworthy, property-driven molecular discovery of ionic liquids.

Abstract: Designing synthesizable materials with targeted properties remains one of the central objectives in materials sciences. Ionic liquids (ILs) are well suited to this goal due to their "tunability", where physical and chemical properties can be precisely customized by modifying their molecular structure, specifically by varying the combination of cations and anions. At the same time, current data-driven property models for ionic liquids often degrade in scarce, noisy, and uneven data regimes, where measurements cover only a small fraction of chemical space and a limited range of thermodynamic conditions. This makes out-of-distribution screening brittle: even when point predictions look plausible, it is difficult to compare candidates consistently, quantify downstream implications, or trust rankings enough to guide molecular discovery. We present a property-prediction and optimal candidate selection workflow that treats data processing, deep learning modeling, and physics-based calculations as a coupled system, linking property prediction with uncertainty estimation and physics-based filtering. By tightening the link between predicted properties and the criteria used for down-selection, the workflow supports reliable out-of-distribution ranking and enables prioritization of previously untested ionic liquid candidates with favorable predicted trade-offs. We demonstrate the application of our workflow by designing novel ionic liquids for CO2 capture from flue gas, predicting their properties and selecting Pareto-optimal candidates based on CO2 solubility, IL viscosity and working capacity.

</details>


### [220] [Evidence of an anisotropic Griffiths phase in a two-dimensional van der Waals magnet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17315)
*Anupam K. Singh,Dhananjay Nair,Holger Meyerheim,Stuart S. P. Parkin,Banabir Pal*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A highly anisotropic Griffiths phase driven by self-intercalation is reported in the van der Waals magnet Fe5GeTe2, arising from short-range-ordered ferromagnetic clusters embedded in a paramagnetic matrix and emerging exclusively under in-plane magnetic fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-dimensional van der Waals magnetism has focused mainly on long-range-ordered magnetic phases, leaving disordered magnetic phases largely unexplored. This work targets that gap by probing disorder-driven magnetism in self-intercalated van der Waals magnets.

Method: Magnetic characterization of self-intercalated Fe5GeTe2, using magnetic susceptibility measurements, power-law divergence analysis, Lee-Yang scaling, and entropy anomaly analysis, with comparison of in-plane versus out-of-plane field orientations.

Result: Evidence of short-range-ordered ferromagnetic clusters embedded in a paramagnetic matrix dominating the magnetic response between the Curie temperature and a higher Griffiths temperature. Anomalous power-law divergence in susceptibility, universal Lee-Yang scaling, and distinct entropy anomalies support the Griffiths phase. The non-analytic Griffiths response appears only under in-plane fields, while out-of-plane fields abruptly alter the behavior.

Conclusion: The findings establish a regime of dimensionally constrained, disorder-driven magnetism and identify self-intercalated van der Waals magnets as a platform for studying the interplay of magnetic disorder, anisotropy, and reduced dimensionality.

Abstract: Over the last decade, the emerging field of two-dimensional van der Waals magnetism has focused mainly on long-range-ordered magnetic phases. Disordered magnetic phases, however, remain largely unexplored. Here, we report a highly anisotropic Griffiths phase driven by self-intercalation in the van der Waals magnet Fe5GeTe2. We find evidence of short-range-ordered ferromagnetic clusters embedded in a paramagnetic matrix, which dominate the magnetic response between the Curie temperature and a higher characteristic Griffiths temperature. An anomalous power-law divergence in the magnetic susceptibility, universal Lee Yang scaling, and distinct entropy anomalies provide further evidence for this phase. Strikingly, the non-analytic Griffiths response emerges exclusively under in-plane magnetic fields, whereas out-of-plane fields abruptly alter this behavior. This directional dependence indicates that the local magnetic interactions within nanoscale clusters are closely coupled to the layered lattice geometry. Our findings establish a regime of dimensionally constrained, disorder-driven magnetism and identify self-intercalated van der Waals magnets as a platform for investigating the interplay between magnetic disorder, anisotropy, and reduced dimensionality.

</details>


### [221] [Collision Cascade Morphology and Defect Nucleation in Pure Tungsten and an Equimolar Refractory W-Ta-Cr-V Alloy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17361)
*Mark Fedorov,Francisco Javier Dominguez-Gutierrez*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Molecular dynamics simulations with machine-learning tabGAP potentials compare collision cascades in pure W and WTaCrV refractory high-entropy alloy (0.1-100 keV PKA). Subcascade formation and light elements (Cr, V) distributed over a larger region in WTaCrV reduce dislocation nucleation and void formation, making the alloy more irradiation-damage resistant than pure W.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pure tungsten is a leading plasma-facing material, but its irradiation-damage resistance is limited. Refractory high-entropy alloys such as WTaCrV are candidate alternatives, yet the atomistic mechanisms governing defect formation under collision cascades—and how they differ from pure W—remain unclear.

Method: Molecular dynamics simulations of collision cascades in pure W and WTaCrV over PKA energies of 0.1-100 keV. Machine-learning tabulated Gaussian approximation potentials (tabGAP) were used, complemented by short-range Ziegler-Biersack-Littmark (ZBL) interactions. Analyses covered heat-spike lifetime, affected region, atomic relocations and surviving defects.

Result: Subcascade formation and the presence of light elements govern defect evolution. Cascade splitting distributes PKA energy over a larger region, nucleating fewer dislocations and smaller voids—voids appear only in W and are absent in WTaCrV. Splitting is more frequent in WTaCrV, which thus shows better resistance to primary irradiation damage. Cr and, to a lesser extent, V accommodate damage as point defects, mitigating dislocation-loop formation.

Conclusion: WTaCrV exhibits superior resistance to primary irradiation damage compared to pure W, driven by more frequent cascade splitting and by light Cr/V atoms that store damage as point defects rather than dislocation loops. These atomistic insights support refractory high-entropy alloys as promising irradiation-tolerant materials.

Abstract: Collision cascades and defect-formation mechanisms in pure W and the WTaCrV refractory high-entropy alloy were investigated using molecular dynamics simulations over primary knock-on atom (PKA) energies of 0.1-100 keV. Machine-learning tabulated Gaussian approximation potentials (tabGAP), complemented by short-range Ziegler-Biersack-Littmark (ZBL) interactions, were employed. Analysis of the heat spike lifetime, affected region, atomic relocations and surviving defects reveals an importance of subcascade formation and presence of light elements in the alloy. Cascade splitting into subcascades contributes to the distribution of the PKA energy over a larger region in the material, nucleating less dislocations and smaller voids - latter being present only in W and absent in WTaCrV. Since cascade splitting is more frequently observed in WTaCrV, the alloy shows better resistance to primary irradiation damage compared to pure W. Apart from cascade splitting, analysis of interstitials shows that Cr and to lesser extent V, being lighter and smaller elements, accommodate the damage in form of point defects, contributing to the mitigation of formation of the dislocation loops.

</details>


### [222] [High-Resolution X-ray Spectro-Microscopy of Au Microcrystallites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17417)
*Chaitali Sow,Martin Seyrich,Johannes Hagemann,Giridhar U. Kulkarni,Thomas F. Keller,Christian G. Schroer,Andreas Schropp*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work demonstrates multimodal X-ray spectro-ptychography on Au microcrystallites, resolving amplitude and phase contrasts across the Au L3 edge (~12 keV) with 45 nm spatial resolution, achieving XANES spectra consistent with standard transmission and fluorescence while enabling quantitative retrieval of anomalous phase variations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding heterogeneous catalyst functionality requires high-resolution nanoscale chemical imaging. X-ray spectro-ptychography can provide XANES-based amplitude and phase contrast imaging at high spatial resolution, but demonstrations at energies above 10 keV (e.g., Au L-edges) remain limited because of weak X-ray absorption cross-sections and low signal levels.

Method: Multimodal spectro-microscopy (X-ray spectro-ptychography) was performed on Au microcrystallites hosting both conventional face-centered cubic (fcc) and unconventional non-cubic lattices relevant to catalysis. Amplitude and phase contrasts across the L3 edge were resolved on thin Au crystallites ranging from 150 nm to 1.71 μm.

Result: Amplitude and phase contrasts were resolved with 45 nm spatial resolution. The resulting XANES spectra show excellent agreement with standard transmission and fluorescence contrasts while offering complementary chemical insights at improved spatial resolution. Quantitative retrieval of anomalous phase variations at ~12 keV was also achieved.

Conclusion: The method is suitable for high-resolution chemical imaging across a wide range of functional materials, extending spectro-ptychography to high-energy edges such as the Au L3 edge.

Abstract: Understanding the functionalities of heterogeneous catalysts requires high-resolution nanoscale chemical imaging tools. X-ray spectro-ptychography offers this capability by enabling X-ray absorption near edge structure (XANES) based amplitude and phase contrasts imaging with high spatial resolution. However, such demonstrations at energies above 10 keV, such as the Au L-edges, remain limited due to weak X-ray absorption cross-sections and correspondingly, low signal levels. Here, multimodal spectro-microscopy has been performed on Au microcrystallites that host both conventional face-centered cubic (fcc) and unconventional non-cubic lattices, particularly important for catalysis. Amplitude and phase contrasts across the $\mathrm{L}_3$ edge are resolved on thin Au crystallites (150 nm to 1.71 $μ\mathrm{m}$) with 45 nm spatial resolution. The resulting XANES spectra exhibit excellent agreement with standard transmission and fluorescence contrasts, while providing complementary chemical insights with improved spatial resolution. Quantitative retrieval of anomalous phase variations ~12 keV further demonstrates the suitability of this method for high-resolution chemical imaging across a wide range of functional materials.

</details>


### [223] [Electric Edelstein Effect in Two Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17535)
*Xiaoran Lv,Tianqi Zhang,Chaoxi Cui,Yaling Li,Yi Shen,Weikang Wu,Ziming Zhu,Yugui Yao,Zhi-Ming Yu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper introduces the electric Edelstein effect (EEE) in 2D systems, where crossed out-of-plane and in-plane electric fields generate gate-tunable spin polarization, with symmetry analysis and first-principles predictions in MnSe2 and SnSe.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional Edelstein effect yields material-fixed spin polarization with limited controllability (one control dimension). A route to gate-tunable, higher-dimensional electrical spin control in 2D quantum materials is needed.

Method: Symmetry analysis across all magnetic layer point groups; microscopic theory linking EEE to band-geometric quantities; first-principles calculations on centrosymmetric ferromagnet MnSe2 and nonmagnet SnSe.

Result: EEE is symmetry-allowed in a wide range of magnetic and nonmagnetic systems (ferromagnets, antiferromagnets, altermagnets). It generates spin polarization where linear EE is forbidden (centrosymmetric materials) and adds a non-collinear gate-tunable component where EE exists, raising control dimension from one to two. Pronounced effects predicted in MnSe2; in SnSe the two knobs cover an extended region of the spin sphere.

Conclusion: EEE establishes a symmetry-guided, gate-tunable route to electrical spin manipulation, opening avenues for multistate spintronics in 2D quantum materials.

Abstract: We propose the electric Edelstein effect (EEE) in two-dimensional (2D) systems, where crossed out-of-plane and in-plane electric fields generate a spin polarization. The gate field converts the Edelstein response from a material-fixed spin polarization into a gate-tunable one. A symmetry analysis over all magnetic layer point groups shows that the EEE is allowed in a wide range of magnetic and nonmagnetic systems, including ferromagnets, antiferromagnets, and altermagnets. The EEE plays two distinct roles: it generates spin polarization where the linear Edelstein effect (EE) is symmetry-forbidden, as in centrosymmetric materials; and it adds a new gate-tunable component, generally non-collinear with the conventional one, where the EE is already present. The two fields then act as independent knobs and raise the control dimension of the spin polarization from one to two. Our microscopic theory traces the EEE to band-geometric quantities, which are strongly enhanced near small band gaps. First-principles calculations predict pronounced effects in the centrosymmetric ferromagnet MnSe2, where the linear EE is symmetry-forbidden. In the nonmagnet SnSe where the EE and EEE coexist, the two knobs cover an extended region of the spin sphere. Our findings establish the EEE as a symmetry-guided, gate-tunable route to electrical spin manipulation and open avenues for multistate spintronics in 2D quantum materials.

</details>


### [224] [Hydrogen-Bond Density Drives the Order-Disorder Phase Transition from Orientational to Vibrational in Polyalcohol Plastic Crystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17774)
*Ares Sanuy,Carlos Escorihuela-Sayalero,Pol Lloveras,María del Barrio,Hassan Allouchi,Josep-Lluís Tamarit,Luis Carlos Pardo,Claudio Cazorla*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper uses classical molecular dynamics of NPG, PG, and PE to show that the microscopic origin of the order-disorder phase-transition entropy in neopentane-derived plastic crystals inverts as hydrogen-bond connectivity increases: orientational disordering dominates in NPG, vibrational entropy dominates (and is pressure-robust) in PE, while PG is intermediate. Hydrogen-bond density is thus proposed as a practical design handle for tuning caloric response magnitude and pressure robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neopentane-derived plastic crystals exhibit order-disorder phase transitions with very large entropy changes, making them promising for thermal energy storage and barocaloric cooling, but it is unclear whether this entropy change is compound-specific or can be understood generally and tuned by molecular design.

Method: Classical molecular dynamics simulations of neopentyl glycol (NPG), pentaglycerine (PG), and pentaerythritol (PE), validated against experimental transition temperatures, enthalpy changes, and phase diagrams, were used to decompose the phase-transition entropy into orientational, conformational, and vibrational channels and to examine their pressure dependence.

Result: The character of the transition entropy inverts with increasing hydrogen-bond connectivity: orientational disordering dominates in NPG, lattice vibrations dominate and are highly pressure-robust in PE, and PG is intermediate with comparable contributions from all three channels. This inversion is traced to two coupled microscopic mechanisms—a growing low-frequency gap between the phonon densities of states of ordered and disordered phases as the hydrogen-bond network densifies, and increasing molecular symmetry that suppresses orientational disordering.

Conclusion: Because the vibrational channel is much less pressure-sensitive than the orientational channel, hydrogen-bond density serves as a chemically accessible design handle for tailoring both the magnitude and the pressure robustness of the caloric response in hydrogen-bonded plastic crystals, with direct implications for discovering new barocaloric and thermal-energy-storage materials.

Abstract: Neopentane-derived plastic crystals undergo order-disorder phase transitions accompanied by exceptionally large entropy changes, making them attractive solid-state materials for thermal energy storage and barocaloric cooling. Yet it remains unclear whether the microscopic nature of this entropy change is an intrinsic property of each compound or can be understood generally and tuned systematically through molecular design. Here, we use classical molecular dynamics simulations of neopentyl glycol (NPG), pentaglycerine (PG), and pentaerythritol (PE), validated against experimental transition temperatures, enthalpy changes, and phase diagrams, to show that the character of the phase-transition entropy undergoes a complete inversion as the hydrogen-bond connectivity of the molecule increases: orientational disordering dominates in NPG, whereas lattice vibrations dominate in PE and become remarkably robust against pressure. PG occupies a balanced intermediate regime in which the orientational, conformational, and vibrational channels contribute comparably. This inversion reflects two coupled microscopic mechanisms: a growing low-frequency gap between the phonon densities of states of the ordered and disordered phases as the hydrogen-bond network densifies, and an increase in molecular symmetry that progressively suppresses orientational disordering. Because the vibrational channel is substantially less sensitive to pressure than the orientational one, hydrogen-bond density emerges as a practical, chemically accessible design handle for tailoring not only the magnitude but also the pressure robustness of the caloric response in hydrogen-bonded plastic crystals, with direct implications for the search for new barocaloric and thermal-energy-storage materials.

</details>


### [225] [Learning from fracture: Teaching language models to design through physical experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17804)
*Bo Ni*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: FracLearn is an experience-abstraction-generalization framework that enables language models to optimize physical designs via a Tetris-inspired fracture game, achieving 11% better composite scores without weight updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether language models can learn to optimize physical designs through experience, using a deliberately unfamiliar fracture game to test the transfer of computational experiments into reusable physical knowledge.

Method: The model proposes architectures and examines simulated fracture responses to accumulate experience. Abstraction organizes observations into conditional principles linking reinforcement placement, crack evolution, and load transfer. Generalization requires combining these principles in new architectures to surpass prior design records.

Result: Campaigns demonstrated progressive refinement and recovery via hypothesis revision, achieving a best composite score 11.0% above the experience-stage record. Fracture analyses linked improved performance to crack deflection, temporary trapping (toughening mechanisms), and greater residual load capacity.

Conclusion: Learning occurs through external knowledge and decisions without model-weight updates, offering a route to scientific agents that convert computational experiments into reusable physical knowledge for materials discovery and engineering design.

Abstract: How can language models learn to optimize physical designs through experience? We introduce FracLearn, an experience-abstraction-generalization framework evaluated through a deliberately unfamiliar, Tetris-inspired fracture game. The model first proposes architectures and examines simulated fracture responses to accumulate experience. Abstraction organizes these observations into conditional principles linking reinforcement placement, crack evolution and load transfer, with supporting evidence and failure conditions. Generalization challenges the model to combine these principles in new architectures and surpass the earlier design record. Campaigns demonstrate progressive refinement and recovery through hypothesis revision, yielding a best composite score 11.0% above the experience-stage record. Fracture analyses connect improved performance to crack deflection and temporary trapping as toughening mechanisms, alongside greater residual load capacity. Learning occurs through external knowledge and decisions, without model-weight updates. This approach offers a route to scientific agents that turn computational experiments into reusable physical knowledge for materials discovery and engineering design.

</details>


### [226] [Polarons in Lead-Halide Perovskites: What the Structural Evidence Does and Does Not Establish](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17809)
*Inhee Maeng,Young Mi Lee,Jinwoo Park,Seung Jae Oh,Min-Cherl Jung*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Three recent findings undermine the standard large-polaron picture in lead-halide perovskites: thermal lattice motion alone can raise carrier effective mass, carrier-lattice coupling channels differ between 2D perovskite families, and the first direct defect-tolerance test favors a soft disordered lattice over polaron screening. Polaron formation, even if established, does not establish polarons as the cause of defect tolerance; separating the two requires measuring lattice distortion versus excitation density across solar and ultrafast regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
