Motivation: The prevailing view holds that photogenerated carriers in lead-halide perovskites move as large polarons whose lattice distortion screens them from charged defects, explaining defect tolerance. Three independent recent results challenge this interpretation, so the field needs a critical reassessment and a decisive experimental test.

Method: The paper is a critical review and perspective rather than new experiments. It examines three lines of recent evidence: (1) time-resolved structural probes measuring photoinduced lattice distortion directly alongside calculations of thermal lattice motion effects on effective mass; (2) comparisons of carrier-lattice coupling in Ruddlesden-Popper versus Dion-Jacobson 2D perovskites, where contrasting behavior is governed by which coupling channel dominates rather than lattice stiffness; and (3) the first direct test of defect tolerance plus new transport data. From this analysis the authors propose experiments measuring lattice distortion as a function of excitation density.

Result: Thermal lattice motion alone can increase carrier effective mass, an effect previously taken as evidence for polarons, so it remains unclear whether lattice distortion around a carrier is truly a polaron. Carrier-lattice coupling strengthens on going from 3D to 2D, with the two main 2D families coupling through different channels. The first direct defect-tolerance test does not support polaron screening; its authors attribute defect tolerance to the soft, disordered lattice. New transport data show polaron screening reduces carrier-carrier scattering but not protection against defects.

Conclusion: Polaron formation, even if established, does not establish polarons as the cause of defect tolerance. Distinguishing the two requires measuring lattice distortion as a function of excitation density over a range spanning the low densities of a solar cell under sunlight and the much higher densities used in most ultrafast experiments. On this basis the authors propose experiments to clarify how photogenerated carriers behave in lead-halide perovskites.

Abstract: Photogenerated carriers in lead-halide perovskites are widely believed to move as large polarons, whose accompanying lattice distortion is thought to screen the carrier from charged defects. Three recent findings call this picture into question. First, time-resolved structural probes now measure the photoinduced lattice distortion directly; yet calculations show that thermal motion of the lattice alone can raise the carrier effective mass-an increase previously taken as evidence for polarons-so that it remains unclear whether the lattice distortion around a carrier is really a polaron. Second, the carrier-lattice coupling strengthens as the material is thinned from 3D to 2D. In the two main families of 2D perovskites, Ruddlesden-Popper and Dion-Jacobson, the carrier couples to the lattice through different channels, and their contrasting behavior is set by which coupling channel dominates rather than by lattice stiffness alone. Third, the first direct test of defect tolerance does not point to polaron screening; its authors attribute defect tolerance to the soft, disordered lattice. New transport data show that polaron screening reduces scattering between carriers, but not that it protects carriers from defects. Polaron formation, even if established, therefore does not establish polarons as the cause of defect tolerance. Separating the two requires measuring the lattice distortion as a function of excitation density, over a range spanning the low densities of a solar cell under sunlight and the much higher densities used in most ultrafast experiments. On this basis, we propose experiments that can clarify how photogenerated carriers behave in lead-halide perovskites.

</details>


### [227] [Orthoexciton-to-paraexciton conversion rate in Cu$_2$O at sub-Kelvin temperatures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17812)
*Yusuke Morita,Yukihiro Akiyama,Yuki Haita,Yuga Hirota,Kosuke Yoshioka*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The conversion rate from 1s orthoexcitons to 1s paraexcitons in Cu$_2$O was measured below 1 K, revealing a rate of $5.9 \times 10^7$ s$^{-1}$ at 64 mK that continues to decrease below 2 K, contrary to prior expectations of a nearly constant value.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work expected the orthoexciton-to-paraexciton conversion rate in Cu$_2$O to become nearly constant below about 1.7 K, but this had not been verified experimentally at very low temperatures. The authors aimed to test this assumption and clarify the low-temperature behavior of the conversion process.

Method: Time-resolved luminescence spectroscopy was performed on 1s orthoexcitons and 1s paraexcitons confined in a strain-induced trap potential, using a dilution refrigerator at temperatures between 64 and 800 mK and an applied stress of 2.2 kbar. The conversion rate was extracted from the time evolution of the $\Gamma_3^-$-LO-phonon-assisted luminescence of 1s orthoexcitons.

Result: A conversion rate of $5.9 \times 10^7$ s$^{-1}$ was obtained at 64 mK under 2.2 kbar stress. Between 64 and 800 mK the rate depends only weakly on temperature and is less than half the value of $1.6 \times 10^8$ s$^{-1}$ previously reported at 2 K under the same stress. The rate thus continues to decrease below 2 K rather than leveling off.

Conclusion: The conversion rate keeps decreasing below 2 K, contradicting the expectation of a nearly constant value below ~1.7 K. This is attributed to the orthoexciton momentum distribution not reaching equilibrium with the lattice during conversion at these low temperatures, motivating a new theory that explains both the small magnitude and weak temperature dependence of the rate below 1 K.

Abstract: We measured the conversion rate from 1s orthoexcitons to 1s paraexcitons in Cu$_2$O below 1 K using a dilution refrigerator. To determine the conversion rate, we performed time-resolved luminescence spectroscopy of 1s orthoexcitons and 1s paraexcitons confined in a strain-induced trap potential. From the time evolution of the $Γ$$_3$$^{-}$-LO-phonon-assisted luminescence of 1s orthoexcitons, we obtained a conversion rate of $5.9 \times 10^7$ s${^{-1}}$ at a mixing-chamber temperature of 64 mK under an applied stress of 2.2 kbar. Between 64 and 800 mK, the conversion rate depends only weakly on temperature, and it is less than half of the value of $1.6 \times 10^8$ s${^{-1}}$ reported at 2 K under the same applied stress. The conversion rate therefore continues to decrease below 2 K, contrary to the previous expectation that it becomes nearly constant below about 1.7 K. We discuss these results in terms of the momentum distribution of orthoexcitons, which does not reach equilibrium with the lattice during the conversion at these low temperatures. These findings call for a theory that accounts for both the small magnitude of the conversion rate and its weak temperature dependence below 1 K.

</details>


### [228] [ARES: a plane-wave density-functional-theory code in Rust](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17854)
*Zehan Liu,Qian Wang,Xiangyu Zheng,Jiaxiang Li,Chuntian Wu,Yongshuo Chen,Xuecheng Shao,Qiang Xu,Yu Xie,Yanming Ma*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: ARES is a Rust-based plane-wave DFT code organized as a 22-crate workspace, whose distinguishing feature is a continuously regenerated validation apparatus comparing every numerical component against Quantum ESPRESSO, achieving sub-0.4 meV median band-energy agreement and a 0.696 meV/atom median delta factor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper's motivation is to present a DFT software whose central engineering choice is verification: establishing trust in numerical correctness through systematic, reproducible comparison against an established reference code rather than through feature breadth alone.

Method: ARES is written entirely in Rust as a layered 22-crate workspace. It supports norm-conserving pseudopotentials and PAW with LDA and PBE, Γ-only and explicit k-point sampling, smearing and mixing, forces, stress, relaxation, band structures, DOS, and dispersion corrections, running serially or via MPI over k-point pools, band groups and plane-wave rows. Each numerical component is validated against Quantum ESPRESSO using identical physical and numerical inputs, organized as numbered repository fixtures whose reference data is regenerated by script rather than captured once.

Result: Across six materials (a semiconductor, three metals and two 2D systems), band energies within 1 eV of the Fermi level agree with the reference to a median below 0.4 meV. The pseudopotentials read by the code are validated against all-electron reference data, yielding a median delta factor of 0.696 meV/atom over all 65 paired elements.

Conclusion: ARES delivers a validated plane-wave DFT implementation with explicitly stated restrictions: only two functionals (LDA, PBE), no spin-polarized or spin-orbit path, a stress implementation of explicit scope, and no accelerator execution.

Abstract: ARES (Ab initio atomic mateRial modEling Software at JLU) is a plane-wave density-functional-theory (DFT) code written entirely in Rust as a layered 22-crate workspace. It supports norm-conserving pseudopotentials and the projector augmented-wave (PAW) method with the local-density approximation (LDA) and Perdew-Burke-Ernzerhof (PBE) functionals, Γ-only and explicit k-point sampling, smearing and mixing, forces, stress, relaxation, band structures, densities of states (DOS) and dispersion corrections; execution is serial or MPI-distributed over k-point pools, band groups and plane-wave rows. The code's distinguishing engineering choice is its validation apparatus. Every numerical component is checked against Quantum ESPRESSO driven from the same physical and numerical inputs, and the comparison is organized as numbered fixtures kept in the repository whose reference data is regenerated by a script instead of being captured once. On six materials spanning a semiconductor, three metals and two two-dimensional systems, band energies within one electronvolt of the Fermi level agree with the reference to a median below 0.4 meV. The pseudopotentials the code reads are validated against all-electron reference data as well: the median delta factor is 0.696 meV/atom over all 65 paired elements. Restrictions are stated with the capabilities: two functionals, no spin-polarized or spin-orbit path, a stress implementation with an explicit scope, and no accelerator execution.

</details>


### [229] [Thermal stability and structural ordering of thick Co$_2$MnAl films grown on MgO and Si substrates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17955)
*David Robles-Cuenca,Juan Antonio González,Juan Pedro Andrés,Miguel Ángel Arranz,Mohamed Salaheldeen,Ricardo López Antón*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: 750 nm-thick Co2MnAl films were grown on MgO(001) and Si to define thermal integration windows; B2 ordering is optimized at 600 °C on MgO, while Si causes interdiffusion, silicides, Kirkendall voids, and A2 disordering above 500 °C, with robust ~600 emu/cm³ magnetization despite Mn-poor stoichiometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most Co2MnAl (CMA) research targets ultrathin films for spintronic junctions, but applications that scale with magnetic volume—such as Anomalous Nernst Effect (ANE) energy harvesters, integrated power inductors, and magnetic MEMS—demand much thicker films. A roadmap for thermally integrating thick CMA layers is therefore needed.

Method: 750 nm thick CMA films were deposited on MgO (001) and Si substrates. Structural analysis was used to track B2 ordering as a function of temperature and substrate, and to identify degradation mechanisms linked to diffusion.

Result: B2 ordering is optimized on MgO at 600 °C. On Si, a disorder transition toward the A2 phase occurs, linked to diffusion-driven degradation. Above 500 °C, Si interdiffusion into the Heusler layer forms interfacial silicides and Kirkendall voids, causing microcracking, resistivity jumps, and a fully disordered A2 phase. Despite Mn-poor stoichiometry (Co2.25Mn0.7Al1.05), films retain ~600 emu/cm³ saturation magnetization, consistent with recent theoretical models for off-stoichiometric Heusler alloys.

Conclusion: The study establishes processing windows for integrating thick CMA layers into multifunctional CMOS-compatible platforms, identifying MgO and temperatures up to ~600 °C as favorable, and highlighting Si-induced degradation above 500 °C.

Abstract: While most studies on Co$2$MnAl (CMA) focus on the ultrathin regime for spintronic junctions, applications scaling with magnetic volume--such as Anomalous Nernst Effect (ANE) energy harvesters, integrated power inductors, and magnetic MEMS--require significantly thicker films. In this work, 750 nm thick CMA films were grown on MgO (001) and Si substrates to establish a roadmap for their thermal integration. Structural analysis reveals that B2 ordering is optimized on MgO at 600 $^\circ$C, which is important for functional devices. In contrast, samples on Si substrates exhibit a disorder transition toward the A2 phase, closely linked to diffusion-driven degradation processes. Above 500 $^\circ$C, Si interdiffusion into the Heusler layer triggers the formation of interfacial silicides and Kirkendall voids, leading to microcracking, electrical resistivity jumps, and a final fully disordered A2 phase. Despite the Mn-poor stoichiometry (Co$2.25$Mn$0.7$Al$1.05$), the films maintain a robust saturation magnetization of ~600 emu/cm$^3$, consistent with recent theoretical models for off-stoichiometric Heusler alloys. These results define the processing windows for integrating thick CMA layers into multifunctional CMOS-compatible platforms.

</details>


### [230] [Field-free Magnetoacoustic Nonreciprocity via Exchange-bias Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17979)
*Weiqi Du,Mingyuan Ma,Zheng Sun,Chong Chen,Shixuan Liang,Deqi Tao,Shihai An,Yifan Fu,Sulei Fu,Feng Pan,Cheng Song*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work demonstrates field-free magnetoacoustic nonreciprocity in an exchange-biased IrMn/FeGaB/Cu/FeGaB multilayer integrated on a shear-horizontal-wave delay line, achieving 21 dB/mm normalized isolation with field-history-programmable sign, and numerically predicting 91 dB/mm via enhanced dynamical magnetic asymmetry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonreciprocal wave propagation is critical for directional signal processing and isolation. Magnetoacoustic platforms offer small footprint and passive operation, but pronounced nonreciprocity conventionally requires an external bias field, which hinders device miniaturization and on-chip integration. A route to zero-field nonreciprocity is therefore needed.

Method: An IrMn/FeGaB/Cu/FeGaB multilayer is integrated with a shear-horizontal-wave delay line. Exchange-bias engineering via the IrMn antiferromagnet stabilizes a remanent magnetic state that simultaneously breaks time-reversal and spatial-inversion symmetries, while tailoring the spin-wave dispersion to attain resonant magnetoacoustic coupling at zero external field. Numerical optimization is then performed to increase the dynamical magnetic asymmetry.

Result: A normalized isolation of 21 dB/mm is measured at zero field, with the sign programmable through the magnetic field-sweep history. Numerical optimization predicts a zero-field normalized isolation of 91 dB/mm upon increasing the dynamical magnetic asymmetry.

Conclusion: Exchange-bias engineering is established as a promising route toward compact RF isolators and circulators, and as a general strategy for magnetoacoustic devices operating without external magnetic fields.

Abstract: Nonreciprocal wave propagation is essential for directional signal processing and isolation. Magnetoacoustic systems are attractive platforms for nonreciprocal devices because of their small footprint and passive operation. However, pronounced nonreciprocity typically requires an external bias field,limiting device miniaturization and integration. Here, we demonstrate field-free magnetoacoustic nonreciprocity in an IrMn/FeGaB/Cu/FeGaB multilayer integrated with a shear-horizontal-wave delay line. Exchange-bias engineering stabilizes a remanent magnetic state with broken time-reversal and spatial-inversion symmetries while tailoring the spin-wave dispersion to achieve resonant magnetoacoustic coupling at zero field. The normalized isolation reaches 21 dB/mm, with its sign programmable by the field-sweep history. Further numerical optimization predicts a zero-field normalized isolation of 91 dB/mm by increasing the dynamical magnetic asymmetry. Our work establishes exchange-bias engineering as a promising route toward compact RF isolators and circulators and as a general strategy for realizing magnetoacoustic devices without external magnetic fields.

</details>


### [231] [Knowledge-Guided Construction of Machine Learning Potentials for Reactive Behavior in Energetic Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17987)
*Guanchen Dong,Huanrong Liu,Lin Wang,Yuxuan Chen,Zhengyu Tu,Qing-an Li,Bin Xu,Jian Li,Shan Zhang,Xiaotong Li,Linghua Tan,Guangcheng Yang,Jun Kang,Rui Su,Pengfei Guan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A refined spherical-harmonic basis set (sphmulti) combined with active-learning strategies yields a data-efficient neural network potential for energetic materials, trained on only 13,680 configurations yet reproducing key properties of 3,3'-diamino-4,4'-azoxyfurazan.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Energetic materials exhibit multiple complex interactions, so constructing reliable machine-learning potentials conventionally requires extensive datasets. The paper aims to reduce data cost while retaining accuracy for such systems.

Method: The authors enhance the neural network interatomic potential (NNAP) with sphmulti, a refined spherical-harmonic basis set combining an extra radial basis with element-specific chemical weighting in each radial channel. They integrate hyperactive learning, farthest-point sampling, and iterative sufficiency criteria with knowledge-guided key events to enable the NNAP to retain essential information for describing the material system.

Result: For 3,3'-diamino-4,4'-azoxyfurazan, the resulting NNAP trained on only 13,680 configurations captures the isomerization barrier, pyrolysis activation energy, and shock Hugoniot behavior. NNAP-driven molecular dynamics simulations reveal that pyrolysis proceeds via furazan-ring opening followed by chain reactions, whereas shock loading induces anisotropic hydrogen transfer driven by interlayer structural deformations.

Conclusion: The proposed scheme enables efficient and reliable machine-learning potentials for energetic materials, achieving accuracy with markedly reduced training data.

Abstract: Energetic materials involve multiple interactions, making reliable machine-learning potentials (MLPs) difficult to construct without extensive datasets. Here, we enhance the ability of the neural network interatomic potential (NNAP) to describe molecular systems using sphmulti, a refined spherical-harmonic basis set that combines extra radial basis with element-specific chemical weighting in each radial channel. We further integrate hyperactive learning, farthest-point sampling, and iterative sufficiency criteria with knowledge-guided key events, enabling the NNAP to retain essential information for describing the material system. For 3,3'-diamino-4,4'-azoxyfurazan, the resulting NNAP is trained on only 13,680 configurations yet captures key properties, including the isomerization barrier, pyrolysis activation energy, and shock Hugoniot behavior. NNAP-driven molecular dynamics simulations further reveal that pyrolysis proceeds through furazan-ring opening followed by chain reactions, whereas shock loading induces anisotropic hydrogen transfer driven by interlayer structural deformations. This work enables efficient and reliable MLPs for energetic materials.

</details>


### [232] [Hydrogen partial pressure as a key parameter in the reactive sputtering of photochromic YHO thin films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18068)
*E. Strods,M. Zubkins,E. Letko,V. Vibornijs,A. Sarakovskis,J. Purans*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: YHO thin films were deposited by reactive magnetron sputtering at varying H2/Ar ratios and post-oxidized; low H2/Ar ratios yield higher transparency, stronger photochromic contrast, faster bleaching, and higher oxygen incorporation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: YHO films are promising photochromic materials, but the influence of hydrogen partial pressure during reactive sputtering on their microstructure, optical, and photochromic properties is not well understood.

Method: Yttrium hydride films were reactively sputtered across a wide range of H2/Ar flow ratios, monitored in situ by plasma optical emission spectroscopy, then post-oxidized in air. Films were characterized by XRD, optical transmittance, photochromic contrast measurements under solar simulator illumination, bleaching kinetics via a double-exponential model, and XPS.

Result: Crystallographic texture changes from highly <100>-textured to polycrystalline with smaller crystallites as H2/Ar increases. Transmittance drops from ~85% to 70% up to H2/Ar = 0.6, then sharply. Photochromic contrast of 690-820 nm films falls from 14% to 7% as H2/Ar rises from 0.08 to 0.60; thinner films show a weaker trend. Effective bleaching time constants are ~1-15 min for H2/Ar < 0.25. XPS shows higher oxygen incorporation at low H2/Ar, deviating from the usual oxygen-content/photochromic-response correlation.

Conclusion: Low hydrogen partial pressure during reactive sputtering is advantageous for achieving higher optical transparency, enhanced photochromic contrast, and faster bleaching kinetics in YHO thin films.

Abstract: Oxygen-containing yttrium hydride (YHO) thin films were synthesised by reactive magnetron sputtering over a wide range of hydrogen-to-argon gas flow ratios (H2/Ar) during yttrium hydride deposition, followed by post-oxidation in air. The deposition process was monitored in situ by plasma optical emission spectroscopy. X-ray diffraction reveals a strong dependence of crystallographic texture on the H2/Ar ratio: highly <100>-textured films formed at low H2/Ar ratios progressively evolve into more polycrystalline microstructures with slightly reduced crystallite size as the hydrogen content increases. The optical transmittance gradually decreases from approximately 85 % to 70 % as the H2/Ar ratio is raised to 0.6, followed by a sharp drop at higher hydrogen contents. The photochromic contrast of 690-820 nm thick YHO films, measured after 10 min of illumination with a solar simulator, decreases from 14 % to 7 % as the H2/Ar ratio increases from 0.08 to 0.60. Thinner films (360-490 nm) exhibit a similar but less pronounced trend. Bleaching kinetics, analysed using a double-exponential model, indicate faster recovery, with effective bleaching time constants of approximately 1-15 min for films deposited at H2/Ar < 0.25. X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy reveals higher oxygen incorporation in films deposited at lower H2/Ar ratios, deviating from the commonly reported correlation between oxygen content and photochromic response in YHO. These results demonstrate that low hydrogen partial pressure during reactive sputtering is advantageous for achieving higher optical transparency, enhanced photochromic contrast, and faster bleaching kinetics in YHO thin films.

</details>


### [233] [Tellurium trioxide as an n-type wide-gap semiconductor with ultrahigh work functions from first principles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18076)
*Tianwei Wang,Teruya Nagafuji,Kota Hanzawa,Hidenori Hiramatsu,Fumiyasu Oba*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: First-principles study identifies β-TeO3 as a wide-gap (~>3 eV) n-type semiconductor with ultrahigh work functions up to ~8 eV, clarifying donor impurities, O-vacancy behavior, and surface effects for hole-transport and transparent-electrode applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Next-generation electronic and optoelectronic devices require wide-gap semiconductors with extreme work functions, motivating the search for materials with ultrahigh work functions and controllable doping/surface properties.

Method: First-principles calculations of β-TeO3, including evaluation of band gap, work functions across low-index nonpolar surfaces, and analysis of point defects (interstitial H, Li, Na, Cu, Mg, Zn; substitutional F; O vacancies) and their effects on electronic properties.

Result: β-TeO3 has a band gap exceeding 3 eV and ultrahigh work functions up to ~8 eV. Interstitial H, Li, Na, Cu, Mg, Zn and substitutional F act as shallow donors; O vacancies are minor carrier-compensating defects that locally trap electrons at neighboring Te sites. Ordered O vacancies are energetically favored on low-index nonpolar surfaces and reduce work functions.

Conclusion: Precise control of O chemical potential is needed to suppress surface O-vacancy formation and preserve the ultrahigh work functions. β-TeO3 shows promise for hole injection/extraction/transport layers and transparent electrodes (e.g., light-emitting devices, photovoltaic cells), with guidance on carrier doping and surface tuning.

Abstract: The exploration of next-generation wide-gap semiconductors with extreme work functions is highly desirable for the continued advancement of electronic and optoelectronic devices. By using first principles calculations, we show that \b{eta}-TeO3, with a wide band gap exceeding 3 eV, emerges as a promising n-type semiconductor exhibiting ultrahigh work functions of up to ~8 eV. Intentionally or unintentionally incorporated interstitial H, Li, Na, Cu, Mg, and Zn impurities and substitutional F impurities are identified as shallow donors, whereas O vacancies act as minor carrier-compensating defects that locally trap electrons at neighbouring Te sites. The work function is evaluated across a series of low-index nonpolar surfaces. The presence of ordered O vacancies is energetically favoured on these surfaces and tends to reduce the corresponding work functions. These findings underscore the significance of precise control over the O chemical potential to suppress surface O-vacancy formation, thereby preserving the exceptional electronic performance of \b{eta}-TeO3 associated with ultrahigh work functions. Collectively, the results highlight the potential of \b{eta}-TeO3 for hole injection/extraction/transport layers as well as transparent electrodes for applications such as light-emitting devices and photovoltaic cells, while providing useful guidance on carrier doping and surface tuning for these practical applications through elucidating the effects of the point-defect and surface configurations on the electronic properties.

</details>


### [234] [High-throughput screening of Heusler alloys for thermoelectrics combining machine-learned densities of states and first-principles transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18179)
*Shivam Randive,Ayana Ghosh,Rahul Dixit,Himanshu Pandey*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper tests whether density-of-states (DOS)-based screening—using both DFT spectra and ML-predicted spectra—can reliably identify promising thermoelectric Heusler compounds, validating rankings against full band-structure and transport calculations across the DXMag database.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discovering new thermoelectrics requires transport calculations that are too computationally expensive to run across entire materials databases. Screening must therefore prioritize which compounds to calculate, and the DOS is increasingly used (often via ML predictions) as the selection criterion—yet these selection choices are rarely validated against actual transport performance.

Method: The authors screened all 106,235 Heusler structures in the DXMag database: 51,257 using density-functional DOS spectra and 54,978 using neural-network-predicted DOS. They then evaluated both screening approaches against 3,625 full band-structure and transport calculations, using a DOS-derived Seebeck indicator as the ranking metric and applying transport and stability filters.

Result: The DOS-derived Seebeck indicator ranks most compounds that pass every transport and stability test within the top 1% of their population. Ten of eleven established half-Heusler thermoelectrics calculated also pass. Predicted spectra lose most band gaps—a failure undetected by spectral-similarity metrics—yet still rank passing compounds near the top. The screen certifies 80 computational candidates, 61 of which lack reported measurements.

Conclusion: Computed or ML-predicted DOS can serve as an effective selector of which compounds to calculate, but it cannot confirm the presence of a band gap; only an explicit band structure can establish whether a gap exists. The screening yields 80 certified candidate thermoelectrics for further study.

Abstract: Thermoelectrics turn waste heat into electricity, but finding new ones requires transport calculations too costly for whole databases. Searches therefore choose what to calculate from the electronic density of states (DOS), increasingly predicted by machine learning, yet such choices are rarely tested. We test them on all 106,235 Heusler structures in the DXMag database, screening 51,257 on density-functional spectra and 54,978 on neural-network predictions, and judging both by 3,625 band-structure and transport calculations. A DOS-derived Seebeck indicator ranks most compounds passing every transport and stability test within the top 1% of their population, and ten of the eleven established half-Heusler thermoelectrics calculated pass. Predicted spectra lose most band gaps, unnoticed by spectral-similarity metrics, yet still rank passing compounds near the top. The screen certifies 80 computational candidates, 61 without reported measurements. Computed or predicted, the DOS can choose what to calculate; only a band structure shows whether a gap exists.

</details>


### [235] [Orientation-Resolved Nanoindentation of Single Crystal Ti-15Mo: Inverted Elastic Anisotropy and Yield-Onset Behavior Beyond Simple CRSS Predictions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18181)
*Kidus Abebe,David Gaetano,Aurelia Moriyama-Gurish,Jana Šmilauerová,Yanfei Gao,Udo D. Schwarz,Amit Datye*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: By combining EBSD with nanoindentation on single-crystal Ti-15Mo, the authors map the orientation dependence of hardness, indentation modulus, strain-rate sensitivity, and yield onset. They find an inverted elastic anisotropy (lowest modulus near ⟨111⟩, contrary to cubic-crystal prediction), a 41% spread in yield onset that is softest near ⟨111⟩, and V* values (18–31 b³) indicating kink-pair-nucleation plasticity, with retained athermal ω proposed as the source of the anomalous behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ti-15Mo is a β-stabilized alloy whose mechanical response is governed by single-grain-scale phase stability and deformation mechanisms, which are intrinsically anisotropic. Since orientation dependence is critical for texture-engineered processing of wrought components, there is a need to quantify the nanomechanical anisotropy of the single-crystal β-phase and test whether simple CRSS/Schmid-factor models capture its elastic and plastic response.

Method: EBSD was combined with nanoindentation to correlate hardness, indentation modulus, strain-rate sensitivity, and indentation yield response with the crystallographic surface normal near ⟨111⟩, ⟨124⟩, ⟨011⟩, ⟨023⟩, and ⟨013⟩ directions in single-crystal Ti-15Mo. Strain-rate-jump tests were used to extract activation volumes V*, and the results were compared against simple CRSS/Schmid-factor models.

Result: Modulus anisotropy is small (~10%) but inverted relative to the prediction for a cubic crystal with A>1, with orientations near ⟨111⟩ showing the lowest moduli. Yield onset is also softest near ⟨111⟩ and spans 41%, whereas hardness spans only 6.6% and follows neither the modulus nor the yield ordering. Yield behavior is not captured by the simple CRSS/Schmid-factor models. Strain-rate-jump tests give V* = 18–31 b³, consistent with kink-pair-nucleation-controlled plasticity, and V* separates the orientations into two groups suggestive of glide-dominated versus twin/transformation-participating response.

Conclusion: An inversion of the elastic anisotropy expected for a homogeneous cubic β-phase and an orientation-dependent yield-onset response not reproduced by simple Schmid-factor models are revealed. Retained athermal ω is proposed as the contributor, because β→ω collapse occurs on {111}β planes and may provide anelastic compliance through the reversible ω→β transformation.

Abstract: Ti-15Mo (wt%) is a well-established $β$-stabilized alloy whose mechanical response is strongly governed by phase stability and deformation mechanisms that operate at the single-grain scale. Because these intragranular mechanisms are intrinsically anisotropic, understanding the orientation dependence of the elastic and plastic response is critical to the texture-engineered processing of wrought components. Here, we quantify the nanomechanical anisotropy of single-crystal Ti-15Mo by combining EBSD with nanoindentation to correlate hardness, indentation modulus, strain rate sensitivity, and indentation yield response with the crystallographic surface normal near $\langle111\rangle,\langle124\rangle,\langle011\rangle,\langle023\rangle$, and $\langle013\rangle$ directions. While the modulus anisotropy is small (~10%), contrary to the prediction for a cubic crystal with A>1, orientations near $\langle111\rangle$ exhibit the lowest moduli. Yield onset is likewise softest near $\langle111\rangle$ and spans 41%, while hardness spans only 6.6% and follows neither the modulus nor the yield ordering. The alloy also exhibits yield behavior not captured by the simple CRSS/Schmid-factor models evaluated here. Strain-rate-jump tests yield V*=18-31b$^3$, consistent with kink-pair-nucleation-controlled plasticity; based on V*, the orientations separate into two groups suggestive of glide-dominated versus twin/transformation-participating response. We reveal an inversion of the elastic anisotropy expected for a homogeneous cubic $β$-phase and an orientation-dependent yield-onset response that is not reproduced by the simple Schmid-factor models examined here. Retained athermal $ω$ is proposed as the contributor because $β\rightarrowω$ collapse occurs on {111}$_β$ planes and may provide anelastic compliance through the reversible $ω\rightarrowβ$ transformation.

</details>


### [236] [MINFF: A Family of Classical Force Fields for Minerals with Tunable Angle Terms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18220)
*Michael Holmboe,Nathan Lavauzelle,Rogers E. Swai*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: MINFF is a new transferable force field for layered and non-layered oxides/hydroxides (Si, Al, Fe, Mg, Ca, Ti, Li, F) that replaces explicit M-O bond terms with electrostatics, dispersion, and a single harmonic M-O triplet angle term, achieving sub-1% reproduction of structural metrics and outperforming CLAYFF and INTERFACE on stability and diffraction/elastic benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Atomistic simulations of minerals are constrained by the accuracy and transferability of existing force fields, which typically rely on explicit metal-oxygen bond terms of limited generality. The authors seek a force field family that is both broadly transferable across many oxide/hydroxide chemistries and tunably accurate, while remaining compatible with established water and ion-pair models.

Method: MINFF omits M-O bond terms; M-O distances emerge from electrostatics and dispersion, with coordination geometry governed by a single harmonic angle term over all M-O triplets. Metal charges use DDEC6 charges from reference minerals; oxygen charges are set by nearest-neighbour charge smearing covering basal, apical, hydroxyl, edge and substitution-adjacent sites. Oxygen Lennard-Jones parameters derive from OPC3, and metal LJ parameters were fitted to unit-cell metrics, bond distances and angles—both globally across >30 reference minerals (GMINFF) and per-mineral (TMINFF)—at four angle force constants (0, 250, 500, 1500 kJ/mol/rad^2).

Result: With angle terms, MINFF reproduces experimental unit-cell metrics within 0.5%, atom-type distances within 0.7-1.4%, and angles within 0.1-0.4%, with Global Instability Index values as low as 0.11 (below CLAYFF 0.34 and INTERFACE 0.21). A moderate angle constant reproduces stiff in-plane and soft shear elastic constants of phyllosilicates, improves Kaolinite librational bands, and yields the lowest residual factor against the 2W 00l pattern in hydrated Na-Montmorillonite among tested models. Oxygen LJ parameters shift less than 5% upon per-mineral re-optimisation, so OPC3 compatibility is measured rather than assumed.

Conclusion: MINFF provides a transferable yet fidelity-tunable force field for oxides and hydroxides, where general (GMINFF) and tailored (TMINFF) parameter sets at four angle stiffnesses give direct control over the transferability-fidelity trade-off. Topologies are accessible via the atom (MATLAB) and atomipy (Python) libraries and at www.atomipy.io.

Abstract: Atomistic simulations of minerals are limited by the accuracy and transferability of the force field. We introduce MINFF, a force field family for layered and non-layered oxides and hydroxides of M being Si, Al, Fe, Mg, Ca, Ti, Li and F. MINFF has no M-O bond terms. Instead M-O distances follow from electrostatics and dispersion, while a single harmonic angle term on all M-O triplets controls coordination geometry. Metal charges are DDEC6 charges from reference mineral structures, and oxygen charges are assigned by nearest-neighbour charge smearing, covering basal, apical, hydroxyl and edge sites and sites adjacent to isomorphous substitutions. The oxygen Lennard-Jones parameters are based on the OPC3 water model and change by less than 5% when re-optimised per mineral, so compatibility with OPC3 and related ion-pair potentials is measured rather than assumed. Metal Lennard-Jones parameters were fitted to unit-cell metrics, bond distances and angles, both synchronously across more than 30 reference minerals (general, GMINFF) and for individual minerals (tailored, TMINFF), each at four angle force constants (0, 250, 500 and 1500 kJ/mol/rad^2). With angle terms, MINFF reproduces experimental unit-cells metrics to within 0.5% and atom-type distances and angles to within 0.7-1.4% and 0.1-0.4%, with Global Instability Index values as low as 0.11, below those of CLAYFF (0.34) and INTERFACE (0.21). A moderate angle constant reproduces the stiff in-plane and soft shear elastic constants of phyllosilicates and improves the Kaolinite librational bands, and in hydrated Na-Montmorillonite MINFF gives the lowest residual factor against the 2W 00l pattern of the models tested. The general and tailored sets at four angle stiffnesses give direct control over the transferability-fidelity trade-off. Topologies can be generated with the atom (MATLAB) and atomipy (Python) libraries and at www.atomipy.io.

</details>


### [237] [Transition-Metal-Induced Uniaxial Anisotropy in YFe$_3$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18303)
*M. Nur Hasan,Bruno L. Fertig,Santa Pile,Rafael M. Vieira,Alena Vishina,Alexander Kovacs,Gotthard Rieger,Stefan Denneler,Samuel J. R. Holt,Martin Lang,Swapneel Pathak,Andrea Petrocchi,Hans Fangohr,Thomas George Woodcock,Thomas Schrefl,Heike C. Herper*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Selective transition-metal substitution (V, Nb, Ta) drives an easy-plane-to-easy-axis transition in YFe3, with YFe2.33Ta0.67 achieving a large uniaxial anisotropy of 2.3 MJ/m3 while preserving high magnetization and thermal stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fe-rich permanent magnets require uniaxial magnetocrystalline anisotropy combined with high magnetization and thermal stability, but YFe3, despite its favorable saturation magnetization and Curie temperature, suffers from intrinsic easy-plane anisotropy that limits permanent-magnet applications.

Method: Systematic first-principles screening of transition-metal dopants; calculation of exchange interactions, concentration-dependent magnetocrystalline anisotropy energy (MAE), atomistic Monte Carlo and micromagnetic simulations; experimental synthesis and heat treatment of V-alloyed YFe3 with characterization by electron backscatter diffraction and Kerr microscopy.

Result: V, Nb, and Ta identified as effective dopants; YFe2.33Ta0.67 shows a large uniaxial MAE of 2.3 MJ/m3 with robust ferromagnetic order and high thermal stability, the highest coercivity and strongest resistance to irreversible demagnetization, while YFe2.33V0.67 reaches a comparable maximum energy product at 300 K. Experimentally, the YFe3 phase is retained at low nominal V (1-3 at.%) but becomes increasingly multiphase at higher V; MAE calculations show a sign reversal between ~11 and 14 at.% V, consistent with observed in-plane magnetic domains in the low-V regime.

Conclusion: Selective transition-metal substitution provides a promising route to tune the magnetocrystalline anisotropy of YFe3 from easy-plane to easy-axis while retaining high Curie temperatures, enabling viable Fe-rich permanent-magnet candidates.

Abstract: Achieving uniaxial magnetocrystalline anisotropy while preserving high magnetization and thermal stability remains a central challenge in the development of Fe-rich permanent magnets. YFe$_3$ is a promising candidate owing to its saturation magnetization and Curie temperature, but its intrinsic easy-plane anisotropy limits its potential for permanent-magnet applications. Here, we show that selective transition-metal substitution drives an easy-plane-to-easy-axis transition in YFe$_3$. Systematic first-principles screening identifies V, Nb, and Ta as effective dopants, with YFe$_{2.33}$Ta$_{0.67}$ exhibiting a large uniaxial magnetocrystalline anisotropy energy of 2.3~MJ/m$^{3}$. Calculated exchange interactions and atomistic Monte Carlo simulations show that the enhanced anisotropy is achieved while preserving robust ferromagnetic order and high thermal stability. Micromagnetic simulations further reveal that YFe$_{2.33}$Ta$_{0.67}$ exhibits the highest coercivity and strongest resistance to irreversible demagnetization, whereas YFe$_{2.33}$V$_{0.67}$ achieves a comparable maximum energy product at 300~K. Experimental characterization of V-alloyed YFe$_3$ shows that the YFe$_3$ phase can be retained at low nominal V concentrations (1-3 at.\%) under the employed synthesis and heat-treatment conditions, whereas higher V contents lead to increasingly multiphase samples. Concentration-dependent MAE calculations reveal a sign reversal between approximately 11 and 14~at.\% V, marking the transition from easy-plane to uniaxial easy-axis anisotropy. Consistent with this concentration dependence, electron backscatter diffraction and Kerr microscopy reveal in-plane magnetic domain structures in the experimentally accessible low-V regime. Therefore, our work offers a promising approach to tuning the magnetocrystalline anisotropy of YFe$_3$ while retaining high Curie temperatures.

</details>


### [238] [Accurately Generalizing Deep-Learning Hamiltonian Methods to Plane-Wave Density Functional Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18317)
*Chuntian Wu,Jiaxiang Li,Qiang Xu,Xuecheng Shao,Congwei Xie,Yu Xie,Yanming Ma*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper proposes a projectability-guided method to construct compact nonorthogonal atomic-orbital (AO) Hamiltonians from plane-wave (PW) eigenstates and eigenvalues, enabling neural networks to learn PBE and HSE Hamiltonians for efficient electronic-structure prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep neural networks that predict DFT Hamiltonians from material structures enable efficient electronic-structure calculations, but extending localized AO Hamiltonian learning to PW calculations with hybrid functionals requires a compact AO representation preserving nonlocal operator effects.

Method: A projectability-guided construction builds nonorthogonal AO Hamiltonians from a small, selected set of PW eigenstates and eigenvalues. High-projectability states are retained, and selected higher-energy states complete the AO subspace, avoiding an empirical shift. By using PW eigenstates and eigenvalues, nonlocal contributions are incorporated without explicitly reconstructing AO matrix elements. Equivariant neural networks then learn the resulting PBE and HSE Hamiltonians.

Result: Benchmarks for MoS2 and sodium phases reproduce PW bands accurately with smaller AO bases than real-space reconstruction. The resulting PBE and HSE Hamiltonians can be learned by equivariant neural networks.

Conclusion: The approach broadens the range of PW DFT calculations available for AO Hamiltonian learning and enables compact, accurate training targets for developing more widely applicable electronic-structure models.

Abstract: Deep neural networks that predict density functional theory (DFT) Hamiltonians from material structures offer a route to efficient electronic-structure calculations. However, extending localized atomic-orbital (AO) Hamiltonian learning to plane-wave (PW) calculations with hybrid functionals requires a compact AO representation that preserves the effects of nonlocal operators. Here we propose a projectability-guided construction of nonorthogonal AO Hamiltonians from a small, selected set of PW eigenstates and eigenvalues. High-projectability states are retained, and selected higher-energy states complete the AO subspace, avoiding an empirical shift and its arbitrary contribution to the learning targets. Because the construction uses PW eigenstates and eigenvalues, it incorporates nonlocal contributions present in the reference calculation without explicitly reconstructing their AO matrix elements. Benchmarks for MoS2 and sodium phases show accurate reproduction of PW bands with smaller AO bases than real-space reconstruction. The resulting PBE and HSE Hamiltonians can be learned by equivariant neural networks, connecting PW reference calculations with electronic-structure prediction. This approach broadens the range of PW DFT calculations available for AO Hamiltonian learning and enables compact, accurate training targets for developing more widely applicable electronic-structure models.

</details>


### [239] [Reversible Magneto-ionic Modification of Metallic Magnetic Thin Films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18396)
*Md Golam Hafiz,Hari Babu Vasili,Philippa Shepley,Mannan Ali,Andrew J. Britton,Oscar Cespedes,Weibin Li,Manuel Valvidares,Rohit Pachat,Wilfried Fotso,Mourad Cherif,Shimpei Ono,Yves Roussigne,Mohamed Belmeguenai,Gavin Burnell*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Ionic liquid gating enables reversible, nonvolatile oxygen migration through fully metallic magnetic multilayers, tuning PMA, coercivity, domain nucleation/pinning, magnetic moment, and DMI, with promise for field-programmable spintronic devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnetic multilayers with structural inversion asymmetry, PMA, and large DMI are important for domain-wall- and skyrmion-based data processing, motivating a need for a controlled, reversible, nonvolatile way to locally modify such systems without degrading their fully metallic structure.

Method: Electrically driven oxygen migration via ionic liquid gating applied to metallic magnetic multilayers, using voltage polarity to move oxygen ions into or out of the magnetic layer and the HM/FM interface; changes in oxidation state and magnetic properties are probed magnetically.

Result: Direct evidence of oxidation-state modification at the HM/FM interface. Oxidation increases Keff and Hc; reduction decreases them. Positive gate voltage extracts oxygen ions from the magnetic layer and strongly reduces the domain nucleation field. Negative voltage drives oxygen ions into the magnetic layer, reversibly increasing domain wall pinning near saturation. Negative voltage also decreases both magnetic moment and DMI.

Conclusion: Magneto-ionic modulation in fully metallic structures provides a controlled, nonvolatile, reversible means to tune key magnetic parameters, beneficial for spintronic applications, especially field-programmable domain wall and skyrmion devices.

Abstract: Magnetic multilayers with structural inversion asymmetry, perpendicular magnetic anisotropy (PMA), and large Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction (DMI) are widely studied for magnetic domain wall- and skyrmion-based data processing applications. In this work, we report a controlled, nonvolatile, but reversible local modification of such a system by electrically driven oxygen migration through the metallic film structure by using ionic liquid gating. Our findings provide direct evidence for the modification of the oxidation state at the heavy metal (HM)/ferromagnet (FM) interface. As a result, we observed changes in fundamental magnetic properties, such as an increase in the effective anisotropy constant (Keff) and coercive field (Hc) with oxidation, and a decrease with the reduction of oxygen ions. Positive gate voltages relative to the thin film extract oxygen ions from the magnetic layer, significantly reducing the domain nucleation field. In contrast, negative voltages drive oxygen ions into the magnetic layer, leading to a reversible increase in the extent of domain wall pinning near saturation. We observe a corresponding decrease in the magnetic moment and DMI, with negative voltage reducing both. This magneto-ionic modulation in fully metallic structures is beneficial for spintronic device applications, particularly in field-programmable domain wall and skyrmion devices.

</details>


### [240] [Substrate-controlled epitaxial-like growth and photochromic properties of yttrium oxyhydride thin films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18448)
*M. Zubkins,E. Strods,E. Letko,H. Mändar,D. Moldarev,K. Mizohata,K. Sarakinos,V. Vibornijs,K. Kundzins,J. Purans*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: YHO thin films were reactively sputtered on YSZ(111)/(100), SiO2, and glass, then oxidized. Substrate orientation controls microstructure and texture (epitaxial-like, mosaic spread ~3-4°), but photochromic response depends mainly on composition and local O/H configuration, not long-range ordering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how crystallographic texture and composition relate to the photochromic properties of yttrium oxyhydride (YHO) thin films, a material of interest for optical and optoelectronic applications.

Method: Reactive magnetron sputtering of β-YHx precursor films onto single-crystalline YSZ(111) and (100) substrates at 200 °C, followed by post-deposition air oxidation. Reference films on amorphous SiO2 and soda-lime glass at 200 °C and RT. Characterization by XRD, SEM, elastic recoil detection analysis, and optical/photochromic measurements.

Result: Substrate orientation strongly influences microstructure and crystallographic texture, giving epitaxial-like growth with mosaic spread ~3-4°. YSZ(100) yields larger crystallites (10-20 nm) than YSZ(111) (~8 nm) and residual in-plane compressive strain while largely relaxed to the substrate. 200 °C films have lower oxygen content (O/Y = 0.71-0.82) than RT films (O/Y = 0.92-1.26). All films are photochromic under solar simulator illumination, with relative transmittance changes of 7-22% after 10 min.

Conclusion: Epitaxial-like films show no systematic improvement in photochromic response despite pronounced structural differences, indicating the effect is governed primarily by composition and local O/H configuration rather than long-range cation ordering. These findings clarify the relationship between crystallographic texture, composition, and photochromic properties in YHO thin films.

Abstract: Yttrium oxyhydride (YHO) thin films are synthesised by reactive magnetron sputtering of \b{eta}-YHx precursor films onto single-crystalline yttria-stabilised zirconia (YSZ) (111) and (100) substrates at 200 °C, followed by post-deposition oxidation in air. For comparison, reference films are also prepared on amorphous SiO2 and soda-lime glass substrates at both 200 °C and room temperature (RT). The structural (X-ray diffraction and scanning electron microscopy), compositional (elastic recoil detection analysis), and optical properties, including photochromic behaviour, of the films are systematically investigated. The results reveal a strong influence of substrate orientation on the film microstructure and crystallographic texture, leading to epitaxial-like growth with a mosaic spread of ~3-4°. YHO deposited on YSZ(100) exhibits larger crystallite sizes (10-20 nm) than YHO grown on YSZ(111) (~8 nm) and exhibits residual in-plane compressive strain while remaining largely relaxed with respect to the substrate. Films deposited at 200 °C exhibit lower oxygen concentrations (O/Y = 0.71-0.82) than films deposited at RT (O/Y = 0.92-1.26). All films exhibit photochromic behaviour under solar simulator illumination, with relative transmittance changes ranging from 7 to 22 % after 10 min of illumination. Despite the pronounced structural differences, the epitaxial-like films show no systematic improvement in photochromic response, indicating that the effect is governed primarily by composition and the local O/H configuration rather than by long-range cation ordering. These findings provide new insights into the relationship between crystallographic texture, composition, and photochromic properties in YHO thin films, contributing to the understanding of this material for optical and optoelectronic applications.

</details>


### [241] [Flexoelectricity and Flexomagnetism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18525)
*Alexander Edström,Massimiliano Stengel*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This review chapter examines how strain gradients (odd under space inversion) and curvature in nanoscale and curved low-dimensional systems enable symmetry-forbidden couplings to electric polarization and magnetism, focusing on flexoelectricity and flexomagnetism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strain gradients break inversion symmetry, unlike homogeneous strain, and unlock couplings strictly forbidden in the parent crystal. At the nanoscale—especially in curved low-dimensional systems—such gradients are readily attainable and often unavoidable, making curvature act as a symmetry-breaking field written into the lattice itself. This motivates a unified review of the resulting couplings to electric polarization and magnetism.

Method: The chapter first establishes the geometric relation between strain gradients and curvature. It then reviews flexoelectricity (bulk, surface, and dynamic contributions; microscopic mechanisms; manifestation in 2D materials; first-principles methodology). Using the magnetoelectric effect as a conceptual bridge, it develops flexomagnetism in a broad sense, covering couplings between magnetism and strain gradients, including linear magnetization response to curvature and curvature-induced Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions beyond the established curvilinear treatment, plus additional effects such as nonlinear, inhomogeneous magnetoelectric and Rashba-type phenomena, and connections to altermagnetism.

Result: The review consolidates flexoelectricity as the better-established reference case and extends the framework to flexomagnetism in a broad sense, covering linear magnetization response to curvature, curvature-induced Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions, nonlinear effects, inhomogeneous magnetoelectric and Rashba-type phenomena, and connections to altermagnetism.

Conclusion: Strain gradients and curvature serve as symmetry-breaking fields in the lattice, enabling a broad family of couplings to electric polarization (flexoelectricity) and magnetism (flexomagnetism). The chapter provides a unified conceptual framework and first-principles methodology for these effects in nanoscale and curved low-dimensional systems.

Abstract: Strain gradients, unlike homogeneous strain, are odd under space inversion and unlock couplings that are strictly forbidden in the parent crystal. At the nanoscale, and in curved low-dimensional systems in particular, such gradients are readily attainable and often unavoidable, so that curvature acts as a symmetry-breaking field written into the lattice itself. This chapter reviews the resulting couplings to electric polarization and magnetism. After establishing the geometric relation between strain gradients and curvature, we review flexoelectricity as the better-established reference case: the bulk, surface and dynamic contributions that complicate its definition, its microscopic mechanisms, its manifestation in two-dimensional materials, and the first-principles methodology now available for its calculation. Using the magnetoelectric effect as a conceptual bridge, we then develop flexomagnetism in a broad sense, covering any coupling phenomena between magnetism and strain gradients, and specifically discuss the linear magnetization response to curvature and curvature-induced Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions, beyond the established curvilinear treatment. Finally, we briefly describe some additional effects fitting under this broad umbrella of flexomagnetism, including nonlinear ones, inhomogeneous magnetoelectric and Rashba-type phenomena, and connections to altermagnetism.

</details>


### [242] [Imaging Gate-Tunable Single-Layer Molecular Kagome Lattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18539)
*Ziyi Wang,Rafal Zuzak,Weichen Tang,Haowen Pang,Yutang Qiao,Dhanvanth Balakrishnan,Yuqiao Yan,Aining Hu,Andrew Sun,Takashi Taniguchi,Kenji Watanabe,Feng Wang,Steven G. Louie,Michael F. Crommie*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Single-layer Ni3(HITP)2 kagome MOF films were grown on van der Waals substrates, including graphene field-effect devices, via metal-salt molecular beam epitaxy. Spectroscopy resolved kagome-derived flat-band and van Hove singularity features matching first-principles calculations, and gating shifted these features, demonstrating electrostatic control of chemically programmable 2D kagome electronic lattices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kagome lattices host novel quantum electronic states, but in conventional kagome materials these states are embedded in chemically complex 3D crystals and are hard to isolate or tune. Molecule-based kagome networks offer structural precision and tunability but have mostly been limited to metallic substrates incompatible with 2D devices.

Method: Metal-salt molecular beam epitaxy was used to grow single-layer Ni3(HITP)2 metal-organic frameworks (MOFs) on van der Waals substrates, including graphene-based field-effect devices. Low-temperature scanning tunneling spectroscopy characterized the electronic structure, supported by first-principles calculations, and gating of hybrid 2D MOF devices was used to tune the kagome spectral features.

Result: Low-temperature scanning tunneling spectroscopy resolved kagome-derived flat-band and van Hove singularity-based spectral features consistent with first-principles calculations. Gating hybrid 2D MOF devices energetically shifted the kagome spectral features.

Conclusion: The work establishes electrostatic control of chemically programmable 2D kagome electronic lattices, enabling molecule-based kagome networks on van der Waals substrates compatible with 2D devices.

Abstract: Kagome lattices provide a simple geometry for engineering electronic states that exhibit novel quantum behavior. In conventional kagome materials, however, the relevant states are often embedded in chemically complex three-dimensional crystals and are difficult to isolate or tune. Molecule-based kagome networks offer structural precision and tunability, but have largely been confined to metallic substrates incompatible with 2D devices. Here we report growth of single-layer Ni$_3$(HITP)$_2$ metal-organic frameworks (MOFs) on van der Waals substrates including graphene-based field-effect devices using metal-salt molecular beam epitaxy. Low-temperature scanning tunneling spectroscopy resolves kagome-derived flat-band and van Hove singularity-based spectral features consistent with first-principles calculations. Gating hybrid 2D MOF devices allows us to energetically shift kagome spectral features, thus establishing electrostatic control of chemically programmable 2D kagome electronic lattices.

</details>


### [243] [Time-Resolved Phonon Dispersions of Thermally Excited Graphite at the Nanoscale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18561)
*Mairi McCauley,Christoph T. Koch,Benedikt Haas*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work demonstrates time-resolved phonon dispersion measurement by combining momentum-resolved vibrational EELS with pulsed laser heating and gated electron detection, tracking graphite phonon branches during microsecond heating/cooling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lattice vibrations govern heat propagation, and time-resolved phonon dispersion measurements would reveal these excitations and their interactions, but no method had directly resolved phonon dispersion at the nanoscale in a time-resolved manner.

Method: The authors integrate momentum-resolved vibrational EELS with pulsed laser heating and synchronised gated electron detection. They record gain and loss spectra at defined delays relative to laser excitation, determine temperature at each delay, and track phonon branch changes versus temperature; gating is compatible with timescales down to 50 ns.

Result: For graphite, the optical phonon branch energy decreases with increasing temperature, accompanied by increased peak width, consistent with temperature-dependent phonon renormalisation and anharmonic scattering.

Conclusion: The study establishes time-resolved phonon dispersion spectroscopy via vibrational EELS and offers a route to probe temperature-dependent phonon dynamics at the nanoscale with simultaneous energy and momentum resolution, extensible to faster dynamics.

Abstract: Lattice vibrations govern the propagation of heat in materials, and investigating their dynamics through time-resolved phonon dispersion measurements would provide insight into these excitations and their interactions. However, time-resolved measurements that directly resolve the phonon dispersion at the nanoscale have not been previously demonstrated. Momentum-resolved vibrational electron energy loss spectroscopy (EELS) provides access to phonon energies and momenta with nanoscale spatial resolution. Here, we combine momentum-resolved vibrational EELS with pulsed laser heating and synchronised gated electron detection to observe the phonon dispersion of graphite during controlled heating and cooling on the microsecond timescale. Recording both the gain and loss spectra at defined times relative to laser excitation, we determine the temperature at each time delay and follow changes to the phonon branches as a function of temperature. The momentum-resolved spectra reveal a decrease in energy of the optical phonon branch with increasing temperature accompanied by an increase in peak width. These changes are consistent with temperature-dependent phonon renormalisation and scattering associated with anharmonic interactions. The approach is compatible with detector gating on timescales down to 50 ns, providing a route towards extending these measurements to faster dynamics. This work demonstrates time-resolved phonon dispersion spectroscopy using vibrational EELS and provides a route towards probing temperature-dependent phonon dynamics at the nanoscale with simultaneous energy and momentum resolution.

</details>


### [244] [Composition-controlled optical fingerprints and anisotropy of self-trapped excitons in double perovskites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18665)
*Simone Grillo,Offir Zachs Maishlos,Liya Koren,Rotem Strassberg,Rachel Lifer,Edi Maizenberg,Umberto De Giovannini,Angel Rubio,Yehonadav Bekenstein,Ofer Neufeld*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Sb-doped Cs2BInCl6 (B = Na to K) nanocrystals are studied by optical spectroscopy and ab initio calculations, revealing that B-site alloying tunes self-trapped exciton (STE) photoluminescence while absorption stays fixed, and that polarization anisotropy probes the STE wavefunction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Double perovskites are attractive for photocatalysis and optoelectronics, but their soft lattices promote strong exciton-phonon coupling and self-trapped excitons, giving broad red-shifted photoluminescence. The roles of doping versus alloying in governing these excitonic processes, and how to probe STE wavefunctions experimentally, remain unclear.

Method: Optical spectroscopy combined with ab initio calculations on lead-free, Sb-doped Cs2BInCl6 nanocrystals with B-site alloying from Na to K; time-resolved polarization anisotropy measurements compared with theoretical predictions.

Result: Alloying tunes PL energy from ~2.48 to ~2.70 eV while absorption near 3.8 eV is nearly unchanged. Sb doping enables bright radiative recombination channels and delocalizes the excitonic wavefunction. STEs are identified as the origin of the large PL red shift, linked to an upward shift of dopant-hybridized in-gap states. Na-to-K substitution selectively alters STE lattice distortion, explaining the composition-dependent PL versus invariant absorption. Theory predicts pronounced PL polarization anisotropy reflecting the STE wavefunction's spatial structure; time-resolved anisotropy measurements closely agree, providing experimental fingerprints of the excitonic wavefunction and its evolution with alloying.

Conclusion: The work disentangles the roles of doping and alloying in lead-free double perovskites and establishes polarization-resolved spectroscopy as a probe of STE wavefunctions.

Abstract: Double perovskites (DPs) offer a compositionally versatile platform for photocatalysis and optoelectronics, but their soft lattices favor strong exciton-phonon coupling and self-trapped excitons (STEs), which produce broad, red-shifted photoluminescence (PL). Here, we combine optical spectroscopy and ab initio calculations to study lead-free, Sb-doped Cs2BInCl6 nanocrystals with B-site alloying from Na to K. Alloying tunes the PL energy from about 2.48 to 2.70 eV, while absorption features near 3.8 eV remain nearly unchanged. Calculations reveal that Sb doping enables bright radiative recombination channels and delocalizes the excitonic wavefunction. We identify STEs responsible for the large PL red shift, associated with an upward shift of dopant-hybridized in-gap states. Na-to-K substitution selectively changes the STE lattice distortion, explaining why PL varies strongly with composition while absorption does not. Theory further predicts pronounced PL polarization anisotropy that reflects the STE wavefunction's spatial structure and depends on B-site composition. Time-resolved anisotropy measurements closely agree with these predictions, providing experimental fingerprints of the excitonic wavefunction and its evolution with alloying. Together, these results disentangle the roles of doping and alloying in lead-free DPs and establish polarization-resolved spectroscopy as a probe of STE wavefunctions.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.dis-nn'></div>

# cond-mat.dis-nn [[Back]](#toc)

### [245] [On physical implementability of reinforcement-learning-driven reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14131)
*Eiji Konishi*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: A negative result: in a physical neural network hardware implementation of reward-driven reasoning, the existence of a chain of thought in the dynamics contradicts the Lyapunov stability of the system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement-learning-driven reasoning has been intensively studied in the machine learning community since around 2025, motivating exploration of its hardware realization in physical neural network models.

Method: Analyze the hardware implementation of the RL-driven reasoning process in a physical neural network model subject to a reward external potential energy for a given reward, and examine the dynamical implications of a chain of thought.

Result: A negative result is obtained: the presence of a chain of thought in the dynamics is incompatible with the Lyapunov stability of the system.

Conclusion: Physical implementations of reward-driven reasoning with chains of thought are fundamentally constrained, since chain-of-thought dynamics contradict Lyapunov stability, indicating an inherent hardware limitation.

Abstract: In the machine learning community, the reinforcement-learning-driven reasoning process has been intensively studied since around 2025. In this article, we present a negative result on the hardware implementation of this reinforcement-learning-driven reasoning process in a physical neural network model in the presence of a reward external potential energy for a given reward. In this hardware implementation, the existence of a chain of thought in dynamics contradicts the Lyapunov stability of the system.

</details>


### [246] [Weyl's theorem and the theoretical origin of Boson peak in two-dimensional scalar spring network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15318)
*Cunyuan Jiang*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This work models amorphous solids as a zero-temperature scalar spring network and mathematically demonstrates that topological disorder shifts the vibrational density of states (VDOS) toward low frequencies, explaining the anomalous low-frequency VDOS universally observed in amorphous solids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The origin of the anomalous low-frequency vibrational density of states universally observed in amorphous solids has been debated for decades, and prior explanations often relied on perturbations or phenomenological assumptions.

Method: Amorphous solids are modeled as a zero-temperature scalar spring network—the simplest model capturing both amorphous structure and anomalous VDOS features. Disorder is defined via the topological connectivity of the network, and the analysis rests solely on Weyl's theorem for Hermitian matrices, free of perturbation and phenomenological assumptions.

Result: It is mathematically shown that disorder can induce a shift of the VDOS toward low frequencies, thereby accumulating the anomalous part at low frequencies.

Conclusion: Stripping away complex factors, the work reveals at the level of mathematical principles a profound and clean mechanism for the origin of the anomalous VDOS in amorphous solids; the framework's validity and universality are general, resting only on Weyl's theorem.

Abstract: The origin of the anomalous low-frequency vibrational density of states (VDOS) universally observed in amorphous solids has been debated for decades. In this work, we model amorphous solids as a zero-temperature scalar spring network, the simplest model that captures both the amorphous structure and the features of the anomalous VDOS. Using this scalar network, we mathematically demonstrate that disorder can lead to a shift of the VDOS toward low frequencies, thereby accumulating the anomalous part there. Disorder is defined through the topological connectivity of the network. The validity and universality of the theoretical framework are general and valuable for real-world scenarios because it rests only on Weyl's theorem for Hermitian matrices and is free of perturbation and phenomenological assumptions. This work strips away complex factors and reveals, at the level of mathematical principles, a profound and clean mechanism for the origin of the anomalous VDOS in amorphous solids.

</details>


### [247] [Eigenstate transitions and critical transport in 3D Aubry-André model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15842)
*Pragna Das,Lev Vidmar,Miroslav Hopjan*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: Numerical study of finite-size commensurate 3D Aubry-André model reveals two critical points — a real-space localization-delocalization transition (analogous to 3D Anderson) and a dual momentum-space transition (no Anderson analog) — both showing quantum ergodicity-to-localization critical hallmarks with sub-diffusive dynamical exponent z = 3.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The 3D Aubry-André model is a quasiperiodic analog of the 3D Anderson model, but its critical behavior, especially whether it hosts multiple critical points and how these relate to quantum ergodicity and localization transitions, has not been fully characterized. The authors aim to determine the number and nature of critical points and to test universal critical-point relations.

Method: Finite-size commensurate approximations of the 3D Aubry-André model are studied numerically. Spectral measures (gap ratio, spectral form factor) and wave-function measures (inverse participation ratio, participation entropy) are used with scaling analysis to locate critical points. Critical-point properties are tested against the universal relation between eigenspectrum compressibility χ and eigenstate fractal dimension d^(1), and against critical wave-packet dynamics governed by the critical dynamical exponent z.

Result: Two critical points are identified via scaling analysis: a real-space localization-delocalization transition analogous to the 3D Anderson model, and a localization-delocalization transition in dual momentum space with no Anderson analog. Their values are estimated from both spectral and wave-function measures. Both critical points display hallmarks of quantum ergodicity-localization transitions: eigenstates and eigenspectrum satisfy the mutual universal χ–d^(1) relation, and wave-packet dynamics obey the critical dynamical exponent with sub-diffusive value z = 3.

Conclusion: The 3D Aubry-André model hosts two distinct critical points, one analogous to the 3D Anderson transition and one unique to quasiperiodic systems in dual momentum space. Both behave as critical points between quantum ergodicity and localization, satisfying universal spectral-eigenstate relations and exhibiting sub-diffusive dynamics with z = 3, establishing strong parallels with the 3D Anderson model.

Abstract: We numerically study spectral and wave-function properties, as well as time evolution, in finite-size commensurate approximations of the 3D Aubry-André model. We explicitly show, through scaling analysis, the existence of two critical points. One of the critical points is a localization-delocalization transition in real-space, similar to the critical point in the 3D Anderson model. The other is a localization-delocalization transition in a dual momentum space, which does not have any analog in the 3D Anderson model. We estimate their values from spectral measures, such as the gap ratio and the spectral form factor, as well as from wave-function measures, such as the inverse participation ratio and the participation entropy. Additionally, we demonstrate that both critical points exhibit hallmarks of critical points between quantum ergodicity and localization. First, the eigenstates and the eigenspectrum at the critical point fulfill the mutual universal relation between the eigenspectrum compressibility $χ$ and the eigenstate fractal dimension $d^{(1)}$ [S. Jiricek et al., Proc. Nat. Acad. Sci. 123, e2518027123 (2026)]. Second, the dynamics of the wave packet at the critical point is governed by the critical dynamical exponent $z$, characterized by the spectral fractal dimension and the dimensionality of the lattice [M. Hopjan and L. Vidmar, Phys. Rev. Lett. 135, 060401 (2025)]. Thus, similarly to the 3D Anderson model, the critical dynamical exponent is sub-diffusive with the value of $z=3$.

</details>


### [248] [Quantum Localization in Geometric Complex Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15874)
*Jan Lewenstein-Sanpera,Tobias Grass,M. Àngeles Serrano,Bruno Juliá-Díaz*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: This work shows that quantum localization of single-particle states can emerge purely from network topology, without energetic disorder. Using the geometric soft configuration model S^1, the authors find that small-world networks support extended states while non-small-world networks localize states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum localization is typically attributed to external potentials, such as onsite random disorder in Anderson localization. However, some systems have complex geometries and may reproduce localization without external potentials, motivating a study of whether underlying topology alone can induce localization.

Method: The authors study single-particle state localization on the geometric soft configuration model S^1. By tuning the model's temperature and heterogeneity parameters, they generate different structural patterns and focus on the transition from a small-world to a non-small-world regime.

Result: Small-world networks tend to exhibit extended states that spread over the whole system size, whereas non-small-world networks tend to localize states. This demonstrates that complex connectivity can play the role normally assigned to random energetic disorder.

Conclusion: Network topology alone, specifically the small-world to non-small-world transition, can govern quantum localization, offering a topological analogue to disorder-induced localization.

Abstract: Quantum localization, the phenomenon by which wave diffusion is halted, here understood as the superposition of propagating waves, can have many different origins, such as onsite random potentials in Anderson localization. However, some systems exhibit complex geometries and could potentially reproduce similar results without external potentials. In this work, we study how localization of single particle states is affected by the underlying topology of a simple but highly predictive complex network model: the geometric soft configuration model $\mathbb S^1$. By tuning the model's temperature and heterogeneity parameters, we are able to generate different structural patterns and focus on the transition from a small-world to a non-small-world regime. Our study suggests that small-world networks tend to have extended states, which spread the whole system's size, in contrast to non-small-world networks which tend to localize, showing how complex connectivity can play the role assigned to random energetic disorder.

</details>


### [249] [Orbital cancellation and reentrant localization in a quasiperiodic trimer ladder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16197)
*Xin Wang,Pei-Jie Chang,Quanfeng Lu,Muxi Zheng,Dong Ruan,Gui-Lu long*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: Uniform orbital detuning counteracts quasiperiodic modulation in a trimer ladder, restoring spatial extent of lower molecular bands through successive avoided crossings and yielding two reentrant high-participation windows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
